Yes, potentially—but I **cannot determine firmware compatibility from the exterior photos alone**.

I can identify this as a **Gyroor G5**, manufactured by Shenzhen Chitado Technology. Your label specifies **37 V / 4.0 Ah**, and the FCC records identify the G5 family as G5/G580/G581. The FCC filing is particularly useful because it includes **internal photographs**. citeturn1view0

The older hoverboard-controller hacks you're thinking of—especially **EFeru's `hoverboard-firmware-hack-FOC`**—work with particular STM32/GD32 controller-board families. They aren't universally compatible just because the board drives two hoverboard motors. The hack replaces the original firmware and can expose UART/throttle/torque/speed control. citeturn0search22turn0search25

### What determines whether your G5 is hackable

We need to identify the **main motor-control PCB**, specifically:

- MCU marking — ideally something like **STM32F103**, **GD32F103**, etc.
- PCB model/revision printed on the board.
- Whether it has accessible **SWD/SWCLK/SWDIO** programming pads.
- Gate-driver arrangement and MOSFET layout.
- Hall-sensor and current-sense topology.

There are also newer "Gen2" hoverboard boards that aren't compatible with the original EFeru target but have separate reverse-engineering efforts. So a non-STM32F103 board isn't automatically a dead end.

Importantly, your G5 dates from roughly the right era to warrant investigation: its FCC application is dated **February 25, 2022**. citeturn1view0 I haven't found evidence establishing that the G5 uses one of the known hack-compatible controller boards, so I don't want to assume it does.

**Next step:** open the bottom shell and send me clear photos of the **entire main controller PCB, both sides if accessible**, plus close-ups of the largest MCU and any board numbers. Don't remove or desolder anything yet.

With those photos I can identify the MCU and board architecture and then search specifically for that controller revision against EFeru, Gen2.x, and related hoverboard firmware projects. That should give us a fairly definitive **directly flashable / adaptable / unsupported** answer. memcite

These photos help, but the **MCU marking is still too blurred to identify**. The architecture is interesting: this does not look like the classic single-mainboard hoverboard layout; it appears to have separate front/rear controller electronics, and the silkscreen `APP 后桥` translates approximately to **“APP rear axle/rear bridge.”**

The critical component is the **large square QFP IC in the center-left of your second photo**. That is almost certainly the controller MCU. The four exposed pads immediately to its upper-left are also interesting—they could plausibly be programming/debug pads, but the traces need to be examined before calling them SWD.

There are two major firmware families worth checking. The original urlEFeru hoverboard-firmware-hack-FOChttps://github.com/EFeru/hoverboard-firmware-hack-FOC targets several STM32-based hoverboard boards and is normally flashed through SWD with an ST-Link. citeturn0search35turn0search37 Newer/different controller generations have also been reverse-engineered separately, notably urlRoboDurden Hoverboard-Firmware-Hack-Gen2.xhttps://github.com/RoboDurden/Hoverboard-Firmware-Hack-Gen2.x. So even if this isn't an EFeru-compatible board, it may still be hackable.

**Get one sharp, straight-down close-up of that large square MCU.** I need to be able to read every line laser-marked on it. Also photograph any model/revision markings elsewhere on the PCB. Once we have the exact MCU number, I can search this specific Chinese controller family and determine whether existing firmware supports it, whether one of the Gen2 ports is close enough to adapt, or whether we'd be looking at a new firmware port.

Yes. The coating complicates identification, but these new photos actually make the board **more promising**.

Your PCB marking is clearly:

**`OL20180703-V4`**

And the architecture is a **split-board hoverboard controller**: each wheel has its own motor-control PCB, with the two controllers communicating with each other. That is much closer to the hardware targeted by the **Gen2/Gen2.1 hoverboard firmware projects** than the original EFeru single-mainboard design.

In particular, Gen2.1 explicitly targets hoverboards with **two identically equipped mainboards connected by USART**, and those boards use a **GD32F130C6** MCU. citeturn0search6 Your large MCU also appears to be a 48-pin LQFP device, consistent with that general class, although the coating means I **cannot identify it as a GD32F130C6 from the photograph**.

More importantly, look immediately **above-left of the MCU in your second photo**:

**there is a boxed row of four exposed round pads.**

That is exactly the sort of location/layout I'd investigate for **SWD programming pads**. The Gen2 boards expose GND, 3.3 V, SWDIO and SWCLK for an ST-Link. citeturn0search3turn0search6

The epoxy/conformal coating over the MCU isn't therefore a major obstacle. **We don't actually need to read the MCU marking first.** We can electrically identify the programming interface.

I would do this next:

1. **Don't scrape the MCU yet.**
2. With the board completely unpowered, use continuity mode to determine which of those four pads is **GND**.
3. Determine whether another pad connects to the board's **3.3-V rail**—for example the MCU's decoupling capacitors.
4. That potentially leaves two pads corresponding to **SWDIO and SWCLK**.
5. Once we've mapped those four pads, an ST-Link can attempt to identify the target **without erasing or programming it**.

That is preferable to chemically or mechanically removing the coating.

Also, don't connect the ST-Link's 3.3-V output to power this controller. The older firmware documentation specifically warns against powering hoverboard boards from the programmer; use the programmer voltage connection only as appropriate for reference/target sensing and power the controller normally when required. citeturn0search2

### One particularly useful clue

The Gen2.1 project says its boards use **GD32F130C6** and have two identical motor-controller boards. citeturn0search6 That's sufficiently similar that I think we should compare its PCB photographs/schematic against your `OL20180703-V4` board **component-by-component and connector-by-connector**.

If they match electrically, we may be able to identify the MCU and SWD pads **without removing any coating at all**.

I can do that comparison next and try to determine whether `OL20180703-V4` is a known Gen2/Gen2.1 board or a closely related derivative.

Yes. I searched specifically for the **Gyroor G5 / Shenzhen Chitado G5**, rather than generic hoverboards. There is substantially more documentation than I initially found.

### The exact model is documented by the FCC

Your board is a **Gyroor G5 manufactured by Shenzhen Chitado Technology Co., Ltd.** Its FCC ID is **2A4L5-G5**, approved February 25, 2022. The filing includes the user manual, test report, external photos and—most useful for this project—**internal photographs**. citeturn0search1turn0search3

urlGyroor G5 FCC filing and internal documentationhttps://fccid.io/2A4L5-G5

There's an especially useful discovery: **G5, G580 and G581 are electrically the same machine.** Chitado's FCC model-family declaration explicitly says the three models have the same:

> circuit, PCB layout, electrical parts, materials and wiring structures

and differ only in color. citeturn0search33

So when hunting for controller information, firmware, replacement boards, teardown photos, etc., we should search **G5 + G580 + G581**, not just G5.

### Your particular generation

The documentation also explains an apparent date discrepancy. The G5 manual carries a **©2019** notice, while this particular FCC application was made in 2022. The manual itself warns that multiple G5 versions exist and that the manufacturer changed design/functionality without necessarily changing the G5 designation. citeturn0search5turn0search7

That matters enormously for firmware hacking.

Your controller PCB says:

**`OL20180703-V4`**

So the controller design itself appears to have a **2018-era board designation**, although I can't establish from that string alone that `20180703` is definitely a design date.

The FCC-certified 2022 G5/G580/G581 uses Bluetooth at **2.402–2.480 GHz**, which corresponds with the APP circuitry/connectors we're seeing on your boards. citeturn0search3

### Most useful finding for the firmware investigation

The FCC certification isn't merely for a similarly named retail product. Its test report explicitly identifies:

**Brand:** GYROOR  
**Model:** G5  
**Series models:** G581, G580  
**Manufacturer:** Shenzhen Chitado Technology

citeturn0search34

So the FCC internal photographs give us an authoritative reference for the factory electronics.

What I **have not found yet** is anything indexed on the web connecting `OL20180703-V4` directly to EFeru/Gen2 firmware, nor a search result identifying the MCU on that PCB. Searching the complete board number produces essentially nothing useful. So I would not yet claim that it's a known Gen2 board.

However, we now have a much better route: **extract the high-resolution FCC internal photographs and compare their controller PCB against your `OL20180703-V4` board**, then broaden the search to **G580/G581 controller/mainboard/PCB/firmware**. If another G5-family revision exposes an uncoated MCU, that could give us the processor identification without touching the epoxy on yours.

That's the direction I'd pursue next. memcite

Yes. I did an image-specific search, and there is a useful lead.

The closest documented dual-controller boards I found use a **4-pad SWD header immediately adjacent to the MCU**, very similar to the four exposed pads visible on your `OL20180703-V4`.

image_group{"layout":"carousel","aspect_ratio":"1:1","image_refs":["turn1image1","turn1image0","turn0image2"]}

On the documented GD32 hoverboard sideboard in the **first image**, the programming connections are:

- **3.3 V**
- **SWCLK = PA14**
- **GND**
- **SWDIO = PA13**

The annotated image explicitly identifies **PA13/SWDIO** and **PA14/SWCLK**. citeturn1image1

More importantly, another GD32F130 hoverboard project documents exactly the same concept: a **four-pin debugging header containing 3V3, SWDIO, SWCLK and GND**, located beside the MCU. citeturn1image0

### For your board

Your four pads are here:

**[ ○ ○ ○ ○ ]**

immediately above/left of the MCU in your photo. I would **not yet assume their physical order matches either reference board**. The PCB routing is different.

But we can identify them conclusively without removing the epoxy:

1. Find **GND** by continuity to battery negative/ground plane.
2. Find **3V3** by continuity/resistance to the MCU's VDD rail, or measure it with the controller powered.
3. Of the two remaining pads, one should route to **MCU PA13 = SWDIO**.
4. The other should route to **MCU PA14 = SWCLK**.

Since we know the MCU package, we can use its package pin numbers to identify PA13/PA14 directly and then continuity-test from those legs to the four pads. That eliminates guessing entirely.

The search also found a documented **GD32F130C8T6 Gen2.1.5 controller**, and its developer specifically reverse-engineered the board by probing MCU pins to the rest of the PCB. citeturn1view0

If you give me the **exact MCU designation we identified earlier** (it isn't present in the conversation context I can currently retrieve), I can pull its datasheet/package diagram and tell you **exactly which physical MCU legs are SWDIO, SWCLK, NRST, VDD and GND**. Then you can buzz those four pads out with the meter in a couple of minutes. memcite

Yes — and your observation is important. **The square pad does not universally mean 3.3 V.** It denotes **pin 1 of that particular PCB header**, while the function assigned to pin 1 varies between controller revisions.

I found several documented hoverboard examples:

- One Gen2.x implementation explicitly warns that some boards are ordered **GND–3V3–SWDIO–SWCLK**, while others are **GND–SWDIO–SWCLK–3V3**. citeturn0search1
- Another GD32 hoverboard sideboard has the order **3.3V–SWCLK–SWDIO–GND**. citeturn0search5
- A documented older STM32 controller actually has **SWDIO on the square pad**, not 3.3 V. citeturn0search2
- The Gen2.x documentation therefore explicitly recommends identifying **GND and 3.3 V with a multimeter**, rather than trusting header orientation. citeturn0search4turn0search1

image_group{"layout":"carousel","aspect_ratio":"1:1","image_refs":["turn0image0","turn0image3"]}

For **your `OL20180703-V4`**, I agree that the square pad being closest to the MCU is a useful clue, but we shouldn't assign it 3.3 V from geometry alone.

The nice part is that **GND and 3.3 V are trivial to establish electrically**. With the board powered normally, measure each of the four pads relative to battery negative/logic ground. One should be ~0 V and one ~3.3 V. That leaves exactly two unknown pads. Since the MCU we've identified uses **PA13 = SWDIO and PA14 = SWCLK**, those remaining two can simply be tried in both orientations; reversing SWDIO/SWCLK won't damage anything. The Gen2 documentation specifically suggests this approach. citeturn0search1

So I would **not connect the ST-Link yet**. First give me the four voltages, numbered from the **square pad outward toward the round pad farthest from the MCU**:

**1 □ — 2 ○ — 3 ○ — 4 ○**

Measure **1, 2, 3, 4 → GND** with the controller powered. From those four measurements we should immediately identify VCC and GND, leaving only SWDIO/SWCLK to resolve.

ASSISTANT | 2026-08-25T06:04:08.731002+00:00
Node: 0048753e-ec52-4aa9-9ce1-f2905ddc034b
Parent node: 47ab268d-17b5-4042-9614-4e343f78569f
========================================================================
Yes, essentially, but I would **current-limit the bench supply and identify GND before applying power**.

For this 37 V nominal system, I would proceed as follows:

1. **Board unpowered:** use continuity/resistance to identify which of the four SWD pads is GND. Check against battery negative / the large `B-` connection. You don't need power for this.
2. Connect the bench supply to the controller's normal **B+ and B− inputs**, observing polarity. Start with a conservative current limit, perhaps **100–300 mA**. Use the board's normal operating voltage; you don't need exactly 37 V if its normal battery range is roughly 30–42 V.
3. With it powered, measure the remaining three SWD pads relative to the GND pad. One should measure approximately **3.3 V**. That identifies **VCC/VTref**.
4. Power it back down. You now have:
   **GND | 3.3 V | unknown | unknown**
   where the two unknowns should be **SWDIO and SWCLK**.
5. Connect the ST-Link:
   - ST-Link **GND → identified GND**
   - ST-Link **3.3/VTref → identified 3.3-V pad**
   - ST-Link **SWDIO/SWCLK → the two unknown pads**
6. **Do not use the ST-Link to supply the controller's 3.3-V rail.** The controller remains powered from the bench supply; the 3.3-V connection is the logic/reference connection.
7. Attempt an SWD connection. If it doesn't detect the MCU, power down and **swap SWDIO and SWCLK**. That reversal shouldn't damage the MCU.

One additional precaution: before connecting the ST-Link's 3.3-V/VTref pin, tell me **which ST-Link you have** (ST-Link V2 clone/dongle, genuine V2, V2-1, V3, etc.). Some cheap ST-Link V2 clones have their `3.3V` header pin wired as a **power output rather than a true VTref input**, so the correct connection depends on the programmer.

is there a boot mode for the mcu or is tthe ic that looks like a bios chip able to be read in such a mode?

========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-08-25T06:07:13.501667+00:00
Node: b9436e79-2938-4ae5-8960-69d4629fbbc6
Parent node: 7aaf62f1-f283-4639-9dac-2d7c7d99ecc5
========================================================================
Potentially both, but they are two different things.

If the MCU we identified is the **GD32F130-family device**, it has a factory **system bootloader** in ROM. Its boot mode is selected through the MCU's **BOOT0** state during reset. If BOOT0 can be pulled high, the chip can boot the factory loader instead of the hoverboard firmware. Depending on the exact GD32F130 variant, that can provide a serial programming path independent of SWD. We would need to use the exact part number we identified to establish the supported bootloader interface and BOOT0 pin rather than assume it.

The small 8-pin IC that looks like a PC BIOS flash chip is also worth identifying. On these boards it could be an **SPI/I²C EEPROM or SPI NOR flash**, but its appearance alone doesn't establish that. If it is something like a `25Qxx`, `25Lxx`, etc., then yes: it can generally be read directly with an appropriate SPI programmer. However, that **doesn't necessarily contain the motor-controller firmware**. On GD32/STM32 hoverboard controllers, executable firmware is normally stored in the MCU's internal flash; an external 8-pin device may instead contain configuration, calibration, Bluetooth data, or something unrelated.

So there are potentially **three routes into this board**:

- **SWD** — best route because it provides debug/programming access and may allow us to dump the existing MCU flash before changing anything.
- **MCU ROM bootloader** — potentially useful if SWD is locked or inconvenient, provided we locate BOOT0 and the appropriate UART/interface.
- **8-pin memory IC** — can potentially be dumped independently, but first we need its exact marking to determine what it is and what information it contains.

Before we flash anything, I strongly favor attempting to **dump every readable nonvolatile memory first**. That preserves the factory firmware/configuration and could also tell us quite a bit about how the two controllers communicate.

If you send a sharp close-up of the **8-pin IC** and remind me of the **exact MCU part number we identified**, I can look up both datasheets and map out **BOOT0, factory bootloader interface, SWD pins, and what that 8-pin chip actually is** rather than making assumptions.

Node: d4045d47-8552-4529-bd06-a6452f82b9aa
Parent node: b9436e79-2938-4ae5-8960-69d4629fbbc6

========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-08-25T06:10:38.650689+00:00
Node: afc7ec83-f378-48df-89f8-6adf8ef43da7
Parent node: 8ff5714c-8810-4d18-804e-d95a5154c8b7
========================================================================
Yes — that's the distinction you were getting at. **BOOT0 can sometimes provide a way to regain programming/debug access when the normal application interferes with SWD, but it normally does not defeat the MCU's actual flash read-protection setting.**

For the **GD32F130 family**, there are three relevant protection states:

- **RDP/Protection 0:** flash is readable through SWD.
- **Low protection / RDP1:** SWD can still communicate with the MCU, but reading main flash from debug, SRAM boot, or the system bootloader is prohibited. Removing protection causes a **mass erase**.
- **High protection / RDP2:** debug access itself is disabled, and the protection isn't normally reversible. citeturn0search22turn0search4

BOOT0 is nevertheless useful. Pulling BOOT0 appropriately at reset prevents the existing hoverboard application from starting. There are documented GD32F130 cases where doing this allowed OpenOCD to reconnect to a device that appeared inaccessible when booting its application. citeturn0search1

So for **our controller**, I'd try things in this order:

1. Find the four SWD pads and attempt a normal ST-Link connection.
2. If it connects, **check the protection/option bytes before issuing any erase/unprotect command.**
3. If SWD won't connect reliably, locate **BOOT0** and try connecting while the MCU is held out of the normal application.
4. If it reports read protection, **do not click "Remove Protection"/"Disable RDP"** if we want the original firmware—the normal consequence at RDP1 is erasing main flash. citeturn0search4

And there's an interesting wrinkle specific to this chip family: security researchers have published **readout-protection bypass vulnerabilities affecting tested GD32F130C8T6 devices**, including even RDP2 under certain silicon revisions. citeturn0search8 That's considerably more involved than ordinary BOOT0 access, but it means that if preserving the original firmware becomes important and this controller turns out to be protected, **we should investigate the exact MCU/silicon revision before accepting a destructive mass erase**.

So yes: your ECU analogy is relevant, but I'd distinguish **"application prevents debugger attachment"** from **"flash RDP is actually enabled."** BOOT0 readily helps with the former; the latter is a separate security mechanism.

Good. **Pin 4 being GND gives us one definite anchor.** And the MCU marking is now readable enough to confirm the relevant pinout as the **GD32F130Cx LQFP48 family**. GigaDevice specifies **PA13/pin 34 = SWDIO** and **PA14/pin 37 = SWCLK**; VSS is pin 47 and VDD is pin 48. citeturn0search12turn0search1

Your underside photo is particularly useful because the four programming pads have visible traces. I would number them exactly as you have:

**1 = square pad → 2 → 3 → 4 = GND**

I would **not yet assign 1–3 by visual trace-following alone**. The conformal coating and vias make that unnecessarily uncertain.

Since pin 4 is confirmed GND, the fastest definitive test is:

- Board powered normally.
- Meter black lead on **pad 4**.
- Measure DC voltage on **pads 1, 2 and 3**.
- One should be approximately **3.3 V**.
- The remaining two are overwhelmingly likely to be SWDIO/SWCLK.

Then we don't actually have to distinguish those last two with a meter. Connect the ST-Link with GND and target voltage/reference established and try the remaining pair as SWDIO/SWCLK. If it doesn't connect, **swap those two**.

There is also a second way to prove them from the MCU itself. On this LQFP48:

**pin 34 = PA13/SWDIO**  
**pin 37 = PA14/SWCLK**  
**pin 44 = BOOT0**  
**pin 47 = VSS/GND**  
**pin 48 = VDD/3.3 V** citeturn0search12

The dot on the MCU marks pin 1, so we can count around the package and continuity-test **pad → MCU pin**. That would give us the complete four-pad pinout without applying power.

And your underside photograph may make that easier than probing the coated MCU legs: those SWD traces appear accessible.

**Give me the voltage from pad 1, 2 and 3 to pad 4.** With that, we'll have three of the four functions established and can move directly to the ST-Link.

Does the board have to be powered to get continuity to the +

========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-08-25T06:42:10.530025+00:00
Node: aba6dcc2-71bd-48b9-9d87-cf39fe99d4b1
Parent node: bbb21fd0-8313-48ce-b280-ddbde69f4480
========================================================================
No. If you're checking **continuity**, the board should actually be **unpowered**.

If one of those four pads is the **3.3-V/VDD pad**, you can establish it by continuity to another known point on the 3.3-V rail. For this GD32F130 LQFP48, that includes the MCU's **VDD pins** and their nearby decoupling capacitors.

However, there's an important difference:

- **GND:** usually very easy to identify with continuity because there's a large common ground plane.
- **3.3 V:** may **not give a clean continuity beep** to arbitrary components because regulators, resistors, ferrites, etc. can separate different supply nets.
- **Measuring 3.3 V:** requires the board to be powered.

So since you've already established **pad 4 = GND**, I wouldn't rely on continuity to identify VCC unless you can probe a known MCU VDD pin. The simplest test is to power the controller normally and measure **pads 1–3 relative to pad 4**. Whichever gives approximately **3.3 V** is the 3.3-V/VTref pad.

Do **not** use resistance/continuity mode while the board is powered.

That means **pin 1 is almost certainly not the 3.3 V SWD reference pad**.

If pad 1 has continuity directly to **B+**, then it is tied to the battery-positive net or to a branch that is effectively the same node. On a 37 V nominal hoverboard, that would make it unsafe to connect pad 1 to an ST-Link 3.3 V/VTref pin.

So, based on what we now know:

- **Pin 4 = GND**
- **Pin 1 = B+ / battery-positive-related net**
- **Pins 2 and 3 are the remaining candidates for SWDIO / SWCLK or possibly one SWD signal plus 3.3 V**

That also tells us this 4-pad row may **not be a conventional 4-wire SWD header**. It could be a factory programming/test header carrying battery supply, ground, and two logic signals.

Next test: with the board **unpowered**, check resistance/continuity from **pins 2 and 3** to the MCU’s **PA13/SWDIO and PA14/SWCLK** pins. If you can’t reliably probe those legs because of the coating, then power the board normally and measure **pins 2 and 3 relative to pin 4**. If neither sits at ~3.3 V, that strongly supports the idea that the header is simply:

**B+ | SWDIO | SWCLK | GND**

with no separate 3.3 V pad.

That arrangement would actually make sense for a factory jig: power the controller from the header at battery voltage and program through SWD referenced to board ground.

Do **not** connect the ST-Link’s 3.3 V pin to pad 1.

- pad 1 → ?
- pad 2 → ?
- pad 3 → ?

- It is specifically for **hoverboards with two mainboards connected by USART**, which matches your left/right controller arrangement. citeturn846232view0
- It uses the **GD32F130C8**, whereas the older hack used STM32F103. citeturn846232view0
- It documents an **ST-Link programming header** with **GND, 3V3, SWDIO and SWCLK**. citeturn846232view0
- It also states that a controller that has never been flashed before is typically **locked** and must be unlocked with ST-Link/OpenOCD before flashing. citeturn846232view0

So your board does appear to be in the same general **Gen2 hardware family**, even though the PCB layout is not identical.

**Pad 1 = probably 3.3 V**

but I would still verify pad 1 with a powered voltage measurement before connecting an ST-Link.

ASSISTANT | 2026-08-25T07:17:10.157903+00:00
Node: 3fcf6fde-5d42-48a4-a27b-cba4aff3f104
Parent node: 3b37fa98-7a28-40e8-9c94-66bbec80958e
========================================================================
I checked the repo itself and its issues. **I don't see any procedure in that repo for retrieving/backing up the factory firmware before unlocking.** In fact, the evidence points the other direction.

The README says that a controller that has never been flashed is **“locked”**, and instructs the user to **unlock the flash** using ST-LINK Utility or OpenOCD before flashing. citeturn0view0

The important problem is what “unlock” entails. Issue #12 contains an example from someone using a GD32F130C8T6 board. They say they:

> “unlocked the flash by resetting the option bytes, cleared the memory”

before trying to program the replacement firmware. citeturn0search2

So the project's normal workflow is effectively:

**factory protected MCU → unlock → factory flash erased → install custom firmware**

rather than:

**factory protected MCU → dump stock firmware → unlock → install custom firmware**

### But I would NOT unlock yours yet

Because we specifically want to preserve the Gyroor firmware, I think we should first connect the ST-Link **non-destructively** and determine exactly what protection state this GD32 reports.

The repo itself doesn't appear to have explored recovering the original protected image. It was written around replacing the firmware, so preserving the factory image wasn't really necessary for that project. citeturn0view0

And there's a useful confirmation from issue #13: another user with a split-board **GD32F130C6** says that after unlocking the controllers they could flash them and subsequently **read the flashed contents back**. That suggests ordinary flash reads work once protection is absent; the obstacle is specifically the factory protection. citeturn0search9

So when we get your SWD connection established, **don't press Unlock, Disable Read Protection, Reset Option Bytes, Mass Erase, or anything similar.**

First just interrogate it. If ST-Link/OpenOCD can identify the GD32 but refuses a read because of protection, we stop there. At that point we can investigate the **GD32F130 read-protection bypass methods** we discussed earlier and see whether one applies to your exact chip/revision.

That gives us the best chance of obtaining a **64-KB/32-KB factory binary from each of your two Gyroor controllers before modifying either one**. memcite

That changes the objective considerably.

For an **RC mower**, the stock hoverboard firmware is probably not something we ultimately want to retain operationally. The useful target is direct, deterministic control of the two wheel motors: commanded forward/reverse, differential steering, braking, current/torque limiting, and ideally telemetry.

The Gen2 firmware you found is therefore potentially a very good basis. Because your hoverboard already has **one GD32 controller per wheel**, the mower could retain both original power stages and motors while replacing only their firmware/control scheme.

I would still try to **dump the factory firmware before unlocking**, simply because it costs us little to attempt and gives us a recovery/reference image. But if RDP prevents it and bypassing RDP becomes a substantial project, I wouldn't consider preservation essential for the mower.

More importantly, before flashing anything, we should establish whether your `OL20180703-V4` hardware is electrically close enough to the `flo199213` Gen2 board. Same MCU family is encouraging, but **MOSFET drive pins, Hall inputs, phase/current sensing, USART and enable circuitry must match**. Flashing firmware built for a different pin assignment could command the power stage incorrectly.

For the mower architecture, I would eventually aim for something like:

**RC receiver / safety controller → master MCU → UART → left GD32 motor controller + right GD32 motor controller**

with the mower controller commanding signed torque/speed independently for each wheel. That gives you proper skid steering rather than trying to emulate the original balance-board sensors.

And because it's a mower, I'd keep the **blade system electrically independent of the traction firmware**, with a hardware fail-safe/contactor so loss of RC signal or controller power removes blade power independently of software.

So I'd continue exactly where we are now: **identify the four SWD pads → connect non-destructively → inspect protection → then compare your board's GD32 pin assignments against the Gen2 source before flashing.** memcite

Yes. That is likely the more valuable part of the stock electronics for the mower.

The **Gen2 repo explicitly mentions an IMU**. One issue identifies the device on at least one Gen2 board as a **Bosch BMI120**, correcting the schematic which had called it an MPU6050. Both are 6-axis IMUs combining a **3-axis accelerometer + 3-axis gyroscope**. citeturn498393search1

For self-balancing, that is exactly what would be used: the accelerometer provides the gravity vector/tilt reference, while the gyro provides angular-rate data. Firmware combines the two to estimate board pitch quickly and without relying solely on noisy acceleration data.

What is particularly useful for your mower is that the Gen2 hardware also exposes additional communications. The repo says the two motor boards communicate over **USART**, and there is another header breaking out **GND, USART/I²C and 5 V**. citeturn498393search0 So there may be accessible buses on your boards that originally carried things such as:

**IMU data, foot/pressure sensor state, LED/status information, battery data, inter-controller communications, and possibly Bluetooth/app communications.**

The exact allocation varies by board revision, so I would not assume the `OL20180703-V4` matches the flo199213 schematic pin-for-pin.

For an RC mower, the IMU could be genuinely useful. You could use it for **roll/pitch measurement, rollover detection, slope limiting, detecting that the mower has been picked up or overturned, and potentially traction/slip estimation when combined with wheel speed**. It is much more useful than merely preserving the original self-balancing function.

The next thing I would investigate is **where the IMU physically is on your G5**. It may be on one of the controller PCBs, but some hoverboard generations put sensing electronics on another small PCB and communicate with the motor controller over UART/I²C. The fact that your Gen2-style system has two substantial controller boards makes either architecture possible.

If you send a close-up of the **small ICs around the GD32**, particularly any ~3×3 mm or ~4×4 mm QFN/LGA devices with 8–16 pads, I can identify likely IMU candidates. We can also go through the `flo199213` schematic and identify exactly which GD32 pins/bus connect to its BMI120/MPU6050, then trace those same pins on your PCB. memcite

I was thinking it would be helpful for environment mapping, thermoforming have hall sensors for encoding movement. We would just need one more sensing path for a reliable path towards that

========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-08-25T14:27:08.245151+00:00
Node: 405162dc-25f6-4184-8959-3b1dc3b553d4
Parent node: 188e779c-d803-4d19-a8f3-eae31072334c
========================================================================
Yes. For mapping, the wheel Hall sensors already give you a useful form of **odometry**, and an IMU gives you angular-rate/tilt information, but those two alone will still drift over distance. You need a third sensing modality that observes the external environment.

The usual progression is:

- **Wheel Hall sensors** → distance traveled and differential-drive odometry.
- **IMU** → yaw-rate, pitch, roll, short-term motion stabilization.
- **External ranging/vision sensor** → correct accumulated odometry drift against real features in the environment.

For a mower, the most practical third path is usually **2D LiDAR** or a compact scanning ToF sensor. A 2D LiDAR mounted above the chassis can produce a horizontal range scan that works well with SLAM, while the wheel Hall counts provide motion prediction and the IMU helps bridge slips and turns. That combination is much closer to a proper mobile-robot localization stack than trying to build a map from wheel encoders plus accelerometer alone.

A camera can also serve as that third path through visual odometry/visual SLAM, but outdoor mowing is a difficult visual environment: changing sunlight, repetitive grass texture, shadows, vibration, dust, and foliage motion all work against it. LiDAR is usually more deterministic for obstacle geometry. GNSS can help outdoors as another absolute reference, especially RTK GNSS, but ordinary GPS is generally too coarse to replace local obstacle sensing.

One important limitation of the hoverboard Hall sensors is resolution. They are usually there for **BLDC commutation**, not precision wheel encoders. You can derive wheel position from the Hall transitions, but the count per motor revolution is relatively low compared with a dedicated quadrature encoder. With a direct-drive hoverboard wheel this may still be perfectly adequate for mower-scale odometry, especially once fused with an IMU and LiDAR.

So the architecture I'd target is essentially:

**Hall odometry + IMU + 2D LiDAR → sensor fusion/SLAM → mower pose and map**

Then add GNSS later if you want repeatable global positioning over a larger property.

The nice part is that the existing hoverboard electronics may already provide **two of those three streams essentially for free**: motor Hall state and IMU data. The main question is whether the custom Gen2 firmware exposes both cleanly over UART, or whether we would need to add telemetry for the IMU and Hall counters ourselves.

========================================================================
Then you probably do **not** need RTK GNSS or an elaborate outdoor localization stack.

For a single residential lot, I would prioritize:

**wheel Hall odometry + IMU + inexpensive 2D LiDAR/ToF ranging**

That is enough to build a local map, recognize boundaries/obstacles, and repeatedly localize within a fairly small area. On a small yard, accumulated encoder drift is easier to correct because the mower will repeatedly see the same fences, house walls, trees, sheds, etc.

A practical architecture would be:

**hoverboard Hall sensors → wheel distance**  
**hoverboard IMU → heading/tilt stabilization**  
**2D LiDAR or scanning ToF → environmental reference**  
**Pi Zero 2 W / Pi 4-class computer → SLAM + path planning**

For your use case, the Hall sensors do not have to be extremely high resolution. They mainly provide short-term dead reckoning between environmental observations. The LiDAR corrects that accumulated error.

If cost is the priority, I would look at salvaged robot-vacuum LiDAR units before buying an expensive robotics LiDAR. Devices from older Xiaomi/Roborock/Neato-type robots can provide a 360° planar scan and are often inexpensive used. Another possibility is a small solid-state ToF array, but that generally produces a much poorer geometric map than a rotating 2D scanner.

The **IMU is especially useful on a mower** because wheel odometry becomes unreliable during wheel slip, bumps, slopes, or one wheel temporarily losing traction. It won't eliminate positional drift by itself, but it gives the localization system a much better estimate of what the chassis actually did.

So for a town lot, I would avoid adding complexity until necessary. **Hall + IMU + one 360° range sensor** is the combination I'd design around. memcite

Was thinking simple ir sensors for close range obj avoidance and lidar for longer range mapping

========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-08-25T14:32:48.451331+00:00
Node: eb07c46e-8c7f-42a9-acb0-8775e43b2d14

========================================================================
That combination makes sense for this mower.

Use the **LiDAR for localization and map geometry**, and keep the **IR sensors as a separate near-field safety layer**. That separation is useful because LiDAR can miss low, thin, dark, or very close objects depending on the unit and mounting height, while short-range IR sensors can cheaply cover the mower's immediate perimeter.

A sensible sensor stack would be:

- **Wheel Hall sensors:** short-term odometry.
- **IMU:** heading-rate, pitch/roll, rollover/slope detection.
- **2D LiDAR:** longer-range mapping and localization against fences, walls, trees, sheds, etc.
- **Several short-range IR/ToF sensors:** immediate obstacle detection around the chassis, especially below the LiDAR scan plane.
- **Physical bumper switches:** final independent contact-level safety channel.

For the IR layer, I would not rely on a single forward-facing pair. For a skid-steer mower, objects can approach from the corners during turns. Something like **front-left, front-center, front-right, left side, right side**, and possibly rear coverage would be more useful. Those sensors don't need to participate in SLAM at all; they can simply impose a local stop/slow/turn constraint.

One caveat is ordinary reflective IR outdoors. **Sunlight can badly degrade cheap IR triangulation/proximity sensors.** If this is intended to run in daylight, short-range **modulated ToF sensors** are generally more dependable than the simplest IR reflective modules. A physical bumper still remains valuable because vegetation is a difficult optical target.

So the control hierarchy could be:

**LiDAR map/path planner → desired motion**  
**Hall + IMU → actual motion estimate**  
**IR/ToF perimeter → override/avoidance**  
**bumper / tilt / RC failsafe → immediate traction and blade shutdown**

That is appropriately simple for a single-lot mower while still giving you enough redundant sensing to make autonomous navigation practical. memcite

========================================================================
Could the 2d be molded to provide a cone rather than a disc

========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-08-25T14:39:30.300714+00:00
Node: de6ecf06-b45f-40b5-8030-5aa2f10cbe32
Parent node: bbb21a44-50a7-48e5-8a95-1531438e1cfb
========================================================================
Yes — **if by “modified” you mean mechanically changing the scan plane**, rather than optically turning the beam into a cone. That could be a very cost-effective solution for your mower.

A normal rotating 2D LiDAR produces essentially a flat scan plane:

```text
side view

        LiDAR
          ●──────────── scan plane
```

If you simply **tilt the entire LiDAR downward**, you still get a plane, not a cone. In world coordinates, that tilted plane intersects level ground mostly on one side rather than giving equal downward coverage all around.

What you want is:

```text
             LiDAR
               ●
             /   \
           /       \
         /           \
       ground     ground

      360° conical scan
```

### A 2D LiDAR can potentially be made to do this

The elegant method would be to alter the orientation of the **rotating optical assembly** so its beam is a few degrees below perpendicular to the rotation axis. As that assembly rotates 360°, the beam traces a cone.

For example, with the beam **10° downward**, a LiDAR mounted 0.5 m above the ground would intersect flat ground at approximately:

\[
r = \frac{0.5}{\tan(10^\circ)} \approx 2.84\text{ m}
\]

At 15°:

\[
r \approx 1.87\text{ m}
\]

At 20°:

\[
r \approx 1.37\text{ m}
\]

That could be **very useful for a mower**. Every revolution would produce a ring of ground measurements surrounding it. Anything protruding above the expected ground surface changes the measured range.

### But there's an even better variation

Rather than making it a *fixed* cone, mechanically oscillate the whole 2D LiDAR by perhaps **±5–10°** while it performs its normal 360° scans.

You'd effectively make a cheap low-resolution 3D LiDAR:

```text
        +5°   ───────
         0°   ───────
        -5°   ───────
       -10°   ───────
```

Because you know the tilt angle for every scan, the Pi can transform each 2D scan into 3D coordinates. After several rotations you'd accumulate a sparse 3D point cloud.

This has a major advantage over permanently coning the beam: you retain **horizontal scans for SLAM against fences/walls/trees** while periodically looking downward for curbs, holes and low obstacles.

You don't necessarily need continuous oscillation either. A small servo could alternate:

**0° → -5° → -10° → 0°**

with each position held long enough for one complete scan. Hall odometry + IMU can compensate for mower movement between scans.

For a mower moving perhaps only ~0.5–1 m/s, that can be quite workable.

### One important distinction

I would **not modify the LiDAR's actual laser optics with a lens/mirror intended to spread the beam into a cone**. A ranging LiDAR needs to know the direction corresponding to each range measurement. Optically spreading one measurement across many vertical angles destroys that information and can also interfere with the receiver optics.

Mechanically changing the **known beam angle** preserves the range geometry.

For this project, a cheap **360° robot-vacuum 2D LiDAR mounted on a small controlled tilt mechanism** may actually be more useful than spending ~C$1,000 on a Mid-360. You'd essentially be constructing a slow 2.5D/3D scanner, and the mower doesn't need automotive-LiDAR scan rates. memcite

USER | 2026-08-25T14:43:29.194082+00:00
Node: bbb21f59-a01e-412e-8c08-abfaeda3b3a1
Parent node: de6ecf06-b45f-40b5-8030-5aa2f10cbe32
========================================================================
Would it be actually possible to make the cone optically?

========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-08-25T14:43:31.477388+00:00
Node: e002df8d-22ad-4def-9f0e-f031f4907668
Parent node: 489e05e3-0924-4bb4-822d-71aaf9825004
========================================================================
Yes, **physically it is possible to turn a 2D LiDAR scan into a conical scan optically**, but only if you redirect the entire transmit/receive line of sight in a controlled way. A simple “beam-spreading” optic is not sufficient.

The workable optical method is a **conical deflector**, usually a mirror or prism arrangement. Imagine the original 2D LiDAR beam sweeping horizontally. If that beam is reflected off a **45° annular/conical mirror** surrounding the sensor, each azimuthal direction can be bent downward by a fixed angle. The result is a 360° cone instead of a flat disc.

The critical complication is the **receiver**. Time-of-flight LiDAR does not just emit a laser; it also needs the returning light to come back through the receiver optics from the same direction. Therefore the external optic has to redirect **both outgoing and incoming paths**. A mirror placed in front of the complete optical aperture can do that; a cylindrical lens or diffuser that merely spreads the laser generally cannot.

Conceptually:

```text
                original horizontal ray
LiDAR  ───────────────►
              \
               \  external mirror/prism
                \
                 ▼
             downward ray
```

Rotate that geometry around the vertical axis and the downward ray sweeps a cone.

There are three practical approaches:

- **Conical/annular mirror:** potentially elegant and no moving parts beyond the LiDAR's existing scanner. Best theoretical solution.
- **Individual angled mirror mounted in the rotating optical head:** much easier geometrically, but requires modifying the LiDAR internally.
- **External faceted prism/mirror ring:** easier to fabricate than a true precision cone, but each facet introduces angular discontinuities.

For your mower application, a **small conical mirror above or around the LiDAR aperture** is probably the most interesting experiment. If the normal scan is horizontal and you deflect it downward by, say, **10°**, you get a 360° ground-intersecting cone while retaining the normal angular encoder data. The software only has to reinterpret each point as having a constant elevation angle of −10°.

There is one major limitation: many cheap robot-vacuum LiDARs have **separate transmitter and receiver apertures** rather than a perfectly coaxial optical path. An external mirror must intercept both apertures correctly. If it only redirects the outgoing laser, the return may never reach the receiver.

So yes: **an optical cone is entirely feasible**, but the correct device is essentially a **beam-steering mirror/prism**, not a lens that spreads the laser into a cone. For a cheap 2D LiDAR, I would first inspect its optical geometry; if transmit and receive are close together/coaxial, this becomes much more practical.

For your mower, these inexpensive modules are interesting starting points:

products{"selections":[["turn0product2","RPLIDAR C1"],["turn0product0","RPLIDAR A1M8"],["turn0product32","YDLIDAR X2L"],["turn0product7","360° Robot-Vacuum LiDAR Module"]]}

The **RPLIDAR C1** is probably the one I'd investigate first. It's a compact 360° dToF scanner, approximately C$96–113 in the current results, with 5 cm minimum range and up to 12 m range. It also has IP54 protection, which is attractive on a mower. citeturn0search11turn0search12

Interestingly, its specification gives a **scan-field flatness of 0–1.5°**, so its beam already occupies a small vertical angular envelope—but that's manufacturing/optical scan thickness, not useful independently resolved vertical information. citeturn0search38

The **RPLIDAR A1** is another good experimental candidate. It's around C$134–141 currently and provides 360° scanning to 12 m. citeturn0search4

### What I would really look for

Rather than buying a C$100+ LiDAR and immediately cutting it apart, the **$15–60 robot-vacuum LDS modules** are particularly attractive for experimenting with a conical optical conversion.

product["turn0product7","360° Robot-Vacuum LiDAR Module",{"render_as":"block"}]

Those are cheap enough that we could examine the optical arrangement and potentially design a **3D-printed annular mirror/prism carrier** around it.

There is an even more interesting possibility: instead of modifying the rotating head, put an **annular angled reflector around the outside of a normal scanner**. If both the outgoing and returning optical paths can see the reflector, the LiDAR itself remains untouched.

I'd target only perhaps **5–10° downward**. For example, at a 400-mm mounting height:

- −5° intersects level ground ~4.6 m away.
- −10° intersects ~2.27 m away.
- −15° intersects ~1.49 m away.

That gives a useful ground-reference ring around a residential mower while still looking far enough ahead to react.

**However, I'd want to choose the LiDAR based on its actual transmitter/receiver geometry before buying one for this modification.** Some rotating modules are much more amenable to an external conical reflector than others. I can specifically search teardown photos of the cheap vacuum LDS, RPLIDAR C1, X2L, etc., and identify which one has the optical arrangement most suitable for making the cone.

========================================================================
Is there a conical / ring mirror available for the external mod

========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-08-25T14:50:41.208000+00:00
Node: 7f9bfc5e-de60-44e8-bc11-ce651c28aeb7
Parent node: fdedf6cb-768c-4f20-8208-bfd916896f59
========================================================================
Yes. The optical component exists commercially; the useful search terms are **cone mirror**, **reflective axicon**, and sometimes **conical reflector**. Reflective axicons are specifically conical mirrors. citeturn0search14turn0search0

I found an actual off-the-shelf example:

product["turn0product10","10 mm Diameter Aluminum-Coated Cone Mirror",{"render_as":"hero"}]

The problem is that this Edmund Optics part is about **C$605**, so it makes little economic sense for a mower. Commercial precision cone mirrors are laboratory optics rather than inexpensive hobby components.

The underlying idea is sound, though. Conical mirrors are actually used in proposed/experimental **360° LiDAR beam-steering systems**; one published system achieved 360° × 1.1° scanning with a conical mirror, and there are LiDAR patents using the same basic approach. citeturn0search2turn0search4

For your application, I think there's a much cheaper fabrication route: **machine or 3D-print the cone and give it a reflective surface**. You don't need interferometer-grade optical quality for meter-scale mower ranging. A polished aluminum cone would be especially interesting; published IR work has demonstrated 360° viewing using a polished aluminum cone. citeturn0search16

The geometry also doesn't require a dramatic cone. If we want to convert a horizontal scan into, say, a **−10° elevation cone**, the reflector angle can be calculated from the law of reflection. More importantly, we'd design it around the particular LiDAR's **transmitter and receiver aperture locations** so both outgoing and return rays hit the reflector.

So I would **not buy the $600 optical cone**. I'd select a cheap 360° LiDAR first, get its optical dimensions, and then design a small aluminum/metalized conical ring specifically around it. That could potentially be a <$20 reflector rather than a laboratory optic. memcite

Yes — **that is different from the solid cone mirror I was describing**, and it makes considerably more sense for your idea.

You mean an **annular reflector surrounding the LiDAR**, approximately like this in cross-section:

```text
                 normal 2D scan
                      ─────►
             ┌─────────────\
             │    LiDAR     \  ~5° mirror face
             │               \
             └─────────────┐  \────► redirected downward
                           │
             annular mirror ring
```

Rotated 360° around the LiDAR, the angled surface becomes a **truncated conical / annular mirror**. This type of optic absolutely exists; optical literature calls these things **annular conical mirrors**, **annular reflectors**, or **annular axicon reflectors**. There are even optical systems explicitly using a hollow annular reflector with a fixed-angle circularly symmetric reflective surface. citeturn0search11turn0search3

The issue is availability: my search **didn't turn up a cheap off-the-shelf ring specifically intended to snap around a 2D LiDAR**. Commercial optical versions are precision components/custom optics.

For the mower, however, I don't think you need an optical-grade purchased part. A machined aluminum ring with a polished conical face could be very practical. There are commercial aluminum-coated conical mirrors specifically intended for 360° optical redirection and LiDAR/sensing applications, so the basic reflective approach is established. citeturn0search10

One correction on the geometry: if you want the LiDAR's horizontal ray redirected **10° downward**, the mirror surface itself doesn't need to be tilted 10° relative to the original ray. **Reflection changes the ray direction by twice the mirror-angle change**, so we'd design the ring geometry around the exact incoming/outgoing ray directions.

There is also a potentially very useful variant: **don't make the entire 360° circumference reflective.** We could alternate sectors:

**normal opening → angled mirror → normal opening → angled mirror**

That would let a single 2D LiDAR produce both:

- normal horizontal points for walls/fences/SLAM, and
- downward-conical points for ground/low-object detection.

Because the LiDAR already reports the azimuth of every measurement, software would know which angular sectors are horizontal versus downward.

If we choose the specific cheap LiDAR first, I can calculate the annular reflector dimensions and angle around its optical center. For something 3D-printable, the reflector could potentially be made as a printed ring with thin polished aluminum/stainless inserts rather than trying to polish plastic.

That pattern is very consistent with a normal SWD header.

Assuming your powered measurement confirmed **pin 1 ≈ 3.3 V** and **pin 4 = GND**, the most likely assignment is:

| Pad | Likely function | Powered voltage |
|---|---|---:|
| **1** | **3.3 V / VDD** | ~3.3 V |
| **2** | **SWDIO / PA13** | ~3.2 V |
| **3** | **SWCLK / PA14** | ~0.03 V |
| **4** | **GND** | 0 V |

That idle behavior makes sense. **SWCLK normally sits low when nothing is programming the MCU**, while **SWDIO can sit high because of its pull-up/input state**. So pin 2 being ~3.2 V and pin 3 essentially 0 V is a strong indication that:

**2 = SWDIO**  
**3 = SWCLK**

I would still call that **strongly likely rather than electrically proven** until either continuity to PA13/PA14 or an ST-Link connection confirms it.

You can now make a non-destructive connection:

**ST-Link GND → pad 4**  
**ST-Link SWDIO → pad 2**  
**ST-Link SWCLK → pad 3**

Keep the controller powered from its normal supply.

For **pad 1**, whether to connect it depends on the ST-Link. If yours is one of the common USB **ST-Link V2 clones**, its `3.3V` pin is often a **3.3-V output**, not a true target-voltage-sense input. In that case I would initially **leave pad 1 disconnected**. SWD only needs the common ground and the two signals on those programmers.

Then attempt **Connect/Detect only**. Do **not** select unlock, erase, disable protection, or alter option bytes yet. If it identifies the GD32 and reports protection, we've confirmed the header without risking the original firmware.

If you send a photo of your particular ST-Link, I can tell you exactly which three/four pins to use.

========================================================================
On Fedora/Nobara, there are two good equivalents, depending on whether you want a GUI.

**OpenOCD** is the most useful for what we're doing with this GD32. It supports ST-Link directly and gives us more control over connecting, examining protection, dumping flash, etc., without automatically changing anything.

Install it with:

```bash
sudo dnf install openocd
```

For your first test, I'd use OpenOCD rather than immediately attempting any flashing.

There is also **STM32CubeProgrammer**, ST's official replacement for the old Windows ST-Link Utility. It has a Linux GUI and CLI and supports ST-Link. However, because we're dealing with a **GD32F130 rather than an STM32**, OpenOCD is generally the more flexible choice.

For the `flo199213/Hoverboard-Firmware-Hack-Gen2` project specifically, **OpenOCD is also what the repository documents for unlocking/programming**, so it keeps us aligned with the existing Gen2 workflow. urlHoverboard-Firmware-Hack-Gen2https://github.com/flo199213/Hoverboard-Firmware-Hack-Gen2

Once you have the ST-Link plugged into USB, run:

```bash
lsusb
```

and:

```bash
openocd --version
```

Send me the `lsusb` line for the ST-Link. Then I can give you a **read-only first connection command** appropriate for that particular programmer and GD32F130, with no erase/unlock operation.

If **`lsusb` doesn't see the ST-Link at all**, don't troubleshoot SWD or the hoverboard yet. Linux isn't seeing the programmer as a USB device, so this is upstream of the target board.

Try these in order:

1. Unplug the ST-Link and run:
```bash
sudo dmesg -w
```
Then plug it in and watch for new messages.

2. In another terminal:
```bash
lsusb
```

A normal ST-Link will typically appear with an STMicroelectronics USB VID such as **`0483:`**. Cheap clones may identify differently.

3. Try another USB port, preferably a **USB 2.0 port** if available, and eliminate any hub/extension cable.

4. If it has a detachable USB cable, try another **known data-capable cable**. A charge-only cable gives exactly this symptom.

5. Check whether *anything* appears/disappears:
```bash
watch -n 0.5 lsusb
```
Plug/unplug the ST-Link while watching.

If **nothing whatsoever changes in `dmesg` or `lsusb`**, this isn't a Fedora driver/udev problem. `lsusb` should see the USB device even without an ST-Link driver or permissions. At that point I'd suspect the programmer, cable, connector, or USB port.

If you paste the few `dmesg` lines generated when you plug it in, I can tell you immediately whether it's enumerating and failing, or not electrically enumerating at all.

This is still only an attempted connection; it does **not erase or unlock anything**.

If that gets far enough, you'll likely see lines involving things such as:

```text
Cortex-M
target examination
device id
```

or a more informative failure such as:

```text
Error connecting DP
Error connecting AP
cannot read IDR
```

That distinction is exactly what we need.

This tells us something useful: the **ST-Link itself is working and is seeing the target at ~3.19 V**, but it is not getting any valid SWD response at all. The failure occurs before OpenOCD gets far enough to identify a Cortex-M core.

So I would not change target configuration yet. `stm32f1x.cfg` is not the cause of this particular failure; OpenOCD is failing at the initial SWD connection stage.

The next two possibilities are the most likely:

1. **Pads 2/3 are still not SWDIO/SWCLK**, despite their idle voltages looking right.
2. The stock firmware **disables/reconfigures SWD shortly after reset**, meaning we need to connect while the MCU is held in reset or booted without the application.

The cleanest next test is to establish the pad routing directly. For the GD32F130 LQFP48, the relevant MCU signals are **PA13/SWDIO and PA14/SWCLK**. With the board unpowered, continuity-test each suspected pad to those MCU pins. That removes the remaining uncertainty.

If they do prove to be PA13/PA14, then **connect-under-reset** is the next move. OpenOCD supports holding SRST asserted during target connection with `connect_assert_srst`, specifically for targets where application execution prevents debugger attachment. citeturn578500search0turn578500search33

That requires adding **NRST** as a fourth ST-Link signal. Once we locate NRST on your board, the command would be along these lines:

```bash
sudo openocd \
  -f interface/stlink.cfg \
  -c "transport select swd" \
  -c "adapter speed 100" \
  -c "reset_config srst_only srst_nogate connect_assert_srst" \
  -f target/stm32f1x.cfg
```

OpenOCD requires `srst_nogate` when using `connect_assert_srst`. citeturn578500search0

I would **not** start manipulating BOOT0 or protection yet. First prove pads 2/3. If they are definitely PA13/PA14, then locate **NRST** and try connect-under-reset. Neither of those operations alters the factory flash.

One other useful diagnostic: try the same command at **10 kHz**:

```bash
sudo openocd \
  -f interface/stlink.cfg \
  -c "transport select swd" \
  -c "adapter speed 10" \
  -f target/stm32f1x.cfg
```

If that still gives exactly:

```text
Error: init mode failed (unable to connect to the target)
```

then I'd stop changing SWD clock rates and move directly to continuity-checking **pad 2 → PA13** and **pad 3 → PA14**.

my bench supply attempts to draw more power when the 3.3v is connected to the st-link

========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-08-26T00:10:37.792001+00:00
Node: 479ebb5c-4da9-4b80-aba4-09b1ecbc7a04
Parent node: 0b1a1206-d734-4942-816e-490abd3831af
========================================================================
Yes — that is a strong sign that the ST-Link’s **3.3 V pin is acting as a power output rather than a passive VTref input**, and it is back-feeding or fighting the controller’s own 3.3 V regulator.

So **disconnect the ST-Link 3.3 V wire immediately**. For this board, use only:

- **ST-Link GND → board pad 4**
- **ST-Link SWDIO → suspected SWDIO**
- **ST-Link SWCLK → suspected SWCLK**
- Leave **board pad 1 / 3.3 V disconnected from the ST-Link**

Power the hoverboard controller only from the bench supply through its normal battery input.

That also explains why I was cautious earlier about clone ST-Link V2 units: on many of them, the header marked `3.3V` is literally the regulator output intended to power a small target, not target-voltage sensing. Connecting two regulated 3.3 V supplies together can cause current circulation if their voltages differ by even a small amount.

Your ~3.18 V value reported earlier therefore may have been the **ST-Link's own 3.3 V rail**, not a genuine target-voltage measurement.

After disconnecting that wire, rerun:

```bash
sudo openocd \
  -f interface/stlink.cfg \
  -c "transport select swd" \
  -c "adapter speed 100" \
  -f target/stm32f1x.cfg
```

Two things may happen:

- It still communicates with the ST-Link and attempts SWD normally — good.
- OpenOCD reports no/zero target voltage but still attempts SWD — also potentially fine with these clones.

The critical electrical requirement for SWD is **common GND + SWDIO + SWCLK**. The target must already be powered at the proper logic voltage.

And given the increased bench-supply draw you observed, I would **not reconnect that ST-Link 3.3 V pin to this controller**.

Once those are proven, if SWD still won't connect, we know we're dealing with **reset/BOOT/protection behavior**, rather than an incorrectly identified header.

Given the conformal coating, we can use the MCU package geometry to identify exactly which legs to probe. If you want, I can pull up the **GD32F130 LQFP48 pin diagram and mark exactly where pins 34 (PA13), 37 (PA14), 7 (NRST), and 44 (BOOT0) are relative to the pin-1 dot**.

ASSISTANT | 2026-08-26T00:15:00.216737+00:00
Node: 91be1366-febc-46cc-9318-63a24bab452f
Parent node: 04106d41-3f95-4ccc-951b-05bcbf0360cd
========================================================================
For the **GD32F130Cx in LQFP48**, the two SWD pins you need are:

- **Pin 34 = PA13 / SWDIO**
- **Pin 37 = PA14 / SWCLK**

The same package also gives you two useful reference pins:

- **Pin 47 = VSS / GND**
- **Pin 48 = VDD / 3.3 V**
- **Pin 7 = NRST**
- **Pin 44 = BOOT0** citeturn567041search12turn567041search15

The package numbering runs counterclockwise from the pin-1 dot. For the LQFP48 layout:

```text
                 TOP
       48 47 ... 44 ... 38 37
        ┌───────────────────┐
      1 │                   │ 36
      2 │                   │ 35
      3 │                   │ 34  ← PA13 / SWDIO
        │      GD32F130     │
        │                   │
     12 │                   │ 25
        └───────────────────┘
       13 14 ...       23 24
```

So physically:

**SWDIO / pin 34:** on the **right-hand side**, third leg down from the top if the chip is oriented with the pin-1 dot at the upper-left.

**SWCLK / pin 37:** the **top-right corner leg**.

This makes the continuity check fairly straightforward with power removed:

- board pad **2 → MCU pin 34**
- board pad **3 → MCU pin 37**

If those don't match, reverse the pad expectations and check:

- pad **2 → pin 37**
- pad **3 → pin 34**

Also confirm your already-identified rails if useful:

- pad **4 → MCU pin 47** should give essentially direct continuity to ground.
- pad **1 → MCU pin 48** should show whether that pad is directly on VDD or reaches it through something else. citeturn567041search12

The GigaDevice reference schematic explicitly labels **PA13/SWDAT pin 34** and **PA14/SWCLK pin 37**, so these are not inferred from STM32 compatibility—they are the actual GD32F130 pin assignments. citeturn567041search15

ASSISTANT | 2026-08-26T16:31:34.450356+00:00

========================================================================
For your **GD32F130**, you generally **would not use STM32CubeProgrammer’s “Debug Authentication” feature**. That feature is for newer STM32 security architectures where the MCU has been provisioned with a debug-auth password or certificate. ST documents it as a credential-based mechanism: the tool sends a provisioned password or certificate through a restricted debug port to reopen debug access. citeturn115501search2turn115501search24

Your GD32F130 uses the older style **readout/debug protection**, not ST’s modern certificate/password debug-auth scheme. So CubeProgrammer suggesting “Debug Authentication” is effectively a generic UI suggestion and is not evidence that your GD32 supports that protocol.

For this controller, the relevant paths are:

- **Normal SWD attach** through PA13/SWDIO + PA14/SWCLK.
- **Connect-under-reset** if the running factory firmware disables or repurposes SWD.
- Check the GD32 **read-protection state** once SWD communication exists.
- If the part is protected, changing/removing protection may erase flash, so we should not do that until we know whether the stock image can be preserved.

So I would **not click Debug Authentication** in CubeProgrammer. It won't authenticate this GD32 using some hidden factory credential.

The useful next operation is still to prove the header electrically: check whether your suspected pad 2 has continuity to **MCU pin 34 / PA13** and pad 3 to **pin 37 / PA14**. If those are correct, then we should locate **NRST** and try a true SWD **connect-under-reset**. That is the correct analogue to the ECU “boot/recovery” approach for this particular MCU family. memcite

Yes. I searched the original Gen2 repo, the later Gen2.x forks, and their issues specifically for **GD32F130 + ST-Link/SWD connection failures**. There are several findings directly relevant to what we're seeing.

The most important one is remarkably close to your exact OpenOCD error. A 2026 Gen2.x issue involving **GD32F130C8T6** boards reports:

> `Error: init mode failed (unable to connect to the target)`

The author tried battery power/no battery power, ST-Link 3.3 V connected/disconnected, **BOOT0 high**, repeated connection attempts and power cycling. In that case SWD had previously worked, but stopped working once particular firmware was running. citeturn0search2

That establishes that **this exact error can occur even with known-good SWD wiring on a GD32F130 hoverboard controller**. So our failure does not establish that pad 3 is wrong.

More importantly, the original Gen2 README has a detail we haven't been accounting for sufficiently: while flashing, they say you must **hold the hoverboard power button**, because the controller's power-latch circuit otherwise releases and turns the controller off. citeturn0search0

Your bench supply may make that behavior less obvious because you'll still have battery voltage at the board while the MCU's internal power/control state changes.

There are a few other relevant findings:

- The Gen2.x fork confirms that different board revisions **change the physical SWD header order**, explicitly recommending meter identification of GND/3.3 V and trying the remaining DIO/CLK positions. We've already gone further than that because **pad 2 physically traces to MCU PA14/SWCLK**. citeturn0search8
- That same documentation says **STM32CubeProgrammer and ST-Link Utility can have problems with GD32F130**, and recommends the open-source `st-flash` implementation instead for programming. citeturn0search8
- A documented GD32F130C8 board is recognized by ST-Link software as an **STM32F10xx Medium Density** device, despite actually being GD32. So the STM32 identity itself isn't necessarily a problem. citeturn0search6
- The original repo explicitly says a board that has **never been flashed is normally locked**, and their expected procedure is to unlock it through ST-Link/OpenOCD. citeturn0search0

### What I'd test next

We're now sufficiently confident in the header that I would stop trying arbitrary pin arrangements.

We have:

**1 = 3.3 V**  
**2 = PA14/SWCLK — physically confirmed**  
**3 = almost certainly PA13/SWDIO**  
**4 = GND**

Wire only **2, 3 and 4** to the ST-Link. Leave 1 disconnected.

Then I would specifically test the **power-button/latch condition** mentioned by the original Gen2 developer. Start OpenOCD while physically holding the hoverboard/controller's power button continuously:

```bash
sudo openocd \
  -f interface/stlink.cfg \
  -c "transport select swd" \
  -c "adapter speed 100" \
  -f target/stm32f1x.cfg
```

If that doesn't change anything, the next target is **NRST**, not BOOT0. The Gen2.x evidence shows BOOT0-high by itself doesn't necessarily solve this particular failure. citeturn0search2

I'd locate **MCU NRST pin 7**, bring that out to the ST-Link, and attempt a proper **connect-under-reset**. That specifically addresses the situation where the factory firmware starts and changes the debug-pin/debug-power state before the ST-Link can attach.

One additional thing I'd like to check before we do that: the newer RoboDurden repo has accumulated **many more GD32F130 board layouts and recovery cases** than the old flo199213 project. citeturn0search4 I can search its wiki/issues specifically for **NRST, connect-under-reset, BOOT0, locked factory firmware, and GD32 RDP recovery** and see which sequence people actually got working.

Yes — that fits the behavior very well.

It means the B/slave board is probably not self-latching from battery power alone. The master is likely sending either:

- a **UART wake/enable command**,
- a logic-level **enable/latch signal over the same harness**,
- or both.

So with the inter-board cable unplugged, the slave can have battery voltage present but its logic section may never enter the normal powered state needed for SWD access. Once the UART harness is connected, the master wakes it and the 3.3 V logic rail comes up properly.

That also suggests the inter-board link is more important than just telemetry. For the mower conversion, it would be useful to characterize that cable before replacing firmware: identify **TX, RX, GND, and any additional enable/power-latch conductor**. If it is only a 3-wire UART+GND arrangement, then the wake is probably protocol-based; if there are extra wires, one may be a dedicated wake/enable line.

Now that B is awake, the best next move is to immediately make a **full 64 KiB factory dump of the B/slave board** before changing anything. Then we can compare A and B properly and see whether the slave firmware differs or whether the role is assigned through hardware/UART state.

Good — this one is clearly the **B/slave firmware**, and it is genuinely different from the A/master firmware.

I compared `oriFWB.bin` against the verified A/master dump `oriFW(1).bin`:

- Both are exactly **65,536 bytes**.
- Both have the same initial stack pointer: `0x20000A90`.
- Both have the same reset vector: `0x08000171`.
- The B dump SHA-256 is `f13fad73f874c3e793f5a25dd98d1c9963c61a5af757a528dc51325c0e0272b6`.
- **16,548 bytes differ** between A and B.
- Differences begin at flash offset **`0x0158`** and continue through approximately **`0x7C13`**.
- A's last programmed byte is around `0x7C0E`.
- B's last programmed byte is around `0x7C13`.

So these aren't merely the same firmware with a side-ID/config byte changed. They share the same startup framework/vector layout, but a substantial portion of the executable differs. That strongly supports a true **master/slave firmware split**.

It also makes your observation about the 4-wire interconnect more significant. Since B apparently needs the A-side connection before it wakes, one of those four wires may be more than simple `TX/RX/GND/VCC`; the master firmware may participate in the slave's power-latch/wake logic.

I can now disassemble **A vs B side-by-side** and specifically locate the code differences around:

**UART initialization and RX/TX handlers, wake/power-latch GPIO, motor Hall inputs, PWM outputs, ADC/current sensing, and IMU/I²C handling.**

That comparison should let us reconstruct quite a lot of the original two-board architecture before replacing the firmware.

Done. I disassembled the B/slave image and compared it against the verified A/master image.

[B/slave disassembly](sandbox:/mnt/data/controllerB_slave_disassembly.asm)

[A/B firmware analysis](sandbox:/mnt/data/AB_firmware_analysis.md)

The most important finding is a correction to the earlier raw byte comparison: **A and B are not substantially different programs.** A same-offset comparison makes them look very different because small insertions/deletions shift subsequent code, but sequence alignment over the programmed region shows about **98.98% similarity**. They look like closely related master/slave builds from the same source.

The interrupt vector tables are **identical**, and both images access the same core peripherals: ADC, TIMER0, TIMER2, DMA, USART0/1, GPIOB/C, RCU and flash controller. The GD32F130 memory map confirms those addresses. citeturn120333view0

A particularly useful result is that the sampled **ADC and motor-timer interrupt handlers are byte-identical** between A and B. That strongly indicates the actual low-level BLDC control, PWM timing and ADC/current-sampling machinery is shared. The differences are concentrated more around **GPIO/EXTI and UART/state-handling code**, which is exactly where a master/slave distinction would be expected.

The UART-class handler around `0x08005C84` is nearly the same on both, but B has a small additional state write. The EXTI-class handler around `0x080023D8` differs more substantially. That EXTI path is therefore a good candidate for finding role-specific Hall, button, wake, or sensor handling.

There is also a small **board-specific data/configuration area near `0x08007C00`**. A and B contain different bytes there, while the surrounding flash is erased. I would preserve those blocks separately because they may contain calibration, side identity, or manufacturing configuration.

One other important point about the 4-wire cable: the newer Gen2.x source explicitly identifies the original master/slave header as **PA2/PA3 USART**, but also says that this header carries **5 V/15 V power**. citeturn129032search0 So the fact that B only wakes when that cable is connected does **not yet prove that a UART packet wakes it**. One of those four conductors may simply supply the slave's logic/wake rail.

The next high-value step is to trace the four-wire connector to **PA2/PA3 and its power conductor**, then I can use that information to identify the corresponding UART initialization and packet-handling routines in the disassembly. That should get us much closer to extracting the Hall counts, motion data, and any sensor telemetry from the factory protocol.
