I found evidence that other people have successfully **read back flashed firmware**, but I did **not find a publicly posted stock factory `.bin` dump for a GD32F130 split-board hoverboard** that we can immediately download and compare byte-for-byte.

The closest relevant cases are these:

- In flo199213 issue #13, a user with split **Jiaan GD32F130C6** boards says they unlocked both controllers and could flash files that could then be successfully **read back**. That confirms readback worked on that hardware, but the issue does not expose a preserved stock image. citeturn871351search9
- The original Gen2 repo assumes factory boards are locked and tells users to unlock them before flashing. That workflow normally sacrifices the original image rather than preserving it, which likely explains why stock dumps are rare. citeturn871351search0
- RoboDurden’s later Gen2.x work covers a large number of GD32F130 split-board layouts, but the repositories mostly contain **replacement binaries and test binaries**, not factory firmware archives. citeturn871351search1turn871351search8
- The original project structure itself makes the master/slave distinction explicit in source modules such as `commsMasterSlave`, which is useful for comparing architecture even without stock binaries. citeturn399875search1

So at the moment, **your A and B dumps may actually be unusually valuable**, because they preserve both sides of a factory GD32F130 split-controller implementation before unlocking.

What we *can* compare against the repos is arguably more useful than a random factory binary anyway: the reverse-engineered **known board layouts and source-level pin assignments**. RoboDurden has accumulated many Gen2.1.x layouts, including GD32F130 variants, and warns that pin assignments vary enough between boards that the wrong layout can damage the power stage. citeturn871351search1

The next comparison I would do is therefore:

**your A/B factory disassembly ↔ flo199213/RoboDurden source and board definitions**

Specifically, I can identify in your binaries which GD32 pins are used for:

- phase PWM outputs,
- Hall A/B/C,
- current-sense ADC channels,
- USART master/slave link,
- power-latch/button,
- I²C/IMU,
- LEDs/buzzer,
- battery-voltage sensing.

Then we can see whether your `OL20180703-V4` corresponds closely to one of RoboDurden’s existing **Gen2.1.x layouts**. If it does, you may not need a new board port at all—just the correct existing layout or a very small variation.

I also found a newer project, `hoverboardhavoc/hoverboard-firmware`, that is explicitly building a runtime-configurable firmware for both single and **split GD32F130 boards**, with configurable Hall, IMU, UART and board-layout mappings. It may be useful as another source of known hardware mappings, although it is newer and still work-in-progress. citeturn399875search0

Yes. I found a **very strong existing-board match**, much stronger than the generic Gen2 comparison we had before:

## Your board is very likely an earlier revision of Gen2.1.20

RoboDurden issue #90 documents a GD32F130 split-board controller marked:

**`OLSZ 0L20190703-V4.1`**

Your PCB is marked:

**`OL20180703-V4`**

I cannot establish that the embedded numbers are dates, so I won't assume that. But the near-identical marking, same MCU family, same two-board master/slave architecture, and—most importantly—the **exact same Hall GPIO assignment found independently in your factory firmware** make this a compelling hardware-family match. The repo calls it **Gen2.1.20 / ClassyWalk**. citeturn570511view0

### Pin-level comparison

| Function | Your factory firmware | Gen2.1.20 | Match |
|---|---|---|---|
| MCU | GD32F130 | GD32F130 | **Yes** |
| Hall 1 | **PC13** | PC13 | **Exact** |
| Hall 2 | **PA1** | PA1 | **Exact** |
| Hall 3 | **PC14** | PC14 | **Exact** |
| BLDC high G | likely PA10 | PA10 | very likely |
| BLDC low G | likely PB15 | PB15 | very likely |
| BLDC high B | likely PA9 | PA9 | very likely |
| BLDC low B | likely PB14 | PB14 | very likely |
| BLDC high Y | likely PA8 | PA8 | very likely |
| BLDC low Y | likely PB13 | PB13 | very likely |
| Master/slave UART | USART1 present | **PA2/PA3 USART1** | strong |
| Button | **PA12 detected in factory code** | PA12 | **Exact** |
| Battery ADC | not yet independently proven | PA4 | pending |
| Self-hold | not yet independently proven | PB12 | pending |
| Phase current | not yet independently proven | PB0 + PA0 | pending |
| IMU | not yet independently proven | PB6/PB7 I²C | pending |

The current `defines_2-1-20.h` has PC13/PA1/PC14 for the Hall inputs, PA2/PA3 for USART1, PA4 for battery voltage, PB12 for self-hold, PA12 for the button, and PB6/PB7 for the IMU/I²C interface. fileciteturn7file0

### The factory binary independently proves the Hall match

This is the part that gives me high confidence. In your **A/master stock firmware**, the routine beginning around `0x08006A34` makes these three GPIO reads:

```asm
08006a36: ldr     r6, [...]          ; GPIOC = 0x48000800
08006a38: mov.w   r1, #0x2000        ; bit 13
...
08006a44: movs    r1, #0x2           ; bit 1
08006a46: mov.w   r0, #0x48000000    ; GPIOA
...
08006a50: mov.w   r1, #0x4000        ; bit 14
08006a54: mov     r0, r6              ; GPIOC
```

That is unambiguously:

**PC13 → PA1 → PC14**

The routine then combines their states into a three-bit Hall state. So these are not merely GPIOs that happen to be referenced—they are behaving exactly like the three motor Hall inputs.

And issue #90 independently found the **same three Hall pins** on `0L20190703-V4.1`: PA1, PC13 and PC14. citeturn189718view0

By contrast, known layouts such as Gen2.1.1 use **PB11/PF1/PC14**, so they can be ruled out. fileciteturn3file0 Gen2.1.7 uses **PB11/PC14/PA1**, which also isn't your board. fileciteturn6file0

### PA12 also matches the power-button input

There is another strong factory-code match. At approximately `0x08006B38`, your A firmware repeatedly reads the GPIOA input register:

```asm
08006b30: mov.w   r6, #0x48000000     ; GPIOA
08006b38: ldr     r0, [r6, #0x10]     ; GPIO input status
08006b3a: lsls    r0, r0, #19
08006b3c: bpl     ...
```

Shifting the input register left by 19 puts original **bit 12** into the sign bit, so this is testing **PA12**. It samples it repeatedly and feeds the result into the surrounding startup/power-state logic.

The mature Gen2.1.20 definition likewise specifies:

```c
#define BUTTON PA12
```

fileciteturn7file0

That's a second exact match obtained independently from your stock firmware.

### The slave-board behavior is almost eerily similar

Issue #90 also specifically says of its `0L20190703-V4.1` slave:

> “The slave board flashes but does not appear to turn on.”

The author couldn't determine how to keep it awake because there was no physical button on the slave. citeturn189718view1

You just discovered that **your B/slave does wake when its 4-wire master/slave harness is connected**.

That is another strong indication that we're dealing with the same controller lineage, and your factory A/B dumps could actually answer a question the Gen2.1.20 reverse-engineering work never resolved.

### Motor-drive pins are also highly likely to match

RoboDurden notes that the GD32F130 has very constrained advanced-timer routing and that all of the Gen2 GD32F130 layouts they had examined used:

```text
PA10 / PB15
PA9  / PB14
PA8  / PB13
```

for the six complementary TIMER0 BLDC outputs. citeturn570511search0

Gen2.1.20 uses exactly those six pins. citeturn570511view0

I haven't yet completed enough control-flow recovery from your binary to label those six outputs independently, so I'm deliberately keeping that as **very likely**, rather than calling it proven.

## There are some important updates to the Gen2.1.20 definition

The original 2024 issue was incomplete. The current source has subsequently been refined. In particular, the current layout says:

```c
#define PHASE_A PB0
#define PHASE_B PA0

#define SELF_HOLD PB12
#define BUTTON    PA12

#define PHOTO_L PC15
#define PHOTO_R PA11

#define MPU_6050old
#define I2C_PB6PB7
```

It also indicates two separate low-side phase-current shunts rather than the original issue's tentative single `CURRENT_DC PA0` definition. fileciteturn7file0

The **IMU entry is particularly relevant to your mower project**: this board family has an existing definition for an MPU6050-compatible IMU on **PB6/PB7 I²C**. That gives us a very specific target to look for in your stock A/master firmware.

## What this means

I would now treat your hardware as:

> **Gen2.1.20-family, probably an `0L201x0703-V4` predecessor/sibling of the documented `0L20190703-V4.1`, until a contradictory pin mapping is found.**

I would **not yet flash an unmodified Gen2.1.20 build**, because the difference between `V4` and `V4.1` could involve something important like current sensing, gate-driver shutdown, power latch or button wiring.

But we are probably no longer looking at a completely unknown board requiring a ground-up port.

The highest-value next reverse-engineering pass is now very well constrained: I can go back through **both your A/master and B/slave binaries using the Gen2.1.20 map as a hypothesis**, and specifically prove or reject **PA4 battery sense, PB12 self-hold, PA2/PA3 master/slave UART, PB6/PB7 IMU/I²C, PA0/PB0 current sensing, and the six TIMER0 gate-drive pins**. That should give us a nearly complete factory pin map and, importantly, tell us how the master wakes the slave. memcite

Yes — after going back through both binaries, **there should be an IMU on each board**, and the factory firmware strongly confirms that.

Both A/master and B/slave actively use an MPU60x0-style IMU path. This is not just unused library code: both images execute code that talks to **I²C address `0x68`**, reads **14 bytes starting at register `0x3B`** (the classic accel/temp/gyro block), and writes the standard configuration registers `0x6B`, `0x19`, `0x1A`, `0x1B`, and `0x1C`. The bus is software-I²C on **PB6/PB7**. That lines up with the current Gen2.1.20 definition, which also specifies the IMU on PB6/PB7. fileciteturn7file0

More importantly, the factory binaries now give us a nearly complete hardware map. The confirmed/common map for **both** A and B is:

| Function | Factory pin mapping | Confidence |
|---|---|---|
| Hall A | **PC13** | confirmed |
| Hall B | **PA1** | confirmed |
| Hall C | **PC14** | confirmed |
| BLDC high-side outputs | **PA8, PA9, PA10** | confirmed |
| BLDC complementary low-side outputs | **PB13, PB14, PB15** | confirmed |
| Master/slave USART | **PA2 / PA3** | confirmed |
| IMU clock | **PB6** | confirmed |
| IMU data | **PB7** | confirmed |
| Button | **PA12** | confirmed |
| Power self-hold/latch | **PB12** | confirmed |
| ADC inputs used | **PA0, PB0, PA6, PA4** | confirmed |

That matches the RoboDurden **Gen2.1.20** definition exceptionally closely. It defines the Hall inputs as PC13/PA1/PC14, the six BLDC pins as PA8–PA10 and PB13–PB15, USART1 on PA2/PA3, self-hold on PB12, the button on PA12, battery sensing on PA4, and the IMU on PB6/PB7. fileciteturn7file0

The PWM mapping is particularly solid: your stock firmware explicitly configures **PA8, PA9, PA10 as one AF2 group** and **PB13, PB14, PB15 as the complementary AF2 group**. So we are no longer inferring those from the repo.

The ADC setup is also interesting. The factory firmware explicitly configures a four-channel sequence:

**ADC0 → PA0**  
**ADC8 → PB0**  
**ADC6 → PA6**  
**ADC4 → PA4**

The current Gen2.1.20 map identifies **PA0 and PB0 as the two phase-current sense inputs** and **PA4 as battery voltage**. fileciteturn7file0 That leaves **PA6** as an additional analog input in your stock design whose exact physical function I have not established yet. I would not label it until we trace either its code usage or the PCB.

One useful correction to the earlier IMU discussion: electrically, a hoverboard does not *need* two IMUs to know chassis pitch. But this particular architecture apparently does use one per controller. That makes sense for two nearly autonomous motor-control boards sharing a common firmware lineage; each side can calculate its own local attitude/control state and then coordinate over the PA2/PA3 link.

The master/slave distinction appears to be **above the low-level motor layer**. The Hall decoding, PWM hardware, ADC setup, and IMU machinery are essentially the same on both. The larger A/B differences cluster around startup/state-machine and communications code, plus a small data/configuration area near `0x08007C00`. That supports the idea of two almost-identical motor controllers with different master/slave roles rather than fundamentally different hardware.

I also confirmed that the stock inter-board link uses the same **PA2/PA3 USART1** arrangement as Gen2.1.20. The repo's general GD32 layout documentation identifies PA2/PA3 as the usual USART1 pair, and the 2.1.20 definition uses exactly that assignment. fileciteturn3file0 fileciteturn7file0

I put the updated findings into a consolidated report here:

[Gyroor G5 factory A/B pin-map analysis](sandbox:/mnt/data/Gyroor_G5_factory_pinmap_analysis.md)

The next particularly useful target is the **master/slave UART protocol**. Since we now know PA2/PA3 with high confidence, I can concentrate on the USART1 RX/TX handlers in A and B and start reconstructing the packet structure—especially looking for **Hall/wheel-speed values, IMU values, wake/keepalive, requested torque/speed, battery information, and fault/status fields**. That would tell us what sensor data can potentially be harvested without replacing all of the stock logic. memcite

it does need pitch for each "half" each motor needs that input as the "controller" telling that motor to "go forward/backward" 


========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-08-26T18:03:27.211136+00:00
Node: 5281b67a-e6d4-42a5-8c01-97f8d00f2e6b
Parent node: 26dfb0ed-29c0-45b2-89e3-b5589405c196
========================================================================
Correct. In a split hoverboard architecture, **each half needs its own local pitch estimate** if each motor controller is independently closing the balance loop for that side.

The control logic is effectively:

**local IMU pitch/gyro → local balance controller → local motor torque command**

for each half, with the master/slave UART coordinating state, direction, speed bias, fault status, and possibly ensuring both halves remain synchronized.

So the IMU on each board is not redundant in the ordinary sense. It is part of that board’s own feedback loop. If one half tilts forward, that side’s controller needs to command its motor forward; if it tilts backward, it commands reverse. The two halves together then produce the rider’s intended translation and steering behavior.

That also explains why the A and B firmware share nearly identical low-level motor, Hall, ADC, and IMU code: both boards are fundamentally full balancing motor controllers. The master role is likely an additional supervisory layer rather than the master being the only board doing attitude estimation.

For the mower conversion, that is useful because each board already has:

**wheel Hall feedback + local IMU + current sensing + motor drive**

which is a fairly complete motion-sensing package. The UART link then becomes the natural place to expose or exchange wheel speed, pitch/gyro, current, fault state, and command values. memcite

Yes. Given the pin mapping we've now recovered, **2.1.20 is the Gen2.x layout I would use as the starting point**.

Your factory firmware independently agrees with 2.1.20 on the critical hardware needed to avoid damaging the controller: the six BLDC gate-drive outputs, Hall inputs, battery ADC, current-sense inputs, self-hold, button, master/slave UART, and PB6/PB7 IMU bus. The 2.1.20 definition explicitly contains those mappings. fileciteturn7file0

There is one distinction I would make: I would **not immediately flash both boards and then connect the motors/battery at full operating conditions**. First flash **one controller**, preferably the A/master, with 2.1.20 and verify that the firmware boots and identifies the Hall sequence correctly with the drive disabled or wheel unloaded. Once the basic I/O behaves correctly, proceed to powered motor testing.

For your immediate RC-mower objective, you don't need the original hoverboard balancing behavior. Gen2.x can treat the board as an ordinary sensored BLDC motor controller, so the architecture can eventually be:

```text
                 RC receiver
                     │
              mower controller
                /         \
          command         command
             │               │
        A controller ←UART→ B controller
             │               │
          left motor      right motor
```

Or initially you can use the Gen2.x master/slave arrangement and inject the RC commands into the master.

One thing **does need to be decided before I tell you exactly which binary/configuration to flash**: what signal does your RC receiver output — **standard servo PWM, PPM, SBUS/iBUS, or something else?** That determines which Gen2.x control mode we should build/use. I don't want to assume the receiver interface and give you a firmware configuration that expects a different signal.

Also keep the A and B factory `.bin` backups untouched. We have recoverable originals now, which gives us a safe route back if 2.1.20 exposes some V4-versus-V4.1 hardware difference. memcite

========================================================================
what is the commonly taken path for RC control of the boards as they would be arranged in the hoverboard

========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-08-27T00:27:38.923361+00:00
Node: de2cee68-2a98-4d5b-944c-834e0034e888
Parent node: 86857912-5e61-41a5-8073-937f90074469
========================================================================
For the **two-board hoverboard arrangement**, the common path is to leave the two motor controllers in their normal **MASTER + SLAVE topology** and feed the external control into the **master only**. The master then commands the slave over the original PA2/PA3 master-slave UART. That is explicitly how the current Gen2.x configuration is structured. citeturn0search0turn0search1

For RC specifically, there are two practical approaches in the current firmware.

**1. CRSF/ExpressLRS directly into the master — probably the cleanest RC solution now.** Gen2.x has native `REMOTE_CRSF` support, which was specifically added for ExpressLRS/CRSF receivers. You flash A as `MASTER`, B as `SLAVE`, retain the original four-wire A↔B connection, and connect the receiver's CRSF UART to the master's separate remote UART. citeturn0search5

Conceptually:

```text
RC transmitter
     │ RF
     ▼
ELRS receiver
     │ CRSF UART
     ▼
 MASTER board
     │
     │ original master/slave UART
     ▼
 SLAVE board
     │
   motors
```

**2. Small Arduino/ESP32/Pico between an ordinary RC receiver and the master.** This was essentially the traditional Gen2 approach. The original project describes controlling the two-board system using an Arduino or other steering device, and even includes a `PPMDeviceArduino` example. citeturn0search1 The intermediary reads ordinary servo PWM/PPM/SBUS/etc., converts throttle + steering into the hoverboard command protocol, and sends it to the master.

Interestingly, **direct conventional servo PWM isn't currently the mainline solution**. There have been requests for `RemotePwm`, but the project's remote abstraction explicitly lists it as something that could be added rather than the established implementation. citeturn0search5turn0search13

### For your mower

I would retain the hoverboard architecture:

```text
                    RC receiver
                         │
                    throttle
                    steering
                         │
                         ▼
                  ┌─────────────┐
                  │ A / MASTER  │
                  └──────┬──────┘
                         │ PA2/PA3
                         │ factory 4-wire
                         │ master/slave link
                         ▼
                  ┌─────────────┐
                  │ B / SLAVE   │
                  └─────────────┘

                 LEFT         RIGHT
                 MOTOR         MOTOR
```

That gives the master the conventional **speed + steering** command, and the firmware performs the differential mixing for the two wheels rather than requiring two independent RC channels wired to two independent motor controllers.

If your existing RC system has an **ExpressLRS receiver with CRSF output**, I'd use `REMOTE_CRSF` directly. That's substantially cleaner than adding another MCU just to translate servo pulses. Gen2.x already has the decoder. citeturn0search0turn0search5

If it's a conventional PWM receiver, I'd use a **Pi Pico/Arduino/ESP32 as the receiver interface** initially rather than modifying the hoverboard firmware to decode servo PWM. That intermediary will also eventually be useful for the mower because it can become the supervisory controller for your LiDAR/navigation system while retaining RC manual override.

One important detail for **2.1.20**: Gen2.x normally reserves **PA2/PA3 USART1 for A↔B**, while external UART/CRSF control normally uses another USART, generally **PB6/PB7 USART0**. citeturn0search0 But on 2.1.20, **PB6/PB7 are also the IMU I²C pins**. fileciteturn7file0 So before choosing CRSF versus an intermediary, I would check exactly how the current 2.1.20 build resolves that peripheral conflict. We don't want to casually sacrifice the IMU if we intend to use its data later. memcite

Yes. That changes the best approach.

Your receiver is a **FlySky FS-iA6B**, paired with an **FS-i6** transmitter. The receiver gives us three useful interface options visible right on it:

- Individual **CH1–CH6 servo PWM outputs**
- **PPM/CH1** combined PPM output
- **i-BUS** serial output

For the hoverboard firmware, I would favor **i-BUS** if Gen2.x already supports it, because it gives all six channels digitally over essentially one serial data connection rather than measuring PWM pulse widths. If it doesn't have native FlySky i-BUS support, **PPM** is the next-best direct interface and is already a well-established route in the hoverboard firmware ecosystem.

I would *not* use six individual PWM connections unless necessary.

For the mower, your FS-i6 also gives us plenty of controls. A sensible initial allocation would be approximately:

```text
CH1    steering
CH2    forward/reverse
CH3/4  available
CH5    mower/blade enable
CH6    mode / auxiliary function
```

The exact channel numbers can be assigned however you prefer; I wouldn't assume the transmitter's current stick/channel mapping until we inspect its setup.

More importantly, this receiver means **we don't necessarily need an Arduino/Pico just for RC control**. We should first check the current Gen2.1.20/Gen2.x code for **FlySky i-BUS and PPM receiver support and exactly which GPIO/UART it expects**. Then we can choose the interface that doesn't conflict with your **PA2/PA3 master/slave UART or PB6/PB7 IMU**.

Given what we've already established about your controller hardware, that's what I would check **before flashing 2.1.20**. It may be possible to configure the master firmware for this receiver directly and have a very clean three-wire receiver connection. memcite

How is it connected?

========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-08-27T01:11:52.745586+00:00
Node: 0aa58422-49d1-40e2-9085-6c72f08c8f77
Parent node: 94a6959a-186a-409f-92bd-6f8964853993
========================================================================
With the **FS-iA6B**, there are two separate questions: how the receiver connects electrically, and how the current Gen2.x firmware accepts RC commands.

The receiver itself can output **PPM** from the bottom `PPM/CH1` connection. The original flo199213 Gen2 project actually includes a `PPMDeviceArduino` example, but the current RoboDurden firmware does **not appear to have a native `REMOTE_PPM` mode**. Its currently exposed remote modes include UART, CRSF, ADC, etc.; its documentation describes using an Arduino/ESP32 as the intermediary for `REMOTE_UART`. citeturn0search0turn0search1turn0search7

So the established path for **your particular receiver** is:

```text
FS-i6 transmitter
       │ 2.4 GHz
       ▼
FS-iA6B receiver
       │
       │ PPM
       ▼
 Arduino / Pico / ESP32
       │
       │ Gen2.x UART protocol
       ▼
A / MASTER hoverboard controller
       │
       │ original 4-wire master/slave link
       ▼
B / SLAVE hoverboard controller
```

The intermediary connects to the master's **additional COMM UART**, not the original four-wire A↔B connector. Gen2.x calls this `REMOTE_UART`; it's normally USART0 on **PB6/PB7**, operating at **19,200 baud**. The master receives the requested speed/steering and relays the appropriate motor command to the slave over the original master/slave USART. citeturn0search5turn0search7

There is, however, a problem specific to **our 2.1.20 board**: we've established that **PB6/PB7 are also the factory IMU I²C connection**. The firmware's configuration confirms that USART0 normally occupies PB6/PB7, while USART1/PA2/PA3 is reserved for the master/slave connection. citeturn0search1

So I don't particularly like sacrificing PB6/PB7 just to accommodate an RC receiver, especially because **we specifically want to retain the IMUs for the mower's eventual navigation/odometry system**.

A better solution for this hardware may be to add a small **native PPM decoder to Gen2.x** and connect the FS-iA6B's single PPM signal to an otherwise-unused timer-capable GPIO on the master. PPM decoding is computationally trivial: detect each pulse edge with a timer/input capture, measure the inter-pulse intervals, and recover all six channels. The firmware already has a remote abstraction intended to permit additional implementations such as `RemotePwm`. citeturn0search3

That would give us:

```text
FS-iA6B
 PPM/CH1
    │
    └──── one signal wire ────► spare MASTER GPIO

FS-iA6B GND ──────────────────► MASTER GND

A MASTER PA2/PA3 ═════════════► B SLAVE PA2/PA3
         PB6/PB7 ═════════════► A-board IMU
```

No Arduino, no extra serial port, and **the original IMU remains functional**.

I think that's the cleaner architecture for your mower. Before choosing a GPIO, I would go through the **2.1.20 pin definition and our factory pin-map analysis** and identify an actually unused pin that has a suitable timer/input-capture channel. Then we can add `REMOTE_PPM` to the firmware rather than compromising an existing peripheral. memcite

With the hardware you already have, I would use the **FS-iA6B receiver in PPM mode** and add a small PPM input path to the Gen2.1.20 master firmware.

That avoids adding another microcontroller, preserves the A↔B master/slave UART, and—most importantly—does **not consume PB6/PB7**, which we want to keep for the onboard IMU.

The architecture I’d use is:

**FS-i6 → FS-iA6B → PPM/CH1 output → one spare timer-capable GPIO on A/master → Gen2.1.20 master → PA2/PA3 link → B/slave**

Electrically, the receiver needs only **signal + ground + 5 V power** from a suitable regulated source. Do not power it from the GD32’s 3.3-V rail; the FS-iA6B is specified for roughly 4–8.4 V.

For controls, I’d initially map:
- CH1 = steering
- CH2 = forward/reverse
- CH5/CH6 left free for later starter/engine functions

The remaining task is choosing the safest unused GPIO on your 2.1.20-family master board. I would pick one that:
1. is actually unused on your V4 board,
2. has a timer/input-capture or clean EXTI path,
3. is exposed on a convenient connector or test pad.

Given the pin map we already recovered, I would **not choose PA2/PA3, PB6/PB7, PA8–PA10, PB13–PB15, PC13/PA1/PC14, PA4, PA0, PB0, PA6, PA12, or PB12** because those are already assigned to critical functions.

So my recommendation is: **flash 2.1.20 as master/slave, keep the stock inter-board cable, and add native PPM input to one confirmed spare master GPIO.** That is the cleanest path with what you already own. memcite

ASSISTANT | 2026-08-27T02:55:57.838684+00:00
Node: 54ce2fdf-6cad-43a9-ab5e-68ce3d9952da
Parent node: 19e6e8f5-395b-47d1-8fe5-41fd0186cbd6
========================================================================
Yes — that is probably the best place to steal a PPM input from.

For the 2.1.20 definition, the LED-related pins are:

- **PB3 = green LED**
- **PA15 = orange LED**
- **PB4 = red LED**
- **PB2 = upper LED**
- **PB5 = lower LED** fileciteturn7file0

Those are much better candidates than touching any of the motor, Hall, ADC, UART, latch, or IMU pins.

For PPM input, I would prefer an LED pin that also has a clean **EXTI interrupt line** and is not entangled with startup/debug behavior. **PB5 or PB4** are attractive candidates. PB2 is less attractive because nearby Gen2 layouts sometimes use PB2 as self-hold, so I would avoid it unless we verify your exact V4 board has no hidden dependence on it. **PA15** can also be awkward on ARM boards because it is commonly associated with JTAG/SWD remap functions.

So my first-choice order would be roughly:

**PB5 → PB4 → PB3 → PA15 → PB2**

The nice thing is that PPM does not actually require a hardware timer input-capture channel. A GPIO EXTI interrupt plus a free-running microsecond timer is enough: each rising edge records the elapsed time since the previous edge, and the long synchronization gap identifies the start of the six-channel frame.

That means we could repurpose, for example, **PB5/LOWER_LED as `PPM_IN`** on the master only and leave the slave firmware unchanged.

Electrically:

```text
FS-iA6B PPM signal  → MASTER PB5
FS-iA6B GND         → MASTER GND
FS-iA6B +V          → regulated 5 V
```

One thing to verify before doing it: the FS-iA6B's PPM signal level. The receiver runs from 4–8.4 V, but its signal output is typically logic-level rather than supply-voltage level. I would measure the PPM pin with a meter/scope before connecting it directly to the GD32. If it is ~3.3 V, direct connection is ideal; if it is 5 V, I'd add a simple divider or level shifter.

So yes: **repurposing one of the unused LED GPIOs is exactly what I would do.** PB5 is currently my preferred candidate. memcite

Yes. The GPIO names aren't useful enough when you're standing over the PCB. For the PPM connection, we should identify a **physical component/pad/connector on your actual V4 master board** that is already connected to one of those LED GPIOs, rather than trying to solder directly to the GD32 leg.

The best route is to use your board photos and the factory firmware together to map something like:

> **PB5 → LED/resistor Rxx → exposed pad at [physical location]**

That would give you an unambiguous solder point.

For the GD32F130 **LQFP48 itself**, the candidate LED GPIO MCU pins are:

| GPIO | MCU pin | 2.1.20 function |
|---|---:|---|
| **PB2** | **20** | Upper LED |
| **PB3** | **39** | Green LED |
| **PB4** | **40** | Red LED |
| **PB5** | **41** | Lower LED |
| **PA15** | **38** | Orange LED |

So **PB5 = MCU pin 41** is the one I was proposing for PPM.

Physically, pins 37–48 form one side of the LQFP48 package, so PB5 is conveniently close to the SWD area:

```text
                 LQFP48
             ┌─────────────┐
        PA14 │37         36│
        PA15 │38         35│
         PB3 │39         34│ PA13 / SWDIO
         PB4 │40         33│
PPM →    PB5 │41         32│
         PB6 │42         31│
         PB7 │43         30│
       BOOT0 │44         29│
             │45         28│
         VSS │47         26│
         VDD │48         25│
             └─────────────┘
```

**But I would rather not have you solder to pin 41.** There should be an LED current-limiting resistor or PCB test point connected to PB5 that is vastly easier and safer.

If you send me a **straight-down, reasonably close photo of the entire A/master PCB**, preferably both sides if accessible, I can correlate the components with the earlier photos and identify the most convenient physical solder point for **PB5 (or PB4/PB3 if one has a much better accessible pad)**.

Yes. **A 2-pin blue-LED JST/header is a much better physical PPM connection point** than anything near the MCU.

If that connector is wired in the usual arrangement:

```text
LED connector
┌────────────┐
│ LED GPIO   │ ─────► use this for PPM signal
│ GND/+V     │ ─────► don't use for signal
└────────────┘
```

then after removing/disconnecting the LED, its GPIO conductor gives us exactly what we need: **one accessible digital input pin**. The receiver ground can go to any convenient controller ground.

The important part is determining **which of the 2-pin blue-LED connectors corresponds to which GPIO**. The 2.1.20 source names:

- `UPPER_LED = PB2`
- `LOWER_LED = PB5`

fileciteturn7file0

So if those two blue LED connectors are the original **upper/lower shell LEDs**, there's a good chance they terminate at **PB2 and PB5**. Of those, I'd choose the connector that traces to **PB5**.

### Easy way to identify it

You don't need to trace PCB tracks or touch MCU legs.

With the **factory firmware still installed**, power the hoverboard normally and measure the two pins of each blue-LED connector relative to controller ground. Then operate/power-cycle the board and record what each pin does.

Even better, if the blue LEDs are still available, tell me **which physical LED each connector originally operated**—for example left/right, front/rear, upper/lower—and show me a close photo of those JST connectors and the silkscreen around them.

The factory firmware disassembly may let us determine which GPIO drives which physical LED. Then we'd know something concrete like:

> **Use A/master connector Jx, pin nearest board edge = PB5/PPM signal.**

That's the level of physical identification I'd want before connecting your FS-iA6B.

And yes, once we've identified that conductor, the RC interface can be extremely simple:

```text
FS-iA6B                         A/master
────────                        ────────
PPM/CH1 signal ───────────────► blue LED JST GPIO
GND            ───────────────► controller GND
+5 V           ◄─────────────── suitable 5-V supply
```

Only **one GPIO signal** is required for all six RC channels when using PPM. memcite

Not yet in the sense you mean.

What we currently have is:

1. **Raw factory `.bin` images** for A/master and B/slave.
2. **ARM Thumb assembly disassemblies** — human-readable at the instruction level (`ldr`, `str`, `bl`, `cmp`, etc.).
3. My higher-level analysis where we've identified things such as the Hall decoder, PWM pins, ADC channels, IMU transactions, UARTs, button and self-hold logic.

We **do not yet have a C-like decompilation** of the complete original firmware. Disassembly translates machine code into assembly; decompilation goes further and reconstructs approximate C-like pseudocode. citeturn0search0turn0search3

For example, instead of this:

```asm
ldr     r0,[r6,#0x10]
lsls    r0,r0,#19
bpl     0x08006b44
```

we'd want something resembling:

```c
if (GPIOA->ISTAT & (1 << 12)) {
    // button handling
}
```

Then, as we identify functions and variables, we can progressively turn generic output like:

```c
FUN_08004a72();
```

into something meaningful like:

```c
read_imu();
update_hall_state();
motor_control();
master_slave_uart_rx();
```

That would be **substantially more useful for what we're doing**. It still wouldn't recover the manufacturer's original source code, names, comments, or exact source structure; decompiler output is reconstructed pseudocode and needs manual annotation. citeturn0search3turn0search10

Given that we now have both factory binaries and a probable **Gen2.1.20 source implementation to use as a reference**, we're actually in a good position to do this properly.

I can produce decompiled/annotated versions of **both A and B**, then correlate functions against Gen2.1.20 and label the recognizable subsystems. That would give us something much closer to a readable reconstruction of the original firmware rather than the enormous raw `.asm` files we made earlier. memcite

========================================================================
Yes. That is exactly the right way to approach it.

Because your `OL20180703-V4` and the Gen2.1.20 `OL20190703-V4.1` appear to share the same fundamental GD32F130 motor-control architecture, the Gen2.1.20 source gives us a **semantic reference** for the factory binaries rather than forcing us to reverse-engineer every register access from scratch.

We can use the known source to identify functions by combinations of peripheral addresses, GPIO masks, call structure, constants, interrupt vectors, and data flow. For example, when the factory binary configures `PA8/PA9/PA10` and `PB13/PB14/PB15` through TIMER0, we can correlate that directly with the Gen2.1.20 BLDC initialization. Likewise, the `PC13/PA1/PC14` reads identify the Hall decoder, `PB6/PB7` plus I²C address `0x68` identifies the IMU routines, and `PA2/PA3` identifies the master/slave UART.

That lets us progressively reconstruct something much closer to:

```c
void hall_update(void)
{
    hall =
        (gpio_read(PC13) << 0) |
        (gpio_read(PA1)  << 1) |
        (gpio_read(PC14) << 2);

    // update wheel position / commutation state
}
```

rather than leaving it as anonymous assembly.

The source is also extremely useful for distinguishing **hardware differences from firmware differences**. If the factory binary and Gen2.1.20 both access the same GPIO/peripheral for a function, confidence becomes high. If the factory binary accesses another pin, we know we've found one of the V4 ↔ V4.1 changes instead of blindly inheriting the newer definition.

What I would reconstruct from both A and B is therefore:

**startup/power latch → GPIO/peripheral initialization → ADC/current sensing → Hall decoding → TIMER0/PWM commutation → IMU acquisition → balance calculations → master/slave UART protocol → LEDs/buttons/accessories → main state machine**

and then annotate role-specific A/B differences.

The end result can be a **C-like reconstructed factory firmware tree** with meaningful names and comments, using Gen2.1.20 as the reference while clearly marking anything inferred versus directly established from the factory binary. It won't reproduce the manufacturer's original source text, but it can become genuinely readable and modifiable rather than just a disassembly.

At this point, the Gen2.1.20 source should be treated almost like a **symbol map/template for decompiling your V4 firmware**. That should make the reconstruction considerably more accurate. memcite

Yes. I went back through both factory images with **Gen2.1.20 treated as the direct V4.1 hardware reference**, rather than merely a similar-board reference.

The deeper result is that the factory firmware is now fairly well partitioned into recognizable subsystems. Both A/master and B/slave contain the full local control stack: **their own MPU60x0 IMU, Hall decoding, phase-current/battery ADC, complementary TIMER0 BLDC drive, power latch/button handling, and PA2/PA3 inter-board USART**. That aligns very closely with the Gen2.1.20 hardware definition, which explicitly assigns the same Hall, BLDC, UART, IMU, latch and ADC pins. fileciteturn7file0

The most interesting new finding is the factory **A↔B protocol**. USART1's interrupt handler ultimately feeds a packet dispatcher around `0x08006E64`. The factory protocol uses a **16-bit CRC/check**, a receive buffer, and at least command IDs `01, 02, 03, 04, 05, 06, 0F, 10, 78`. Command `0x78` is particularly large and decodes several signed 16-bit quantities plus numerous packed flags, making it a strong candidate for the main motion/state packet. This is substantially richer than the replacement Gen2.x master/slave protocol, whose current source sends a much simpler master→slave PWM/state packet and slave→master current/speed/odometer packet. fileciteturn12file0

I also confirmed that the IMU code is not merely linked-in library code. On **both boards**, a large active routine reads 14 bytes from MPU register `0x3B`, reconstructs all six accel/gyro axes, performs stability/calibration checks, sign/orientation conversion, filtering and additional numerical processing. That supports exactly the architecture you described: each half computes local attitude information for its own wheel-control loop.

The factory interrupt layout is also consistent with Gen2's general control architecture: TIMER0 is used for BLDC timing, ADC/DMA participate in the fast motor loop, and USART handles the inter-board communications. The open Gen2.x code follows that same broad division, with TIMER0 triggering ADC work and DMA completion driving the BLDC calculation. fileciteturn15file0

I put the detailed address map, reconstructed pseudocode, A/B comparison, and remaining unknowns into a consolidated report:

[Download the deep V4.1 factory firmware analysis](sandbox:/mnt/data/Gyroor_G5_V4_1_factory_firmware_deep_analysis.md)

The **highest-value next step is now a logic-analyzer capture of the original four-wire A↔B link while the factory firmware is still installed**. Because we already know where the receive parser, CRC and command dispatcher are, controlled tests—tilt A only, tilt B only, rotate each wheel, etc.—should let us assign names to the `0x78` fields much faster than static disassembly alone. That could expose the factory **pitch, gyro, wheel-speed/odometry, torque/request and status fields** before you replace the firmware. memcite

For the two boards on the bench now, the critical conclusion is that they are **V4.1 / Gen2.1.20-compatible hardware**, with the important motor-control pinout already matching the open-source firmware. That means you can reasonably use the open firmware as the traction controller for the RC mower rather than trying to adapt the factory balance logic.

For the **FS-iA6B**, the physically simplest path is still likely to be **PPM into one repurposed LED-header GPIO on the A/master board**, because that uses one signal wire plus ground, preserves the stock A↔B UART, and preserves PB6/PB7 for the onboard IMU. The two-pin blue LED JSTs are therefore exactly the kind of connection point worth using.

- flash one V4.1 pair with the **Gen2.1.20 master/slave configuration**;
- confirm both motors run correctly under the open firmware;
- identify which blue LED JST maps to the best spare GPIO;
- add/use a PPM decoder on that GPIO;
- feed CH1 steering + CH2 forward/reverse from the FS-iA6B into the master;
- let the master perform differential mixing and command the slave over the original four-wire link.

The untouched third hoverboard is valuable for a different reason: it gives you a **live reference platform for the stock firmware**. Because we now have the factory binaries and a partial semantic map, that complete unit can later be used to correlate UART traffic, IMU values, Hall counts, balance commands, fault states, and other sensor behavior against the disassembly. That is much more useful than having only dead binary images.

You already know each controller has **local Hall odometry, a local IMU, current sensing, a motor bridge, and an inter-board communications path**. That gives you much more to work with than treating the boards as anonymous BLDC controllers.

The immediate next step I would take is to build a **known-good Gen2.1.20 MASTER and SLAVE pair for these V4.1 boards**, without touching the receiver input yet. Once both motors are proven under that firmware, we can make the smallest possible PPM modification for the FS-iA6B. memcite

The first step is to get a completely stock **Gen2.1.20 MASTER/SLAVE build running on one pair**, with no RC modifications yet.

For the master build, configure the GD32 Gen2.x firmware for **layout 20 / MASTER**. For the slave, compile the same layout as **SLAVE**. Keep the original PA2/PA3 four-wire master/slave connection between them. The open firmware's master/slave implementation already has the master sending the slave its motor command and state, while the slave returns current, speed and odometer information. fileciteturn12file0

Before flashing, I want to inspect the **current repository's exact configuration options for layout 20** rather than give you stale `#define`s. The project has changed considerably from the original Gen2 firmware—the current startup code, for example, conditionally initializes the available USARTs, I²C/IMU, ADC, PWM and BLDC driver according to the selected target/layout/configuration. fileciteturn9file0

The process will therefore be:

1. **Clone the current GD32 Gen2.x repository on your Windows 11 machine.** Since you're already using STM32CubeProgrammer there, we can keep flashing on Windows. Building can be done with the project's supported toolchain.

2. **Make two configurations:** `V4.1 MASTER` and `V4.1 SLAVE`, both using Gen2.1.20. Initially we leave the motor-control parameters at conservative/default values and don't add PPM.

3. **Build two binaries** and label them unmistakably, e.g. `V4.1_2.1.20_MASTER.bin` and `V4.1_2.1.20_SLAVE.bin`. Your original A/B dumps remain untouched.

4. **Flash the slave first**, reconnect its original four-wire link, then flash the master. Initially keep the wheels unloaded/off the ground.

5. **Power the pair normally.** Verify self-hold, A↔B communication and that neither controller reports a fault before asking either motor to move.

6. Once that works, use whatever **built-in test/control facility Gen2.x provides** to command very low motor output. Verify Hall sequence, direction and current sensing independently on each side.

7. **Only after the pair is proven** do we modify the master for the FS-iA6B. At that point we'll identify one of the two blue LED JSTs as PB5/PB2 and turn that GPIO into `PPM_IN`. The slave shouldn't need any RC-specific modification.

There is one especially useful safety advantage here: the open firmware's motor loop is structured around TIMER0-triggered ADC sampling and DMA-driven BLDC calculation, so the current sensing isn't just ancillary telemetry—it participates directly in the motor-control infrastructure. fileciteturn15file0

**Don't flash anything quite yet.** The next thing I should do is pull the current `config.h`, `defines_2-1-20.h`, build instructions, and relevant master/slave configuration files from the repository and give you the **exact changes and build commands for our V4.1 pair**. That removes ambiguity about which current options correspond to MASTER, SLAVE, target, layout, IMU and driving mode. memcite

========================================================================
Agreed. For the RC mower phase, we should **borrow the control architecture from the mature RC-hoverboard implementations**, not invent a new control path just because your board is split Gen2 hardware.

The most directly relevant reference I found is the FOC family that already supports exactly the interfaces we care about: **PPM, PWM, and FlySky iBUS**. One fork explicitly lists `VARIANT_IBUS` for a FlySky receiver, plus `VARIANT_PPM` and `VARIANT_PWM`; the upstream EFeru config also has native PPM support with six channels and optional tank steering. citeturn355988search6turn355988search7

For your mower, the architecture I would copy is:

```text
FS-i6 transmitter
       │ RF
       ▼
FS-iA6B receiver
       │
       │ PPM-Sum   ← simplest physical connection
       ▼
Gen2.1.20 MASTER
       │
       │ speed + steer mixing
       │
       ├────────► local motor
       │
       └── PA2/PA3 master/slave UART
                    │
                    ▼
               Gen2.1.20 SLAVE
                    │
                    ▼
                 motor
```

That is essentially the same architecture used by the established RC variants: **one receiver feeds one controller, the firmware converts throttle/steer into two motor commands**, and the motor layer handles the two traction motors. The distinction in your case is simply that the second motor is physically controlled by a separate slave PCB rather than the same MCU. The current Gen2.x master/slave code is already designed to send the slave its requested motor command and receive speed/current/odometry back. fileciteturn12file0

I would use **PPM-Sum first**, despite your receiver also supporting iBUS. The mature FOC implementation already demonstrates the correct RC behavior: six-channel PPM decoding, center/deadband calibration, throttle/steering mixing, failsafe handling, and optional tank steering. citeturn355988search7 We can port that *input/control layer* into Gen2.1.20 while leaving its motor-control and master/slave layers alone.

That also means the firmware modification should be relatively contained:

1. Take the **PPM decoder/input normalization** approach from EFeru's RC variant.
2. Assign it to one unused LED-header GPIO on the Gen2.1.20 master.
3. Decode all six FS-iA6B channels.
4. Initially use only **steering + forward/reverse**.
5. Implement receiver-loss timeout so loss of valid PPM immediately commands zero drive.
6. Feed the resulting normalized `speed` and `steer` into Gen2.x's existing master mixer.
7. Let the existing master/slave code command the second motor.

I would **not use tank steering initially**. Your FS-i6 is more naturally operated as one steering axis plus one throttle axis, and the firmware can produce:

```text
left  = throttle + steering
right = throttle - steering
```

with saturation to the permitted range. That's the usual differential-drive arrangement.

The reason I'm now more confident about this route is that we're not designing the RC behavior from theory. We're combining two already-proven pieces:

**mature RC/PPM hoverboard input architecture** → **your verified Gen2.1.20 motor/master-slave architecture**. citeturn355988search6turn355988search8

So before flashing, I would modify the Gen2.1.20 source to add a `REMOTE_PPM` implementation modeled specifically on EFeru's PPM path. Then your first firmware can already be the **actual RC-mower build**, instead of flashing a test build and replacing it immediately afterward. memcite

ASSISTANT | 2026-08-27T21:26:12.292954+00:00
Node: df96bd52-e122-4b55-bd59-e42050ce1405

========================================================================
Yes. I searched specifically for **RC mower/yard-robot projects using hacked hoverboard controllers**, rather than just generic hoverboard RC conversions. There is a particularly relevant example because it uses essentially **your exact radio hardware**.

### Most relevant: Wijkbot/Hoodbot

The Wijkbot project explicitly uses **FlySky FS-i6/FS-i6S transmitters with FS-iA6B receivers** and a hacked hoverboard controller. citeturn1view0

Their preferred connection is **iBUS**, not PPM and not an Arduino intermediary:

```text
FlySky FS-i6
      │
      │ RF
      ▼
FS-iA6B
      │
      │ iBUS
      ▼
hoverboard controller
      │
      ├── left motor
      └── right motor
```

They enable `VARIANT_IBUS` in EFeru firmware and physically connect the receiver with only:

```text
FS-iA6B             Hoverboard PCB

iBUS signal ───────► PB11/RX
GND         ───────► GND
VCC         ───────► 3.3 V
```

Their instructions specifically say to solder a wire to `PB11/RX/SDA`, then connect the FS-iA6B using **PB11 + 3.3 V + GND**. They also note that PB10/PB11 can be reversed on some controller revisions. citeturn1view0

That is very relevant to our situation because it demonstrates that **FS-iA6B → hacked hoverboard controller directly, with no Arduino**, is already a proven mower/robot architecture.

### Other mower projects

The projects divide into three main approaches.

**Direct RC → hoverboard controller** is used when the hacked firmware already understands the receiver protocol. An AgOpenGPS zero-turn mower project uses EFeru firmware's **PWM variant**, connecting two receiver channels directly to the hoverboard controller: one for steering and one for speed. citeturn1search4

The EFeru firmware also supports **PPM-SUM directly**. Its documented wiring is receiver PPM → one sensor-cable input, plus receiver power and ground. Six channels are decoded from that one signal wire. citeturn1view1

There is also a master/slave FOC fork specifically supporting direct RC. Its PPM configuration connects **one receiver PPM signal to USART1 RX**, with CH1/CH2 assigned to the two motor commands; its PWM configuration instead connects two individual receiver PWM channels to TX/RX. citeturn1search2turn1search3

The other common architecture is **receiver → ESP32/Arduino → hoverboard UART**. HoverMower and ROSMower take this approach because they need a higher-level computer anyway. HoverMower flashes the hoverboard controller for serial motor commands and connects an MCU/Raspberry Pi/etc. to it; ROSMower similarly uses UART so it can receive speed, odometry and battery telemetry. citeturn1search0turn1search8 A newer AutoMo autonomous mower likewise uses an ESP32 between the high-level system and a UART-controlled hoverboard board. citeturn1search5

### What that changes for our mower

For **your present RC mower**, I would now revise my earlier recommendation:

**Use the FS-iA6B directly. Don't add an Arduino/Pico.**

And I'd investigate **iBUS first**, because there is already a mower/yard-robot implementation using your exact **FS-i6 + FS-iA6B** combination this way. PPM becomes the fallback.

The difficulty is simply that their Gen1 controller had a convenient PB11 UART input, whereas your Gen2.1.20 V4.1 has different resource allocation:

```text
Your V4.1:

PA2/PA3  → MASTER ↔ SLAVE UART       KEEP
PB6/PB7  → onboard MPU6050 I²C       KEEP

LED GPIOs → presently unnecessary    AVAILABLE
```

So the question becomes whether we can take the **existing EFeru iBUS receiver decoder** and attach it to one of your unused LED-header GPIOs/UART-capable alternatives without sacrificing the IMU or A↔B link.

If that turns out awkward because iBUS really wants a hardware UART, **PPM is the easiest electrically**. The established EFeru PPM architecture is literally one receiver signal wire to the controller, and all six channels arrive on it. citeturn1view1

So my priority order for your V4.1 hardware would now be:

**direct iBUS if we can expose a spare UART cleanly → direct PPM through a blue LED JST GPIO → intermediary MCU only if neither is practical.**

The next thing I'd check is the **GD32F130 alternate-function map against every currently unused Gen2.1.20 connector GPIO**. There may be an unused LED-header pin that can actually be remapped to a USART RX peripheral, in which case we could essentially duplicate the proven Wijkbot **FS-iA6B iBUS → controller** arrangement without adding hardware. memcite

You're right. I broadened the search beyond what you asked.

I searched again specifically for **actual RC lawn-mower builds**, with particular attention to builds using **hoverboard motors/controllers** and how the RC receiver is physically connected.

The most directly applicable build I found is an RC lawn mower that actually reflashes a hoverboard controller. Its author states that essentially any PWM RC radio works and connects **two ordinary receiver channel outputs directly to two ADC inputs on the hoverboard controller**:

```text
RC receiver                         Hoverboard controller
───────────                         ─────────────────────
Aileron / steering signal ────────► ADC1
Elevator / throttle signal ───────► ADC2

Receiver +V ◄────────────────────── Hall 5 V
Receiver GND ─────────────────────► Hall GND
```

There is **no Arduino, ESP32, PPM decoder or iBUS decoder between them**. The modified hoverboard firmware reads the two RC servo PWM signals directly. citeturn0search3

That's much closer to what we're building.

I also found an independent RC-mower/hoverboard discussion where the builder says their previous hoverboard had been reflashed specifically for **RC lawn-mower PPM control**, using EFeru firmware configured for the RC input. citeturn0search8

A separate actual RC mower using your exact **FS-i6 + FS-iA6B** radio pair uses CH1 for steering and CH2 for forward/reverse. Its control unit reads the receiver's individual PWM outputs. citeturn0search2

So among the mower projects, there are really two demonstrated direct approaches relevant to us:

**Two individual PWM channels**
```text
FS-iA6B CH1 signal ──► controller input 1
FS-iA6B CH2 signal ──► controller input 2
FS-iA6B GND        ──► controller GND
FS-iA6B VCC        ──► suitable receiver supply
```

or **PPM**
```text
FS-iA6B PPM ─────────► one controller input
GND        ──────────► GND
VCC        ──────────► receiver supply
```

For **your mower**, I now favor the **two-channel PWM approach** if we can repurpose two accessible LED JST signal pins.

Why? It most closely copies an **already-working hacked-hoverboard RC mower**, and it requires essentially no protocol stack. We only measure two 1–2 ms servo pulses:

```text
CH1 → steering
CH2 → forward/reverse
```

Then Gen2.x does:

```text
left  = throttle + steering
right = throttle - steering
```

and sends the appropriate command to the slave.

Your board conveniently has **two 2-pin blue-LED connectors that you're no longer using**. If those prove to be two independent GPIOs, that potentially gives us an extremely clean physical implementation:

```text
                FS-iA6B
              ┌─────────┐
CH1 signal ───┤ CH1     ├────► Blue LED JST #1 signal
CH2 signal ───┤ CH2     ├────► Blue LED JST #2 signal
GND ──────────┤ -       ├────► controller GND
+5 V ─────────┤ +       │
              └─────────┘

                       A / MASTER
                         │
                         │ stock PA2/PA3 link
                         ▼
                       B / SLAVE
```

This also leaves **PB6/PB7 and both IMUs completely untouched**.

So I would change our immediate plan slightly: **before writing a PPM implementation, identify the GPIOs behind those two blue LED JSTs.** If they are suitable interrupt/timer GPIOs, copying the proven RC-mower **CH1 + CH2 direct PWM architecture** is probably the simplest solution physically *and* in firmware. memcite

For **your two V4.1 boards**, I'd do it in two stages. We do **not** need to solve the FS-iA6B interface before proving the Gen2.1.20 firmware.

### 1. Get the unmodified Gen2.1.20 pair running

Use the current urlRoboDurden Gen2.x GD32 firmwarehttps://github.com/RoboDurden/Hoverboard-Firmware-Hack-Gen2.x-GD32.

The current `config.h` confirms the intended architecture: `MASTER` and `SLAVE` builds use USART1 for the inter-board connection, while the motor-control configuration is selected by the GD32 target and `LAYOUT`. citeturn0search0

For both builds we want:

```c
#ifdef GD32F130
#define LAYOUT 20
#endif
```

Then make **two binaries**.

Master:

```c
#define MASTER
//#define SLAVE
//#define SINGLE
```

Slave:

```c
//#define MASTER
#define SLAVE
//#define SINGLE
```

And initially I'd use:

```c
#define BLDC_SINE
#define DRIVING_MODE 0
```

The repository itself warns that incorrect layouts can short the bridge, so the first powered test should be current-limited. citeturn0search1

We already have unusually strong evidence that layout 20 is correct because we've compared its critical pin assignments against your factory binaries.

### 2. First test with `REMOTE_DUMMY`

For the **master test build**, leave:

```c
#define REMOTE_DUMMY
```

enabled.

That's actually useful here. The current firmware says Dummy generates its own repeating command, so we don't need the RC receiver connected for the first test. citeturn0search0

That gives us:

```text
MASTER 2.1.20
      │
      │ original 4-wire harness
      ▼
SLAVE 2.1.20

   │           │
 motor A     motor B
```

Put both drive wheels **off the ground**.

I'd initially power the pair through a **current-limited supply**, rather than giving it unrestricted battery current. The project itself recommends roughly a 1–2 A current-limited source during this kind of testing. citeturn0search0turn0search4

If Dummy makes both motors behave correctly, we've established the important part:

> **Gen2.1.20 correctly operates your actual power stages, Hall sensors and master/slave arrangement.**

Then we stop changing the motor-control side.

### 3. Then make it an RC mower

The actual RC-mower example we found uses an extremely simple arrangement: **two ordinary receiver PWM outputs directly into the hacked hoverboard controller**, one steering and one throttle. No intermediate Arduino. citeturn0search2

We can reproduce that architecture on your Gen2 board:

```text
                    FS-iA6B
              ┌────────────────┐
              │                │
CH1 signal ───┤ steering       │
              │                │
CH2 signal ───┤ throttle       │
              └───────┬────────┘
                      │ GND
                      │
             A / MASTER V4.1
              │           │
       PWM input 1    PWM input 2
              │           │
           steering    throttle
                  │
             differential
                mixing
             /          \
        local motor    slave command
                           │
                      PA2 / PA3
                           │
                           ▼
                       B / SLAVE
                           │
                         motor
```

The RC-mower reference connects the receiver's aileron and elevator servo outputs directly to two controller inputs and uses the hoverboard Hall 5-V/GND supply for the receiver. citeturn0search2

### 4. Use your LED connectors instead of soldering to tiny pads

This is where we'll adapt that mower architecture to your board.

You have those accessible **blue two-pin LED connectors**. We don't care about the LEDs anymore, so I'd repurpose two LED GPIOs:

```text
FS-iA6B CH1 signal → blue LED connector #1 GPIO
FS-iA6B CH2 signal → blue LED connector #2 GPIO

FS-iA6B GND → controller GND
FS-iA6B +V  → suitable regulated receiver supply
```

Then we add a very small `REMOTE_PWM` implementation to Gen2.x that measures the two servo pulse widths.

Something conceptually like:

```c
CH1: 1000–2000 us → steering -1000...+1000
CH2: 1000–2000 us → throttle -1000...+1000

left  = throttle + steering;
right = throttle - steering;
```

with neutral deadband, range limiting and—critically—a **receiver-loss timeout that commands zero**.

That duplicates the proven RC-mower control architecture while leaving Gen2.1.20 responsible for everything underneath.

### What I suggest we do right now

Don't modify anything for RC yet.

**First build the Gen2.1.20 MASTER-DUMMY and SLAVE pair and test the two motors.**

Once that works, we'll identify the two physical blue LED connectors and modify the master firmware specifically for **FS-iA6B CH1/CH2 PWM input through those connectors**. That keeps each troubleshooting step isolated and gives us a known-working motor-control baseline before adding RC. memcite

For the initial Gen2.1.20 test, the setup is simply:

```text
Power supply / battery
        │
        ├──────── MASTER V4.1 ─── Motor A
        │             │
        │        4-wire stock
        │          A↔B cable
        │             │
        └──────── SLAVE V4.1 ──── Motor B
```

We can compile the master with the firmware's **dummy/test input**, so the master generates the motor commands internally. That lets us verify the master/slave communication, Hall sensors, motor phases, direction and basic motor operation without an RC receiver or ESP32.

Once that works, the intended mower configuration is also **ESP32-free**:

```text
FS-i6X
  ↓ radio
FS-iA6B
  ├─ CH1 PWM ──► MASTER steering input
  └─ CH2 PWM ──► MASTER throttle input
                       │
                       ├──► Motor A
                       │
                       └──► SLAVE ──► Motor B
```

The ESP32 would only become useful later if you want a higher-level controller for autonomy, navigation, telemetry, GPS/LiDAR integration, etc. It isn't necessary for basic RC operation.

So right now you need essentially **the two V4.1 boards, two motors, their A↔B cable, power, and your programmer for flashing**. The FS-iA6B can stay disconnected until we've proven the basic firmware. memcite

ASSISTANT | 2026-08-29T18:49:37.279982+00:00
Node: 1ea30226-8dae-4589-add7-e02be422d1f7
Parent node: c645e9b9-3610-4b6b-b3fd-0e413e03fafd
========================================================================
For the **eventual autonomous mower**, that makes sense—but there are two different roles here.

An **RP2040** is much better than an ESP32 as a deterministic real-time I/O coprocessor. Its PIO blocks are excellent for decoding RC PWM/PPM, reading encoders, generating precise timing, handling watchdog/E-stop logic, and acting as a clean bridge between higher-level software and the hoverboard controllers. It is not, however, substantially more powerful than an ESP32 in the general-compute sense; its advantage is predictable timing and flexible peripherals.

A **Pi Zero 2 W** is far more capable computationally. That is the better platform for things like navigation, mapping, route planning, Wi-Fi UI, logging, camera processing at modest rates, GPS integration, and eventually LiDAR. I would **not** make Linux directly responsible for motor failsafe timing, though.

The architecture I'd favor for the finished machine is therefore:

```text
Pi Zero 2 W
   │
   │ high-level commands / telemetry
   │ UART or USB
   ▼
RP2040
   │
   ├── RC receiver input
   ├── hard watchdog / failsafe
   ├── E-stop / engine kill logic
   ├── auxiliary servos / starter
   │
   └── motor command interface
            │
            ▼
Hoverboard MASTER
      │
      └── stock master/slave link
                 ▼
          Hoverboard SLAVE
```

That gives you a useful hierarchy: the **Pi decides where to go**, the **RP2040 decides whether it is safe to accept that command and handles real-time I/O**, and the **GD32 hoverboard boards continue doing the actual motor commutation/current control**.

For the current bench testing, though, I still wouldn't add either one. First prove that the two V4.1 boards run correctly on the Gen2.1.20 firmware. Then we can decide whether to connect the FS-iA6B directly to the master for the first drive test, or introduce the RP2040 immediately as the RC/failsafe controller.

Given where you ultimately want this mower to go, I would probably choose the **RP2040 + Pi Zero 2 W combination**, rather than trying to make one board do everything. memcite

ASSISTANT | 2026-08-29T18:51:46.536376+00:00
Node: c931ff93-8889-4379-a142-ede295b26f07
Parent node: e3472204-dbf6-47c6-9bdf-e68fa0d922c2
========================================================================
Correct. In this architecture an RP2040 would mostly be redundant. The **GD32F130 on the hoverboard controller is already the real-time motor/servo controller**.

A cleaner eventual architecture is:

```text
                 Pi Zero 2 W
              high-level control
          navigation / mapping / UI
                     │
               UART commands
                     │
                     ▼
             GD32 MASTER V4.1
          real-time vehicle control
              │             │
              │             └── A↔B UART ──► GD32 SLAVE
              │                              └── Motor B
              └── Motor A

RC mode:
FS-iA6B ─────────────► GD32 MASTER
```

The **master GD32** can handle RC pulse decoding, steering/throttle mixing, command validation, timeout/failsafe, acceleration/ramp limits, Hall feedback, current limiting, local motor control, and commands to the slave. Those are all jobs that benefit from deterministic MCU timing.

Later, the Pi doesn't need to generate PWM or directly control motors. It can send higher-level commands such as:

```c
drive.speed = 350;
drive.steering = -120;
```

or preferably explicit differential commands:

```c
drive.left  = 300;
drive.right = 400;
```

The GD32 remains responsible for turning those requests into safe motor operation.

I'd also make **RC control independent of the Pi**. The master could have two command sources:

```text
             FS-iA6B
                │
                ▼
          ┌──────────────┐
Pi ──────►│ GD32 MASTER  │
          │              │
          │ RC override  │
          │ failsafe     │
          │ mixer        │
          │ motor limits │
          └──────────────┘
```

If the Pi crashes, reboots or loses communications, the GD32 stops rather than continuing the last Pi command. If valid RC input is present, we could have it **override autonomous control entirely**.

That actually makes modifying the Gen2.1.20 firmware properly now worthwhile. Instead of treating the GD32 firmware as merely a motor driver, we can structure it as the permanent **low-level vehicle controller**. Then adding the Pi Zero later is just adding another command source.

For the gas mower, the GD32 also has enough capability to handle things such as **engine throttle servo, starter relay, engine kill, blade enable and RPM input** if we have sufficient accessible GPIOs. That would leave the Pi completely outside the safety-critical control path. memcite

The project still supports separate `MASTER`, `SLAVE`, and `SINGLE` builds, and the master/slave link remains `MASTERSLAVE_USART 1`, which is PA2/PA3 on these boards. citeturn758459search0turn758459search1

For the first test, make the master configuration essentially:

```c
#ifdef GD32F130

#define LAYOUT 20

#endif

#define DRIVING_MODE 0

#define MASTER
//#define SLAVE
//#define SINGLE

#if defined(MASTER) || defined(SINGLE)

#endif
```

And the slave:

```c
#ifdef GD32F130

#define LAYOUT 20

#endif

#define BLDC_SINE
#define DRIVING_MODE 0

//#define MASTER
#define SLAVE
//#define SINGLE
```

Leave:

```c
#define MASTERSLAVE_USART 1
```

alone. That's the original PA2/PA3 master/slave interface. citeturn758459search0

The **dummy remote is exactly what we want for the first test**. The project's documentation describes its dummy/test firmware as ignoring external speed input and automatically cycling the command, historically roughly from `-300` to `+300`. That lets us test the entire drive system without the FS-iA6B, Pi, RP2040, or anything else attached. citeturn758459search2

So the bench setup is:

```text
            stock 4-wire cable
MASTER V4.1 ====================== SLAVE V4.1
    │                                  │
    │                                  │
 motor A                            motor B
    │                                  │
 wheel                              wheel

        common hoverboard battery/power
```

Both motors should be connected exactly as they originally were, including their Hall connectors.

### One thing I want to be careful about

The current repository has changed since the older Gen2.1.20 builds. It now defaults to `BLDC_SINE`, whereas older firmware used block commutation. That's not inherently a problem—the current project explicitly supports the sine implementation—but for our **very first proof-of-hardware test**, I would actually consider starting with the control mode known to have been used by the older Gen2.1.20 firmware if we can establish that definitively.

The repository also specifically warns to first test unfamiliar configurations with about **1–2 A current-limited power** because a wrong layout can produce destructive bridge switching. citeturn758459search0turn758459search2

In our case, however, we're in a much better position than someone guessing a layout: we've already matched layout 20's Hall, PWM, UART, button, self-hold, current-sense and IMU pins against your **factory V4.1 binaries**. So layout 20 isn't merely a visual-board-match guess.

### What I would do next

Before you edit/build anything, I can pull the **actual `defines_2-1-20.h` and the current project build files** and give you a precise step-by-step for your Windows machine:

**clone/download → edit these exact lines → select GD32F130 target → compile MASTER → compile SLAVE → locate the `.bin` files → flash them with STM32CubeProgrammer.**

That way we aren't guessing at the current Keil project layout or output filenames. memcite

Yes. We can pin this down substantially better now.

The **Gen2.1.20 hardware definition gives the LED GPIOs as**:

- RGB/3-color LED outputs:
  - **Green = PB3**
  - **Orange = PA15**
  - **Red = PB4**
- Separate LED outputs:
  - **Upper LED = PB2**
  - **Lower LED = PB5** fileciteturn24file0L1-L2

And this is not just an arbitrary open-source assignment. Our stock-firmware reverse engineering has already shown that the Gen2.1.20 definition matches your factory firmware on essentially all of the critical pins we checked—Hall inputs, BLDC outputs, PA2/PA3 UART, PB6/PB7 IMU, PA12 button, PB12 self-hold, PA4 battery ADC, and PA0/PB0 current sense. fileciteturn19file5L411-L423

So the likely physical arrangement on your board is:

```text
4-pin RGB LED header
--------------------
common supply/ground
PB3  = green
PA15 = orange
PB4  = red
```

and the two individual blue LED connectors should correspond to:

```text
upper blue LED → PB2
lower blue LED → PB5
```

The important distinction is that **the stock binary analysis we previously did did not yet explicitly label the LED GPIO writes**; that was left as an unresolved item in the report. fileciteturn19file0L90-L92 But because layout 20 has now matched the factory firmware on every major hardware signal we've tested, **PB3 / PA15 / PB4 for the RGB header and PB2 / PB5 for the two other LEDs are high-confidence candidates**.

For our RC mower, this actually gives us several attractive spare GPIOs:

```text
PB2  ─ possible RC PWM input
PB5  ─ possible RC PWM input

PB3  ─ possible RC input
PB4  ─ possible RC input
PA15 ─ possible RC input
```

I particularly like **PB2 + PB5**, because those appear to be the two separate LED outputs, so we wouldn't even need to disturb the RGB header. If the two blue 2-pin JSTs are indeed `UPPER_LED` and `LOWER_LED`, we could potentially do:

```text
FS-iA6B CH1 PWM → PB2 JST
FS-iA6B CH2 PWM → PB5 JST
GND             → controller GND
```

That would be almost ideal for what you wanted: **two stock connectors, no soldering to the MCU, and direct steering/throttle PWM into the GD32.**

The next useful step is to go back into **our actual A/master stock binary** and specifically trace the GPIOB bit-2/bit-5 and PB3/PB4/PA15 operations. That should let us verify from the factory firmware itself which ones it initializes as LED outputs and ideally distinguish **upper vs lower vs RGB**, rather than relying solely on the Gen2.1.20 definition.

ASSISTANT | 2026-08-30T00:13:44.289758+00:00
Node: d411e86e-a570-4091-a7fa-228fcf9262d7
Parent node: f454dd9c-b821-461a-9597-e71c371c8e80
========================================================================
Yes — within limits, we should be able to get much closer to a **source-level reconstruction** of the stock firmware than we normally could from a raw binary.

The reason is that Gen2.1.20 appears to be not merely “similar hardware,” but a very close hardware match. Its pin definitions line up with the factory binary on the important peripherals: Hall sensors, BLDC outputs, PA2/PA3 inter-board UART, PB6/PB7 IMU bus, button, self-hold, battery ADC, and phase-current ADC. fileciteturn19file5L411-L423 The Gen2.1.20 header also fills in the remaining board-level signals such as PB3/PA15/PB4 LEDs, PB2/PB5 upper/lower LEDs, PB9 buzzer, PC15/PA11 photosensors, etc. fileciteturn24file0L1-L2

That means we can use Gen2.1.20 as a **hardware-symbol dictionary** while decompiling the factory binary. Instead of seeing something like:

```c
gpio_bit_write(GPIOB, GPIO_PIN_12, SET);
```

and having to guess what PB12 does, we can rename it:

```c
SELF_HOLD = 1;
```

Likewise:

```text
PB6/PB7  → IMU software I²C
PA2/PA3  → master/slave USART
PC13/PA1/PC14 → Hall A/B/C
PA8-10 + PB13-15 → motor bridge
PA4 → battery voltage
PA0/PB0 → phase currents
PA12 → power button
PB9 → buzzer
PB3/PA15/PB4 → RGB LEDs
PB2/PB5 → upper/lower LEDs
```

That dramatically improves decompilation quality because a large part of embedded reverse engineering is identifying what each peripheral access represents.

What we **cannot** recover is the literal original source file with the manufacturer's variable names, comments, formatting and function names. Those are gone from the binary. But we can produce something functionally very close, for example:

```c
void imu_init(void)
{
    imu_write(0x6B, ...);
    imu_write(0x19, ...);
    imu_write(0x1A, ...);
    imu_write(0x1B, ...);
    imu_write(0x1C, ...);
}
```

because the factory firmware actually contains those MPU register accesses, and we've already located that subsystem. fileciteturn19file6L438-L508

The same applies to the Hall reader. We've already reconstructed the factory code far enough to show it reading PC13, PA1 and PC14 to build the Hall state. fileciteturn19file6L518-L539 That can become a clean source function rather than anonymous assembly.

The practical workflow would be:

1. **Use Gen2.1.20 only for hardware identities and likely subsystem structure.**
2. **Use the factory A/B binaries as the authority for actual behavior.**
3. Decompile the factory code in Ghidra.
4. Rename peripheral accesses according to the confirmed Gen2.1.20 pin map.
5. Identify functions by comparing their behavior with the open-source implementation.
6. Rewrite each recovered function into clean C.
7. Compare A vs B to identify the exact master/slave role differences.
8. Eventually compile our reconstructed C and compare the generated machine code/behavior against the original binary.

The important distinction is that the open-source firmware is a **replacement implementation**, not the manufacturer's original source. Our own report explicitly cautions against treating it as instruction-for-instruction equivalent. fileciteturn19file0L22-L45 So we should not simply rename the open-source Gen2.1.20 code “stock firmware.”

But because the hardware match is this strong, we can use it to turn the stock binaries from largely anonymous ARM code into something much more like:

```text
factory/
    main.c
    gpio.c
    motor.c
    hall.c
    adc.c
    imu.c
    balance.c
    uart_master_slave.c
    protocol.c
    leds.c
    power.c
```

with reconstructed C functions inside each.

That is probably the best route if the long-term goal is not merely to run Gen2.1.20, but to understand and preserve the **original Gyroor control behavior** while adding RC and later autonomous control.
