Yes — that marking is a much stronger clue than I initially gave it credit for.

Your board is:

**`OL20180703-V4.1`**

The Gen2.1.20 master photo has:

**`S1_G1_A_24V-V4.1_1905`**

and the repository explicitly identifies those photos as the Gen2.1.20 master/slave hardware from a ClassyWalk hoverboard. citeturn936188view0

The important part is the exact shared **`V4.1`** on boards whose circuit topology and GPIO mapping already match extremely closely. That makes it very likely these are variants of the **same underlying controller design**, rather than two unrelated boards that happen to use a GD32F130.

I would provisionally decode the Gen2.1.20 marking like this:

- **`S1_G1`** — platform/project/customer designation
- **`A`** — very likely board A/master; the slave photo should tell us whether the counterpart says `B`
- **`24V`** — nominal voltage variant
- **`V4.1`** — PCB/circuit revision
- **`1905`** — probably a manufacturing/design date code, plausibly 2019-05, but not proven

That last `1905` is particularly interesting because it is separate from the board's actual model string. It suggests the manufacturer may append a short production/date code to some versions.

So the comparison may be something along these lines:

```text
Your Gyroor:
OL20180703 - V4.1

ClassyWalk / Gen2.1.20:
S1_G1_A_24V - V4.1 - 1905
```

That suggests **`OL20180703` and `S1_G1_A_24V` may occupy roughly the same "board model/project" position in two different production variants**, while **V4.1 identifies the common electrical revision**.

That also changes how I look at `0703` on yours. It may not need to be part of a date. For example:

```text
OL | 2018 | 0703 | V4.1
manufacturer | year/family | project/model | circuit revision
```

is now at least as plausible as `2018-07-03`.

One additional thing worth checking immediately is the **slave Gen2.1.20 PCB marking**. If it says something like:

```text
S1_G1_B_24V-V4.1_1905
```

then we have effectively decoded `A/B`, and `S1_G1_24V` becomes a very useful platform identifier to search for other OEM boards.

I searched the exact `S1_G1_A_24V-V4.1_1905` string and variants; it does not appear to be indexed as text outside the photograph. That is unsurprising for PCB silkscreen. But now we can search images for **`S1_G1`, `G1 24V`, `V4.1`, OLSZ/OL**, and similar controller boards rather than requiring the exact full string.

Also, the current Gen2 repository independently states that **Gen2.2.20 is identical to Gen2.1.20 except for using an STM32F103 instead of the GD32F130**, which reinforces the idea that “20” denotes a specific board/layout family rather than one hoverboard brand. citeturn621357search0

I think our next step should be to compare the **master and slave silkscreens from the Gen2.1.20 photos character-for-character**. That may tell us exactly which portions mean board role, platform, voltage, and production run. memcite

- We do **not** yet know what `S1`, `G1`, `A`, `1905`, `0703`, or even the complete `OL20180703` structure means.
- `A = master`, `1905 = May 2019`, and `20180703 = date` are all **hypotheses**, not established facts.
- `SZ = Shenzhen` is plausible, but also not established.
- We also have not yet proven that **OLSZ is the actual board manufacturer** rather than a design house, PCB brand, controller family, or another supplier identifier.

The useful observation you made is simpler: **another board in this same apparent hardware family uses a structured model marking that is different from yours**. That gives us additional strings to search, especially:

`S1_G1_A_24V-V4.1_1905`  
`S1_G1_A_24V`  
`S1_G1`  
`OL20180703-V4.1`  
`OLSZ`

What I should do next is search those identifiers **without assigning meanings to their fields**, then look for occurrences on other controller-board photographs or supplier/manufacturer pages. If multiple boards link one of those identifiers to a company, *then* we can infer something about the manufacturer.

ASSISTANT | 2026-08-31T00:56:32.023782+00:00
Node: bd37d209-e0e8-4163-9dbf-6ae8a55e32f1
Parent node: bb31e60a-4093-4afa-8a70-84e49f271ba9
========================================================================
Yes. In fact, the **stock A↔B UART is probably the best way to attack the stock firmware before replacing it**.

We already know from the factory binaries that the stock firmware uses **USART1 on PA2/PA3** for the inter-board link, and that the receive handler feeds a real packet dispatcher. fileciteturn26file0L24-L44 The dispatcher checks a **16-bit CRC/check value** and recognizes commands including `0x01–0x06`, `0x0F`, `0x10`, and `0x78`. fileciteturn26file0L46-L74 So this is not just a heartbeat line—we have a genuine command protocol to reverse-engineer.

### What I would do first

Leave both factory boards running normally with the original 4-wire harness.

Tap the UART with a logic analyzer:

```text
Factory master A                    Factory slave B

PA2 TX  --------------------------> PA3 RX
PA3 RX  <-------------------------- PA2 TX

             |
             +--- logic analyzer CH0
             +--- logic analyzer CH1

GND  ------------------------------ GND
             |
             +--- analyzer GND
```

Importantly, **don't connect an external TX yet**. Just listen.

The firmware analysis confirms PA2/PA3 are the stock inter-board UART on both boards. fileciteturn26file3L336-L351

A cheap Saleae-compatible 8-channel USB logic analyzer is actually preferable to a USB-UART adapter for this stage because it can record **both directions simultaneously** and determine the baud from the bit timing.

A USB-UART adapter will also work, but you'd ideally use **two RX channels/adapters**:

```text
Adapter #1 RX <- master TX
Adapter #2 RX <- slave TX
both grounds -> controller GND
```

No adapter 5 V or 3.3 V power connection is required.

### Then exercise the stock controller

Record traffic while separately doing things that should alter individual fields:

- stationary and level
- tilt one board forward/backward
- tilt left/right
- rotate one wheel slowly by hand
- rotate it the other direction
- rotate the opposite wheel
- press/release power
- trigger whatever footpad/photo sensors are accessible

This should make the packet structure fall out surprisingly quickly.

We already found that **command `0x78` contains a long structured payload** with several signed 16-bit values, bytes and packed flags. fileciteturn26file0L76-L111 For example, our static analysis recovered something approximately like:

```c
int16_t f0 = be16(payload[2], payload[3]);
int16_t f1 = be16(payload[4], payload[5]);
int16_t f2 = be16(payload[6], payload[7]);

uint8_t flags0 = payload[8];
uint8_t flags1 = payload[9];

uint16_t f3 = be16(payload[10], payload[11]);
```

So if one of those values changes directly with wheel rotation or board pitch, we'll know what it represents almost immediately.

## Then we can actually transmit to the stock firmware

Once we know the framing, baud and CRC, I would **not initially inject onto an active TX line**, because then our transmitter and the factory MCU could electrically fight each other.

Instead, disconnect only the UART signal we're replacing.

For example, to impersonate the master to the slave:

```text
MASTER                         SLAVE

A PA2 TX  ----X--------------> B PA3 RX
                              ^
                              |
USB-UART/Pico TX -------------+

A PA3 RX  <------------------- B PA2 TX
              |
              +---- analyzer RX
```

Keep the other harness wires connected because we already know the slave's awake/powered state depends somehow on that harness; we haven't established whether that's power, enable, communications, or some combination. fileciteturn26file0L11-L20

Then we can replay an **exact packet captured from the real master**.

That is the key first test. We don't even have to understand the packet yet:

```text
1. Capture valid master -> slave packet.
2. Disconnect master TX.
3. Replay byte-for-byte from our UART.
4. Verify slave accepts it.
5. Alter one field.
6. Recalculate CRC.
7. Observe what changes.
```

That gives us a controlled black-box experiment.

### And we have an advantage

We aren't starting blind. From the binary we already know approximately where the machinery is:

```text
USART1 IRQ:
    ~0x08005C84

receive buffer:
    ~0x20000478

packet dispatcher:
    ~0x08006E64

CRC/check routine:
    immediately before dispatcher
```

The IRQ and dispatcher locations are already identified in the factory firmware. fileciteturn26file2L265-L315

So after obtaining even **one UART capture**, we can work backward through the binary to determine:

- start/sync byte(s)
- packet length field
- sender/receiver ID if any
- command byte location
- CRC algorithm
- byte order
- packet frequency
- master→slave commands
- slave→master responses

And because we have **both the master and slave factory binaries**, we can trace both the encoder and decoder rather than having only one side.

### Hardware I would use

If you already have any of these, they're suitable:

**Best for discovery:** cheap 8-channel USB logic analyzer.

**Fine for replay:** CP2102, FT232, CH340, Pico/RP2040, ESP32, Arduino with a 3.3-V UART, etc.

The GD32 side is **3.3-V logic**, so don't feed it a 5-V UART TX signal.

I would start with the logic analyzer rather than flashing anything. **If the stock protocol exposes motor commands, we may be able to retain the factory motor-control firmware entirely and simply replace/emulate its command source.** That would be a considerably cleaner route for the mower than immediately replacing the commutation/controller firmware. memcite

ASSISTANT | 2026-08-31T00:59:03.659031+00:00
Node: cf257aca-ef45-4a0c-ac9c-c422a5bfab48
Parent node: 52782ba8-2c44-4a9b-b6b6-e02d1c93b34d
========================================================================
Yes. It would help **a lot**, especially because we already have the binaries and have located the UART machinery.

Static disassembly tells us *what instructions execute*. Live UART observation tells us *what those instructions mean*. Combining the two is much more powerful than either alone.

For example, we've already located the stock USART1 receive path on PA2/PA3, the IRQ around `0x08005C84`, the receive buffer near `0x20000478`, and the dispatcher around `0x08006E64`. fileciteturn26file0L24-L58 We also know the dispatcher recognizes commands including `0x01–0x06`, `0x0F`, `0x10`, and `0x78`. fileciteturn26file0L58-L74

Suppose we capture a packet like:

```text
AA ?? ?? 78 00 14 FF E2 03 00 ...
```

and while rotating the wheel we observe one signed 16-bit field change:

```text
stationary:  00 00
forward:     00 31
faster:      00 A7
reverse:     FF B8
```

Then in the disassembly, wherever the `0x78` handler writes that field into RAM, we can rename that destination from something like:

```c
word_20000342
```

to:

```c
wheel_speed_slave
```

and then follow **every reference to it**.

That can expose an entire control path:

```text
UART packet
   ↓
decode field
   ↓
wheel_speed_command
   ↓
limit/ramp function
   ↓
motor controller
   ↓
PWM/current target
```

That is how a fairly anonymous binary gradually becomes readable reconstructed C.

The same applies to the IMU. If tilting the board makes two bytes in packet `0x78` vary exactly with pitch, we can trace the receiving variable backward/forward and associate it with the MPU processing routines we've already identified. The factory firmware definitely has an active MPU60x0 data path on PB6/PB7. fileciteturn26file1L143-L151

### It helps in several specific ways

**1. Identifies variables.**  
Instead of `ram_20000422`, we can get names like:

```c
pitch_angle
wheel_speed
requested_torque
fault_flags
hall_position
battery_voltage
```

**2. Identifies functions.**  
If a function runs whenever a certain packet arrives, we can rename:

```text
FUN_08004114
```

to something like:

```text
process_master_control_packet()
```

**3. Reveals structures.**  
If command `0x78` contains repeated 16-bit and flag fields, we can reconstruct an actual C struct:

```c
typedef struct {
    int16_t speed;
    int16_t pitch;
    int16_t torque;
    uint8_t flags1;
    uint8_t flags2;
    ...
} MasterControlPacket;
```

We already know its handler decodes multiple signed 16-bit fields and packed flags. fileciteturn26file0L76-L102

**4. Reveals state machines.**  
Capturing startup, power-button, fault, idle and motion traffic lets us correlate state transitions with specific branches in the binary.

**5. Validates the decompiler.**  
If we reconstruct something as:

```c
if (state == 3)
    motor_enable = 1;
```

we can deliberately put the board in the suspected state and see whether the behavior agrees.

**6. Separates master-only and slave-only behavior.**  
Since the two binaries are closely related role-specific builds, tracing actual traffic lets us determine exactly what each side generates versus consumes. fileciteturn26file0L115-L128

### SWD makes this even stronger

Because you've already managed to access the GD32 through SWD, we're not limited to UART sniffing.

Once we identify an interesting packet field, we can potentially use the debugger to:

```text
break on UART packet handler
inspect receive buffer
single-step packet decoder
watch destination RAM variable
set watchpoint on that variable
see which control functions read it
```

That is essentially **dynamic reverse engineering**.

For example:

```text
UART capture says bytes 6–7 = wheel request
             ↓
break at 0x08006E64
             ↓
packet received
             ↓
single-step until bytes 6–7 are loaded
             ↓
see destination = 0x200003A8
             ↓
hardware watchpoint on 0x200003A8
             ↓
resume
             ↓
CPU stops inside motor-control function
```

Now we've connected a physical behavior all the way to the motor-control code.

### How close can we get to “source”?

We cannot recover the manufacturer's literal source code:

```text
original variable names
comments
source filenames
macro names
typedef names
formatting
```

Those aren't present in the compiled image.

But with:

- both factory binaries,
- the near-matching Gen2.1.20 hardware definitions,
- UART traffic,
- controlled packet injection,
- SWD breakpoints/watchpoints,
- live IMU/Hall/motor experiments,

we can realistically produce a **functional source reconstruction** that looks something like:

```c
void process_control_packet(const uint8_t *packet)
{
    requested_speed  = read_be16(&packet[4]);
    requested_torque = read_be16(&packet[6]);

    control_flags.raw = packet[8];

    if (control_flags.enable)
        motor_enable();
}
```

rather than a pile of anonymous assembly.

So yes: **capturing and manipulating the stock UART is directly useful to reconstituting the firmware source**, not merely useful for making the motors move. It gives us the behavioral labels that static disassembly currently lacks.

Yes. From here on I’ll use **decimal wherever it improves readability**, and keep hexadecimal mainly where it is technically meaningful, such as:

- memory addresses: `0x08006E64`
- register addresses: `0x6B`
- bitmasks: `0x20`
- raw packet bytes / command IDs: `0x78`
- CRC values when comparing captures

But decoded values will be shown primarily in decimal. For example:

```text
0x78 command = command 120
0x0014 = 20
0xFFE2 as signed int16 = -30
0x00A7 = 167
```

For protocol work, I’ll generally show both when useful:

```text
command 120 (0x78)
speed = -30 (raw 0xFFE2)
```

That should make the reconstructed code and UART analysis much easier to follow.

Exactly — I understand now. For anything where the raw register/configuration value can be translated into a meaningful engineering value, I’ll give the **actual value**, not just the encoded number.

So for example:

- UART baud divisor → **115,200 baud** (once we resolve it from the factory init)
- IRQ/vector → **USART1 IRQ number _n_**, while separately giving handler address `0x08005C84`
- timer prescaler/ARR → **PWM frequency in Hz/kHz**
- ADC timing → **sample rate in samples/s**
- clock setup → **system/peripheral clock in MHz**
- packet interval → **milliseconds / packets per second**
- timer counts → **microseconds or milliseconds** where applicable
- ADC readings → **volts/amps** once the scaling is known
- motor speed values → **RPM or rad/s** once correlated
- delays → **milliseconds**, rather than just loop/timer counts

Hexadecimal should remain mainly for things where the hexadecimal representation is inherently useful: **memory addresses, peripheral addresses, register addresses, bit masks, raw packet bytes, CRCs, and opcodes/command IDs**.

So instead of:

> USART IRQ handler at `0x08005C84`

I should ideally report:

> **USART1 receive interrupt, IRQ n**, handler located at `0x08005C84`.

And instead of reporting the UART baud-register contents, I should calculate and report:

> **Inter-board UART: 115,200 baud, 8-N-1**

assuming that is what the stock firmware actually configures.

For this reverse-engineering work, I'll specifically try to **resolve those quantities from the binary rather than leaving them as register-level values** whenever the information is sufficient.

so what do i need for a uart sniffer, and can the st-link be used to send what commands we have/can figure out

========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-08-31T01:05:59.658229+00:00
Node: 2c9483c8-9a7f-4353-b92a-388e64543651
Parent node: 022b525b-a78b-4939-ae74-b5407bbffa8d
========================================================================
For this board, the simplest useful setup is a **cheap 8-channel USB logic analyzer**—the common Saleae-compatible 24 MHz units are sufficient. You only need three connections for passive sniffing:

```text
Logic analyzer GND  -> controller GND

CH0 -> A/master PA2 TX
CH1 -> B/slave  PA2 TX
```

Each board uses **PA2 as TX and PA3 as RX** for the stock inter-board USART, so watching the two PA2 lines gives you both directions simultaneously. Leave the original A↔B harness connected and operating normally. Do **not** connect the analyzer's VCC pin. The signals are 3.3 V logic. The factory firmware confirms PA2/PA3 as the inter-board USART. fileciteturn26file3L336-L351

A logic analyzer is preferable to a USB-UART adapter initially because we don't yet know the stock baud rate. PulseView/Sigrok or Saleae software can measure the bit period and decode likely UART settings. Once we resolve the initialization code, we should be able to state something concrete such as **115,200 baud, 8-N-1**, rather than merely a register value. The existing analysis specifically left baud-rate determination as something to resolve statically or from a capture. fileciteturn27file0L8-L12

### Can the ST-Link send the commands?

**Not directly as a UART transmitter through its SWD pins.** SWDIO/SWCLK are a debugging interface, not the PA2/PA3 UART.

Some genuine ST-Link versions contain a separate **Virtual COM Port UART**, but the common ST-Link V2 clones generally do not. If yours only exposes:

```text
SWDIO
SWCLK
GND
3.3V
5V
```

then it does not provide a normal UART port.

However, the ST-Link is still extremely valuable because it gives us a second way to manipulate the running stock firmware.

We can use SWD to:

- put breakpoints in the UART receiver;
- inspect the receive buffer;
- inspect the decoded packet variables;
- set hardware watchpoints on decoded control values;
- alter those RAM variables manually;
- determine what variables actually control torque/speed/state;
- potentially call/reuse the factory transmit routines while debugging.

The factory receive buffer is around `0x20000478`, and the dispatcher is around `0x08006E64`. fileciteturn26file0L46-L58

That means we have two complementary approaches.

**UART injection:**

```text
PC / Pico / USB-UART
       |
       TX
       |
       v
slave PA3 RX
```

We send an actual factory-formatted packet with the correct command ID, length and CRC.

**SWD injection:**

```text
ST-Link
   |
  SWD
   |
   v
GD32 RAM / CPU state
```

We bypass some or all of the UART protocol and manipulate the variables *after* the packet decoder has written them.

The second method is particularly useful while reverse-engineering. Suppose command 120 (`0x78`) writes a signed field into RAM and we think it is requested motor torque. We can stop the CPU, change:

```text
0 -> 20
20 -> 50
50 -> -20
```

and see what downstream code reads it. That can identify the control variable even before we've completely decoded the UART packet.

### What I would buy/use

You really only need two inexpensive pieces:

1. **8-channel 24 MHz USB logic analyzer** for sniffing.
2. **3.3-V USB-TTL UART adapter** for later packet injection.

A CH340, CP2102 or FT232-based adapter is fine provided its **TX logic level is 3.3 V**. Don't rely merely on the adapter's power-output selector—the actual TX voltage is what matters.

If you have an **RP2040/Pico** already, it can replace the USB-UART adapter and is actually better for experimentation because it can simultaneously:

```text
UART RX master
UART RX slave
UART TX generated packets
timestamp traffic
modify/replay packets
```

while the ST-Link remains attached to SWD.

So the ideal bench arrangement is:

```text
                    +---------------- Logic analyzer CH0
                    |
MASTER PA2 TX ------+----------------> SLAVE PA3 RX

MASTER PA3 RX <------+---------------- SLAVE PA2 TX
                    |
                    +---------------- Logic analyzer CH1


ST-Link:
 SWDIO -> SWDIO
 SWCLK -> SWCLK
 GND   -> GND


later:

Pico/USB-UART TX ---> selected PA3 RX
```

I would **sniff first, inject second, use ST-Link simultaneously to inspect what each packet changes**. That combination gives us the most information for both controlling the stock firmware and reconstructing its source. memcite

ASSISTANT | 2026-08-31T01:06:45.712988+00:00

Parent node: 7f3a90a3-1d03-4fb0-9584-a5487fe50496
========================================================================
Yes. An STM32 can absolutely do this, and for what we need it can replace both the logic analyzer and later the USB-UART injector.

A basic STM32 such as an **STM32F103 “Blue Pill”** is already fast enough. The ideal arrangement is:

```text
Hoverboard MASTER PA2 TX ---> STM32 UART RX #1
Hoverboard SLAVE  PA2 TX ---> STM32 UART RX #2
Hoverboard GND -------------> STM32 GND

STM32 USB / serial ----------> PC
```

The STM32 would passively receive both directions and send something like this to the PC:

```text
12.381 ms  M>S : A5 10 78 ...
12.927 ms  S>M : A5 08 03 ...
```

That gives us both the bytes and the timing between packets.

The only complication is that we **don't yet know the baud rate**. We can handle that in firmware. Before enabling the UARTs, the STM32 can measure the pulse widths on PA2 using a timer/input-capture peripheral. From the shortest recurring pulse we can determine the approximate UART bit period:

```text
8.68 µs  -> ~115200 baud
17.36 µs -> ~57600 baud
104.2 µs -> ~9600 baud
```

Those are just examples; we'll measure the actual board.

Once the baud is established, switch the STM32 pins to hardware UART RX and capture the complete byte streams.

Then the same STM32 can become our **packet injector**:

```text
              STOCK MASTER
                   |
                   X    disconnect TX when injecting
                   |
STM32 TX ----------+----------> SLAVE PA3 RX

SLAVE PA2 TX -----------------> STM32 RX
```

That lets us:

- capture stock packets;
- replay them exactly;
- change selected fields;
- calculate the CRC;
- send modified packets;
- observe the response;
- eventually emulate the master completely.

For source reconstruction, I would make the STM32 firmware support **three modes**:

```text
1. SNIFF
   Listen to both directions without transmitting.

2. REPLAY
   Capture and retransmit a recorded packet.

3. INJECT
   Build/modify packets and transmit them.
```

A fourth useful mode later would be **MITM**:

```text
MASTER <---> STM32 <---> SLAVE
```

where the STM32 receives every packet, logs it, optionally alters it, and forwards it. That would be extremely useful for figuring out which fields correspond to speed, torque, balance state, etc.

One important detail: the hoverboard GD32 UART is **3.3-V logic**, so an STM32 is electrically a very good fit. Use a common ground and do not connect either board's 3.3-V rail unless we specifically need to power the STM32 that way.

If you tell me **which STM32 board/chip you have**—or send a photo—I can map the exact pins and write the sniffer firmware for it.

the man in the middle would be useful for later, can you search and see if anyone has made something like this?

ASSISTANT | 2026-08-31T16:48:00.497346+00:00
Node: b69b3a98-4a41-47cb-acce-bf470b0191a9
Parent node: 9340038b-dd33-48f7-92c6-0823ca3576f6
========================================================================
Yes. I found a few projects that are very close to what we want, although I did **not** find one specifically for the Gen2.1.20/OLSZ stock master-slave protocol.

The closest conceptual match is Google’s **Serberus**, which is explicitly described as a **serial man-in-the-middle hardware hacking tool**. It has multiple serial channels plus JTAG/SWD support, so the intended workflow is essentially the same: sit between two embedded devices, observe traffic, and manipulate it. citeturn961696search0

Even more relevant to our use case is a recent **Navee ST3 Pro scooter reverse-engineering project**. That repository includes **Arduino UART MITM sketches**, UART sniffers, protocol documentation, and SWD/Ghidra work. In other words, they used almost exactly the process we're discussing: intercept the stock internal UART, decode the protocol, inject/alter messages, and correlate that with firmware analysis. citeturn914656view0

For hoverboards specifically, I found several master/slave UART projects. One fork for HOVER-1 boards runs separate master and slave controllers over UART2, with another UART on the master used for external Arduino/ESP32 control; the author's project is actually a mower/vehicle platform. citeturn321726search2turn321726search4 That isn't a stock-firmware MITM, but it confirms the architecture we're considering is practical on hoverboard hardware.

For the STM32 itself, there are also established examples of using STM32 hardware as high-throughput UART bridges. One STM32F072 project implements **DMA-accelerated multi-UART USB CDC**, specifically to avoid data loss at higher throughput. citeturn797119search0 ST also supplies examples of UART communication using DMA between devices, so we wouldn't need to invent the low-level buffering design from scratch. citeturn797119search1

### What I think we should build

Rather than just a sniffer, I would design the STM32 firmware from the beginning as a **four-wire transparent MITM**:

```text
       OLSZ MASTER                      OLSZ SLAVE
       stock GD32                        stock GD32

        PA2 TX                              PA3 RX
           |                                  ^
           v                                  |
      STM32 RX_A ---- buffer/filter ---- STM32 TX_B


        PA3 RX                              PA2 TX
           ^                                  |
           |                                  v
      STM32 TX_A <--- buffer/filter ----- STM32 RX_B
```

So the original direct UART wires are actually separated. The STM32 has:

```text
UART A RX <- master TX
UART A TX -> master RX

UART B RX <- slave TX
UART B TX -> slave RX
```

Initially the code does nothing except:

```c
Master -> STM32 -> Slave
Slave  -> STM32 -> Master
```

byte-for-byte.

At the same time it logs both directions.

Then we add modes like:

```text
PASS
  Forward everything unchanged.

LOG
  Forward + timestamp everything.

DROP
  Suppress selected packet/command.

MODIFY
  Change selected fields and recompute CRC.

INJECT
  Generate a new packet.

REPLAY
  Replay a previously captured packet.

EMULATE_MASTER
  Stop forwarding master commands and generate our own.

EMULATE_SLAVE
  Same idea in the other direction.
```

That is substantially more useful than a one-purpose sniffer.

### The Navee project is particularly worth borrowing from

Their repository structure is almost a template for what we want:

```text
firmware dumps
Ghidra analysis
UART sniffers
UART MITM
protocol documentation
SWD access
packet injection
```

They explicitly include Arduino sketches for a UART MITM between dashboard and controller. citeturn914656view0

Our advantage is that **we've already gotten further into the OLSZ binary than they had to start with**: we already know where the master/slave UART, receiver, packet dispatcher and command handlers are in the stock firmware.

### One consideration for choosing the STM32

For a real MITM, I would prefer a device with **at least three hardware UARTs**:

```text
UART 1 = master side
UART 2 = slave side
UART 3 = debug/logging to PC
```

or a chip with native USB, in which case:

```text
UART 1 = master
UART 2 = slave
USB CDC = PC/logging/control
```

That is cleaner.

A Blue Pill STM32F103C8 can do it; it has enough USARTs. Something newer with native USB and more RAM is even nicer, but there's no need to buy anything if you already have a suitable STM32 board.

Once you tell me **what STM32 boards/chips you actually have**, I can check their UART/USB capabilities and pick the best one for a permanent **sniffer → MITM → command injector** platform.

Also, I found no public Gen2.1.20 stock-UART MITM implementation in the searches I ran, so if we do this successfully we'll likely be documenting a protocol that hasn't already been published. memcite

ASSISTANT | 2026-08-31T17:07:24.134243+00:00
Node: 3cd12788-fa92-4699-ab8f-7fd1e76b0005
Parent node: ee9954a7-a524-4c88-b5d1-ae78d65d306e
========================================================================
Yes. The **Blue Pill is probably the best fit** from what you have.

It gives us everything needed for a proper two-way MITM while staying at **3.3-V logic**, which matches the GD32 hoverboard controllers.

A clean assignment would be:

```text
Blue Pill STM32F103C8

USART1
 PA10 RX <- hoverboard MASTER PA2 TX
 PA9  TX -> hoverboard MASTER PA3 RX

USART2
 PA3  RX <- hoverboard SLAVE PA2 TX
 PA2  TX -> hoverboard SLAVE PA3 RX

USB
 PA11/PA12 -> PC for logging/control

SWD
 PA13 SWDIO
 PA14 SWCLK
```

So physically:

```text
        STOCK MASTER                     STOCK SLAVE
        GD32 board                       GD32 board

PA2 TX ------> PA10 RX   Blue Pill   PA2 TX ------> PA3 RX
PA3 RX <------ PA9 TX                PA3 RX <------ PA2 TX

                    STM32F103
```

The original master/slave UART signal wires would therefore be **broken and routed through the Blue Pill**. The other harness wires stay connected normally.

Initially, though, I would **not break the UART connection**. We should first run the Blue Pill purely as a passive sniffer.

### Stage 1 — passive sniffer

We only use RX inputs:

```text
Master PA2 TX ----+----> Slave PA3 RX
                  |
                  +----> Blue Pill PA10

Slave PA2 TX -----+----> Master PA3 RX
                  |
                  +----> Blue Pill PA3
```

Blue Pill ground connects to hoverboard ground.

That allows us to determine:

- actual baud rate
- framing, probably 8-N-1 but we'll verify
- packet timing
- packet lengths
- which direction sends each command
- startup sequence
- command 120 (`0x78`) traffic
- responses to tilt, wheel movement, power button, etc.

The factory firmware already confirms the controllers communicate over PA2/PA3 and have a fairly substantial packet protocol rather than a simple command word. fileciteturn26file0L24-L74

### Stage 2 — transparent MITM

Once we can decode traffic successfully, cut/disconnect the two UART signal paths and put the Blue Pill inline:

```text
Master TX -> Blue Pill RX1
Blue Pill TX2 -> Slave RX

Slave TX -> Blue Pill RX2
Blue Pill TX1 -> Master RX
```

Firmware initially does exactly:

```c
master_byte_received:
    send_to_slave(byte);

slave_byte_received:
    send_to_master(byte);
```

No modification whatsoever.

At the same time, it copies everything to the PC with timestamps.

### Stage 3 — packet-aware MITM

Once packet boundaries and CRC are understood, instead of forwarding individual bytes immediately, buffer whole packets:

```text
MASTER
  |
  v
[receive complete packet]
  |
[identify command]
  |
[log it]
  |
[optional modification]
  |
[recalculate CRC]
  |
  v
SLAVE
```

Then we'll be able to do things like:

```text
PASS command 1
PASS command 2
PASS command 120
DROP command 15
MODIFY command 120 field 3
INJECT command 120
```

And eventually:

```text
master says speed = 0
             ↓
Blue Pill changes it to speed = 50
             ↓
slave receives valid stock packet
```

That will be extremely useful for finding what each field actually does.

### Stage 4 — use SWD simultaneously

One particularly nice feature of using the Blue Pill is that its SWD pins are separate from all of this.

We can have:

```text
PC
 |
 +-- ST-Link --> hoverboard GD32 SWD
 |
 +-- USB ------> Blue Pill MITM
```

So the Blue Pill captures/modifies UART while the ST-Link lets us stop the **stock GD32 firmware** and inspect what the packet did to RAM.

For reverse engineering, that's about as good a setup as we could ask for.

For example:

```text
Blue Pill:
command 120
field at packet offset 6 changed:
0 -> 100

             ↓

GD32 debugger:
break at packet handler 0x08006E64

             ↓

watch which RAM address gets 100

             ↓

hardware watchpoint on that RAM address

             ↓

continue

             ↓

CPU stops when motor-control code reads it
```

That directly connects:

**UART field → RAM variable → control routine → motor behavior**

which will greatly improve our reconstructed C.

### Why Blue Pill instead of your other boards

Your **Mega** has plenty of UARTs, but it runs 5-V logic. We'd have to level-shift anything it transmits to the GD32.

The **Uno** only has one hardware UART and is also 5 V, so it's a poor fit.

The **Black Pill** would work extremely well and has substantially more processing capacity, but we don't need it yet.

The **RP2040** would arguably make the ultimate protocol analyzer/MITM because its PIO blocks are excellent for arbitrary serial capture and timing, but again, that's more capability than necessary.

The Blue Pill gives us:

```text
72 MHz Cortex-M3
3.3-V GPIO
3 hardware USARTs
multiple timers
DMA
native USB device
SWD debugging
```

which is plenty for this protocol.

I would actually reserve the **RP2040 and Black Pill** for later rather than consume them on the first version.

### One modification I'd make to the plan

I'd use **USART1 + USART2 for the hoverboard links**, but keep **USART3 free**.

That gives us a fallback debug interface if USB CDC gives us trouble:

```text
USART1 = master-facing UART
USART2 = slave-facing UART
USART3 = optional PC/debug UART
USB     = primary PC interface
```

The common Blue Pill USB implementation can occasionally be annoying depending on the particular clone, whereas USART3 through a USB-TTL adapter is nearly foolproof.

And because we need to determine the unknown UART baud first, I would have the initial firmware use one of the STM32 timers to measure edge timing before enabling the USART decoders. That way the Blue Pill itself becomes our **baud-rate measurement instrument**, so you don't need to buy the logic analyzer.

The first firmware we should build, therefore, is a **non-invasive dual-channel sniffer/baud detector**, not the MITM yet. Once it successfully dumps the actual stock traffic, evolving it into the MITM is straightforward. memcite

Done. I built the first **Blue Pill passive dual-UART sniffer + automatic baud detector**.

[Download the complete ZIP](sandbox:/mnt/data/BluePill_Hoverboard_UART_Sniffer.zip)

Or individually:

[BluePill_Hoverboard_UART_Sniffer.ino](sandbox:/mnt/data/BluePill_Hoverboard_UART_Sniffer/BluePill_Hoverboard_UART_Sniffer.ino)  
[README / wiring instructions](sandbox:/mnt/data/BluePill_Hoverboard_UART_Sniffer/README_WIRING.txt)

For this first test, keep the original master/slave cable intact and add only:

```text
Hoverboard MASTER PA2 TX  ---> Blue Pill PA10
Hoverboard SLAVE  PA2 TX  ---> Blue Pill PA3
Hoverboard GND             ---> Blue Pill GND
```

**Do not connect Blue Pill PA9 or PA2 yet.** Those will become our MITM transmit lines later.

The firmware first timestamps edges on both channels using the F103's 72 MHz cycle counter and compares them against standard baud rates. It reports something like:

```text
Master TX candidate: 115200 baud, edge fit 97%, ...
Slave TX candidate : 115200 baud, edge fit 96%, ...
Combined candidate : 115200 baud, ...
```

That `115200` is only an example—the firmware will actually measure yours.

It then automatically starts both hardware UARTs at the detected rate and prints traffic like:

```text
1253812 us  M>S  [12]  AA 55 78 ...
1254364 us  S>M  [8]   AA 55 03 ...
```

The current STM32duino core supports the Blue Pill's native USB CDC, and its hardware serial mapping gives us USART1 on PA10/PA9 and USART2 on PA3/PA2, which is exactly what we need. citeturn198941search0turn198941search4turn198941search6

For Arduino IDE, use roughly:

```text
Board group:       Generic STM32F1 series
Board part number: BluePill F103C8
USB support:       CDC (generic Serial supersede U(S)ART)
Upload method:     STM32CubeProgrammer (SWD)
```

ST-Link programming:

```text
ST-Link GND   -> Blue Pill GND
ST-Link SWDIO -> PA13
ST-Link SWCLK -> PA14
```

If the Blue Pill is powered from USB, leave the ST-Link's **3.3 V disconnected**. ST's current upload documentation specifically cautions against tying the programmer's 3.3-V supply to an independently powered target. citeturn159451search1

Once it is running, give me the **complete terminal output**, especially the three baud-candidate lines and 10–30 seconds of captured `M>S` / `S>M` traffic. From that we can start identifying packet framing and CRC before converting this same Blue Pill into the transparent MITM.

shouldn't the stlink be connected to the swd connection?

========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-09-01T02:32:56.073757+00:00
Node: d7e0a37a-c16a-4284-9bca-c177ac0fb3d0
Parent node: 3a2c4eb3-dd24-4624-9175-6bb3a813658d
========================================================================
Yes. The ST-Link should be connected to the **Blue Pill’s SWD pins** for programming/debugging.

On a Blue Pill that is:

```text
ST-Link SWDIO  -> Blue Pill PA13 / SWDIO
ST-Link SWCLK  -> Blue Pill PA14 / SWCLK
ST-Link GND    -> Blue Pill GND
```

Optionally:

```text
ST-Link NRST   -> Blue Pill NRST
```

That reset line is not always required, but it can make flashing/debug attachment more reliable.

The important distinction is that the **hoverboard UART sniffing wires are separate**:

```text
Hoverboard master TX -> Blue Pill PA10
Hoverboard slave TX  -> Blue Pill PA3
```

So the Blue Pill uses:

```text
PA13 / PA14 = SWD programming/debugging
PA10 / PA3  = UART sniffing inputs
```

And yes, if the Blue Pill is already powered from USB, I would leave the ST-Link’s **3.3 V output disconnected** and just share ground.

========================================================================
there is a swd 4 pin header on the end of the blue pill oposite the usb connection

========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-09-01T02:33:59.681237+00:00
Node: 670a4abe-19e1-4f7c-820a-720a573c359f
Parent node: 6d4782f5-8b37-4a10-a362-9d2e6b589926
========================================================================
Yes. On the common Blue Pill, that **4-pin header at the end opposite the USB connector is the dedicated SWD header**, and that is the cleaner place to connect the ST-Link.

It is typically labeled something like:

```text
3V3
SWIO
SWCLK
GND
```

So the ST-Link connection should be:

```text
ST-Link SWDIO -> SWIO
ST-Link SWCLK -> SWCLK
ST-Link GND   -> GND
```

The **3V3 pin on that header is mainly a target-voltage/reference/power connection**, depending on the ST-Link. Since we can power the Blue Pill from USB, I would leave the ST-Link 3.3 V unconnected unless we specifically need the programmer to power the board.

So yes: rather than using loose PA13/PA14 pins elsewhere on the board, use that **4-pin SWD header**. PA13 and PA14 are simply what those header pins connect to internally.

how do i program the stm32 via stlink with an ino? dont i need a bin?

========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-09-01T02:35:44.046375+00:00
Node: 4af07d6a-135f-48a9-b6f4-87bf84ae1bc9
Parent node: a76335eb-2289-48d6-964b-650151292e61
========================================================================
Yes—the `.ino` itself is not what gets written to the STM32. It gets **compiled first**, producing an ELF/HEX/BIN, and then that compiled image is flashed through the ST-Link.

You have two practical ways to do it.

### Easiest: let Arduino IDE compile and flash through ST-Link

With the STM32duino core installed:

```text
Tools → Board
  Generic STM32F1 series

Tools → Board part number
  BluePill F103C8

Tools → Upload method
  STM32CubeProgrammer (SWD)
```

Then connect the ST-Link to the Blue Pill's 4-pin SWD header:

```text
ST-Link        Blue Pill SWD header
-----------------------------------
SWDIO    --->  SWIO
SWCLK    --->  SWCLK
GND      --->  GND
```

Power the Blue Pill from USB for now, so leave the ST-Link 3.3 V disconnected.

Then click the normal **Upload** button in Arduino IDE.

Arduino does approximately this behind the scenes:

```text
.ino
 ↓
C++ compiler
 ↓
ELF
 ↓
BIN/HEX
 ↓
STM32CubeProgrammer
 ↓
ST-Link
 ↓
STM32F103 flash
```

So you do **not** have to manually create a `.bin`.

### Or create the BIN yourself

If you want to use **STM32CubeProgrammer directly**, then yes, export a compiled binary.

In Arduino IDE:

```text
Sketch
→ Export Compiled Binary
```

It will generate a `.bin` in the sketch/build location.

Then in STM32CubeProgrammer:

```text
Connection: ST-LINK
Connect
```

Choose the generated `.bin` and program it at:

```text
0x08000000
```

That is the normal flash base address for the STM32F103.

For our Blue Pill sniffer, I would use the **first method** initially: compile + upload directly from Arduino IDE through SWD. It makes changes much quicker while we're developing the sniffer.

One other important setting for the firmware I gave you is the USB output. We want the Blue Pill's USB connector to appear as a serial port on your PC, so select the STM32duino USB option for **CDC serial** before compiling. After flashing, the ST-Link is only needed for programming/debugging; the captured UART data comes back through the Blue Pill's own USB connector.

========================================================================
do i not need to program a bootloader to the blue pill first?

========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-09-01T04:20:25.776516+00:00
Node: 381d5626-f17f-4103-995d-14dc65bcb5dd
Parent node: c99b8eaf-f5d0-47df-a8c1-f7d435b7e640
========================================================================
No. If you're programming the Blue Pill through **ST-Link over SWD**, you do **not** need to install a bootloader first.

The ST-Link writes directly to the STM32F103's internal flash, bypassing any bootloader entirely.

So the path is simply:

```text
Arduino IDE
  ↓ compile
STM32 binary
  ↓
ST-Link over SWD
  ↓
STM32F103 flash
```

A bootloader is only useful if you later want to upload firmware without the ST-Link, for example over the Blue Pill's USB connector or UART.

For this project, I'd actually **skip the bootloader entirely**. Using ST-Link is preferable because:

- it is reliable;
- it gives us SWD debugging;
- it doesn't consume flash for a bootloader;
- it avoids USB-bootloader compatibility issues on clone Blue Pills.

The STM32duino core can compile firmware that uses the Blue Pill's native USB peripheral for **CDC serial output** without having a USB bootloader installed. The application itself initializes USB after reset.

So you can have:

```text
ST-Link -> programs Blue Pill through SWD

Blue Pill USB connector -> serial/logging to PC
```

with no bootloader involved.

The only thing to watch is the Blue Pill's **BOOT0 jumper**. For normal execution after flashing, it should be:

```text
BOOT0 = 0
```

which boots from internal flash at power-up/reset.

Most likely the STM32 core simply isn’t installed in Arduino IDE yet, or the Boards Manager URL is missing.

For the current STM32duino core, use **Arduino IDE 2.x**. The STM32 core documentation says releases 2.8.0+ support Arduino IDE 2 only. citeturn126906search3turn126906search7

Do this:

1. Open **File → Preferences**.
2. In **Additional Boards Manager URLs**, add:

`https://github.com/stm32duino/BoardManagerFiles/raw/main/package_stmicroelectronics_index.json`

That is the current official STM32duino package URL. citeturn126906search3

3. Open **Tools → Board → Boards Manager**.
4. Search for:

**STM32 MCU based boards**

5. Install the package published by **STMicroelectronics**. The STM32duino setup documentation explicitly identifies that as the package to install. citeturn126906search1

After installation, you should get an STM32 family in the board selector. For your Blue Pill, look for something equivalent to:

```text
Generic STM32F1 series
```

and then under the board-specific options:

```text
Board part number:
BluePill F103C8
```

The STM32 core does support generic STM32F1 boards, including Blue Pill-style F103 boards. citeturn126906search5

For our sniffer, the eventual settings should be approximately:

```text
Board:          Generic STM32F1 series
Board part:     BluePill F103C8
USB support:    CDC (generic Serial supersede U(S)ART)
Upload method:  STM32CubeProgrammer (SWD)
```

One correction to something I said earlier: on newer STM32duino versions, the old **“STLink” upload method is deprecated**. The preferred SWD route is through **STM32CubeProgrammer**, using the ST-Link as the physical programmer. citeturn126906search6

If you already added an older URL like:

```text
.../STM32/package_stm_index.json
```

replace it with the current `package_stmicroelectronics_index.json`; that older index was deprecated starting with STM32 core 2.0.0. citeturn126906search1

If you tell me **which Arduino IDE version you currently have installed**, I can give you the exact menu path for that version.

Yes — those compile errors come from two issues in the sketch I gave you, both exposed by the current STM32 Arduino core 3.0.0.

The first is straightforward:

```cpp
static const PinName MASTER_RX_PIN = PA10;
static const PinName SLAVE_RX_PIN  = PA3;
```

On your Blue Pill variant, `PA10` and `PA3` are Arduino pin-number macros stored as integers, not `PinName` enum values. I changed those to:

```cpp
static const uint32_t MASTER_RX_PIN = PA10;
static const uint32_t SLAVE_RX_PIN  = PA3;
```

The second error:

```text
'CaptureFrame' was not declared in this scope
```

is an Arduino `.ino` preprocessing problem. Arduino automatically generates function prototypes, and it can place a generated prototype such as:

```cpp
void flushFrame(CaptureFrame &f, ...);
```

before it has seen the `CaptureFrame` structure definition.

I moved the custom structures into a separate header, `SnifferTypes.h`. Because headers are processed before Arduino's generated prototypes, that avoids the problem completely.

I made a corrected **v2** package:

[Download Blue Pill UART Sniffer v2](sandbox:/mnt/data/BluePill_Hoverboard_UART_Sniffer_v2.zip)

The folder contains:

```text
BluePill_Hoverboard_UART_Sniffer_v2.ino
SnifferTypes.h
README_WIRING.txt
```

Keep the `.ino` and `.h` in the **same Arduino sketch folder**.

The passive wiring remains unchanged:

```text
Hoverboard MASTER PA2 TX  -> Blue Pill PA10
Hoverboard SLAVE  PA2 TX  -> Blue Pill PA3
Hoverboard GND             -> Blue Pill GND
```

Do **not** connect Blue Pill PA9 or PA2 to the hoverboard yet.

Try **Verify/Compile** on v2 first. If it throws another error, paste the entire compiler output again; since we now know you're using **STMicroelectronics STM32 core 3.0.0 + BluePill F103C8**, I can target that exact toolchain instead of relying on older STM32duino behavior.

That linker error is clear: on the Blue Pill variant in STM32 core 3.0.0, **`Serial2` is not instantiated automatically**.

STM32duino’s documented approach is to explicitly create additional hardware serial ports with the RX and TX pins, e.g. `HardwareSerial Serial1(PA10, PA9);`. citeturn133413search0turn133413search4

I changed the sniffer to explicitly create our two UARTs:

```cpp
HardwareSerial MasterUART(PA10, PA9);  // RX, TX
HardwareSerial SlaveUART(PA3,  PA2);   // RX, TX
```

Then the code uses `MasterUART` and `SlaveUART` instead of assuming `Serial1`/`Serial2` exist.

[Download corrected v3](sandbox:/mnt/data/BluePill_Hoverboard_UART_Sniffer_v3.zip)

The wiring is still exactly:

```text
Hoverboard MASTER PA2 TX -> Blue Pill PA10
Hoverboard SLAVE  PA2 TX -> Blue Pill PA3
Hoverboard GND           -> Blue Pill GND
```

PA9 and PA2 on the Blue Pill remain **unconnected for now** because those are our future transmit lines for the MITM.

This particular fix is directly supported by STM32duino's HardwareSerial API; the constructor order is **RX, TX**. citeturn133413search5

Try compiling v3 before wiring anything further.

Yes. For this sniffer, there are a few settings I’d set deliberately rather than leaving them arbitrary.

Use these:

```text
Board:                  Generic STM32F1 series
Board part number:      BluePill F103C8

Upload method:          STM32CubeProgrammer (SWD)

USB support:            CDC (generic 'Serial' supersedes U(S)ART)
USB speed:              Low/Full Speed

U(S)ART support:        Disabled (no generic Serial support)

Optimize:               Debug (-Og)

Debug symbols/core logs: Symbols Enabled (-g)
                         or None if you don't intend to debug

C Runtime Library:      Newlib Nano (default)
```

For our code, **U(S)ART support can be disabled** because we're explicitly creating:

```cpp
HardwareSerial MasterUART(PA10, PA9);
HardwareSerial SlaveUART(PA3, PA2);
```

and using USB CDC as `Serial`. That avoids the core trying to reserve a hardware UART as the generic `Serial` object. STM32duino examples using Blue Pill + USB CDC commonly use this configuration. citeturn876983search1turn876983search9

For **Optimize**, I'd use:

```text
Debug (-Og)
```

during development. `-Og` retains reasonable optimization while making debugging and generated code much easier to follow. STM32duino specifically recommends the debug optimization when using ST-Link/GDB. citeturn111022search0

For **Debug symbols**, use:

```text
Symbols Enabled (-g)
```

if you want to step through this with the ST-Link later. It adds debug information to the ELF but does not meaningfully change the UART protocol itself. citeturn111022search0

For **C Runtime Library**:

```text
Newlib Nano
```

is exactly what we want. There's no reason to use full Newlib for this application. The STM32 core defaults to Nano unless you explicitly request the standard library. citeturn111022search2

### One setting I would avoid for now

Don't use:

```text
Smallest (-Os) with LTO
```

yet.

It will probably work, but link-time optimization can make debugging and disassembly considerably less straightforward. We're specifically building something we may want to debug through SWD, so **`-Og` + debug symbols** is a better development configuration.

Once everything works, we could switch to `-O2` or `-Os` if there were a reason, but this code is nowhere near the F103's resource limits.

So the exact configuration I'd use right now is:

```text
Generic STM32F1 series
BluePill F103C8

U(S)ART support:
    Disabled (no Serial support)

USB support:
    CDC (generic 'Serial' supersedes U(S)ART)

USB speed:
    Low/Full Speed

Optimize:
    Debug (-Og)

Debug:
    Symbols Enabled (-g)

C Runtime Library:
    Newlib Nano

Upload method:
    STM32CubeProgrammer (SWD)
```

And yes, **the `Serial2` linker error was still a code issue rather than one of these settings**. Explicitly creating the two `HardwareSerial` objects in v3 is the correct fix for that. citeturn876983search3

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-01T05:52:33.296919+00:00

Yes — **for our project we absolutely need the UART peripherals enabled**, because we are using USART1 and USART2 as the two sniffing channels.

The subtlety is the Arduino menu item **“U(S)ART support”**. That setting mainly controls whether the core creates a generic Arduino `Serial` object backed by a hardware UART. It does **not** mean the STM32's UART hardware disappears when disabled. Our sketch explicitly creates the UARTs with:

```cpp
HardwareSerial MasterUART(PA10, PA9);
HardwareSerial SlaveUART(PA3, PA2);
```

So those hardware UARTs are still used directly.

For this sketch, the cleaner setting is:

```text
U(S)ART support:
Enabled (no generic 'Serial')
```

if that exact option exists in your Tools menu.

Then:

```text
USB support:
CDC (generic 'Serial' supersedes U(S)ART)
```

so:

```text
Serial       = USB CDC to the PC
MasterUART   = USART1, PA10/PA9
SlaveUART    = USART2, PA3/PA2
```

That is exactly the division we want.

If your STM32 core 3.0.0 menu only gives you:

```text
Enabled (generic 'Serial')
Disabled (no Serial support)
```

then choose **Disabled** because USB CDC is supplying `Serial`, while our two `HardwareSerial` instances explicitly claim the UART peripherals. That configuration has been used with Blue Pill + USB CDC in STM32duino. citeturn694540search4turn694540search2

So the key point is:

**Yes, we need USART1 and USART2 hardware. We just don't need the core's generic hardware-UART `Serial` object.** Our code creates the two UART objects explicitly.

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-01T05:59:30.964175+00:00

========================================================================
That error is from a real **STM32 core 3.0.0 breaking change**, not from your board settings.

In STM32duino 3.0.0, the concrete STM32 serial class was renamed from `HardwareSerial` to **`Uart`**. The release notes call this out explicitly, and the 3.0.0 source shows `Uart` implementing the abstract Arduino `HardwareSerial` interface. citeturn307950search2 fileciteturn31file0

The 3.0.0 constructor we need is:

```cpp
Uart(pin_size_t rx, pin_size_t tx);
```

which the actual 3.0.0 source confirms. fileciteturn32file0

So our declarations should be:

```cpp
Uart MasterUART(PA10, PA9);  // USART1: RX PA10, TX PA9
Uart SlaveUART(PA3,  PA2);   // USART2: RX PA3,  TX PA2
```

I corrected the project specifically for your installed **STM32 core 3.0.0**:

[Download Blue Pill UART Sniffer v4](sandbox:/mnt/data/BluePill_Hoverboard_UART_Sniffer_v4.zip)

The important part is that you do **not** need to change the board settings to fix this particular error. The previous code was written against the pre-3.0 STM32duino API.

For v4 I'd keep:

```text
Board:             Generic STM32F1 series
Board part number: BluePill F103C8
USB support:       CDC, with USB CDC as Serial
Upload method:     STM32CubeProgrammer (SWD)
Optimize:          Debug (-Og)
C runtime:         Newlib Nano
```

And the passive sniffer wiring remains:

```text
Master controller PA2 TX -> Blue Pill PA10
Slave controller  PA2 TX -> Blue Pill PA3
Controller GND           -> Blue Pill GND
```

Leave **Blue Pill PA9 and PA2 disconnected** for now.

This v4 change is based on the actual **3.0.0 source/API**, so it should get us past the `HardwareSerial` constructor/abstract-class errors.

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-01T06:33:54.091744+00:00

Yes. I checked the actual **STM32duino 3.0.0 core**, the Blue Pill F103C8 variant, its UART pin map, USB configuration, serial-selection logic, debug settings, and the 3.0.0 release notes before changing anything further.

There are **three things we needed to correct/lock down**.

First, the `HardwareSerial → Uart` change is definitely the official 3.0.0 breaking change. fileciteturn33file0 The concrete `Uart` class has the RX/TX constructor we need. fileciteturn32file0

Second, I previously gave you the wrong advice when I said **U(S)ART support = Disabled**. Do **not** use that. In core 3.0.0:

```text
Enabled (no generic 'Serial')
```

sets:

```text
HAL_UART_MODULE_ENABLED
HWSERIAL_NONE
```

That is exactly what we want: UART hardware support remains compiled in, while the core does **not** create its own generic hardware `Serial` instance. fileciteturn37file0

This matters because the Blue Pill variant's default generic hardware serial is **USART1 on PA10/PA9**. fileciteturn42file0 We want USART1 exclusively for our sniffer.

Third, USB should be:

```text
CDC (generic 'Serial' supersede U(S)ART)
```

Core 3.0.0 explicitly maps that configuration's generic `Serial` to `SerialUSB`. fileciteturn37file5 fileciteturn39file0

So the clean architecture becomes:

```text
Serial       = native USB CDC -> PC
MasterUART   = USART1 -> PA10 RX / PA9 TX
SlaveUART    = USART2 -> PA3 RX / PA2 TX
```

I also verified the actual 3.0.0 F103 pin map:

```text
PA10 = USART1 RX
PA9  = USART1 TX

PA3  = USART2 RX
PA2  = USART2 TX
```

Those mappings are explicitly present in the Blue Pill/F103C8 peripheral table. fileciteturn55file0

The Blue Pill variant also confirms that the core runs it at **72 MHz CPU clock**, with USB derived at **48 MHz**, so our cycle-counter baud measurement assumptions are correct. fileciteturn44file0

One other setting turned out to matter: **don't enable Core Logs**. The core's UART debug path defaults to the board's `PIN_SERIAL_TX`, which on the Blue Pill is **PA9**, with a default debug baud of 9,600. fileciteturn48file0 That is exactly the USART1 TX pin we intend to use later for the MITM.

Use:

```text
Debug symbols and core logs:
    Symbols Enabled (-g)
```

That option also defines `NDEBUG`, disabling those core debug messages. fileciteturn37file0 fileciteturn58file0

So the exact settings I want you to use are:

```text
Board:
  Generic STM32F1 series

Board part number:
  BluePill F103C8

U(S)ART support:
  Enabled (no generic 'Serial')

USB support:
  CDC (generic 'Serial' supersede U(S)ART)

USB speed:
  Low/Full Speed

Optimize:
  Debug (-Og)

Debug symbols and core logs:
  Symbols Enabled (-g)

C Runtime Library:
  Newlib Nano (default)

Upload method:
  STM32CubeProgrammer (SWD)
```

`Newlib Nano` is fine; none of our code requires the full C runtime or float `printf`. The 3.0.0 menu confirms Nano is the default. fileciteturn37file0

I also changed the pin declarations to the new Arduino API's native `pin_size_t` and explicitly cast the UART constructor arguments. The interrupt API in 3.0.0 also uses `pin_size_t`, and `digitalPinToInterrupt()` remains valid, so that portion of our code is compatible. fileciteturn40file0 fileciteturn41file0

I made a **v5 specifically audited for STM32 core 3.0.0**, and added compile-time checks so that if one of those critical menu options is wrong, it should tell you directly instead of producing another obscure linker error.

[Download the audited v5](sandbox:/mnt/data/BluePill_Hoverboard_UART_Sniffer_v5_core300.zip)

I would use **v5 rather than trying v4**.

No additional Arduino libraries should be needed for **v5**.

Everything it uses is supplied by the **STMicroelectronics STM32 core 3.0.0** or the compiler:

- `Arduino.h` — STM32 Arduino core
- `Uart` — STM32 Arduino core
- USB CDC / `SerialUSB` — STM32 Arduino core
- `attachInterrupt()` — STM32 Arduino core
- ARM DWT cycle counter — CMSIS included with the STM32 core
- `math.h` — standard C library
- `SnifferTypes.h` — included in the ZIP; this is our own header, not a library

So you **do not need to install anything from Arduino Library Manager**.

**STM32 MCU based boards by STMicroelectronics — 3.0.0**

And for SWD uploading, the STM32 tooling associated with that core/upload method must be present. If Arduino successfully gets as far as compiling/linking or invoking the ST-Link uploader, that part is already installed.

```text

SnifferTypes.h
```

Then compile it with the settings we established.

Yes — this one is just a **flash-size problem**, not another API error.

Your selected **BluePill F103C8** target gives the linker 64 KiB of flash, and v5 compiled with `-Og` is **6,168 bytes too large**.

The first change should be:

```text
Optimize:
    Smallest (-Os default)
```

rather than:

```text
Debug (-Og)
```

Keep:

```text
Debug symbols and core logs:
    Symbols Enabled (-g)
```

The `-g` debug information is mainly stored in the ELF debug sections and isn't what is consuming the MCU's 64 KiB flash. More importantly, that STM32 core option also gives us `NDEBUG`, which prevents the core debug logging we don't want on PA9.

I also noticed another unnecessary flash consumer in my sniffer: the baud-detection code used floating-point arithmetic and float output. That's needless on an F103. I've rewritten that whole portion to use **integer arithmetic only**.

Use this revised version:

[Download v6 — STM32 core 3.0.0 / 64K version](sandbox:/mnt/data/BluePill_Hoverboard_UART_Sniffer_v6_core300_64k.zip)

```text
    Smallest (-Os default)

Debug symbols and core logs:
    Symbols Enabled (-g)

    Newlib Nano (default)

    STM32CubeProgrammer (SWD)
```

You could actually try **v5 with `Smallest (-Os)` first**; there's a decent chance that alone would recover the required 6 KB. But v6 is preferable anyway because the F103 has no reason to spend code space doing floating-point math for baud detection.

how exactly am i supposed to listen to the serial if the ide cannot connect to the stm32, the serial in the ide cannot connect

========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-09-01T08:30:43.996566+00:00
Node: d00e6212-3778-4679-a4e8-d5988c10b58c
Parent node: 91e975a3-0206-4f9f-a57f-3e17506ae252
========================================================================
Correct — if you're only connected through the **ST-Link**, Arduino Serial Monitor has nothing to connect to. I should have made that distinction explicit.

The intended setup uses **two separate USB connections**:

```text
PC
├── ST-Link USB
│    └── SWD -> Blue Pill
│         used only for flashing/debugging
│
└── Blue Pill micro-USB
     └── native USB CDC -> COM port
          used for sniffer output
```

The ST-Link SWD connection is **not serial**. A basic ST-Link V2 clone generally does not provide a virtual COM port.

For our F103C6, native USB is valid: the STM32duino 3.0.0 C6 variant uses **PA11 = USB D− and PA12 = USB D+**, and configures the USB clock from the 72 MHz system clock. fileciteturn40file0

So after flashing the sniffer, the exact sequence should be:

1. Leave the **ST-Link attached to SWD** if you want.
2. Connect a **USB data cable directly to the Blue Pill's micro-USB socket**.
3. Make sure **BOOT0 = 0**.
4. Reset/power-cycle the Blue Pill.
5. Windows should detect a new USB CDC device and assign something like `COM5`.
6. In Arduino IDE, select that new `COM` port and open Serial Monitor.

The COM port is created by the **firmware running on the Blue Pill**, not by the programmer.

### If you're already doing that and no COM port appears

Then we have a separate USB-enumeration problem. Don't proceed with the hoverboard wiring yet.

Look in **Windows Device Manager** while plugging and unplugging the Blue Pill's own USB cable. We want to determine which of these is happening:

```text
A. New COM port appears
   -> good; Arduino Serial Monitor should use that port.

B. "Unknown USB Device" appears
   -> Blue Pill USB is electrically present but enumeration is failing.

C. Absolutely nothing changes
   -> likely cable/connector/USB hardware/pull-up issue,
      or the firmware isn't running.

D. Device repeatedly connects/disconnects
   -> firmware/clock/USB hardware issue.
```

There is also a common Blue Pill hardware issue worth checking if we get **B/C**: some clones have the wrong USB D+ pull-up resistor. USB FS expects approximately **1.5 kΩ from D+ to 3.3 V**; some Blue Pill clones notoriously used a much larger value. I wouldn't modify anything until we see what Device Manager does.

One other important point: the **F103C6 only exposes USART1 and USART2 in the STM32duino peripheral map**. fileciteturn42file0 We are using both of those to watch the two hoverboard directions:

```text
USART1 RX PA10 <- master TX
USART2 RX PA3  <- slave TX
```

So native USB CDC isn't just a convenience here — it's how we get the captured data back to the PC **without consuming one of our two available UARTs**.

Tell me what **Device Manager does when you plug the Blue Pill's micro-USB cable in**, separate from the ST-Link. That will tell us exactly where the problem is.

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-01T08:32:34.192969+00:00

No—the **bootloader is not required for USB CDC** on this STM32. It would only be required if we wanted to *upload the sketch through USB*. We are uploading over SWD/ST-Link.

I checked the actual STM32duino 3.0.0 implementation. `SerialUSB.begin()` directly calls `CDC_init()` from the application firmware. fileciteturn45file0 And our v7 does exactly this in `setup()`:

```cpp
#define LOG_PORT Serial

void setup()
{
    LOG_PORT.begin(115200);
    ...
}
```

With:

```text
USB support:
CDC (generic 'Serial' supersede U(S)ART)
```

`Serial` is mapped to `SerialUSB`, so that call initializes the USB peripheral itself. There is no dependency on a bootloader.

The STM32F103C6 also definitely has a **USB 2.0 Full Speed peripheral**; ST's C6 datasheet lists it explicitly. citeturn461182search4turn461182search23

So if Windows sees nothing from the Blue Pill's USB connector after the sketch is flashed and running, we need to troubleshoot **USB enumeration**, not install a bootloader.

The quickest way to isolate it is to forget the hoverboard/sniffer for a moment and flash a minimal USB test:

```cpp
void setup()
{
    pinMode(PC13, OUTPUT);

    Serial.begin(115200);

    while (!Serial) {
        digitalWrite(PC13, !digitalRead(PC13));
        delay(250);
    }
}

void loop()
{
    Serial.println("USB CDC WORKING");
    delay(1000);
}
```

Use exactly:

```text
Board part number:
    BluePill F103C6 (32K)

U(S)ART support:
    Enabled (no generic 'Serial')

USB support:
    CDC (generic 'Serial' supersede U(S)ART)

USB speed:
    Low/Full Speed

Optimize:
    Smallest (-Os default)

C Runtime:
    Newlib Nano

Upload:
    STM32CubeProgrammer (SWD)

BOOT0:
    0
```

Flash that through ST-Link. Then **disconnect or leave the ST-Link alone and connect the Blue Pill's own USB socket to Windows**.

If the LED blinks continuously but Windows never creates a USB device, then the CPU is running and the failure is specifically between the F103 USB peripheral and the PC. At that point the first thing I'd measure is the Blue Pill's USB pull-up network, because bad/incorrect D+ pull-up resistors are common on Blue Pill clones.

One additional distinction: **Arduino IDE does not need to “connect to the STM32” through USB for programming.** Your workflow is:

```text
ST-Link/SWD
    ↓
program firmware

Blue Pill resets and runs firmware
    ↓
firmware initializes USB CDC
    ↓
Windows creates COM port
    ↓
Arduino Serial Monitor opens that COM port
```

So your instinct that something is missing is reasonable, but it isn't a bootloader. If the application USB doesn't enumerate, either **the application isn't running correctly or the Blue Pill USB hardware isn't presenting correctly**.

I would test the tiny sketch above before we touch the sniffer again. It cleanly separates the USB problem from everything else.

========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-09-01T08:34:57.051299+00:00
Node: e3818ae5-0766-4eb2-b5aa-0c698e10f201
Parent node: 92743c4f-c3d6-405f-aadc-aa311bbffb22
========================================================================
If the minimal USB test firmware is running but Windows still sees no USB device, the next thing I would check is the **Blue Pill's USB hardware**, not install a bootloader.

The STM32F103C6 itself does support USB 2.0 Full Speed; ST lists it explicitly. citeturn603610search0 And STM32duino's application firmware initializes USB CDC itself with `CDC_init()`, so a bootloader is not required for the application to enumerate. fileciteturn45file0

The common Blue Pill failure is the **D+ pull-up resistor**. Many boards were built with 10 kΩ or 4.7 kΩ instead of about 1.5 kΩ. citeturn892450search0turn892450search3

With the Blue Pill **completely unpowered**, measure resistance between:

```text
PA12  <---- meter ---->  3.3V
```

Then:

| Measurement | Meaning | Fix |
|---:|---|---|
| ~1.5 kΩ | Correct | Don't change it |
| ~10 kΩ | Common bad Blue Pill value | Add ~1.8 kΩ PA12→3.3V |
| ~4.7 kΩ | Also common bad value | Add ~2.2 kΩ PA12→3.3V |
| Open / very high | Missing/broken pull-up | Add 1.5 kΩ PA12→3.3V |

Those parallel values work out nicely:

```text
10k || 1.8k ≈ 1.53k

4.7k || 2.2k ≈ 1.50k
```

So you don't even need to replace the tiny SMD resistor initially. We can tack a normal resistor between **PA12 and 3.3 V** for testing.

Before doing that, though, there's one important distinction: **does PC13 blink with the little USB test sketch I gave you?**

If PC13 **does blink**, then we know the C6 is running our firmware and the USB physical interface becomes the prime suspect.

If PC13 **doesn't blink**, don't touch the USB resistor yet. Then our problem is that the application isn't actually executing after the ST-Link flash, and we'd check BOOT0/reset/flash execution first.

If the LED is blinking and PA12→3.3 V already measures about 1.5 kΩ, the next measurements are continuity from the USB connector:

```text
USB D+ -> PA12
USB D- -> PA11
```

At that point we can determine whether the connector/traces are good or whether this particular F103C6/clone has a USB issue. Some Blue Pill clones have also been documented to fail USB CDC even when the pull-up is corrected. citeturn892450search7

So give me **the resistance from PA12 to 3.3 V**, with the board unpowered. That's the most useful next measurement.

USER | 2026-09-01T08:36:56.786000+00:00
Node: de15b24d-4d5c-47c7-a3c4-7563a5b2e612
Parent node: e3818ae5-0766-4eb2-b5aa-0c698e10f201
========================================================================
what should the boot0 and boot1 jumpers be at

========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-09-01T08:37:02.596206+00:00
Node: e2b79a9a-7af3-4f83-8861-4bb98f244dc5
Parent node: de15b24d-4d5c-47c7-a3c4-7563a5b2e612
========================================================================
For normal operation after flashing your sketch:

```text
BOOT0 = 0
BOOT1 = 0
```

On a typical Blue Pill, that means both jumpers are in the **0 / GND position**.

That selects:

```text
BOOT0=0, BOOT1=0  -> boot from main flash
```

which is what you want for code programmed by ST-Link.

The other combinations are:

```text
BOOT0=1, BOOT1=0  -> system memory bootloader
BOOT0=1, BOOT1=1  -> system memory bootloader
BOOT0=0, BOOT1=1  -> SRAM
```

For our USB CDC test and the hoverboard sniffer, keep them both at **0**.

If BOOT0 is accidentally at 1, the MCU will ignore your flashed sketch and enter the factory ROM bootloader instead, which would absolutely explain why our application USB CDC never appears.

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-01T08:40:23.821852+00:00
Node: 9c47f727-272e-4aac-b13a-7f4749279188
Parent node: 32b41353-6a7f-4a04-896a-24986c03ba99
========================================================================
The search results you found are almost certainly talking about **USB uploading**, not whether a running sketch can expose a USB serial port.

For the official STM32duino core, a bootloader is **not required for USB CDC when the sketch itself was programmed over ST-Link**. There is even an STM32duino Blue Pill report whose reproduction steps are: compile with USB CDC enabled, upload through **ST-Link/SWD**, and then Windows shows **“STMicroelectronics Virtual COM Port.”** citeturn574813search0 The core's `SerialUSB.begin()` directly calls `CDC_init()` from the application firmware. fileciteturn45file0

So these are two different paths:

```text
USB upload:
PC -> USB -> bootloader -> application
              ^ bootloader required

Our intended method:
PC -> ST-Link/SWD -> application
                       |
                       +-> application initializes USB CDC -> COM port
                           no bootloader required
```

That said, **your board clearly isn't enumerating**, so continuing to fight its native USB isn't helping this project. And on a **32 KB F103C6**, installing a Maple/HID bootloader is especially unattractive because it consumes some of the very limited flash we need for the sniffer.

I think we should change the sniffer architecture and **not depend on the Blue Pill USB at all**.

The clean option with hardware you already have is:

```text
Hoverboard master TX ──> Blue Pill USART1 RX
Hoverboard slave TX  ──> Blue Pill USART2 RX

Blue Pill spare GPIO ──> software/debug serial TX
                          |
                          v
                    Arduino Mega
                          |
                          v
                        USB PC
```

The Blue Pill would only need a **one-way TX logging channel**, so we can bit-bang a spare GPIO at something conservative such as **115200 baud** or even **57600 baud**. The Mega then acts as the USB-to-serial bridge. That eliminates:

- Blue Pill USB enumeration completely;
- the STM32 USB CDC stack;
- the need for a bootloader;
- a substantial amount of flash usage on the C6.

It should actually make the 32 KB C6 version **much smaller** as well.

I would use a spare pin such as **PB10 or PB11** for the log TX, provided we're not otherwise using it. The F103C6 core confirms our two required hardware UARTs remain PA10/PA9 and PA3/PA2. fileciteturn42file0

So I would stop troubleshooting native USB for now and make the next version:

```text
USART1 RX PA10 = hoverboard master TX
USART2 RX PA3  = hoverboard slave TX
PB10           = PC log TX
GND            = common
```

with the **Mega as the USB bridge**.

That is a much better fit for this C6 than adding a USB bootloader.

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-01T08:48:09.529978+00:00
Node: 84419c37-53a7-48ab-ab18-ab500c4fb9ba

Yes. Right now the IDE has no port to select because the Blue Pill is not presenting a USB CDC COM device.

With the route you want, the sequence is:

```text
ST-Link -> install HID bootloader
             ↓
Blue Pill appears as USB HID
NOT a COM port yet
             ↓
Arduino uploads sniffer through HID
             ↓
sniffer starts USB CDC
             ↓
Windows creates COMx
             ↓
Arduino Serial Monitor can select COMx
```

Importantly, **the first HID-bootloader upload does not require a COM port**. The HID uploader finds the USB HID bootloader directly. The COM port only appears afterward when the uploaded application starts its USB CDC interface. citeturn920881search3turn941098search0

For the F103C6, HID Bootloader 2.2 is the right choice because it only reserves **2 KiB**. STM32duino 3.0.0 automatically sets the application offset to `0x800`, so your 32 KiB C6 has **30,720 bytes available for the sketch**. Maple Bootloader 2.0 would consume 8 KiB, which is much worse here. citeturn923539view0turn920881search1

I rebuilt the sniffer specifically around that limitation:

[Download v8 — F103C6 + HID Bootloader](sandbox:/mnt/data/BluePill_Hoverboard_UART_Sniffer_v8_C6_HID.zip)

V8 is substantially leaner. It completely removes the STM32duino `Uart` objects and HAL UART support; USART1 and USART2 are driven directly through the F103 registers. It also reduces the buffers and diagnostic strings. Another useful change is that it initializes USB CDC and then **waits for you to actually open the COM port before doing the one-shot baud detection**, so we won't lose the result before Serial Monitor is open.

Use this installation sequence:

1. Get the current **STM32 HID Bootloader** release. Your F103C6 is a low/medium-density F103, and the Blue Pill PC13 build is `hid_generic_pc13.bin`. The project explicitly identifies that binary for Blue Pill boards. citeturn253851search0turn253851search1  
   urlSTM32 HID Bootloader releaseshttps://github.com/Serasidis/STM32_HID_Bootloader/releases

2. Leave:
   ```text
   BOOT0 = 0
   BOOT1 = 0
   ```
   Connect the ST-Link through SWD.

3. In STM32CubeProgrammer, program:
   ```text
   hid_generic_pc13.bin
   ```
   at:
   ```text
   0x08000000
   ```
   That is the documented Blue Pill installation method. citeturn253851search1

4. Reset the board and connect the Blue Pill's own USB port. At this point **do not look for a COM port**. Windows should see the HID bootloader as a USB HID device. The HID bootloader is driverless on Windows. citeturn941098search0

5. Open v8 in Arduino and use:

   ```text
   Board:
       Generic STM32F1 series

   Board part number:
       BluePill F103C6 (32K)

   U(S)ART support:
       Disabled

   USB support:
       CDC (generic 'Serial' supersede U(S)ART)

   USB speed:
       Low/Full Speed

   Optimize:
       Smallest (-Os default)

   Debug symbols and core logs:
       Symbols Enabled (-g)

   C Runtime Library:
       Newlib Nano (default)

   Upload method:
       HID Bootloader 2.2
   ```

   Note the important change from the older sniffer: **U(S)ART support is now `Disabled`** because v8 talks directly to the USART registers.

6. For that **first HID upload**, you can upload even though Arduino's **Port** menu is empty. That's expected. After v8 uploads and runs, its USB CDC interface should enumerate as something like:

   ```text
   COM5
   ```

   Then choose that COM port and open Serial Monitor. citeturn920881search3

There is also a very useful diagnostic advantage here: if `hid_generic_pc13.bin` successfully enumerates in Windows as the HID bootloader, we have proven that the Blue Pill's **USB connector, PA11/PA12 lines, clock, pull-up, and Windows USB path are functional**. If HID works but v8 subsequently fails to create a COM port, we can focus entirely on STM32duino's CDC application configuration instead of wondering whether the physical USB interface works.

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-01T09:01:14.548697+00:00
