========================================================================
That error is because **Arduino’s “Burn Bootloader” command is the wrong tool for this STM32 setup**. It expects an Arduino-style programmer definition, so you get “missing programmer.”

For the Blue Pill, install the HID bootloader directly with the **ST-Link**, not with `Tools → Burn Bootloader`.

Use this sequence:

1. Download the current Serasidis HID bootloader release and use the **low/medium-density** Blue Pill binary:
   ```text
   hid_generic_pc13.bin
   ```
   The project explicitly identifies that as the Blue Pill PC13 build. citeturn964443search0turn964443search2

2. Set:
   ```text
   BOOT0 = 0
   BOOT1 = 0
   ```

3. Connect the ST-Link over SWD:
   ```text
   ST-Link GND   -> Blue Pill GND
   ST-Link SWDIO -> Blue Pill SWDIO
   ST-Link SWCLK -> Blue Pill SWCLK
   ```

4. Open **STM32CubeProgrammer** and connect using **ST-LINK**.

5. In the download/programming screen, select:
   ```text
   hid_generic_pc13.bin
   ```

6. Set the start address to:
   ```text
   0x08000000
   ```

7. Program it, then disconnect/reset the board.

That is the documented installation method: the bootloader goes at `0x08000000` and occupies 2 KiB; application firmware then begins at `0x08000800`. citeturn964443search8

Afterward, in Arduino IDE use:

```text
Upload method:
    HID Bootloader 2.2
```

Do **not** choose `Burn Bootloader` again.

Also, after installing the bootloader, the first thing Windows sees is a **USB HID device**, not necessarily a COM port. Once our sniffer application launches its USB CDC interface, Windows should create the COM port for Serial Monitor.

If you want, I can walk you through the exact STM32CubeProgrammer screen/buttons for flashing `hid_generic_pc13.bin`.

For your Blue Pill in normal use, including running the HID bootloader and then the sniffer:

```text
BOOT0 = 0
BOOT1 = 0
```

So both jumpers should be on the **0 / GND side**.

Use that same setting when:
- flashing the HID bootloader with ST-Link
- resetting afterward
- uploading via HID
- running the sniffer

Only move BOOT0 to `1` if you specifically want the STM32’s built-in ROM bootloader. For what we’re doing, keep both at `0`.

That's fine — we can capture **both UART directions from the master board alone**, as long as the normal master/slave harness remains connected.

At the **master board**:

```text
Master PA2 = master TX  ─────> Blue Pill PA10
Master PA3 = master RX  ─────> Blue Pill PA3
Master GND             ─────> Blue Pill GND
```

Why this works:

```text
MASTER PA2 TX ───────────────> SLAVE PA3 RX
              ^
              tap here -> Blue Pill PA10

SLAVE PA2 TX ────────────────> MASTER PA3 RX
                                ^
                                tap here -> Blue Pill PA3
```

So we **do not actually need physical access to the slave board**. The slave→master data is already present on the master's **PA3 RX line**.

Keep the original 4-wire master/slave cable connected normally. The Blue Pill is only listening, so do **not** connect its TX pins PA9 or PA2.

Thus with v7:

```text
Blue Pill PA10 = capture master → slave
Blue Pill PA3  = capture slave → master
```

That's actually the cleaner place to connect everything.

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-01T10:13:11.423916+00:00
Node: eb594b7b-c487-4355-a01d-f0ea191800f8

========================================================================
On the Blue Pill, **PA10 is on the header side that also has PA12, PA11, PA9, PA8**.

With the **USB connector at the top and the component/MCU side facing you**, that side of the header is arranged approximately:

```text
USB end
   |
3.3V
GND
5V
PB9
PB8
PB7
PB6
PB5
PB4
PB3
PA15
PA12
PA11
PA10   <── connect master-board PA2 TX here
PA9
PA8
PB15
PB14
PB13
PB12
```

So **PA10 is between PA11 and PA9**, about two-thirds of the way down that side.

For your sniffer hookup:

```text
Master board PA2 TX -> Blue Pill PA10
Master board PA3 RX -> Blue Pill PA3
Master board GND    -> Blue Pill GND
```

Use the printed `A10` marking on the Blue Pill if visible; that's the safest reference rather than relying only on left/right orientation.

Yes — those readings are plausible for **active 3.3 V UART lines**, especially if you're using a multimeter.

A UART line normally switches between roughly:

```text
LOW  ≈ 0 V
HIGH ≈ 3.3 V
```

but a DMM only shows the average. So:

- **2.6 V** could be a UART line that spends most of its time HIGH, which is normal because UART idles HIGH.
- **0.5–1.8 V fluctuating** could be a line with much heavier traffic, where the meter is averaging lots of HIGH/LOW transitions.

So don't use the DC voltage to decide which is TX versus RX. The useful test is to connect the sniffer and see which input produces edges/data.

For the master-board-only hookup:

```text
master PA2 -> Blue Pill PA10
master PA3 -> Blue Pill PA3
master GND -> Blue Pill GND
```

Keep the master/slave harness connected.

Then reset the Blue Pill while the hoverboard boards are powered. V7 should tell us whether it sees transitions on each side and what baud candidate it derives.

If one input gives lots of edges and the other gives none, then we've either got the wrong pin or only one direction is active at that moment.

A scope or logic analyzer would make the distinction obvious, but the sniffer itself should be enough. If you tell me **which master pin is reading 2.6 V and which is the fluctuating 0.5–1.8 V**, I can tell you what I expect before you hook it up.

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-01T10:21:55.821829+00:00

Correct. We know the **MCU UART pins** from the firmware analysis, but we have **not yet established which contacts on the 4-wire master/slave connector correspond to them**. So the voltage readings alone are not enough to assign TX/RX.

For the GD32F130C8T6 in LQFP48, the relevant MCU pins are:

```text
PA2 = physical MCU pin 12 = factory USART1 TX
PA3 = physical MCU pin 13 = factory USART1 RX
```

That pin assignment is in GigaDevice's datasheet. citeturn365668search24turn365668search25

The safest way to map the connector is with the board **unpowered** and the master/slave cable unplugged:

```text
Meter in continuity / low-ohms mode

connector contact 1 -> MCU pin 12 (PA2)
connector contact 2 -> MCU pin 12
connector contact 3 -> MCU pin 12
connector contact 4 -> MCU pin 12

then repeat to MCU pin 13 (PA3)
```

There may be a small series resistor between the MCU and connector, so don't require a dead-short beep. A reading of, say, **tens to a few hundred ohms** could still be the UART path. Trace visually for any resistor immediately between the connector and MCU if continuity isn't direct.

Once identified:

```text
connector contact that traces to PA2 -> master TX
connector contact that traces to PA3 -> master RX / slave TX
```

Then our Blue Pill hookup becomes:

```text
master connector PA2 contact -> Blue Pill PA10
master connector PA3 contact -> Blue Pill PA3
master board ground          -> Blue Pill GND
```

The other two contacts should be left alone until we identify them; one or both may be involved in power/wake rather than UART.

And yes, that means my earlier wording treating the connector pins as already known was too definite. We need to **trace PA2 and PA3 to the plug first**. memcite

========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-09-01T10:22:57.550911+00:00
Node: f7873ec1-e5ff-497b-8412-4771567996fa
Parent node: e1b6ea3f-7dd2-4bbf-98a5-f2e7deb78ba2
========================================================================
With the slave unplugged, only the **master’s TX line can actively generate UART traffic**.

The master’s RX line is just an input at that point, so with nothing driving it you’d expect it to sit at whatever its biasing gives it—typically near **3.3 V** if there’s an internal/external pull-up, or possibly float if there isn’t one.

So, with the 4-wire slave cable disconnected:

- a pin that is **actively fluctuating** is a strong candidate for **master TX**
- a pin that sits **steady near 3.3 V** is a strong candidate for **master RX**
- the other two pins are likely power/wake/ground-related and need separate identification

One caveat: if the master firmware stops transmitting when it detects the slave is absent, TX may only show a short burst at startup and then go quiet. So the best test is to power-cycle the master while watching the suspect pins.

Given your earlier readings, the **0.5–1.8 V fluctuating pin** is the better TX candidate, while the **2.6 V pin** is more consistent with an undriven or mostly-idle UART input—but we should verify with the slave unplugged before assigning them.

From your photo I can give you the exact locations.

With the board oriented exactly as shown:

- **PA10** is on the **bottom header row**, labeled `A10`. It is between `A9` and `A11`.
- **PA3** is on the **top header row**, labeled `A3`. It is between `A4` and `A2`.

So for the sniffer:

```text
Hoverboard master TX candidate  -> A10
Hoverboard master RX candidate  -> A3
Hoverboard GND                   -> G
```

Your board's printed rows are:

```text
TOP:
G  G  3.3  R  B11 B10 B1 B0 A7 A6 A5 A4 A3 A2 A1 A0 ...

BOTTOM:
B12 B13 B14 B15 A8 A9 A10 A11 A12 A15 B3 B4 B5 B6 B7 B8 B9 5V G 3.3
                       ^ PA10
```

So **A10 is the bottom row, roughly directly below the MCU's left-middle area**. Do not use the separate vertical 4-pin header on the right for this; that's the SWD programming header.

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-01T10:32:12.592829+00:00
Node: d76ee534-674f-4d0d-b500-e6369ded3177
Parent node: b6a6f50c-3871-47cb-95f7-c8fcbf1e0e20
========================================================================
This capture is useful, and it shows that **we are definitely seeing a periodic digital signal on the Blue Pill PA3 sniffer channel**—but the UART decode itself is wrong.

I parsed the whole capture: it contains 9,469 lines. fileciteturn57file0L1-L8 The traffic repeats almost exactly every **3.99 ms**, or about **250 Hz**. More importantly, the decoded payload contains only four byte values:

```text
00
78
80
F8
```

You can see that pattern continuously at the beginning fileciteturn57file0L12-L30 and it is still present at the end of the capture. fileciteturn57file1L40-L69

That is **not credible as the actual factory data stream**. Notice that every one of those values has its bottom three bits cleared:

```text
00 = 00000000
78 = 01111000
80 = 10000000
F8 = 11111000
         ^^^
       always 000
```

That pattern strongly suggests v7's auto-baud detector has locked onto a **harmonic of the real baud**, very likely about **4× too fast**. At 4× the real baud, the real UART start bit occupies roughly four decoder bit periods; that naturally produces approximately three zero data samples at the beginning of each falsely decoded byte.

So I would **not** interpret the apparent `F8 F8 78 78...` as a header or treat those `0x78`s as the command 120 packet we found in the firmware. They're almost certainly decoding artifacts.

Also, every captured record says:

```text
S>M
```

That specifically means **v7 received it through its PA3/USART2 input**. Because we still haven't traced the 4-wire connector to MCU PA2/PA3, we should treat `S>M` as our *sniffer-channel label*, not proof yet that this connector wire really is slave→master.

### What I need next

The **`Starting ... baud`** value is especially important.

I think our next version should also fix the auto-baud algorithm so it explicitly detects these harmonic locks instead of simply choosing the highest-scoring candidate.

The good news is that the hardware hookup is doing something useful: **we have a very stable ~250 Hz signal on that connector wire.** Now we need to decode it at the correct baud.

### SWD at the same time

If you mean the **hoverboard controller's SWD header**, no—not yet.

```text
UART connector -> actual live master/slave communications
SWDIO/SWCLK    -> processor debug interface
```

SWD is not another copy of the serial traffic. An ST-Link attached there can later be extremely useful for:

- watching the factory UART receive/transmit buffers in RAM;
- setting watchpoints on the packet buffer;
- seeing which variables change when a particular UART field changes;
- tracing from the USART interrupt into the packet dispatcher;
- eventually correlating packet fields to pitch, wheel speed, command values, etc.

But doing that now adds another variable while we're still establishing the physical UART lines and correct baud.

If you mean the **Blue Pill's own SWD header**, the ST-Link may remain physically connected, but it isn't helping the sniffing. I would disconnect it once v7 is flashed, particularly its power/reference wiring, and just use the Blue Pill USB for the serial output.

So our immediate objective is very simple:

```text
Slave disconnected
Master powered
Master TX -> Blue Pill A10
Master RX candidate -> Blue Pill A3
Blue Pill reset
```

Then we want to prove that **A10 alone produces the master traffic** and get the baud-detection lines. Once that is correct, reconnect the slave and A3 should suddenly begin showing the return traffic. That will give us a very strong confirmation of the connector TX/RX pinout.

This capture is **good**. We have the UART nailed down now.

V7 detected **115200 baud, 8-N-1** with about a 93% edge fit, and after swapping the taps the real traffic is on the `M>S` channel. fileciteturn59file1L57-L72

More importantly, I checked the complete capture rather than just eyeballing it. There are **3,925 normal 17-byte packets**, and **every single one passes CRC-16/MODBUS**. So my earlier suspicion that 115200 might be a harmonic was wrong; **115200 is definitely the correct baud**.

The packet format is now very clear:

```text
Byte 0      7B          start/header
Byte 1      78          command = 0x78
Byte 2      0F          length = 15 bytes before CRC
Bytes 3-14  xx ... xx   12-byte payload
Bytes 15-16 CRC16       CRC-16/MODBUS, low byte first
```

For example:

```text
7B 78 0F A6 00 17 00 00 FF E6 83 21 FF C0 00 1A B6
|  |  |  <------------- payload -------------> |   |
|  |  |                                         CRC
|  |  length
|  command 0x78
header
```

For that packet, CRC-16/MODBUS over the first 15 bytes is:

```text
0xB61A
```

and the transmitted final bytes are:

```text
1A B6
```

exactly as expected. The subsequent packets keep the same 17-byte structure while payload fields change. fileciteturn59file1L70-L80

The timing is also extremely consistent: approximately **3.993 ms per packet**, or about **250.4 packets/sec**.

### The RX-line question is resolved too

With the slave disconnected, the whole capture contains essentially:

```text
M>S : thousands of valid packets
S>M : essentially nothing
```

There are only isolated one-byte `00` events on `S>M`, such as the single event after the long run. fileciteturn59file0L19-L34 Those are consistent with the undriven RX wire floating/glitching during reset/shutdown, not actual packets.

So our connector assignment is now strongly established:

```text
Master connector active wire -> Blue Pill PA10
                               = MASTER TX

Master connector quiet wire  -> Blue Pill PA3
                               = MASTER RX
```

And `0x78` being the command byte is especially significant because it matches the `0x78` command we had already identified in the factory firmware dispatcher.

### What to do next

Now I would **reconnect the slave while leaving both Blue Pill taps exactly where they are**:

```text
Master TX wire -> Blue Pill PA10
Master RX wire -> Blue Pill PA3
Common GND     -> Blue Pill GND
```

Reset the Blue Pill after the master/slave pair is powered.

What I expect is:

```text
M>S -> these 17-byte 7B 78 0F ... CRC packets
S>M -> actual slave response packets now appear
```

That capture will tell us the **return-packet structure** and may immediately expose which message fields are commands versus telemetry.

I would still **leave the hoverboard SWD alone for this next capture**. UART is giving us pristine protocol data now. Once we have both directions decoded, SWD becomes much more valuable: we can correlate a specific payload byte/word with its RAM variable and the code consuming it rather than searching blindly.

One other useful finding: the 12-byte payload is structured, not random. Several bytes are fixed while others change slowly and repeatably. I would **not assign meanings to those fields yet**; after the slave is reconnected we can do controlled tests—tilt, wheel rotation, etc.—and correlate each field. memcite

```text
Master TX candidate:  7B 78 0F A6 ...
```

```text
BD packet wire -> Blue Pill PA3
```

### Best way to identify it

Rather than swapping randomly among the four connector contacts, the definitive test is with power off:

```text
Master MCU PA2 -> connector contact = master TX
Master MCU PA3 -> connector contact = master RX / slave TX
```

We already know from the factory firmware that the controller's inter-board USART is:

```text
PA2 = TX
PA3 = RX
```

on both boards. fileciteturn61file4L448-L458

So on the **master connector**:

```text
contact tracing to master PA2 -> PA10 on Blue Pill
contact tracing to master PA3 -> PA3 on Blue Pill
```

That removes all ambiguity.

The fact that PA3's edge detector reported 96 transitions does **not** prove it was on the UART RX line. Crosstalk or another switching line can produce edge timing that looks UART-ish; the absence of valid `7B 78 0F ... CRC` frames is what matters.

Once those two connector contacts are traced and connected correctly, I expect to see simultaneously:

```text
M>S  ... A6 ...
S>M  ... BD ...
```

at roughly 250 packets/sec each. If we get that, we've effectively solved the electrical and framing layer and can move on to decoding the `0x78` fields. memcite

Yes. That is the cleaner architecture now that we know the link is **115200 baud, 8-N-1**. fileciteturn62file0L11-L18

The Blue Pill has exactly what we need: two full-duplex hardware UARTs. Instead of passively tapping two wires, we put it **inline**:

```text
                 BLUE PILL

MASTER TX ────> PA10  USART1 RX
MASTER RX <──── PA9   USART1 TX

SLAVE  TX ────> PA3   USART2 RX
SLAVE  RX <──── PA2   USART2 TX
```

And internally:

```text
Master TX
   ↓
PA10 / USART1 RX
   ↓
Blue Pill forwards byte
   ↓
PA2 / USART2 TX
   ↓
Slave RX


Slave TX
   ↓
PA3 / USART2 RX
   ↓
Blue Pill forwards byte
   ↓
PA9 / USART1 TX
   ↓
Master RX
```

That gives us a true MITM. The factory controller side is confirmed as PA2=TX and PA3=RX. fileciteturn61file4L448-L458

The important physical difference is that **the original two UART signal wires can no longer remain directly connected master-to-slave**. We need to break/intercept just those two wires. Keep the other two wires in the 4-wire harness connected straight through because we still haven't resolved their power/wake functions.

The easiest way is probably to cut or depin the **two UART conductors in the cable**, leaving the other two untouched:

```text
Original UART conductor #1:

MASTER side ──> Blue Pill PA10
                Blue Pill PA2 ──> SLAVE side


Original UART conductor #2:

MASTER side <── Blue Pill PA9
                Blue Pill PA3 <── SLAVE side
```

This actually solves the wiring confusion we've been having, because once it's inline the Blue Pill knows unambiguously which physical UART produced every byte.

I've changed v7 into a fixed-115200 full-duplex MITM version. It starts both UARTs immediately and forwards the byte **before** doing USB logging, so Serial Monitor isn't in the critical path.

[Download Blue Pill MITM v9](sandbox:/mnt/data/BluePill_Hoverboard_UART_MITM_v9_C6.zip)

The sketch uses:

```text
Blue Pill PA10 = master-facing RX
Blue Pill PA9  = master-facing TX

Blue Pill PA3  = slave-facing RX
Blue Pill PA2  = slave-facing TX

GND            = common hoverboard GND
```

Use the same Arduino settings as v7. I removed baud auto-detection because we've established 115200 well enough that there's no benefit to delaying the bridge during startup.

For the first test, I would **power the Blue Pill from USB first**, then power the hoverboard pair. If everything is connected correctly, Serial Monitor should immediately show alternating streams something like:

```text
M>S [17] 7B 78 0F ...
S>M [17] 7B 78 0F ...
```

and the two stock boards should behave normally through the Blue Pill.

Once that works, this same program can evolve from transparent bridging into exactly what we wanted earlier: **log → replay → modify fields → inject packets → emulate either master or slave**. memcite

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-01T18:26:05.261859+00:00
Node: 9632794f-3b9e-4c11-8985-c4ffc1db2cbe
Parent node: 227a4609-cf7c-4ca1-9d47-0bb882ceb4d3
========================================================================
That makes sense, and it explains the confusing captures very well.

If you’re using **two stock 4-wire UART cables as extension leads**, then the cable halves themselves are fine, but at the point where you coupled them together you may have paired the two signal conductors incorrectly. In other words, you may have accidentally done one of these:

```text
TX -> TX
RX -> RX
```

or crossed one tap relative to the other.

For the MITM setup, the cleanest approach is to stop thinking in terms of “wire colors” and identify each cable end by **function**.

On the master-side cable:

```text
wire that traces to master PA2 = MASTER TX
wire that traces to master PA3 = MASTER RX
```

On the slave-side cable:

```text
wire that traces to slave PA2 = SLAVE TX
wire that traces to slave PA3 = SLAVE RX
```

Then connect them to the Blue Pill like this:

```text
MASTER TX -> Blue Pill PA10
MASTER RX <- Blue Pill PA9

SLAVE  TX -> Blue Pill PA3
SLAVE  RX <- Blue Pill PA2
```

The two non-UART conductors in each stock 4-wire cable should remain straight-through between master and slave for now, because we still haven't proven whether they are ground, power, wake/enable, or some combination.

So with two stock cables your final harness should conceptually be:

```text
MASTER CABLE                 BLUE PILL                 SLAVE CABLE

PA2 / TX  -----------------> PA10

PA3 / RX  <----------------- PA9


                            PA3  <------------------ PA2 / TX

                            PA2  ------------------> PA3 / RX


other wire #1  ------------------------------------ other wire #1
other wire #2  ------------------------------------ other wire #2
```

One particularly useful sanity check: with everything **powered off**, measure continuity through the cables and make yourself a small table like:

```text
Master plug position 1 -> junction wire ?
Master plug position 2 -> junction wire ?
Master plug position 3 -> junction wire ?
Master plug position 4 -> junction wire ?

Slave plug position 1  -> junction wire ?
...
```

Because stock cables can also be **mirrored physically at opposite connector ends**, so “left wire to left wire” is not necessarily the same electrical pin. That is very likely where the mixing came from.

Once the Blue Pill is actually inline, v9 eliminates this ambiguity entirely: each board gets its own RX/TX pair, and the Blue Pill does the crossover in software. That is a much better setup for everything we want to do next.

========================================================================

```text

PA3  = sniffer input for the other UART direction

PA9  = leave disconnected
PA2  = leave disconnected
```

Because you are using **two stock 4-wire cables that meet at the Blue Pill**, the two actual UART paths still need to be physically connected through at that junction:

```text
MASTER TX ─────┬─────> SLAVE RX
               └─────> Blue Pill PA10

SLAVE TX ──────┬─────> MASTER RX
               └─────> Blue Pill PA3
```

So yes: with v7, if you got the two UART conductors crossed on **one of the stock cables**, swapping the two signal wires of **that one cable at the Pill-side junction** is the appropriate correction. Don't change both sides at once, because that can simply recreate the same error.


The other two stock-cable conductors stay connected straight through.

```text
actual meaning: PA10 received data

physical source: SLAVE TX
```

what does it mean if i plug in the stlink and the red led flashes

========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-09-01T19:10:25.461269+00:00
Node: 64c9fba8-42df-4416-97be-6df9f56e6649
Parent node: 344ba10f-9717-44a2-a253-7380550d9abc
========================================================================
If you mean the **red LED on the ST-Link itself**, it depends on exactly when it flashes. On the common ST-Link V2 clones, the LED implementation is not standardized enough for “red flashing = one specific error.”

For our setup, these cases matter:

- **Flashes when CubeProgrammer/OpenOCD is trying to connect:** usually just SWD communication/activity, or repeated failed connection attempts.
- **Flashes continuously as soon as you connect it to the hoverboard board, even with no software accessing it:** that is more suspicious. It can indicate the ST-Link is being power-cycled or seeing an electrical conflict.
- **Starts flashing specifically when you connect the ST-Link 3.3 V pin:** disconnect that pin. We do **not** want the ST-Link powering/backfeeding this controller.

For the powered hoverboard controller I would connect only:

```text
ST-Link GND   -> controller GND
ST-Link SWDIO -> controller SWDIO
ST-Link SWCLK -> controller SWCLK
```

Leave:

```text
ST-Link 3.3V -> disconnected
```

unless we establish that your particular clone uses that pin purely as **VTref/input** rather than a power output.

A useful test is to plug the ST-Link into USB with **nothing attached to its SWD header**. Note what the red LED does. Then connect only GND, then SWDIO/SWCLK. If the LED changes behavior only after one specific connection, that tells us quite a bit.

If the **red LED you're referring to is actually one of the LEDs you connected to the slave controller**, that's a different question—the flashing could be firmware status/fault indication rather than ST-Link status.

the red led on the pill

========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-09-01T19:10:56.758360+00:00
Node: fc3789cd-1798-4f31-813a-dfc7608e8075
Parent node: 84773c29-02c4-43ac-94c0-bb73f67ed74e
========================================================================
If it’s the **red LED on the Blue Pill itself**, that is usually the **power LED**, not the PC13/user LED.

So if that red LED starts **flashing when you plug in the ST-Link**, I would treat that as a **power problem**, not normal SWD activity. Reset or SWD traffic should not normally make the power LED blink.

Most likely causes are:

```text
ST-Link 3.3 V connected to Blue Pill 3.3 V
while the Blue Pill is already USB-powered

or

ST-Link is backfeeding / loading the 3.3 V rail
```

For the Blue Pill while it is powered from USB, use:

```text
ST-Link GND   -> Blue Pill GND
ST-Link SWDIO -> Blue Pill SWDIO
ST-Link SWCLK -> Blue Pill SWCLK
```

and leave:

```text
ST-Link 3.3 V -> DISCONNECTED
```

The red power LED should then stay **steadily on**.

If your particular Blue Pill clone uses a **red PC13/user LED instead of the usual green one**, that's different—the firmware could intentionally blink it. But if it's the LED that normally stays continuously illuminated whenever USB power is present, flashing means the **3.3 V supply is cycling or collapsing**.

A very quick test is: power the Pill from USB, confirm red LED steady, then connect the ST-Link **one wire at a time: GND, SWDIO, SWCLK**. If it only starts flashing after one particular wire is connected, that will tell us where the conflict is.

when i think there was some type of power fault that fried the pill

========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-09-01T19:11:46.958563+00:00
Node: 9a3e90f1-9681-428c-8aa4-a69f6b45d424
Parent node: eb75387a-faa5-4de9-8b55-e6de2c248ae1
========================================================================
Yes, that is plausible. If the Blue Pill's normally steady **red power LED started flashing**, that can indicate the 3.3 V rail was repeatedly collapsing and restarting—exactly what you can see from a short, overvoltage, or regulator/current-limit fault.

Given the wiring work we were doing, I'd especially suspect either the ST-Link 3.3 V connection/backfeed or one of the two still-unidentified hoverboard harness conductors accidentally reaching a Blue Pill signal/power pin.

Before writing the Pill off, test it completely isolated:

```text
Disconnect:
- hoverboard harness
- ST-Link
- every jumper wire

Leave only USB connected.
```

Then measure:

```text
Blue Pill 5V -> GND    ≈ 5.0 V
Blue Pill 3V3 -> GND   ≈ 3.3 V
```

The results will narrow it down quickly:

| Measurement | Likely condition |
|---|---|
| 5 V good, 3.3 V near 0 V | 3.3 V regulator failed or 3.3 V rail shorted |
| 5 V good, 3.3 V pulsing | regulator repeatedly entering protection; likely short |
| 5 V good, 3.3 V good | power system survived; MCU/USB may still be damaged |
| 5 V also collapsing | USB/input-side short or severe board damage |

With the Pill **unpowered**, also measure resistance:

```text
3V3 -> GND
5V  -> GND
```

Don't worry about the initial number while the capacitors charge; watch where it settles. If `3V3 -> GND` settles at only a few ohms, something on that rail is probably cooked.

One particularly useful test is temperature: power it from USB for only a few seconds and feel/check whether the **STM32 itself** or the little 3.3 V regulator gets hot.

```text
Regulator gets hot + 3.3 V low
    -> regulator or downstream 3.3 V short

STM32 gets hot
    -> MCU very likely damaged
```

If you give me the **5 V voltage, 3.3 V voltage, and unpowered 3V3-to-GND resistance**, we can tell pretty quickly whether the Pill is actually dead or whether only its regulator was sacrificed.

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-01T19:15:32.076372+00:00
Node: 903d9616-ba6d-4cc8-be21-b1fcc5be35b1

========================================================================
Yes — that would fit what you saw, but electrically it would be better described as a **ground-potential/backfeed fault** rather than voltage “coming through ground.”

The dangerous situation would be something like:

```text
Hoverboard board "ground"/harness conductor
            |
            |  unexpected voltage relative to PC ground
            |
Blue Pill GND
            |
USB GND
            |
PC / ST-Link ground
```

If the conductor you assumed was ground was actually carrying a supply, wake voltage, or simply sat at a different potential from USB ground, connecting it to the Blue Pill while USB/ST-Link was attached would force the two potentials together. The resulting current could absolutely damage the Pill.

That would also explain a **flashing red power LED**: the Blue Pill's supply could have been repeatedly collapsing/resetting as the fault current pulled its rail around.

Before connecting another Blue Pill, I would stop treating any of the four stock-cable conductors as ground by assumption. We know only the two UART MCU functions with confidence; the other two harness conductors were never electrically identified.

With everything disconnected from the Blue Pill, use the meter and reference everything to **battery negative / known controller PCB ground** on the master:

```text
Harness conductor 1 -> known controller GND
Harness conductor 2 -> known controller GND
Harness conductor 3 -> known controller GND
Harness conductor 4 -> known controller GND
```

Measure both:

```text
POWER OFF: resistance/continuity
POWER ON:  DC voltage
```

We want to positively identify:

```text
GND
Master TX
Master RX
fourth conductor (power/wake/enable/etc.)
```

Especially measure that fourth conductor. If it turns out to be, say, battery voltage or some intermediate supply, that would explain a lot.

Also, because the Blue Pill is USB-powered, **its GND is effectively PC USB ground**. Once we use it with the hoverboard, there should be exactly one intentionally verified connection between:

```text
Blue Pill/USB GND <-> hoverboard logic GND
```

not a guessed harness conductor.

If you have the damaged Pill handy, one particularly telling measurement is now:

```text
unpowered Blue Pill:
GND -> 3V3 resistance
GND -> 5V resistance
```

If the fault entered through its ground network, we may find the STM32 or regulator now presents a very low resistance to ground.

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-01T19:16:45.651226+00:00

Understood — if the wiring was unchanged and the **slave itself developed a fault**, then the fault source is the slave board, not a mistaken ground connection.

A very plausible failure path is that the slave put an abnormal voltage onto one of the harness conductors. If that was one of the UART lines, the Blue Pill could have been damaged through its input protection network:

```text
Slave fault
   ↓
UART TX/RX driven above ~3.3 V
   ↓
Blue Pill GPIO protection diode conducts
   ↓
Blue Pill 3.3 V rail gets back-fed
   ↓
regulator/MCU/USB rail conflict
```

That can make the Blue Pill's red power LED flash as the 3.3 V rail repeatedly collapses or is being driven from two directions.

It could also happen if the slave's **logic ground itself rose relative to the master/Blue Pill ground** because of a failed ground path on the slave. In that case all of the slave's signal voltages would effectively shift upward relative to the Pill, even though no wires were moved.

Before connecting another Pill, I would check the slave completely by itself. With master and Blue Pill disconnected, measure these relative to a known solid slave PCB ground/battery negative:

- slave 3.3 V logic rail
- each of the four harness conductors
- resistance from slave logic ground to battery negative, power off
- resistance from each UART line to 3.3 V and ground, power off

For the UART pins, anything much above about **3.3–3.6 V** when powered is immediately suspicious. Likewise, if the slave's ground is not essentially at battery-negative potential, that would explain the apparent “voltage through ground.”

Given that the slave's LEDs stopped behaving normally at about the same time, I would treat the slave board as suspect until those voltages are checked. I would **not connect the replacement Blue Pill to it yet**.

i am using a different pill now and am running into issues again with windows not recognising it

========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-09-01T21:38:54.934824+00:00
Node: 1f44cc94-7c45-4ec7-81b5-a732c07defbd

With a **different Blue Pill**, the first thing to establish is whether it already has **v7 flashed onto it**. A fresh/blank STM32F103C6 Blue Pill will **not necessarily enumerate over USB at all**. The USB CDC device we were using comes from the v7 firmware itself; it does not require a bootloader, but the firmware must first be programmed through SWD.

For this replacement Pill, I’d do it in this order:

1. Disconnect it completely from the hoverboard wiring.
2. Power it only from USB.
3. Make sure:
   ```text
   BOOT0 = 0
   BOOT1 = 0
   ```
4. Connect ST-Link:
   ```text
   ST-Link GND   -> Blue Pill GND
   ST-Link SWDIO -> Blue Pill SWDIO
   ST-Link SWCLK -> Blue Pill SWCLK
   ```
   Leave ST-Link 3.3 V disconnected if the Pill is USB-powered.
5. Flash **v7** using the same Arduino settings that worked previously:
   ```text
   Board: Generic STM32F1 series
   Board part: BluePill F103C6 (32K)
   U(S)ART support: Enabled (no generic 'Serial')
   USB support: CDC (generic 'Serial' supersede U(S)ART)
   USB speed: Low/Full Speed
   Upload method: STM32CubeProgrammer (SWD)
   ```
6. After programming, unplug the ST-Link, leave USB connected, and reset/power-cycle the Pill.

At that point Windows should detect a USB device and normally create a COM port.

If **v7 is already flashed**, open Device Manager and watch what happens while you unplug/replug the USB cable. There are three useful outcomes:

```text
Nothing changes at all
    -> USB cable/connector/D+/D- hardware problem

"Unknown USB Device" appears
    -> electrical USB enumeration problem or wrong firmware/clock setup

A USB/CDC device appears but no COM port
    -> Windows driver/device association issue
```

Also remember the previous problem turned out to be the **USB cable**, so I would use the exact cable that was proven to work with the previous Pill before changing anything else.

One replacement-board-specific issue is worth checking too: cheap Blue Pills sometimes have the **wrong USB D+ pull-up resistor** fitted. Genuine/reference F103 USB expects about **1.5 kΩ from D+ to 3.3 V**; some older clones famously used roughly **10 kΩ**, which can make enumeration unreliable depending on the PC.

But first: **has this replacement Pill already had v7 flashed to it through the ST-Link?** If not, Windows failing to recognize it over USB is expected.

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-01T22:48:23.762274+00:00

That narrows it down considerably. **“Device Descriptor Request Failed” means Windows is detecting that a USB device has attached, but the Blue Pill is failing during USB enumeration.** So this is not the same as Windows seeing absolutely nothing.

Since v7 worked on the previous Pill, I’d focus on this replacement board’s USB hardware or clocking.

The quickest checks are:

- Use the **same known-good USB cable** that worked with the previous Pill.
- Disconnect the ST-Link and all hoverboard wiring. USB only.
- Confirm v7 was flashed successfully to this replacement F103C6.
- Power-cycle it after flashing.

Then check the USB lines. On the STM32F103:

```text
PA11 = USB D-
PA12 = USB D+
```

With the board **unpowered**, measure continuity/resistance from the Micro-USB connector:

```text
USB pin 2 D- -> PA11
USB pin 3 D+ -> PA12
```

There are normally small series resistors, commonly around **22 Ω**, so you may see roughly that rather than zero ohms.

The especially important Blue Pill check is the **D+ pull-up resistor**. These boards are notorious for having the wrong value on some clones.

For USB full-speed enumeration, D+ should have approximately:

```text
D+ -> 3.3 V ≈ 1.5 kΩ
```

Some Blue Pills were built with something around **10 kΩ**, which can cause exactly this sort of flaky or failed enumeration.

So with the board unpowered, measure:

```text
PA12 / D+ -> 3V3
```

Tell me what resistance you get.

Also measure the rails while USB-powered:

```text
5V  -> GND ≈ 5.0 V
3V3 -> GND ≈ 3.3 V
```

If those are good, another strong possibility is the **8 MHz crystal / USB clock**. The F103 USB peripheral requires a valid **48 MHz USB clock**. Our v7 configuration derives that from the normal Blue Pill clock setup. If this replacement board has a bad crystal, wrong crystal, or counterfeit/odd MCU, the firmware can still flash over SWD while USB gives:

```text
Unknown USB Device
Device Descriptor Request Failed
Code 43
```

So my priority order would be:

1. Confirm v7 flashes successfully by SWD.
2. Measure `PA12 -> 3V3` resistance.
3. Check D+/D− continuity to PA12/PA11.
4. Verify 3.3 V rail.
5. If all of that is correct, test whether the 8 MHz oscillator is actually running.

The **PA12-to-3V3 resistance** is the one I'd check first; it can quickly tell us whether this replacement Pill is one of the bad-pull-up Blue Pill variants.

That is a much more useful symptom. On the STM32F103, **“The core is locked up” does not automatically mean the MCU is permanently damaged.** It commonly means the Cortex-M3 entered its hardware lockup state while executing bad firmware—for example, a fault occurring while already handling another fault. ST also recommends connecting **under reset** to regain control of MCUs in this condition. citeturn408588search0turn408588search1

For this replacement **F103C6 Blue Pill**, I would recover it before worrying about USB enumeration.

Connect the ST-Link like this:

```text
ST-Link GND   -> Blue Pill GND
ST-Link SWDIO -> Blue Pill SWDIO / PA13
ST-Link SWCLK -> Blue Pill SWCLK / PA14
ST-Link NRST  -> Blue Pill NRST       <-- important here

Blue Pill powered from USB
ST-Link 3.3 V left disconnected
```

Then in STM32CubeProgrammer use:

```text
Port:         SWD
Frequency:    100–400 kHz initially
Connect mode: Under Reset
Reset mode:   Hardware reset
```

Then click **Connect**.

If you don't have NRST connected, you can approximate this manually:

```text
1. Hold the Blue Pill RESET button down.
2. Click Connect in CubeProgrammer.
3. Release RESET as CubeProgrammer attempts to connect.
```

Once connected, do a **Full Chip Erase / Mass Erase**. Don't immediately reflash v7 yet. After the erase:

```text
BOOT0 = 0
BOOT1 = 0
power-cycle the Pill
```

A completely erased F103 won't give you our USB CDC device, which is expected. The important thing is that CubeProgrammer should stop reporting a running core lockup.

### If Under Reset still won't connect

Move:

```text
BOOT0 = 1
BOOT1 = 0
```

and power-cycle it. That causes an F103 to boot from its system memory instead of executing whatever is currently at `0x08000000`. Then try CubeProgrammer again using **Under Reset**, preferably with NRST connected.

Once you've erased it successfully:

```text
BOOT0 = 0
BOOT1 = 0
```

and flash v7 again.

### One distinction matters a lot

If CubeProgrammer says something like:

```text
Download verified successfully
...
Warning: The core is locked up
```

**only after it tries to start the program**, that's strong evidence that programming/SWD are working and **v7 is crashing immediately on this particular Pill**. That would also fit Windows subsequently reporting:

> Device Descriptor Request Failed

In that case I would *not* keep reflashing the same binary. We'd investigate why this replacement Blue Pill differs from the previous one—clock/crystal, actual MCU marking, board variant, etc.

If instead **CubeProgrammer can't even connect/read the chip and immediately reports core lockup**, then use the under-reset/BOOT0 recovery above first.

If you paste the **10–20 lines of CubeProgrammer log surrounding “core is locked up”**, I can tell which of those two cases you have.

```text
4. BOOT1 = 0

7. Test USB
```

```text
Bootloader flashed:
    programming verifies
    -> MCU starts
    -> "core locked up"

v7 flashed directly:
    ?
```

That direct-v7 test will tell us whether we're dealing with a **bad bootloader** or something genuinely wrong with this Pill.

The log gives us the cause. The bootloader you are flashing, `hid_generic_pc13.bin`, is **not built for the STM32F103C6 RAM size**.

CubeProgrammer successfully programs the 1.93 KB bootloader at `0x08000000`, so the flash operation itself is working. fileciteturn71file3L51-L60 The failure happens when CubeProgrammer tries to start it:

```text
PC  = 0x08000015
MSP = 0x20005000
```

and immediately afterward:

```text
Warning: failed to run application!
Warning: The core is kept under Reset!
Error: Start operation failed
```

fileciteturn71file1L9-L19

The critical value is:

```text
MSP = 0x20005000
```

An **STM32F103C6 has 10 KB of SRAM**:

```text
RAM start = 0x20000000
RAM size  = 0x00002800
RAM end   = 0x20002800
```

But this bootloader expects:

```text
stack top = 0x20005000
```

which corresponds to **20 KB RAM**—the normal STM32F103C8 configuration.

So as soon as the bootloader executes code that uses the stack, its stack pointer is way outside the C6's valid SRAM:

```text
C6 valid RAM:
0x20000000 ───────────── 0x20002800

Bootloader MSP:
                            0x20005000  <-- invalid
```

That very neatly explains both symptoms:

**bootloader starts → faults immediately → CubeProgrammer says it cannot run/core under reset → Windows sees USB attach behavior but descriptor request fails.**

So we should **not use `hid_generic_pc13.bin` on this C6**.

For our v7 sniffer you don't need a bootloader anyway. I would:

```text
1. Mass erase the Blue Pill.
2. BOOT0 = 0, BOOT1 = 0.
3. In Arduino select BluePill F103C6 (32K).
4. Upload v7 directly with STM32CubeProgrammer (SWD).
5. Disconnect ST-Link.
6. Power-cycle USB.
```

v7's USB CDC then runs directly from `0x08000000`.

If we actually want a HID bootloader later, I can modify/rebuild the bootloader specifically for the **F103C6**, with the SRAM stack top at `0x20002800` and an application offset that fits comfortably inside the C6's 32 KB flash.

This also means the replacement Pill probably **isn't damaged at all**. The log shows its SWD, flash erase, and programming operations are functioning; it's the C8-targeted bootloader that is crashing. fileciteturn71file1L9-L19

That points to **flash write-protection option bytes**, not a dead MCU. It would explain why CubeProgrammer can connect and read the F103C6 but refuses to erase some flash pages.

On the replacement Blue Pill, do this in **STM32CubeProgrammer**:

1. Connect by **SWD**.
2. Open the **Option Bytes** tab.
3. Look for **WRP / Write Protection**.
4. Disable/uncheck **all protected flash ranges**.
5. Also check **RDP / Read Out Protection** and make sure it is **Level 0 / not protected**.
6. Click **Apply** / **Program Option Bytes**.
7. Let the MCU reset, then reconnect.
8. Run **Full Chip Erase** again.

For the **STM32F103C6**, flash is only:

```text
0x08000000 - 0x08007FFF
32 KB total
```

and it uses 1 KB flash pages. So we don't need any protected region for what we're doing.

One important distinction:

```text
WRP enabled
    -> individual flash page groups cannot be erased/programmed

RDP enabled
    -> debugging/readout restrictions
    -> returning it to Level 0 normally causes a mass erase
```

If CubeProgrammer shows something like:

```text
WRP0: enabled
WRP1: enabled
...
```

or ranges such as:

```text
0x08000000 - 0x08000FFF protected
```

that's almost certainly the immediate issue.

I would **clear the protection before flashing anything else**. Once the Full Chip Erase completes successfully, flash v7 directly rather than reinstalling that `hid_generic_pc13.bin` bootloader, because the log showed that bootloader was built with a 20 KB-RAM stack configuration and is inappropriate for this F103C6.

If you send me a screenshot of the **CubeProgrammer Option Bytes page**, I can tell you exactly which boxes/values to change.

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-02T02:17:27.425044+00:00
Node: 36b0da82-2374-47d6-9c0f-da904668bbb1
Parent node: 836d3a0c-2f4b-4609-96fe-0d1ec1480f2a
========================================================================
Yes. We have drifted into debugging the Blue Pill instead of reverse-engineering the hoverboard controllers.

For the actual objective, I would **stop pursuing the UART MITM for now**. It gave us some useful protocol information, but it isn't giving us enough visibility into what the factory firmware is doing internally. The next phase should be **active SWD debugging of the original GD32F130C8T6 boards while their stock firmware is running**.

One terminology distinction matters: we cannot passively “sniff SWD” the way we sniff UART. SWDIO/SWCLK are normally quiet until a debugger initiates transactions. Instead, the ST-Link becomes an **active non-destructive probe into the running processor**. The GD32F1x0 has the Cortex-M3 CoreSight debug system, including SWD and trace/debug facilities; SWD is PA13/SWDIO and PA14/SWCLK. citeturn642386search20turn179977search26

### What SWD buys us

This is much closer to what we actually need. We can use it to:

- read the entire **8 KB SRAM** of the GD32F130C8 while the factory firmware is intact;
- inspect CPU registers and peripheral registers;
- set **hardware execution breakpoints** in flash without modifying flash;
- set **DWT data watchpoints** on RAM variables;
- single-step specific routines;
- determine **which instruction writes a variable**;
- correlate physical actions—tilt, wheel rotation, current, button presses—with RAM values and the code manipulating them.

The C8 has 64 KB flash and 8 KB SRAM, so its RAM range is essentially `0x20000000–0x20001FFF`. citeturn702945search0

And this fits the structure we've already recovered. Both factory images contain the complete local motor-control stack: six complementary PWM outputs, three Halls, ADC/current sensing, local MPU60x0 IMU processing, power latch, and UART. The major A/B differences are supervisory/communication logic rather than one board being merely a dumb slave. fileciteturn76file5L339-L369

That is exactly why converting **each board into an independent motor controller** is realistic.

### The key reverse-engineering technique

The most useful technique is going to be:

```text
physical action
    ↓
find RAM value that changes
    ↓
put DWT write-watchpoint on that RAM address
    ↓
run stock firmware
    ↓
processor halts at instruction that updates it
    ↓
we now know the function responsible
    ↓
label that function/variable in Ghidra
```

For example, suppose we take RAM snapshots:

```text
flat
tilted +10°
tilted -10°
wheel stopped
wheel forward
wheel backward
```

and discover that a signed 16-bit value at, say, `0x2000032A` follows board pitch.

We then put a hardware watchpoint on `0x2000032A`.

The next time the stock firmware updates pitch, the processor stops **at the actual pitch-processing code**. From there we inspect the call chain backwards to the MPU read and forwards to the control loop.

That is substantially better than trying to infer everything from anonymous disassembly.

### We already have excellent anchors

We are not starting blind.

The factory binaries already give us the critical hardware map:

| Function | Factory mapping |
|---|---|
| Hall sensors | PC13, PA1, PC14 |
| BLDC PWM high | PA8, PA9, PA10 |
| BLDC PWM complementary | PB13, PB14, PB15 |
| Board UART | PA2 / PA3 |
| IMU software I²C | PB6 / PB7 |
| IMU address | `0x68` |
| Power button | PA12 |
| Self-hold | PB12 |
| ADC inputs | PA0, PB0, PA6, PA4 |

fileciteturn76file0L18-L45

We also already have some excellent software breakpoints:

```text
0x08005C84   USART1 IRQ
0x08006E64   packet dispatcher
0x20000478   UART receive-buffer region
0x080023D8   interesting EXTI-class ISR
```

The first three came directly from the factory-image analysis. fileciteturn76file1L82-L120 The `0x080023D8` handler is particularly interesting because A and B differ there more than in many of the common low-level routines. fileciteturn76file4L283-L296

So one very easy first experiment answers the UART question definitively:

```text
hardware breakpoint @ 0x08005C84
run board normally
```

If it hits, USART1 is servicing something.

If it doesn't, we're no longer guessing from external wires—the processor itself tells us no USART1 interrupt occurred.

### Then move immediately to the motor and IMU paths

I would prioritize the stock firmware in this order:

```text
1. Startup / self-hold
2. TIMER0 setup
3. Motor-control timer ISR
4. Hall-state processing
5. ADC DMA/interrupt/current processing
6. IMU read routine
7. IMU attitude-processing routine
8. Motor demand / torque calculation
9. Protection / fault handling
10. Master/slave supervisory communications
```

Notice UART is now near the **bottom**, not the top.

The UART protocol only matters if we want to understand the stock two-board coordination. It is **not required** to understand how one board drives one motor.

### The IMU is especially attractive

We have firm static evidence that **both boards actually execute** an MPU60x0-style path: they bit-bang PB6/PB7, address `0x68`, read the standard 14-byte block beginning at `0x3B`, and configure the MPU registers. fileciteturn76file2L188-L208

So with SWD we can locate:

```text
raw accel XYZ
raw gyro XYZ
        ↓
filtered/scaled values
        ↓
pitch/roll estimate
        ↓
balance/control calculation
```

We don't need the final standalone firmware to retain the self-balancing controller. We can repurpose the same IMU for mower functions such as:

```text
roll / pitch
slope measurement
rollover / excessive-tilt shutdown
traction/slip information
acceleration detection
possibly vibration/impact detection
```

### The end firmware should not be a byte-for-byte reconstruction

I think we should reconstruct the factory code far enough to understand every motor-critical subsystem, then write a **clean standalone firmware** using that knowledge.

Something along these lines:

```text
              GD32F130 standalone motor controller
              ====================================

command input ───────────────┐
                            ↓
                       speed/torque demand
                            ↓
IMU ──> pitch/roll       limits/safety
                            ↓
Halls ───────────────> commutation
                            ↓
current ADC ──────────> current control
                            ↓
                       TIMER0 PWM
                            ↓
                     3-phase bridge
                            ↓
                          motor

battery ADC ───────> undervoltage/telemetry
temperature/etc ──> protection

PA2/PA3 ──────────> new command/telemetry interface
```

Then A and B could run essentially the **same source tree**, with only a small board configuration for things such as:

```text
motor direction
Hall sequence
phase/current calibration
IMU mounting orientation
ADC offsets
power/latch behaviour
```

The existing binaries already show that the low-level A/B hardware-control code is highly similar, with the major differences concentrated in startup/state/communications and a small board-specific tail. fileciteturn76file0L10-L16

One thing we must preserve during this work is each board's small factory-specific area around `0x08007C00`; A and B contain different data there. We should keep copies even if we later determine it is irrelevant calibration/configuration. fileciteturn76file4L285-L296

### One important SWD pin correction

The official GD32F130 LQFP48 pinout gives:

```text
physical pin 34 = PA13 / SWDIO
physical pin 37 = PA14 / SWCLK
physical pin 7  = NRST
```

citeturn179977search27

So if we previously traced one of the controller's SWD pads to **MCU pin 37**, that pad is **SWCLK**, not SWDIO. We should use continuity to positively label the stock-board header before this next round rather than relying on our old pad labels.

### What I would do next

Put the Blue Pill aside. Take **one original master board with its factory firmware untouched**, wheels safely unloaded, and connect the ST-Link directly to its SWD header:

```text
ST-Link GND   -> controller GND
ST-Link SWDIO -> confirmed PA13/pin 34 conductor
ST-Link SWCLK -> confirmed PA14/pin 37 conductor
ST-Link NRST  -> optional initially

NO ST-Link 3.3 V power connection
```

Power the controller normally.

Then we use **OpenOCD + GDB**, not CubeProgrammer, because CubeProgrammer is primarily a flash tool. OpenOCD/GDB gives us the hardware breakpoints, DWT watchpoints, live memory examination and register inspection that we need. OpenOCD supports Cortex-M debug targets and GDB access; existing GD32 users also use the GD32 Cortex-M SWD TAP with OpenOCD. citeturn332742search4turn332742search6

Our first session should be completely non-destructive: **no erase and no flash write**.

I would start by proving the debug connection, dump the complete 8 KB RAM, then put a hardware breakpoint at `0x08005C84`. After that, rather than spending more time on UART, we'll identify the **motor-control timer ISR and the MPU read routine** from the existing disassembly and start dynamically naming the factory firmware.

That gets us back on the path toward **reconstructing the stock motor/IMU control code and producing two independent standalone controllers**, which is the actual useful endpoint. memcite

Node: 72b6f74c-da44-4763-92d5-ab6c934bf9f8

========================================================================
Yes. The **optical switches belong on the reverse-engineering checklist**, and they are actually a good early SWD target because they give us an easy physical input that we can change while watching the processor.

For the Gen2.1.20 hardware definition that has matched this board very closely, the two photo inputs are:

```text
PHOTO_L = PC15
PHOTO_R = PA11
```

That comes directly from the current Gen2.1.20 definition. citeturn603912view0 We have **not yet proven from the factory binary that those are the optical switches on your exact board**, so our next SWD work should confirm it rather than assume it. Our previous static analysis specifically left the exact LED/photo-input roles unresolved. fileciteturn77file2L14-L21

The nice part is that with CubeMonitor we can test those two pins almost immediately. The GD32F130 GPIO input-status register is at offset `0x10`; GPIOA begins at `0x48000000` and GPIOC at `0x48000800`. citeturn347422search12turn188558search0 That gives:

```text
PA11:
GPIOA_ISTAT = 0x48000010
bit 11       = 0x00000800

PC15:
GPIOC_ISTAT = 0x48000810
bit 15       = 0x00008000
```

So in **STM32CubeMonitor**, assuming it will talk to the GD32 through your ST-Link, we don't even need an ELF with symbols yet. CubeMonitor supports adding a **custom variable by raw address and type**. citeturn730703search1turn730703search2 Add two unsigned 32-bit variables:

```text
name: GPIOA_ISTAT
address: 0x48000010
type: unsigned 32-bit

name: GPIOC_ISTAT
address: 0x48000810
type: unsigned 32-bit
```

Then operate/block each optical switch separately. We are looking for:

```text
PHOTO_R candidate:
GPIOA_ISTAT changes by 0x00000800

PHOTO_L candidate:
GPIOC_ISTAT changes by 0x00008000
```

That directly confirms the electrical pin mapping.

The **more important reverse-engineering step** comes immediately after that. Once we know, for example, that the optical switch really is PA11, we set a **read watchpoint on `0x48000010`** while running the stock firmware. A DWT watchpoint doesn't trigger because the external pin changes; it triggers when the **CPU reads that register**. When the factory program checks the photo sensor, the processor halts at the actual instruction doing the read:

```text
optical sensor changes
        ↓
factory code polls GPIOA_ISTAT
        ↓
DWT read-watchpoint trips
        ↓
halt at factory instruction
        ↓
PC tells us exactly which function uses PHOTO_R
```

Then we take that PC address back into our disassembly/Ghidra and label that routine. Do the same for PC15. That is exactly the sort of dynamic-to-static correlation we need.

The tools you downloaded are useful, but I would divide their jobs this way:

- **STM32CubeMonitor:** graph raw RAM/peripheral addresses while the controller runs. It can manually monitor an address even without source symbols. citeturn730703search1
- **STM32CubeIDE:** debugger front-end, register/memory views, disassembly, breakpoints. ST's IDE can use **OpenOCD as its GDB server**, including custom OpenOCD command/configuration options. citeturn887393search35
- **OpenOCD + GDB:** probably our most dependable low-level path because this is a **GD32**, not an STM32. ST officially describes its own ST-LINK GDB server in terms of STM32 devices, so I would not depend on every Cube feature recognizing the GD32 correctly. citeturn887393search33

And your plan for the sensors makes sense. Once we've identified them and documented their electrical interface, you can desolder them and reposition them as **short-range bumper/proximity detectors**. We can preserve their original GPIOs in the replacement firmware:

```text
PC15 -> left bumper
PA11 -> right bumper
```

or swap their semantics however physically convenient. The replacement firmware does not need to care that they were originally hoverboard foot/photo sensors.

I'd expand our reconstruction map to this:

```text
Factory subsystem                What we need to recover
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
TIMER0 / MOSFET PWM              commutation + motor drive
PC13 / PA1 / PC14                Hall sequence + wheel speed
PA0 / PB0                        phase-current sensing
PA4                              battery voltage
PA6                              unknown analog input
PB6 / PB7 + MPU @ 0x68           IMU + attitude processing
PC15 / PA11                      optical switches  ← ADD
PB12                             power/self-hold
PA12                             button
fault/protection logic           overcurrent/UV/etc.
PA2 / PA3                        UART, lower priority
```

The existing analysis already confirms the motor, Hall, ADC, IMU, button and latch mappings on both boards. fileciteturn76file0L18-L45

For the **first live-debug session**, I would actually start with the optical switches rather than UART or the motor loop. They're ideal for proving that our entire SWD workflow is working: monitor `0x48000010` and `0x48000810`, operate each switch, confirm the bits, then set a read watchpoint and catch the stock routine that consumes them. Once we've successfully done that once, we use exactly the same technique on the **Hall inputs, ADC variables and IMU processing**. That is the route to turning the disassembly into a substantially reconstructed source tree. memcite

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-02T02:33:58.114799+00:00
Node: 2ba03d7c-06f8-4a8b-911d-c176a13564d0
Parent node: f61400f0-21b7-4e38-b5de-28f62f1c7edc
========================================================================
For the reverse-engineering work, **power the controller normally from its battery side**. Do **not** try to power the hoverboard controller through the SWD header.

Use the SWD connection only for debug signals:

```text
ST-Link GND   -> controller logic GND
ST-Link SWDIO -> SWDIO
ST-Link SWCLK -> SWCLK
ST-Link NRST  -> NRST   (optional/useful)

ST-Link 3.3 V -> DO NOT use as board power
```

The reason is that the controller has several rails and power-state circuitry. Feeding 3.3 V into the MCU rail through the debug header can partially power the board through protection diodes/regulators and give us exactly the kind of strange backfeeding/fault behavior you saw earlier. It also would not reproduce normal startup, self-hold, ADC readings, gate-driver state, IMU power, etc.

For dynamic debugging, the best setup is:

```text
battery / current-limited bench supply
        ↓
hoverboard controller power input
        ↓
controller generates its own logic rails

ST-Link
  GND ───────── controller GND
  SWDIO ─────── SWDIO
  SWCLK ─────── SWCLK
```

If you're using a bench supply instead of the actual pack, supply the board at its normal battery-input voltage and use a conservative current limit initially. Wheels/motor should be unloaded while we're putting breakpoints into motor-control code.

One complication is the **slave board**. We already found evidence that its normal powered state depends on the four-wire master/slave harness, and that harness may carry a power/wake rail in addition to PA2/PA3 UART. So for the first SWD sessions:

- **Master:** power normally from the battery input.
- **Slave:** leave it connected to the master by the stock four-wire harness and power the system normally, at least until we identify exactly what the extra two harness conductors do.

That way we're observing the factory processor in its genuine operating state rather than a partially powered approximation.

If your ST-Link has a pin labelled `3.3V`, `VCC`, `VAPP`, or `VTref`, tell me exactly which ST-Link version/clone you have before connecting that pin. On some programmers it is a **target-voltage sense input**; on cheap clones it may actually be a **3.3 V output**, and those are not interchangeable.

That’s fine. You **do not need NRST** for the first live-debug work.

Use just:

```text
ST-Link GND   -> controller GND
ST-Link SWDIO -> SWDIO
ST-Link SWCLK -> SWCLK
```

and power the controller normally from the battery side.

As long as the GD32 is running normally and SWD hasn’t been disabled by firmware, CubeIDE/OpenOCD should be able to attach to the live core without reset control.

What you lose by not having NRST is mainly recovery convenience. You may have trouble attaching if:

- the firmware quickly reconfigures or disables the SWD pins;
- the core crashes immediately after boot;
- you need “connect under reset” behavior.

But for our purpose—**observing a normally running stock controller**—that is actually acceptable. We want to attach after the board has booted and initialized itself anyway.

For the first test, I’d do:

```text
1. Power controller normally.
2. Wait for it to finish booting.
3. Connect ST-Link USB.
4. Connect GND, SWDIO, SWCLK.
5. In CubeIDE/OpenOCD, attach to running target.
6. Do NOT erase or program anything.
7. Read CPU registers and RAM first.
```

If attaching works, we can immediately test whether we can:

```text
halt
read PC
read 0x20000000-0x20001FFF
resume
```

Then we can move on to the optical inputs, Hall states, ADC variables, and IMU path.

One thing I would avoid: **do not use CubeProgrammer’s default “download/run” workflow** for this stage. We are not programming; we are using the ST-Link as a debugger against factory firmware.

So the lack of NRST is not a blocker for the reverse-engineering plan.

These logs are actually useful. They show that the **SWD pin identification is correct and the ST-Link can talk to the GD32**, but the connection is unstable.

The strongest evidence is this section:

```text
Voltage      : 3.18 V
SWD freq     : 4000 KHz
Connect mode : Normal
Device ID    : 0x410
```

fileciteturn78file0L27-L37

Later, it even successfully reads 1024 bytes from `0x08000000`:

```text
Address : 0x08000000
Data read successfully
```

before losing the debug connection a few seconds later. fileciteturn78file0L94-L120

So we are **not fighting an incorrect SWD pinout anymore**. The problem is maintaining the SWD session.

The first change I would make is the SWD clock. **4 MHz is unnecessarily aggressive** for flying wires onto this board, especially with the controller powered normally and potentially switching peripherals. Set it to:

```text
100 kHz
```

for the first experiments. Once stable, try:

```text
400 kHz
1 MHz
```

There is no advantage to running 4 MHz while we're reverse engineering.

Also, because you do **not have NRST connected**, don't use:

```text
Under Reset
Hardware Reset
```

Your first attempts were configured that way and failed to get the core ID. fileciteturn78file0L1-L22

For what we're doing, use something equivalent to:

```text
Connect mode: Hot Plug / Normal
Reset:        None
SWD:          100 kHz
```

We specifically want to attach to the already-running factory firmware without resetting it.

### The second log is a different thing

This:

```text
Device Name: STM32F101/F102/F103 Medium-density
>>>>>> Reception Started <<<<<<
>>>>>> Reception Ended <<<<<<
```

fileciteturn78file1L1-L8

looks like the **SWV/trace reception console**.

The empty reception is expected.

SWD and SWV are different:

```text
SWDIO + SWCLK
    ↓
active debugger access
    ↓
read RAM/registers
halt/resume
breakpoints/watchpoints


SWO
    ↓
one-way trace output
    ↓
only works if target firmware
actually configures ITM/SWO
```

Our factory firmware almost certainly isn't intentionally printing debugging information through ITM/SWO, and the SWD header doesn't appear to give us an exposed SWO line anyway.

So **don't use “Reception Started” as our sniffer**. It isn't going to magically show what the processor is doing.

What we actually want is the debugger actively reading the running processor over SWD.

### CubeMonitor is useful for exactly that

Once we have a stable 100–400 kHz connection, CubeMonitor can repeatedly read raw addresses while the stock firmware continues running.

The first experiment I'd do is the optical switches because they're easy to manipulate physically.

Our Gen2.1.20 reference gives the candidates:

```text
PHOTO_R = PA11
PHOTO_L = PC15
```

So instead of looking for serial output, monitor the GPIO input registers:

```text
GPIOA ISTAT = 0x48000010
GPIOC ISTAT = 0x48000810
```

and watch:

```text
PA11 = bit 11 = 0x00000800
PC15 = bit 15 = 0x00008000
```

Operate one optical switch at a time.

If PA11 really is one of them, the value at:

```text
0x48000010
```

should toggle by `0x00000800`.

If PC15 is the other:

```text
0x48000810
```

should toggle by `0x00008000`.

That would give us our first **live confirmation of a factory peripheral mapping**.

### Then we start watching RAM

Once the connection is stable, I'd make our first complete SRAM capture:

```text
0x20000000
through
0x20001FFF
```

because the GD32F130C8 has 8 KB RAM.

Take several snapshots:

```text
A: board sitting flat
B: tilted forward
C: tilted backward
D: wheel rotated
E: optical sensor 1 blocked
F: optical sensor 2 blocked
```

Then we can diff those dumps.

That will immediately reveal candidate variables for:

```text
raw IMU data
filtered pitch
wheel/Hall position
motor speed
optical sensor state
ADC/current values
controller state
```

Once we have an address that clearly tracks something physical, we set a **data watchpoint** on it in CubeIDE/OpenOCD and catch the factory code accessing it.

That's how we turn this:

```text
anonymous binary
```

into:

```c
imu_read();
update_pitch();
read_halls();
calculate_speed();
read_current();
read_bumper_left();
read_bumper_right();
motor_control();
```

piece by piece.

### What your logs tell us right now

We have already crossed an important threshold:

```text
ST-Link
   ↓
SWD
   ↓
GD32 acknowledges
   ↓
Device ID 0x410
   ↓
flash memory successfully readable
```

So the next task isn't wiring or UART anymore. It's simply getting that connection **stable at a much lower SWD clock**.

I would set **100 kHz + Normal/Hot Plug + no reset**, attach to the powered master board, and try reading `0x20000000` for `0x2000` bytes several times in succession. If it can do that repeatedly without dropping, we're ready to start live reconstruction. memcite

Yes. At this point, **flashing Gen2.1.20 onto one spare controller is a sensible test**, and it may save us a lot of reverse-engineering time.

We have already confirmed that your factory firmware and the Gen2.1.20 definition agree on the critical hardware: Hall inputs, all six BLDC gate-drive pins, PA2/PA3 UART, PB6/PB7 IMU, button, self-hold, battery ADC, and phase-current ADC. fileciteturn76file0L18-L45 The current Gen2.1.20 definition also contains the optical inputs we want, `PC15` and `PA11`. citeturn271719view0

There is one configuration detail I can now resolve definitively: in the **current GD32 repository**, `TARGET=1` means GD32F130, and the build system includes `defines_2-TARGET-LAYOUT.h`. Therefore for **Gen2.1.20**, we want:

```c
#define TARGET 1       // GD32F130
#define LAYOUT 20      // -> defines_2-1-20.h
```

The current repository's stock `config.h` happens to say `LAYOUT 1`; that would select `defines_2-1-1.h`, **not our board**. The include mechanism confirms this. citeturn554482view0

For the first test I would configure it as a **single board**, not master/slave:

```c
//#define MASTER
//#define SLAVE
#define SINGLE
```

That is already a supported mode in the current code. citeturn522469search0

However, **do not flash the current config unchanged**, because it currently enables:

```c
#define REMOTE_DUMMY
```

and the repository explicitly describes that mode as generating a repeating motor command; it is intended for testing and can make the motor move automatically. citeturn522469search0

For our first boot, I want a deliberately boring diagnostic firmware:

```c
#define LAYOUT 20
#define SINGLE

#define BLDC_SINE
#define DRIVING_MODE 0
#define BAT_CELLS 10

//#define REMOTE_DUMMY
//#define REMOTE_UART
//#define REMOTE_ADC

#define TEST_HALL2LED
#define RTT_REMOTE
```

We may need a very small source adjustment if the project insists on having a `REMOTE_*` implementation, but I'd rather do that than let `REMOTE_DUMMY` command the wheel on the first boot.

The first test should establish only:

1. MCU boots.
2. `PB12` self-hold works.
3. buzzer/LED outputs behave.
4. battery ADC gives a sensible voltage.
5. Hall inputs respond when the wheel is rotated manually.
6. MPU at `0x68` initializes and produces data.
7. `PA11/PC15` optical inputs respond.
8. PWM outputs remain at zero until we intentionally command them.

The repo itself recommends first testing unfamiliar layouts from a **1–2 A current-limited supply**, specifically because a wrong BLDC pin assignment can damage MOSFETs. citeturn317677search0turn317677search2 In our case the risk is substantially reduced because we independently recovered the same six PWM pins from your factory binary, but I'd still use current limiting for the first power-up.

Most importantly, before flashing the test board, preserve:

```text
full 64 KiB factory flash dump
option bytes
small board-specific block near 0x08007C00
```

We already have the factory dumps, and A/B contain different data around that final configuration area, so I would keep the test board's copy explicitly associated with that physical controller. fileciteturn76file4L285-L296

If Gen2.1.20 boots and correctly sees the **motor Halls + current sensing + IMU + optical sensors** on one of your boards, our project changes substantially. Instead of reconstructing the entire factory firmware, we can use Gen2.1.20 as the functioning base and concentrate on modifying it into exactly what we want:

```text
one board = one independent motor controller
          + wheel Hall odometry
          + current sensing
          + battery sensing
          + IMU
          + left/right optical bumper inputs
          + new RC/Pi command interface
```

That would be a much shorter path.

If you want, the next thing I can do is go through the **current Gen2.x-GD32 source** and give you the exact `config.h` changes for a **safe Gen2.1.20 first-flash build with motor command disabled but all sensing/RTT diagnostics enabled**. memcite
