I worked through the **current Gen2.x-GD32 source**, rather than relying on the older Gen2.1.20 notes. For the first flash, I would make a dedicated **sensor bring-up build**: Gen2.1.20 hardware, single-board mode, factory self-hold/button/IMU/Halls/photo inputs active, RTT available, but with the motor bridge forcibly disabled in software.

The important finding is that the current repo defaults to `LAYOUT 1`, while its include logic constructs `defines_2-TARGET-LAYOUT.h`. `TARGET 1` is GD32F130, so your board needs **`LAYOUT 20` → `defines_2-1-20.h`**. citeturn216396search0turn710072view0 The current 2.1.20 definition contains the same critical pins we independently found in the factory firmware, including Halls, six BLDC outputs, PB12 self-hold, PA12 button, PB6/PB7 IMU and PA11/PC15 photo inputs. citeturn710072view1 fileciteturn76file0L18-L45

### 1. `Inc/config.h`

For the GD32F130 section, change:

```c
#ifdef GD32F130 // TARGET = 1

#define LAYOUT 1
#define LAYOUT_SUB 1
```

to:

```c
#ifdef GD32F130 // TARGET = 1

#define LAYOUT 20
//#define LAYOUT_SUB 1
```

Then use this configuration for the first board:

```c
//#define BLDC_BC
#define BLDC_SINE

#define DRIVING_MODE 0
#define BAT_CELLS 10

//#define MASTER
//#define SLAVE
#define SINGLE

#if defined(MASTER) || defined(SINGLE)

#define REMOTE_DUMMY

#ifdef REMOTE_DUMMY
    #define REMOTE_PERIOD 6
    #define TEST_HALL2LED
    #define RTT_REMOTE
    #define WINDOWS_RN
#endif

#define SEND_IMU_DATA

#define SPEED_COEFFICIENT -1
#define STEER_COEFFICIENT 1

#define MASTER_OR_SINGLE

#endif

#define DC_CUR_LIMIT 15

// Our temporary first-flash protection:
#define SAFE_BRINGUP
```

Keep `REMOTE_DUMMY` defined for now. The program expects a `RemoteUpdate()` implementation, and dummy mode conveniently gives us RTT and Hall diagnostics. **But we must neutralize its motor command**, because the stock dummy code deliberately generates a back-and-forth speed command. The repo currently sets dummy PWM to 200 and generates that repeating command. citeturn483009view0

Also, `SEND_IMU_DATA` is useful even though we're not sending IMU data over UART: in the current `main.c`, that define is what causes `MPU_ReadAll()` to be called periodically in the default pilot. citeturn275694view1

### 2. `Src/remoteDummy.c`

Find this section:

```c
steer = 0;

speed = CLAMP((fRemoteScaleMax*iRemoteMax/100) *
    (ABS((int)(((msTicks-msTicksInit)/iRemotePeriod+250) % 1000) - 500) - 250),
    -iRemoteMax, iRemoteMax);

speed = CLAMP(speed, -iRemoteMax, iRemoteMax);

//speed = 300;
//speed = 0; // uncomment this to turn the motor manually when TEST_HALL2LED
//speed = 1.5*1024;
```

Change it to:

```c
steer = 0;

speed = CLAMP((fRemoteScaleMax*iRemoteMax/100) *
    (ABS((int)(((msTicks-msTicksInit)/iRemotePeriod+250) % 1000) - 500) - 250),
    -iRemoteMax, iRemoteMax);

speed = CLAMP(speed, -iRemoteMax, iRemoteMax);

/* SAFE FIRST-BRINGUP:
   Never command motor torque. */
#ifdef SAFE_BRINGUP
    speed = 0;
#endif
```

Interesting detail: the repository itself already contains the commented `speed = 0` line specifically for manually turning the motor while using `TEST_HALL2LED`. citeturn483009view0

I would also fix a small problem in the RTT logger while we're here. It currently does:

```c
if (iCounterLog%10==0)
{
    PIDInit* pPID = &aoPIDInit[iDrivingMode-1];
```

With:

```c
DRIVING_MODE 0
```

that indexes `aoPIDInit[-1]`. Change it to:

```c
if ((iCounterLog % 10 == 0) && (iDrivingMode > 0))
{
    PIDInit* pPID = &aoPIDInit[iDrivingMode-1];
```

The out-of-range expression is present in the current source, so I don't want it in our diagnostic build. citeturn865975view0

### 3. `Src/main.c` — hard-disable the bridge

This is the most important part.

Near the bottom of the main loop, the current source eventually does:

```c
if (bPilotTimeout || bRemoteTimeout || (wState & STATE_Disable))
    enable = RESET;

// Enable channel output
SetEnable(enable);
```

citeturn275694view2

Change that to:

```c
if (bPilotTimeout || bRemoteTimeout || (wState & STATE_Disable))
    enable = RESET;

#ifdef SAFE_BRINGUP
    /* Independent second lockout:
       even if speed/remote logic goes wrong,
       do not enable the BLDC bridge. */
    SetBldcInput(0);
    enable = RESET;
#endif

SetEnable(enable);
```

So the first build has **two independent software interlocks**:

```text
REMOTE_DUMMY speed = 0
            +
BLDC enable = RESET
```

The first one prevents a command. The second prevents the bridge from being enabled even if something elsewhere manages to produce a nonzero command.

The firmware will still initialize TIMER0/PWM during boot, but the BLDC control code maintains a separate `bldc_enable` state and has logic for disabling the timer outputs when it isn't enabled. citeturn275694view3

### 4. Add explicit optical-switch diagnostics

Since the photo sensors are one of the things we specifically want to verify, I would add two simple globals to `main.c`, somewhere with the other globals:

```c
volatile uint8_t diagPhotoL = 0xFF;
volatile uint8_t diagPhotoR = 0xFF;
```

Then inside the main `while(1)` loop, shortly after `steerCounter++`, add:

```c
#ifdef PHOTO_L
    diagPhotoL = digitalRead(PHOTO_L);
#endif

#ifdef PHOTO_R
    diagPhotoR = digitalRead(PHOTO_R);
#endif
```

With layout 20 those compile as:

```text
diagPhotoL -> PC15
diagPhotoR -> PA11
```

because those are explicitly defined by the current Gen2.1.20 file. citeturn710072view1

That makes them trivial to watch in CubeMonitor by symbol instead of figuring out raw GPIO register bits.

### What you should be able to monitor

Once we build with debug symbols, these are the variables I'd put in CubeMonitor:

| Symbol | What it tells us |
|---|---|
| `diagPhotoL` | PC15 optical switch |
| `diagPhotoR` | PA11 optical switch |
| `hall` | 3-bit Hall state |
| `realSpeed` | wheel speed calculated from Halls |
| `iOdom` | Hall-based wheel position/odometry |
| `batteryVoltage` | PA4 battery measurement |
| `mpuStatus` | whether IMU initialized successfully |
| `mpuData.accel.x/y/z` | accelerometer |
| `mpuData.gyro.x/y/z` | gyro |
| `mpuData.temperature` | MPU temperature |
| `speed` | should remain **0** |
| `iBldcInput` | should remain **0** |
| `bldc_enable` | should remain **RESET/0** |

The IMU driver has a global `MPU_Data mpuData`, and `MPU_ReadAll()` populates all six accelerometer/gyro axes plus temperature from the sensor at address `0x68`. citeturn462238view0 The normal startup sequence initializes GPIO, asserts PB12 self-hold, probes I²C, initializes the MPU, initializes ADC and finally the BLDC subsystem. citeturn275694view0

There is one limitation I found that is important for where we're ultimately going: **the current Gen2.1.20 source defines `PHASE_A = PB0` and `PHASE_B = PA0`, but the generic firmware does not currently appear to use those as its DC-current measurement.** `CURRENT_DC` is explicitly undefined for layout 20 because this board has no single DC-bus shunt. citeturn710072view1 The generic BLDC source even initializes `currentDC` to `0.42` when no real DC-current pin exists, so **ignore the RTT “A” current reading on this first build**. citeturn275694view3

That is something we'll fix later. The factory firmware definitely configures PA0/PB0 as ADC inputs, so when we get to actual motor control I want to port the **two phase-current channels into the open firmware** rather than giving up that hardware capability. fileciteturn76file0L37-L45

### First-flash test sequence

I would do the first test in this order:

1. Build for **GD32F130 / Target 1 / Layout 20 / SINGLE** with the changes above.
2. Keep the motor/wheel unloaded and use the lowest practical current-limited battery-voltage supply for the first power-up. The repo itself recommends roughly 1–2 A current limiting for unverified layouts. citeturn216396search0
3. Flash the test controller at `0x08000000` through SWD. Given the marginal connection we saw at 4 MHz, I'd program at **100–400 kHz SWD**.
4. Power-cycle it normally.
5. It should assert PB12 self-hold and play the startup melody. PB12 and PA12 are both independently confirmed in your factory binary, so those are good immediate checks. fileciteturn76file0L32-L35
6. The motor should have **no commanded torque**. Turn it manually and verify the Hall LEDs / `hall`, `realSpeed`, and `iOdom`.
7. Block each optical sensor independently and watch `diagPhotoL` and `diagPhotoR`.
8. Tilt/rotate the board and verify all six `mpuData` motion channels move sensibly.
9. Verify `batteryVoltage` against your meter.

If those all work, we've proven essentially all of the hardware we need **without energizing the motor bridge**.

At that point I would make the next build specifically for motor validation: keep a hard PWM ceiling around 5–10%, add the missing PA0/PB0 phase-current acquisition, log the six Hall states and phase sequence, and only then briefly enable commutation. That will tell us whether Gen2.1.20's existing Hall-to-phase table is correct for these exact motors before we allow meaningful torque.

Also, the current repository is primarily a **Keil project**, even though you've installed CubeIDE; CubeIDE/CubeMonitor remain useful for SWD debugging and monitoring. citeturn216396search2 If you have the repository downloaded, I can next give you the exact file paths and make these changes against a copy of the current source so we end up with one clean **`SAFE_BRINGUP` patch** rather than editing it piecemeal. memcite

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-02T19:39:08.905708+00:00
Node: c78ee8da-6c8d-4883-83a9-8edf76e722c8
Parent node: 1a146d84-4bac-46c3-92b6-fadaf878e720
========================================================================
Yes — **if you mean “take EFeru’s FOC firmware and adapt it to your specific GD32F130 split board,” I think that is a better long-term direction for your mower than building on the ordinary Gen2.x sine/commutation firmware.**

There is an important distinction, though: the exact URL you pasted, `EFeru/hoverboard-firmware-hack-FOCmodified`, does not exist as a repository. I checked it. I assume you meant **EFeru/hoverboard-firmware-hack-FOC, modified for your board**.

More importantly, I found something that changes the plan substantially:

**There is already a project specifically attempting to port EFeru's FOC to GD32F130 split hoverboard boards:**

`matixan/Hoverboard-hack-FOC-for-split-boards`

That project is based on the Gen2 split-board firmware and incorporates EFeru's FOC controller. Its own description explicitly says it is for split boards, uses the GD32F130 family, and already has sinusoidal operation, motor sensing, ADC phase-current measurements, CLI, power control, and LED support working. fileciteturn90file0

So I would change our approach.

### Why this is a better foundation

EFeru's original FOC project is aimed at the older **single central mainboard** architecture, typically STM32F103RCT6 or GD32F103RCT6. It drives two motors from that one MCU. fileciteturn84file0

Your G5 is fundamentally different:

```text
Your Gyroor G5

 GD32F130C8                GD32F130C8
 ┌──────────────┐ UART ┌──────────────┐
 │ controller A │<---->│ controller B │
 │ one motor    │      │ one motor    │
 │ one inverter │      │ one inverter │
 │ 2 phase      │      │ 2 phase      │
 │ shunts       │      │ shunts       │
 └──────────────┘      └──────────────┘
```

The `matixan` fork has already done the architectural surgery needed to make EFeru's controller operate as **one motor/one inverter per GD32F130**. It even has explicit build definitions for both GD32F130C6 and C8 at 72 MHz. fileciteturn91file2 fileciteturn91file3

That saves us a significant amount of work.

### And parts of its hardware abstraction are already extremely close to your board

I compared its `Hover1_defines.h` against the pin map we've established for your `OL20180703-V4.1`.

The **entire six-MOSFET PWM mapping matches your board**:

| Function | FOC split-board fork | Your board |
|---|---:|---:|
| Green high | PA10 | PA10 |
| Green low | PB15 | PB15 |
| Blue high | PA9 | PA9 |
| Blue low | PB14 | PB14 |
| Yellow high | PA8 | PA8 |
| Yellow low | PB13 | PB13 |
| PWM timer | TIMER0 | TIMER0 |
| Master/slave TX | PA2 | PA2 |
| Master/slave RX | PA3 | PA3 |
| Battery ADC | PA4 | PA4 |

Those definitions are directly visible in that fork. fileciteturn98file0

And our current Gen2.1.20 definition for your board independently gives:

```c
#define HALL_A PC13
#define HALL_B PA1
#define HALL_C PC14

#define VBATT PA4

#define PHASE_A PB0
#define PHASE_B PA0

#define SELF_HOLD PB12
#define BUTTON PA12

#define BLDC_GH PA10
#define BLDC_GL PB15
#define BLDC_BH PA9
#define BLDC_BL PB14
#define BLDC_YH PA8
#define BLDC_YL PB13

#define USART1_TX PA2
#define USART1_RX PA3
```

fileciteturn97file0

So this isn't a case of trying to shoehorn totally foreign FOC code onto your hardware. **The actual timer/inverter arrangement it expects is already basically the same.**

### What we'd have to change

This is a much smaller and more concrete porting job than adapting original EFeru from scratch.

The main changes would be:

1. **Create a `Gyroor_G5_OL20180703_V4_1_defines.h`.**

   We'd take the exact known mappings from RoboDurden layout 2-1-20 rather than guessing.

2. Change its Hall A from whatever board variant it currently uses to:

```c
HALL_A = PC13
HALL_B = PA1
HALL_C = PC14
```

3. Set the two phase-current inputs to:

```c
PHASE_A = PB0
PHASE_B = PA0
```

4. Change self-hold and button to:

```c
SELF_HOLD = PB12
BUTTON    = PA12
```

5. Add your LEDs, photo sensors and MPU6050 wiring if we want them.

That part is straightforward.

There is, however, **one substantial difference we have to deal with**.

### Your board does not have a DC-link shunt

The FOC split-board fork assumes:

```c
current_dc
phase_a
phase_b
```

in its ADC buffer. fileciteturn99file0

Its controller derives:

```c
curL_phaA
curL_phaB
curL_DC
```

and feeds all three to the FOC model. fileciteturn101file0

It also has a second current-protection stage that shuts TIMER0 off when:

```c
ABS(curL_DC) > curDC_max
```

fileciteturn101file0

Your board instead has:

```text
Phase A current shunt → PB0
Phase B current shunt → PA0
Phase C → mathematically reconstructed
DC bus shunt → NONE
```

That is **not a problem for FOC itself**.

Two phase-current measurements are sufficient because:

\[
I_A+I_B+I_C=0
\]

so:

\[
I_C=-(I_A+I_B)
\]

That is a completely normal FOC topology.

What we would do is remove the nonexistent DC-current ADC channel from this fork and rely initially on the FOC model's **phase-current limiting**.

Later, if we want a DC-link current estimate, we can calculate it from phase currents and switching duty rather than pretending there is a physical shunt.

### In fact, this firmware already does the important part we need

Look at what it feeds into the EFeru controller:

```c
rtU_Left.b_hallA  = ...
rtU_Left.b_hallB  = ...
rtU_Left.b_hallC  = ...

rtU_Left.i_phaAB  = curL_phaA;
rtU_Left.i_phaBC  = curL_phaB;

BLDC_controller_step(rtM_Left);
```

Then the FOC controller produces the three phase duty commands:

```c
ul = rtY_Left.DC_phaA;
vl = rtY_Left.DC_phaB;
wl = rtY_Left.DC_phaC;
```

which go straight to TIMER0 PWM. fileciteturn101file0

That is almost exactly the control chain we would have eventually needed to build ourselves.

### The big caveat

I would **not flash this fork unchanged**.

Its README literally says:

> `FOC not tested yet`

and lists upgrading the FOC drivers and testing master/slave communication as unfinished work. fileciteturn90file0

Its most recent commit I found is from April 2023.

It currently defaults to:

```c
#define CTRL_TYP_SEL SIN_CTRL
```

rather than FOC, even though the FOC controller code is present. fileciteturn96file0

So I would regard it as an **excellent porting scaffold**, not finished firmware.

### I think our best architecture is now this

Rather than choosing between RoboDurden and EFeru wholesale, I'd combine the best parts:

```text
RoboDurden Gen2.x-GD32
        │
        ├── exact GD32F130 support
        ├── exact 2-1-20 G5 hardware map
        ├── IMU
        ├── LEDs / photo sensors
        ├── self-hold
        ├── current modern board support
        │
        ▼
 Gyroor-specific HAL
        │
        ▼
EFeru FOC motor-control core
        ▲
        │
matixan split-board port
        │
        ├── GD32F130 adaptation already done
        ├── one motor per MCU
        ├── ADC phase currents
        ├── TIMER0 FOC PWM
        └── split-board architecture
```

That is much more attractive than taking Gen2.x's existing sine controller and trying to improve it later.

For an RC mower in particular, EFeru FOC gives us exactly the things I eventually want for your traction system: **true current/torque control, strong controlled torque at low speed, smoother differential steering, current limiting based on motor current rather than just requested PWM, regenerative/electric braking options, and closed-loop speed mode if we want it.** The original EFeru firmware explicitly provides voltage, speed, and torque FOC modes and motor-current limiting. fileciteturn84file0

So yes: **I would switch direction now, before we invest more effort into the previous Gen2.x bring-up build.**

I would not port straight from EFeru's original repo. I would start from **`matixan/Hoverboard-hack-FOC-for-split-boards`**, replace its hardware layer with our now-well-established **Gyroor G5 / OL20180703-V4.1 pinout**, remove its assumption of a DC-link current shunt, and then compare its 2023 FOC controller files against the current EFeru versions to see which improvements should be brought forward.

That gives us a much cleaner path to the firmware we actually want rather than first making the simpler firmware work and then replacing its motor controller later.

i thought this was a 3-phase bldc essentially the motor power is three wire

========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-09-02T19:43:22.881127+00:00
Node: 4e7ac030-e335-41a0-82f5-e8c52f2d1317
Parent node: a2422b1f-2df9-44b6-8bf0-cd03b52ea5b4
========================================================================
Yes — exactly. Your hoverboard motor is a **3-phase BLDC/PMSM motor with three power wires**. My reference to “two phase-current channels” was about the **current sensors**, not the number of motor phases.

The power stage is essentially:

```text
                 Battery +
                    │
       ┌────────────┼────────────┐
       │            │            │
     High A       High B       High C
       │            │            │
       ├── Phase A  ├── Phase B  ├── Phase C
       │            │            │
     Low A        Low B        Low C
       │            │            │
       └────────────┴────────────┘
                    │
                 Battery -
```

So there are **six power MOSFET switches**, arranged as three half-bridges:

```text
Phase A: PA10 / PB15
Phase B: PA9  / PB14
Phase C: PA8  / PB13
```

and those produce the **three motor wires**.

### Why only two current sensors?

On your board, we've identified two low-side current-shunt channels:

```text
PB0 → phase-current measurement
PA0 → phase-current measurement
```

but there are still **three motor phases**.

For a three-wire motor with no neutral connection accessible:

\[
I_A + I_B + I_C = 0
\]

therefore measuring any two currents gives the third:

\[
I_C = -(I_A + I_B)
\]

So an FOC controller only needs **two independent phase-current measurements**.

For example:

```text
measured:
IA = +4.2 A
IB = -1.7 A

calculated:
IC = -(4.2 - 1.7)
IC = -2.5 A
```

and:

```text
4.2 + (-1.7) + (-2.5) = 0
```

### That actually makes the EFeru FOC approach more appropriate

FOC fundamentally wants to know the three-phase current vector. Two physical current sensors are sufficient to reconstruct it:

```text
IA ─────────────┐
                │
IB ─────────────┼──> reconstruct IA, IB, IC
                │
IC = -(IA + IB)─┘
                       ↓
                 Clarke transform
                       ↓
                 α / β currents
                       ↓
                  Park transform
                       ↓
                    Id / Iq
                       ↓
              torque / flux control
```

`Iq` is essentially the torque-producing component of motor current, while `Id` is the flux-axis component.

So for a mower drive, this potentially gives us much better control than simply saying:

```text
motor = 35% PWM
```

We can eventually say something much closer to:

```text
left motor  = +8 A torque command
right motor = +5 A torque command
```

and let the FOC controller determine the appropriate phase PWM.

### The DC current sensor is a separate thing

This is where my previous answer could have been clearer.

There are two different quantities:

```text
PHASE currents:
IA, IB, IC
    ↑
three motor wires

DC-link current:
IBAT
    ↑
battery feeding inverter
```

Some hoverboard controllers have a separate shunt here:

```text
Battery
   │
DC-link shunt
   │
3-phase inverter
   │
motor
```

Your Gen2.1.20 hardware definition indicates **no separate DC-bus shunt**, but it does have two phase-current shunts. That is completely compatible with a three-phase FOC motor controller. The current layout definition explicitly describes PB0 and PA0 as two low-side phase-current shunts and says there is no third phase shunt or DC-bus shunt. fileciteturn97file0

In fact, that's one reason the FOC code has a `pwm_margin`: with low-side shunts, there are portions of the PWM cycle where a particular phase current can't be measured reliably. The controller has to sample the ADC during a valid switching window.

So yes: **three motor wires, three driven phases, six MOSFET switches, two physical current measurements.** Nothing inconsistent there.

And that makes adapting the FOC split-board project to your exact board look **more promising**, not less.

Yes — and that connection is more important than I initially gave it credit for.

The EFeru FOC documentation explicitly recognizes **GD32F130C8/C6 split boards** and directs them to the Gen2 firmware family. citeturn972224view0 The older Gen2 documentation even uses an `f` suffix to describe some split-board hardware as **“FOC capable.”** citeturn853001view0

The distinction is between **FOC-capable hardware** and **FOC currently implemented in the Gen2 firmware**. The current RoboDurden Gen2.x-GD32 source only has:

```text
BLDC_BC    -> block commutation
BLDC_SINE  -> sinusoidal commutation
```

and its current architecture documentation explicitly describes `BldcFOC.c` as a **future motor-control module**, including exactly how it should plug into the existing BLDC abstraction. fileciteturn108file0

That is actually good news for our board. Your Gen2.1.20 definition already contains exactly the hardware FOC needs:

```c
#define PHASE_A PB0
#define PHASE_B PA0

#define HALL_A PC13
#define HALL_B PA1
#define HALL_C PC14

#define BLDC_GH PA10
#define BLDC_GL PB15
#define BLDC_BH PA9
#define BLDC_BL PB14
#define BLDC_YH PA8
#define BLDC_YL PB13
```

and specifically documents the two phase-current shunts and the absence of a separate DC-bus shunt. fileciteturn97file0

So I would refine our direction again:

**Use the current Gen2.x-GD32/Gen2.1.20 code as the base**, because it already knows your exact board layout, GD32F130, MPU, optical sensors, self-hold, UART, LEDs, Halls, ADCs, etc. Then implement `BLDC_FOC` using the **current EFeru FOC controller core**, with the old `matixan` split-board FOC project as a reference showing how the EFeru controller was previously adapted to one-motor-per-GD32F130 operation.

That is cleaner than adopting the 2023 `matixan` fork wholesale.

Conceptually we'd end up with:

```text
Current Gen2.1.20
│
├── exact OL20180703-V4.1 hardware
├── GD32F130 support
├── MPU6050
├── PHOTO_L / PHOTO_R
├── halls
├── phase-current ADC
├── battery ADC
├── UART
├── self hold / button
│
└── BLDC selector
      ├── BLDC_BC
      ├── BLDC_SINE
      └── BLDC_FOC   ← we add this
            │
            └── EFeru FOC controller
```

And the current Gen2 architecture is already deliberately structured for exactly that addition: `InitBldc()` and `bldc_get_pwm()` are the abstraction points a future FOC module is expected to implement. fileciteturn108file0

So I think **this is now the strongest route**: first prove untouched Gen2.1.20 works on one spare board in safe sine mode, then add EFeru FOC to that same codebase. We retain all the board-specific features we're interested in instead of having to transplant the IMU and optical-switch support back into an older FOC fork.

USER | 2026-09-02T20:04:13.200000+00:00

there is some mention of using another imu in some of the boards in th gen2.1.20 issues section we should leave the option in the code for the alternate chip

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-02T20:05:12.085984+00:00
Node: 490add97-fa49-48c3-871d-52b033df14d3
Parent node: 2ec9b888-ab9b-435a-915f-ac5cc56dc9eb
========================================================================
message_reaction👍Yes. We should deliberately preserve **multiple IMU-driver paths** rather than baking your board to one MPU6050 implementation.

The Gen2.1.20 layout already reflects this uncertainty. Its current definition has:

```c
//#define MPU_6050      // Gemini tried for clones..
#define MPU_6050old
#define I2C_PB6PB7
```

So the project maintainers themselves are treating the IMU as potentially one of two MPU6050-compatible implementations. fileciteturn97file0

Issue #90 goes further. One contributor photographed an IMU marked `16A 3C6A C3C`; another Gen2.1 board fork concluded that the part was probably **not a genuine MPU-6050 but some compatible clone**. Basic register access worked, but uploading the MPU6050 DMP firmware did not, so quaternion/DMP operation was disabled. fileciteturn115file0 RoboDurden also specifically asked for testing of `MPU_6050old` versus the newer `MPU_6050` clone path on 2.1.20 boards. fileciteturn112file2

For our firmware, I would make the IMU interface look something like:

```c
#define IMU_MPU6050_OLD
// #define IMU_MPU6050_COMPAT
// #define IMU_OTHER
```

with a common interface above them:

```c
bool IMU_Init(void);
bool IMU_Read(IMU_Data *data);
bool IMU_WhoAmI(uint8_t *id);
void IMU_Calibrate(void);
```

Then the rest of our mower code never cares which physical IMU is fitted.

Even better, once we know enough about both implementations, we can probably make it **runtime-detected**:

```text
boot
 ↓
probe I²C bus PB6/PB7
 ↓
try known WHO_AM_I/register behaviour
 ↓
┌───────────────────────┬───────────────────────┐
│ genuine/old MPU path  │ compatible clone path │
└───────────────────────┴───────────────────────┘
 ↓
same IMU_Data interface
```

I would also keep **DMP support separate from basic IMU support**. For our mower we do not need the MPU's internal DMP to get useful acceleration, gyro, pitch/roll, rollover detection, slope sensing, etc. We can read raw accel/gyro and run our own lightweight filter. That avoids making a clone IMU unusable merely because it doesn't accept InvenSense DMP firmware.

There was also a GD32F130C8-specific issue in that IMU fork: the I²C interrupt enables had to be explicitly enabled before its handlers ran:

```c
i2c_interrupt_enable(MPU_I2C, I2C_INT_ERR);
i2c_interrupt_enable(MPU_I2C, I2C_INT_EV);
i2c_interrupt_enable(MPU_I2C, I2C_INT_BUF);
```

The author noted this was necessary on the C8 implementation they tested. fileciteturn115file0 That is worth preserving in our GD32F130C8 path as well.

So our eventual Gen2.1.20 + FOC fork should retain:

```text
Motor control
 └─ BLDC_FOC

Sensors
 ├─ Hall sensors
 ├─ phase-current sensing
 ├─ optical bumper L/R
 └─ IMU abstraction
      ├─ MPU6050-old
      ├─ MPU6050-compatible/clone
      └─ future alternate driver
```

That way we don't accidentally lose compatibility with other otherwise-identical V4.1 boards just because their supplier changed the IMU chip.

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-02T20:21:56.912649+00:00
Node: c3272bd0-a261-4a8b-b2b5-9504a6af6f67
Parent node: af4e8281-5477-44df-8f18-fabc7441d05f

Yes. I’ve started it as a **Phase-1 integration**, with current Gen2.x-GD32/2.1.20 as the hardware/base firmware and EFeru’s generated FOC controller as the new motor-control backend.

[Download the Phase-1 Gen2.1.20 + EFeru FOC integration](sandbox:/mnt/data/Gyroor_G5_Gen2_FOC_Phase1.zip)

The approach preserves the current Gen2 architecture instead of replacing it. That is useful because current Gen2 already has the TIMER0-synchronized ADC/DMA → `CalculateBLDC()` execution path we need for phase-current control. fileciteturn153file0 It also already has the common IMU framework for `MPU_6050old`, `MPU_6050`, `MPU_6500`, and `BMI_160`; I kept those options rather than stripping the firmware down to one IMU. fileciteturn119file1

The package currently does these things:

- Selects **GD32F130 / layout 20** and adds `BLDC_FOC` alongside `BLDC_BC` and `BLDC_SINE`.
- Imports only EFeru's generated FOC core: `BLDC_controller.c`, `BLDC_controller_data.c`, `BLDC_controller.h`, and `rtwtypes.h`, pinned to a specific EFeru commit and Git-blob verified. Those are the actual generated FOC controller components rather than EFeru's STM32F103 board/application layer. fileciteturn126file0
- Changes the FOC ADC DMA layout to `PA4 = battery`, `PB0 = phase-current A`, `PA0 = phase-current B`, matching the existing 2.1.20 hardware definition. fileciteturn97file0
- Adds `bldcFOC.c` implementing the same `InitBldc()` / `bldc_get_pwm()` abstraction that current Gen2 explicitly reserves for a future FOC backend. fileciteturn108file0
- Keeps `PC15 / PA11` optical inputs untouched so we can later use the desoldered sensors as left/right bumpers.
- Makes the 2.1.20 IMU choice configurable as `MPU6050-old`, MPU6050-compatible/clone, MPU6500, or BMI160, while retaining `PB6/PB7` as the physical bus.
- Adds CubeMonitor-visible FOC/current variables so the first build is useful for instrumentation.
- Sets **`FOC_SAFE_BRINGUP = 1`**, which forcibly returns zero phase output even if `REMOTE_DUMMY` requests motion. The FOC controller runs and we can observe it, but the bridge is not commanded to drive.

I also made the patcher deliberately conservative. It knows the current Git blob IDs of the Gen2 files we're modifying and stops if upstream has changed instead of blindly patching a different revision. It creates `.phase1.bak` files before modifying anything.

The current provisional phase-current conversion is **99 ADC counts/A**. That's derived from the layout's documented 4 mΩ shunts and approximately 20× amplifier gain:

\[
\frac{4095}{3.3}\times0.004\times20 \approx 99.27\text{ counts/A}
\]

I've explicitly marked that as **uncalibrated**. We will measure the actual amplifier scaling and polarity before allowing motor output.

There is one important build gate now: the GD32F130C8 gives us **64 KiB flash and 8 KiB SRAM**, and EFeru's generated FOC core is sizeable. I don't have `arm-none-eabi-gcc` installed in this execution environment, so I could not do the actual GD32 link/size test here. I did syntax-check the new `bldcFOC.c` adapter against the EFeru interface and tested the patcher's transformations successfully.

On your machine, use a clean current Gen2 clone and run:

```bash
python3 apply_phase1.py /path/to/Hoverboard-Firmware-Hack-Gen2.x-GD32
```

The README in the ZIP has the exact sequence and all the CubeMonitor symbols.

**The next thing I want to determine is whether this Phase-1 tree fits the C8.** Once you run the build, send me the compiler/linker output—especially the flash/RAM size. If it fits, we can immediately move to the non-driving SWD/CubeMonitor test and verify `PB0`, `PA0`, Hall ordering, IMU selection, and FOC outputs before removing the software lockout.

one more thing, as i plan to use the platform to push snow in the winter is there a way we could have a hight torque "setting"

========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-09-05T14:28:49.369580+00:00
Node: 7aabf8de-17a9-4b89-8441-460c7e241394
Parent node: 988fd9b6-1aac-4575-b108-664ba77cf6b0
========================================================================
Yes. With FOC, a **snow / high-torque mode** is much cleaner than with simple PWM control because we can raise the allowable **q-axis motor current (`Iq`)**, which is the component that produces torque.

The EFeru controller already supports FOC torque mode and a configurable maximum motor phase current. Its config exposes `FOC_CTRL`, `TRQ_MODE`, and `I_MOT_MAX`; internally that maximum is converted into the controller's current units. fileciteturn138file1 fileciteturn165file2

I would not make it simply “remove the current limit.” I would build three limits into our combined firmware:

```c
typedef enum {
    DRIVE_NORMAL,
    DRIVE_SNOW,
    DRIVE_SNOW_BOOST
} DriveProfile;
```

Conceptually:

```text
NORMAL
  moderate phase-current limit
  normal acceleration ramp
  full normal travel speed

SNOW
  higher phase-current / Iq limit
  slower acceleration ramp
  reduced maximum wheel speed
  stronger low-speed torque
  more aggressive speed regulation under load

SNOW BOOST
  highest allowed phase current
  short-duration only
  intended for starting a heavy push / getting unstuck
```

The key point is that **snow mode would raise torque without requiring high wheel speed**. For a plow, that's exactly what we want.

For FOC:

\[
T \propto I_q
\]

within the motor's useful range. So instead of increasing arbitrary PWM, the controller can deliberately command more torque-producing current.

### I would separate peak and continuous current

Something like this structurally:

```c
#define MOTOR_CURRENT_HW_MAX      /* established by testing */

#define NORMAL_CURRENT_MAX        /* continuous normal */
#define SNOW_CURRENT_MAX          /* higher continuous/limited */
#define SNOW_BOOST_CURRENT_MAX    /* short peak */

#define SNOW_MAX_RPM              /* reduced speed */
#define BOOST_MAX_TIME_MS         3000
```

I don't want to assign the actual amp values yet because **we haven't calibrated the PA0/PB0 current sensing or established the MOSFET/motor thermal limits**. Those measurements should determine the numbers.

This is particularly important for snow pushing because the worst operating point is essentially:

```text
wheel speed ≈ 0
torque command = high
phase current = high
```

At that point there is very little mechanical power leaving the motor but substantial copper and MOSFET heating.

So I'd also add an **I²t current model**:

```text
current
   ↓
I² × time
   ↓
estimated thermal load
   ↓
reduce allowable torque if necessary
```

That allows, for example, a large current burst to get the plow moving while preventing it from sitting against an immovable snowbank at maximum current indefinitely.

### Snow mode should probably use speed control + torque limiting

I wouldn't necessarily run the mower in pure `TRQ_MODE` from the RC stick.

A better driving interface is:

```text
RC throttle
    ↓
requested wheel speed
    ↓
speed controller
    ↓
requested Iq
    ↓
SNOW current limit
    ↓
FOC
```

So when you push into snow:

```text
wheel slows
     ↓
speed error increases
     ↓
controller automatically increases torque
     ↓
up to SNOW_CURRENT_MAX
```

That gives the vehicle much more natural behaviour.

You command:

> “go forward slowly”

rather than:

> “apply 17 A of torque.”

The FOC controller already supports speed and torque operating modes, so we have the underlying mechanisms available. fileciteturn138file1

### We can also exploit the two boards independently

Because each wheel gets its own controller, the snow profile can operate independently:

```text
             commanded forward speed
                      │
             ┌────────┴────────┐
             ↓                 ↓
        LEFT SPEED         RIGHT SPEED
        CONTROLLER         CONTROLLER
             ↓                 ↓
         left Iq             right Iq
             ↓                 ↓
        current limit       current limit
```

If the left wheel encounters packed snow and the right doesn't, the left motor can automatically increase torque without us blindly increasing both motor PWMs.

That should substantially improve traction.

I would also add **wheel-slip detection later**. We have:

- individual Hall-derived wheel speeds;
- IMUs on the controller boards;
- eventually the Pi and other vehicle-state information.

So we can detect something like:

```text
commanded vehicle movement low
+
one wheel suddenly accelerates
+
motor current drops/changes
=
probable wheel spin
```

and momentarily reduce that wheel's torque. Essentially a primitive traction-control system.

### One unresolved ADC input may become useful here

We still have **PA6** from the factory ADC sequence whose purpose we haven't identified.

Before repurposing it, I want to trace it electrically. If it happens to be connected to a thermistor or power-stage temperature circuit, that would be extremely valuable for snow mode:

```text
MOSFET / board temperature
          ↓
dynamic current limit
          ↓
SNOW BOOST available only while cool
```

I'm **not saying PA6 is temperature**—we haven't established that—but it's worth resolving before we change the hardware.

So I'll treat this as part of the combined firmware architecture:

```text
FOC drive profiles
├── NORMAL
├── SNOW
│    ├── higher Iq/current ceiling
│    ├── reduced speed ceiling
│    ├── stronger speed regulation
│    └── thermal/I²t protection
└── SNOW BOOST
     ├── short peak-current allowance
     └── automatic timeout/derating
```

And importantly, the ultimate **hardware current ceiling remains common to all modes**. Snow mode can use more of the available inverter/motor capacity, but no software setting will be allowed to exceed the experimentally established safe phase-current ceiling.

I would add this profile framework to the Gen2.1.20 + EFeru integration now, while we're building the control interface, rather than retrofit it later.

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-05T14:33:05.584729+00:00

That photo is very useful. The MOSFET marking is **`100N8F6`**, which identifies them as **STMicroelectronics STP100N8F6** N-channel MOSFETs.

Key ratings are:

- **80 V VDS**
- **100 A continuous at TC = 25 °C**
- **70 A continuous at TC = 100 °C**
- **8 mΩ typical / 9 mΩ maximum RDS(on)** at 50 A with 10 V gate drive
- **400 A pulsed** subject to the safe-operating-area limits
- **0.85 °C/W junction-to-case thermal resistance**
- **100 nC typical gate charge** citeturn217334view0turn427058search0

So these are actually quite substantial MOSFETs for a hoverboard controller.

The important part for our **snow/high-torque mode** is that the `100 A` marking absolutely should **not** become our software current limit. That rating assumes the MOSFET case is held at 25 °C. ST already derates it to 70 A with the case at 100 °C. citeturn217334view0 The actual controller limit will depend on the heatsink, PCB copper, shunts, phase wiring, connectors, gate drive, and motor windings.

For perspective, using the datasheet's 9 mΩ worst-case room-temperature RDS(on), conduction loss in one MOSFET while conducting would be approximately:

```text
20 A →  3.6 W
30 A →  8.1 W
40 A → 14.4 W
50 A → 22.5 W
```

and RDS(on) rises as the junction heats, so those figures become worse hot. In FOC each individual MOSFET isn't carrying the full phase current continuously, but this shows why thermal management becomes the limiting factor well before the headline 100 A rating.

Also, the arrangement in your photo is consistent with what we'd expect from the inverter: the **green motor phase wire appears to terminate at the switching node associated with a high-side/low-side MOSFET pair**. With three such half-bridges, that gives the three motor phases.

This makes me more confident in the snow-mode architecture:

```text
NORMAL
  conservative phase-current limit

SNOW
  higher phase-current / Iq limit
  reduced maximum speed
  sustained torque allowed subject to thermal model

SNOW BOOST
  still higher phase-current limit
  short-duration only
  automatic I²t timeout/derating
```

I would also keep the **PWM frequency at the stock Gen2 value initially**. The STP100N8F6 has about 100 nC gate charge, so raising switching frequency unnecessarily would increase gate-drive and switching losses. citeturn217334view0

The next critical measurement for setting real snow-mode amperage is therefore **not the MOSFET rating**; it is calibrating **PA0/PB0 phase-current sensing**. Once we know exactly how many ADC counts correspond to 1 A, we can start with a conservative limit and increase it while watching the heatsink/MOSFET temperature.

These MOSFETs give us considerably more headroom than I would have assumed from a generic hoverboard controller. I would definitely retain the **short-term high-torque boost mode** in the combined firmware.

those are both measurments that are taken by the MCU regularly right, so that could be a "self tuning" thing

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-05T14:37:58.255798+00:00
Node: 2c84e609-e8b3-4ca2-b1af-82663c304b38
Parent node: f9a7158d-b954-456e-97d7-c9214ddb47bb
========================================================================
Yes — **phase current is already something the MCU can sample continuously**, and if we confirm that the remaining analog channel is a board-temperature sensor, then those two signals are enough to make the high-torque limit substantially **adaptive/self-tuning**.

There are two different kinds of “self tuning” here, though.

The **current sensor zero offset** can genuinely self-calibrate every startup. With the bridge disabled and motor current known to be zero, the firmware can average PA0/PB0 for perhaps a few hundred ADC samples:

```text
power-up
   ↓
PWM disabled
   ↓
average PA0/PB0
   ↓
store zero-current offsets
   ↓
enable motor control
```

That compensates for op-amp offset, ADC offset, temperature drift, board-to-board variation, etc. We should absolutely do this.

The **amps-per-ADC-count gain**, however, cannot initially calibrate itself from its own reading alone. It needs one known-current reference measurement. Once we measure one controller with a meter/current clamp or known load, we can establish the approximate gain. After that the firmware can automatically maintain the zero point and potentially refine consistency between the two phase channels.

For the snow-mode current limit, though, yes — that can become largely self-adapting.

Conceptually:

```text
phase current
    │
    ├───────────────┐
    ↓               ↓
instantaneous     I² accumulator
overcurrent          │
protection           ↓
                 estimated heating
                      │
temperature ─────────┤
                      ↓
             allowable current
                      ↓
                  Iq limit
```

So instead of hard-coding:

```c
SNOW_CURRENT_MAX = 35 A;
```

we can have something more like:

```text
controller cold:
    allow large short-term torque

controller warming:
    reduce continuous current

controller hot:
    reduce current substantially

controller cooling:
    progressively restore torque
```

And because current is being sampled at the motor-control rate, the firmware knows exactly how much electrical stress it has been applying.

### We can actually make it learn the thermal behavior

If we obtain a real board/MOSFET temperature measurement, the controller can estimate parameters such as:

```text
temperature rise per I²·second
thermal time constant
cooling rate
steady-state current capability
```

For example, suppose during operation it observes:

```text
25 °C → 45 °C
while averaging 22 A phase current
over 90 seconds
```

and then sees how quickly the board cools when current drops.

Over repeated runs we can fit a simple thermal model:

```text
estimated MOSFET temperature
        =
measured board temperature
        +
estimated junction rise from recent I² losses
```

Then `SNOW BOOST` doesn't really need a fixed **3-second** timeout. Its duration can depend on actual accumulated thermal headroom:

```text
cold controller + cold ambient
→ boost might remain available much longer

already-hot controller
→ very little boost available

controller cooling while travelling unloaded
→ boost capacity regenerates
```

That's significantly better for snowplowing.

### Temperature is the part we still need to confirm

The phase-current channels are established:

```text
PA0
PB0
```

The factory firmware also samples **PA6**, but we haven't established what PA6 measures yet.

If PA6 turns out to be an NTC/temperature signal, that's nearly ideal because the factory MCU is already wired for exactly the measurement we need.

If PA6 isn't temperature, the GD32 itself has an internal temperature sensor, but **MCU die temperature isn't the same as MOSFET/heatsink temperature**. It could still provide some useful protection, but I wouldn't use it as the primary measurement for aggressive snow-mode current limiting.

If there's no existing power-stage temperature sensor, adding a cheap NTC to the heatsink is very easy and would be worthwhile.

### There is another useful self-calibration available

Because we have **two phase-current sensors**, when the motor is operating in switching states where both are valid, the controller can continually check their relationship through:

\[
I_A+I_B+I_C=0
\]

It can't determine absolute current gain without a reference, but it **can detect drift, offsets, sensor disagreement, saturation, or a failed channel**.

That gives us continuous current-sensor diagnostics.

So I would design the combined firmware to have:

```text
CURRENT CALIBRATION
├─ automatic zero-current offset on every boot
├─ stored current gain calibration
├─ cross-check phase-current sensors while running
└─ temperature compensation if needed

THERMAL SELF-LEARNING
├─ measured phase current
├─ measured board/heatsink temperature
├─ learned heating rate
├─ learned cooling rate
├─ I²t/junction-temperature estimate
└─ continuously calculated safe Iq limit

DRIVE MODES
├─ Normal
├─ Snow
└─ Snow Boost
```

So yes — rather than us eventually saying **“the board seems safe at 32 A, so set it to 32 A forever,”** we can make the controller learn and continuously calculate how much torque headroom it actually has.

The one thing I'd investigate before locking that design in is **where PA6 physically goes**. If that's already the power-stage temperature channel, then most of the hardware for this adaptive system is already sitting on the board.

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-05T14:40:08.624553+00:00
Node: 43d12194-ae86-4042-ad82-614227d27a10
Parent node: 08794059-27e8-4add-914d-5080feb80cb3
========================================================================
Yes. I would make that a **global capability/safety layer**, not part of any particular drive mode.

The modes should only change *what the vehicle asks for*. A separate adaptive layer should decide *what the hardware can safely deliver right now*.

The control stack should look more like:

```text
RC / Pi command
      ↓
Drive profile
Normal / Snow / etc.
      ↓
requested speed / torque
      ↓
┌──────────────────────────────┐
│ ADAPTIVE CAPABILITY MANAGER  │
│                              │
│ phase current                │
│ current-sensor calibration   │
│ battery voltage              │
│ controller temperature       │
│ motor thermal/I²t estimate   │
│ inverter thermal/I²t model   │
│ current-sensor validity      │
│ stall / low-speed condition  │
│ accumulated thermal load     │
│ cooling history              │
└──────────────────────────────┘
      ↓
maximum permissible Iq NOW
      ↓
clamp requested torque
      ↓
FOC
      ↓
hardware overcurrent shutdown
```

So, for example, suppose the adaptive layer calculates:

```text
Available safe phase current right now = 28 A
```

Then:

```text
Normal asks for 12 A  → gets 12 A
Snow asks for 24 A    → gets 24 A
Boost asks for 40 A   → gets 28 A
```

Later, when everything is cold:

```text
Available = 40 A
```

then boost could actually receive 40 A.

And after prolonged pushing:

```text
Available = 19 A
```

every mode is constrained to 19 A until thermal headroom recovers.

### The profiles therefore become behavioural settings

Something like:

| Profile | Main difference |
|---|---|
| Normal | normal speed/acceleration/torque response |
| Snow | lower speed, stronger torque response, less wheel-speed droop |
| Boost | requests maximum available torque |
| Future mower mode | appropriate acceleration/speed limits |
| Autonomous | Pi-generated velocity commands |

But **none of them owns the protection limits**.

That also means if we later add something like an `ECO` mode, it simply deliberately uses less than the available capability:

```text
Adaptive maximum = 35 A

ECO cap          = 15 A
Normal cap       = 24 A
Snow cap         = 32 A
Boost cap        = 35 A
```

The important wording there is that those profile caps are **preferences**, while the adaptive maximum is the actual safety ceiling.

### I would actually use two thermal models

Because we're protecting two physically different things:

```text
INVERTER MODEL
MOSFET/shunt/PCB heating
    ↑
phase current + switching activity
    ↑
board/heatsink temperature if available
```

and:

```text
MOTOR MODEL
winding copper heating
    ↑
phase current² × time
    ↑
speed/cooling estimate
```

The MOSFETs may still be perfectly happy while a stalled motor is cooking its windings.

So the final current allowance becomes approximately:

```text
allowable Iq =
    minimum(
        inverter thermal limit,
        motor thermal limit,
        absolute hardware current limit,
        battery/voltage limit,
        sensor-validity limit
    )
```

### Startup calibration should also be universal

Every boot:

```text
bridge OFF
   ↓
collect PA0/PB0 samples
   ↓
calculate phase-current zero offsets
   ↓
sanity-check both channels
   ↓
load calibrated gain
   ↓
initialize thermal models from measured temperature
   ↓
motor permitted to enable
```

And periodically while running, whenever the switching state permits reliable measurements, we can check the current channels for drift/plausibility.

### One thing should remain non-learning

I would **not** let the firmware learn its own absolute maximum current upward without bounds.

There should always be hard experimentally established ceilings such as:

```c
ABSOLUTE_PHASE_CURRENT_MAX
ABSOLUTE_MOSFET_TEMP_MAX
ABSOLUTE_MOTOR_I2T_MAX
ABSOLUTE_BATTERY_VOLTAGE_MIN
```

The adaptive/self-learning model can determine whether it should permit **20 A, 27 A, 34 A, etc. within that envelope**, but it cannot decide one day that 70 A must be safe because it hasn't failed yet.

So I'd structure the combined firmware around a common module, probably something like:

```c
DriveCapability_Update();
DriveCapability_GetMaxIq();
```

with every control mode passing through it before the FOC command is accepted.

That is the architecture I would use going forward: **one continuously adaptive protection/capability system, with Normal/Snow/Boost/etc. layered above it.**

Done. I rolled it into a **Phase 2 firmware integration** rather than keeping the adaptive logic as a Snow-only feature.

[Download the Phase 2 Gen2.1.20 + EFeru FOC firmware bundle](sandbox:/mnt/data/Gyroor_G5_Gen2_FOC_Phase2.zip)

The new `driveCapability.c/.h` layer now sits between every drive profile and EFeru's FOC current limit. It continuously reconstructs the third phase current from PA0/PB0, calculates three-phase RMS current, accumulates an I²-based thermal model, validates the current ADCs, and dynamically writes the permitted current into EFeru's `rtP_Left.i_max`. EFeru already exposes that motor-current limit as part of the FOC controller parameters. fileciteturn165file2

The structure is now:

```text
Normal / Snow / Boost / future Pi mode
                ↓
        profile preference
                ↓
       GLOBAL CAPABILITY
       ├─ current sensors
       ├─ I² thermal history
       ├─ absolute hardware ceiling
       ├─ optional battery derating
       └─ optional temperature derating
                ↓
          permitted Iq/current
                ↓
             EFeru FOC
```

So Snow or Boost cannot bypass the adaptive ceiling. At present the deliberately conservative bring-up settings are **5.00 A absolute**, **3.00 A thermal-continuous model**, with Normal requesting 65%, Snow 85%, and Boost 100% of the absolute ceiling. Those are placeholders until we calibrate PA0/PB0 and characterize the real hardware.

I also left `FOC_SAFE_BRINGUP = 1`, so **the firmware still cannot drive the motor yet**. The capability system runs and can be observed, but phase outputs remain forced to zero.

The useful new CubeMonitor variables include:

```text
cap_current_rms_centiamp
cap_thermal_load_permille

cap_absolute_limit_centiamp
cap_thermal_limit_centiamp
cap_global_limit_centiamp
cap_profile_limit_centiamp
cap_effective_limit_centiamp

cap_current_sensor_valid
cap_fault_flags
cap_profile

foc_dynamic_i_max_q4
```

For example, `425` in a `_centiamp` variable means **4.25 A**.

I also put in the temperature interface now:

```c
DriveCapability_SetTemperatureDeciC(...)
```

but left it disabled until we identify whether PA6 or another circuit actually measures controller/heatsink temperature. Battery derating is similarly implemented but disabled until we settle on the final mower battery configuration.

The current-zero calibration still happens automatically at every boot, independent of drive mode. The absolute amps-per-ADC-count scale still requires a real calibration measurement; the firmware does **not** allow itself to “learn upward” past its defined hardware ceiling.

I host-compiled the new capability module and FOC adapter for syntax and ran a simulation of the adaptive logic. In the simulated Boost profile, sustained current caused the thermal load to rise and the global current allowance to progressively derate as intended. I still cannot perform the final GD32F130 **64 KiB/8 KiB link-size check** here because the ARM embedded GCC toolchain isn't available in this runtime.

If you already applied Phase 1, you can run the Phase 2 patcher on that same checkout; it recognizes the Phase-1 markers and upgrades the overlay rather than trying to reapply the base modifications.

In the **current Gen2.1.20 definition, PA6 is not assigned to anything**. The layout file defines PA4 as battery voltage, PB0/PA0 as the two phase-current channels, PC15/PA11 as the photo sensors, etc., but **PA6 does not appear in `defines_2-1-20.h` at all**. citeturn378462view0

So for Gen2.1.20 specifically:

```text
PA4  = VBATT
PB0  = PHASE_A current
PA0  = PHASE_B current
PA6  = currently unidentified / unused by the Gen2.1.20 port
```

That means my earlier statement that “the factory firmware samples PA6” is still relevant to **your stock Gyroor firmware**, but it is **not something the Gen2.1.20 project has identified or named**.

The best next step is to trace PA6 on your actual board. Since we already know the factory ADC sequence includes it, PA6 is very likely connected to some analog quantity the original firmware cared about. Candidates include temperature, another supply/current sense, or a pedal/tilt-related analog signal, but those would just be hypotheses until we trace it.

If you want, I can go back through the factory disassembly and isolate **exactly how the stock firmware uses the PA6 ADC result**. That may tell us what it is before you even probe the PCB.

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-05T15:38:45.799646+00:00
Node: 73a355de-592c-4c68-a869-c409fb36ad39
Parent node: 399c8acf-035d-44fb-b7a9-cae85f8b1e22
========================================================================
I dug through the factory firmware more deeply, and this changes the conclusion quite a bit:

**PA6 is almost certainly a resistive temperature-sensor input — very likely an NTC thermistor circuit.** What we still do **not** know is where that sensor physically is.

The factory ADC setup confirms four channels in this order:

```text
PA0  ADC ch0  → phase current
PB0  ADC ch8  → phase current
PA6  ADC ch6  → unknown analog
PA4  ADC ch4  → battery voltage
```

The sequence is explicitly programmed by the stock firmware. fileciteturn177file2L250-L265

The interesting part is what happens to **PA6 afterward**.

The ADC ISR stores the third conversion separately, and the firmware runs it through a **128-sample moving average**. By comparison, the PA4 battery-voltage channel gets a 32-sample average. That much heavier filtering already suggests PA6 is a slowly changing physical quantity rather than motor current. fileciteturn177file1L145-L168

Then I found the decisive part in the B/slave firmware around `0x08001766`.

It effectively does:

```c
adc = filtered_PA6;

R = 10000 * adc;
R = R / (32768 - adc);
```

The actual instructions are:

```asm
8001768: movw r1, #0x2710      ; 10000
800176c: ldrh r0, [r0]         ; filtered PA6
800176e: muls r1, r0, r1
8001770: rsb  r0, r0, #0x8000  ; 32768 - ADC
8001774: sdiv r1, r1, r0
```

fileciteturn177file0L46-L54

That calculation is the standard resistance-divider rearrangement:

\[
R_{sensor}=10000\frac{ADC}{32768-ADC}
\]

In other words, the factory code is converting PA6's ADC value into a **resistance in ohms using a 10 kΩ reference resistor**.

Then it compares that resistance against two thresholds:

```text
0x1F40 = 8000 Ω
0x1770 = 6000 Ω
```

and uses long persistence counters before setting status flags. fileciteturn177file0L56-L78 fileciteturn177file0L79-L97

That pattern is extremely characteristic of an **NTC overtemperature detector**:

```text
temperature rises
      ↓
NTC resistance falls
      ↓
R < 8 kΩ for sustained period
      ↓
first temperature state

R < 6 kΩ for sustained period
      ↓
second/higher temperature state
```

I would now consider PA6's role **high-confidence temperature sensing**, rather than “unknown analog.”

### But I agree with your physical observation

I would **not** call it a MOSFET thermistor yet.

You showed the power stage, and there's no obvious bead thermistor strapped to the STP100N8F6 MOSFETs or heatsink.

So the likely possibilities are more like:

- a tiny SMD NTC somewhere on the PCB near the power stage;
- an NTC on the underside of the PCB;
- an SMD part under/adjacent to the heatsink or thermal interface;
- a general controller-board temperature sensor rather than a MOSFET-case sensor.

The firmware only tells us:

> **PA6 sees a temperature-dependent resistance through a nominal 10 kΩ divider.**

It does **not** tell us where the physical sensing element is.

And those thresholds don't give us temperatures yet. We know:

```text
factory level 1 = 8.000 kΩ
factory level 2 = 6.000 kΩ
```

but without knowing the thermistor's nominal resistance/Beta curve, I don't want to arbitrarily turn those into °C.

### This is excellent for our new firmware

We should revise the capability manager accordingly.

Instead of leaving PA6 unused, I would add:

```text
PA6 raw ADC
    ↓
128-sample filtered value
    ↓
NTC resistance
    ↓
temperature conversion once characterized
    ↓
DriveCapability temperature input
```

And initially, even before we know the °C conversion, we can expose:

```c
pa6_adc_raw
pa6_adc_filtered
pa6_resistance_ohm
```

in CubeMonitor.

We can also retain the **factory 8 kΩ and 6 kΩ resistance points** as reference diagnostics while we characterize it.

### We can find the physical sensor very easily now

With the **factory firmware still on one untouched board**, monitor the filtered PA6 value or calculated resistance over SWD.

Then gently warm small regions of the PCB one at a time:

```text
power MOSFET/heatsink area
gate-driver area
MCU area
IMU area
other small SMD parts
```

The sensor is likely going to reveal itself very clearly because a 128-sample averaged temperature signal should move slowly and monotonically when its local area is warmed.

Even easier, once we flash our bring-up firmware, I'll expose the resistance directly:

```text
PA6 = 7,843 Ω
```

instead of having to interpret raw ADC counts.

So this is actually better than we expected: **the stock board appears to already have a temperature channel we can feed into the universal adaptive-current/thermal system.** We just need to locate the sensing element and determine its resistance-vs-temperature curve. memcite

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-05T20:43:53.306799+00:00
Node: 5f47a8ea-f1c3-45c9-9d88-bfc557167b2e
Parent node: 02af9e30-5cfd-4ef0-b47b-2520152bd7e7
========================================================================
Yes — that actually fits the evidence **better than a board-temperature sensor**.

The factory PA6 processing looks like a 10 kΩ-divider reading a resistive sensor, and if each motor has **one otherwise-unidentified low-current wire in addition to the three phase wires and Hall harness**, a motor-winding NTC is a very strong candidate. The factory code explicitly samples PA6 as its own fourth analog channel and converts the filtered reading into an apparent resistance using a 10 kΩ reference. fileciteturn177file0L46-L54

A motor NTC can require only **one additional wire** if the other end of the thermistor is tied internally to the Hall-sensor ground:

```text
controller                         motor

3.3 V
  │
 10 kΩ
  │
  ├──────── PA6
  │
  └──────── extra wire ───── NTC ───── Hall GND
                                      │
Hall GND ─────────────────────────────┘
```

That topology would produce exactly the resistance-divider equation we found in the stock firmware:

\[
R_{NTC}=10000\frac{ADC}{32768-ADC}
\]

and explains why there would be only **one extra motor wire**, not two.

It also makes much more functional sense for a hoverboard. Motor winding temperature is particularly valuable because these hub motors can get hot at high phase current and low speed while the controller MOSFETs are still reasonably cool. That's exactly the condition we'll encounter pushing snow.

The factory thresholds we found were approximately:

```text
8.000 kΩ  → first temperature-related condition
6.000 kΩ  → second/higher condition
```

with persistence counters rather than instantaneous triggering. fileciteturn177file0L56-L97

That behavior is very consistent with motor thermal protection.

### We can prove it with a meter

With the controller disconnected from the motor, measure:

```text
unknown motor wire → Hall ground
```

If it's the NTC, I'd expect a **finite resistance in the several-kΩ range at room temperature**, likely around or above 10 kΩ depending on the thermistor specification.

Then simply warm the wheel/motor moderately. If it is an NTC:

```text
temperature ↑
resistance ↓
```

If, for example, you see:

```text
cold/room temp:  ~10–15 kΩ
warmer motor:     ~8–12 kΩ
```

we've essentially identified it.

Also check continuity:

```text
unknown wire → PA6
```

through the controller. It may not be direct zero-ohm continuity because there may be an RC/input resistor between the connector and MCU, but tracing that wire toward PA6 would close the loop.

### That improves our firmware architecture

If PA6 is motor temperature, I would change the universal capability manager to maintain **two distinct thermal estimates**:

```text
MOTOR
├─ PA6 physical motor-temperature NTC
├─ phase-current I²t
└─ motor thermal limit

INVERTER
├─ phase-current I²t
├─ MOSFET conduction/switching model
└─ estimated controller thermal limit
```

Then:

```text
effective current limit =
min(
    motor temperature limit,
    motor I²t limit,
    inverter estimated limit,
    battery limit,
    absolute phase-current limit
)
```

That's actually better than PA6 being a MOSFET thermistor, because the motor is the component whose temperature is otherwise hardest for us to infer accurately.

I would now treat **PA6 = motor NTC as the leading hypothesis**, with the one-wire-to-Hall-ground arrangement explaining the physical wiring very neatly. The resistance measurement between that unidentified wire and Hall ground should settle it quickly.

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-05T20:48:41.588569+00:00
Node: 05e6aa08-818e-4abd-8eac-a1e425e278cb
Parent node: c43203e3-9599-4753-bb8c-44794edf9000
========================================================================
Yes. I traced the PA6-derived value farther through the factory firmware, and we can now say considerably more about what the **8 kΩ and 6 kΩ thresholds actually do**.

The PA6 ADC reading is first converted into the calculated resistance we've been discussing:

\[
R = 10000\frac{ADC}{32768-ADC}
\]

The resulting resistance is stored in RAM and then passed through two independent persistence/fault detectors. fileciteturn180file1L104-L118

| Resistance condition | Persistence logic | Result |
|---|---|---|
| **R < 8,000 Ω** | counter rises toward 3000; flag asserts above 2900 | sets flag at **0x20000069** |
| **R ≥ 8,000 Ω** | that counter counts back down | avoids triggering if condition was brief |
| **R < 6,000 Ω** | counter rises toward 2000; flag asserts above 1900 | sets flag at **0x2000006A** |
| **R ≥ 6,000 Ω** | that counter counts back down | avoids triggering on a transient |

The 8 kΩ logic is visible at `0x08001780–0x080017B4`: it compares against `0x1F40 = 8000`, increments/decrements the persistence counter, and ultimately writes `1` to structure offset `+5`. fileciteturn180file1L118-L140 The 6 kΩ stage immediately follows it, compares against `0x1770 = 6000`, and eventually writes `1` to offset `+6`. fileciteturn180file1L141-L160

The really useful finding is where those flags go.

### They feed directly into the motor-control limiting logic

In the B/slave control loop around `0x08003F80`, the firmware explicitly checks:

```asm
[r7 + 5]   ; 8 kΩ-derived flag
[r7 + 6]   ; 6 kΩ-derived flag
[r7 + 7]   ; another protection flag
```

If **any one of those equals 1**, they all branch into the same protection path. fileciteturn180file0L19-L27

That protection path takes a motor-control quantity and does:

```c
if (control_value > 10)
    control_value -= 10;
else
    control_value = 0;
```

The assembly is literally:

```asm
ldr   r0, [r9,#0x2C]
cmp   r0,#10
sub   r0,#10
str   r0,[r9,#0x2C]

; eventually
str   0,[r9,#0x2C]
```

fileciteturn180file0L35-L44

So this is **not an instantaneous MOSFET-off overtemperature trip**. The factory controller intentionally **ramps a motor-demand/control magnitude toward zero** when the temperature fault flag becomes active.

I don't want to name that RAM variable definitively as `Iq`, torque, PWM, or speed demand yet because this factory firmware predates our FOC port and the surrounding control structure is complicated. But it is clearly part of the motor-demand path, and the temperature flags cause it to be progressively reduced to zero.

That strongly supports this interpretation:

```text
PA6 resistance decreases
        ↓
8 kΩ threshold persists
        ↓
temperature fault flag
        ↓
motor output progressively derated
        ↓
eventually zero
```

and then at still lower resistance:

```text
6 kΩ threshold persists
        ↓
second/higher temperature flag
```

Interestingly, in this particular motor-control branch, **both temperature flags produce the same ramp-down behavior**. The distinction between the two is retained elsewhere as separate state flags.

### The 8 kΩ value is also used directly by the alarm/status logic

There's another important use.

The calculated resistance is stored at **`0x20000114`**. Elsewhere, around `0x08001B0E`, the firmware loads that exact value and directly compares it to:

```asm
cmp.w r1,#0x1F40
```

again, **8,000 Ω**. fileciteturn181file0L14-L22

That result selects between different timed output patterns. Those paths eventually alternate between:

```c
function(0);
```

and:

```c
function(5000);
```

at `0x080019CC` and `0x080019E8`. fileciteturn182file0L23-L34

That function configures a timer/PWM output based on the supplied frequency. fileciteturn182file1L97-L126 Combined with the GPIO address/mask used alongside it—GPIOB bit 9—this is the **buzzer path**.

So the 8 kΩ threshold appears to have **two consequences**:

```text
8 kΩ
├── persistent fault flag → motor derating toward zero
└── direct state-machine input → warning/alarm pattern
```

### The 6 kΩ threshold looks like the more severe state

I searched the factory images specifically for `0x1770 = 6000`. Unlike 8,000, I only find it in this temperature/persistence routine.

So the current picture is:

```text
Temperature sensor resistance

normal
  │
  ▼
8 kΩ
  │
  ├── alarm behavior
  └── after long persistence → fault flag #1
                              → motor derating
  │
  ▼
6 kΩ
  │
  └── after persistence → fault flag #2
                          → motor derating
```

Both flags appear **latched in this routine**: I see code that sets the bytes to `1`, but this routine does not clear them again when the resistance recovers. The counters themselves count back down if the resistance rises before the fault is established. fileciteturn180file1L132-L159

That makes sense for a thermal protection system: brief excursions are filtered, but once a sufficiently severe/sustained overtemperature condition is recognized, the fault is latched until some higher-level reset/power-cycle logic clears it.

This also makes your **motor-NTC hypothesis considerably stronger**. The factory firmware isn't merely displaying this analog value—it uses it as a persistent protection input that **actively removes motor output and generates an alarm**. That's exactly what I'd expect from an embedded hub-motor temperature sensor.

For our firmware, I would preserve this general philosophy but improve it: instead of waiting for fixed resistance thresholds and then ramping to zero, our capability manager can use the actual NTC temperature continuously to begin derating progressively **before** reaching the factory fault thresholds.

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-05T21:20:00.314274+00:00
Node: 11114639-f59a-4505-9911-817c412e0008
Parent node: a3c18161-dac5-46e7-b794-83c58a9d9036
========================================================================
Yes — that lines up very well with what the factory code is doing, and it strengthens the case that **PA6 is the motor-temperature NTC**.

The behavior we reconstructed is not just “overtemp = shutdown.” The firmware first converts PA6 into a resistance value, then uses the **8.0 kΩ and 6.0 kΩ thresholds** to establish persistent thermal states, and those states feed directly into the motor-control path where a demand variable is progressively reduced toward zero. fileciteturn180file1L108-L159 fileciteturn180file0L19-L44

That matches what you observed:

```text
motor cold
   ↓
full expected power / speed

motor warms
   ↓
NTC resistance falls
   ↓
factory thermal state begins asserting
   ↓
allowed motor output is reduced
   ↓
available speed and pushing power fall

motor gets hotter still
   ↓
stronger thermal state
   ↓
further derating / eventual near-zero output
```

So the important conclusion is that the stock controller appears to implement **thermal derating, not merely thermal cutoff**.

That is exactly the behavior we should preserve, but improve.

For our firmware I would use the motor temperature continuously rather than only as two thresholded fault states:

```text
measured motor temperature
        ↓
continuous allowable-current curve
        ↓
maximum permitted Iq
        ↓
FOC torque limit
```

For example, conceptually:

```text
cold motor      → 100% available current
warming         → still 100%
approaching hot → smoothly reduce current
hot             → substantial derating
critical        → minimum/zero torque + latched fault
```

The exact temperatures should come from the actual NTC characterization, not guesses.

This is better than the stock method because it avoids a relatively abrupt transition at fixed resistance thresholds. We can make the motor feel much more consistent:

```text
temperature increases gradually
→ torque ceiling decreases gradually
```

instead of waiting for a flag and then stepping the internal demand downward.

It also gives us a very useful way to **calibrate the NTC**. We can monitor PA6 resistance while heating the motor and record:

```text
motor surface/winding temperature
PA6 calculated resistance
point where factory derating becomes noticeable
```

If the observed derating starts around the same time the calculated resistance crosses **8 kΩ**, that would essentially close the loop on the identification.

And because the stock firmware directly compares that resistance to 8 kΩ in its warning/state machinery as well as using the persistent fault flags for motor derating, this is much more likely to be a deliberately monitored thermal sensor than some miscellaneous analog input. fileciteturn181file0L14-L40

So for the new capability manager, I would now change the model from:

```text
motor temperature = optional future sensor
```

to:

```text
motor temperature = PA6 NTC
```

with the caveat that the **NTC curve still needs physical calibration before we assign °C values**.

That should become one of the primary inputs to the global adaptive current limit for every drive mode.

Yes. I’ve added it tentatively as **Phase 3**.

[Download the Phase 3 Gen2.1.20 + EFeru FOC firmware bundle](sandbox:/mnt/data/Gyroor_G5_Gen2_FOC_Phase3.zip)

The basis is strong enough to justify instrumenting it now: the factory firmware samples PA6, converts it using the equivalent of a 10 kΩ divider, and compares the resulting resistance against **8.0 kΩ and 6.0 kΩ** thresholds. fileciteturn180file1L108-L159 Those resulting thermal flags feed the stock motor-control path and progressively reduce a motor-demand quantity. fileciteturn180file0L19-L44

Phase 3 now samples:

```text
ADC DMA
0 -> PA4  battery
1 -> PB0  phase current A
2 -> PA0  phase current B
3 -> PA6  tentative motor NTC
```

It calculates:

\[
R_{NTC}=10000\frac{ADC}{4096-ADC}
\]

and exposes the actual resistance directly in CubeMonitor, so we don't need to pretend we know the thermistor's °C curve yet.

For the moment the measured-temperature behavior is:

```text
>= 8.0 kΩ
    full temperature-derived capability

8.0 → 6.0 kΩ
    progressively reduce allowable motor current

<= 6.0 kΩ
    motor-NTC current allowance = 0
```

The important part is that this is **combined with**, rather than substituted for, our current-heating model:

```text
effective hardware capability =
    minimum(
        absolute current ceiling,
        phase-current I² heating model,
        PA6 motor-NTC limit,
        battery limit,
        any future controller-temp limit
    )
```

So the I² model can predict accumulating heat from motor current, while PA6 gives us feedback about what the motor is actually doing thermally.

I also deliberately set:

```c
#define CAP_MOTOR_NTC_REQUIRE_VALID 0
```

for now. If PA6 is disconnected, shorted, or at an ADC rail, it raises:

```text
CAP_FAULT_MOTOR_NTC_INVALID
```

but **doesn't yet disable the motor**, because we haven't physically proven that unidentified motor wire is the PA6 NTC lead. Once you measure that wire and confirm it, we'll change that to `1` so a failed motor-temperature sensor becomes fail-safe.

New useful CubeMonitor variables are:

```text
foc_adc_motor_ntc_raw
cap_motor_ntc_raw
cap_motor_ntc_filtered_raw
cap_motor_ntc_ohm
cap_motor_ntc_valid
cap_motor_ntc_limit_centiamp
```

And `FOC_SAFE_BRINGUP` is still **1**, so none of this can accidentally drive the motor yet.

I also corrected the integration package so the Keil project now explicitly includes the new FOC/capability sources and the vendored EFeru controller files; that was worth fixing before we start trying to build it.

The next very useful measurement is the unidentified motor wire **to Hall ground** at room temperature. If it reads somewhere around the expected thermistor resistance range and decreases when the motor is warmed, we can promote PA6 from “tentative motor NTC” to confirmed.

Yes. I made a CubeMonitor bundle specifically around the **Phase-3 FOC/current/NTC bring-up**.

[Download the Gyroor G5 CubeMonitor Phase-3 project bundle](sandbox:/mnt/data/Gyroor_G5_CubeMonitor_Phase3.zip)

It generates two importable STM32CubeMonitor flows from the **actual compiled `.axf` or `.elf`**, because the RAM addresses of our globals are assigned by the linker and can change each build. CubeMonitor supports custom variables by address and JSON flow import, so generating the flow from the final symbol table avoids hard-coding stale addresses. citeturn847686search1turn326693search0

The bundle contains:

```text
generate_cubemonitor_project.py
README.md
MONITOR_VARIABLES.md
FAULT_BITS.txt
```

After you build the Phase-3 firmware, run:

```text
py generate_cubemonitor_project.py C:\path\to\Hoverboard.axf
```

It will produce:

```text
Gyroor_G5_Bringup_Monitor.json
Gyroor_G5_Deep_Diagnostics_Monitor.json
Gyroor_G5_Monitor_Addresses.csv
```

The **Bringup** dashboard is the one I want us to use first. It watches the current-sensor offsets/raw ADC, calculated RMS current, all of the adaptive current limits, PA6 raw/filtered NTC data and calculated resistance, thermal load, fault flags, profile, Hall signals when available, and general FOC status.

The **Deep Diagnostics** dashboard adds:

```text
foc_current_a_model
foc_current_b_model
foc_iq_raw
foc_id_raw
foc_target_model
foc_dynamic_i_max_q4
foc_speed_rpm_x16
foc_out_phase_a
foc_out_phase_b
foc_out_phase_c
```

I separated them because reading every diagnostic continuously is unnecessary load on the SWD connection during first bring-up.

The dashboard is already divided into useful plots:

```text
Current and capability       0.01 A units
Motor NTC resistance        ohms
Motor NTC ADC               ADC counts
Thermal load                per-mille
Phase-current/battery ADC    ADC counts
Status/fault/Hall signals
FOC current/Id/Iq            deep flow
FOC outputs                  deep flow
FOC speed                    deep flow
```

I also included the fault-bit decoding:

```text
0x01  current offsets not ready
0x02  current ADC at rail
0x04  I² thermal hard limit
0x08  battery low
0x10  second temperature high
0x20  PA6 motor NTC invalid
0x40  PA6 motor NTC hot
```

For the first session, keep:

```c
#define FOC_SAFE_BRINGUP 1
```

and in CubeMonitor select the same ST-Link in the `Probe OUT` and `Probe IN` nodes. I set the instructions around **400 kHz SWD**, dropping to **150 kHz** if your connection remains marginal.

One useful detail: you do **not** need to manually enter 30+ addresses. The generator directly reads the symbol table from the Keil `.axf`, and it even derives `adc_v_batt_raw` from the `adc_buffer` structure address.

Once you have the first Phase-3 `.axf`, you can also upload it here and I can generate the final ready-to-import JSON for that exact build myself.
