is there an easier program that we can use for monitoring the data

========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-09-06T03:22:37.296801+00:00
Node: 265434d5-c724-4bf7-ab02-e0e70e8a8b3b
Parent node: fe63f85e-319a-4513-9b96-1d7f809b74f8
========================================================================
Yes. For what we're doing, I think **STM32CubeIDE's Live Expressions is much easier than CubeMonitor**, especially since you already installed CubeIDE.

ST's current CubeIDE supports ST-Link debugging, memory inspection, and live data, and the **Live Expressions** view continuously updates global/static variables while the processor is running. citeturn628934search3turn628934search36

For us, the best solution would be to make the firmware deliberately easy to monitor. Instead of adding 25 separate variables, I would create one global diagnostic structure such as:

```c
typedef struct {
    /* ADC */
    uint16_t phase_a_adc;
    uint16_t phase_b_adc;
    uint16_t motor_ntc_adc;
    uint16_t battery_adc;

    /* Current */
    int16_t phase_a_cA;
    int16_t phase_b_cA;
    int16_t phase_c_cA;
    uint16_t current_rms_cA;

    /* Motor temperature */
    uint32_t motor_ntc_ohm;
    uint8_t motor_ntc_valid;

    /* Adaptive capability */
    uint16_t thermal_load_permille;
    uint16_t absolute_limit_cA;
    uint16_t thermal_limit_cA;
    uint16_t temperature_limit_cA;
    uint16_t effective_limit_cA;

    /* Motor state */
    uint8_t hall;
    int16_t speed_rpm;
    int16_t iq;
    int16_t id;

    /* FOC output */
    int16_t phase_a_pwm;
    int16_t phase_b_pwm;
    int16_t phase_c_pwm;

    /* Status */
    uint32_t fault_flags;
    uint8_t drive_profile;
    uint8_t bridge_enabled;
} G5_MonitorData;

volatile G5_MonitorData g5mon;
```

Then in CubeIDE you would only add:

```text
g5mon
```

to **Live Expressions** and expand it.

CubeIDE can display structures and their members directly in Live Expressions, which is exactly what we need here. citeturn628934search36

Your workflow becomes essentially:

```text
Open our .elf/.axf
      ↓
Connect ST-Link
      ↓
Start Debug
      ↓
Window → Show View → Live Expressions
      ↓
Add:
g5mon
      ↓
Resume processor
      ↓
watch everything live
```

ST specifically documents Live Expressions as the view for runtime globals/static variables rather than ordinary breakpoint-only Expressions. citeturn628934search2turn628934search7

### I think this is better than CubeMonitor for initial testing

We mainly need to see numbers:

```text
PA0 current ADC
PB0 current ADC
PA6 NTC ADC
PA6 calculated resistance
battery voltage
Hall state
wheel RPM
Iq / Id
thermal model
available current
fault flags
FOC outputs
```

CubeMonitor is useful once we want pretty real-time graphs, but its Node-RED-style project setup is adding complexity we don't really need yet.

For **graphing later**, I would actually skip CubeMonitor too. Once the firmware is operational, we can output a telemetry stream and feed it into something like **PlotJuggler**, which is specifically designed for drag-and-drop live time-series plotting. citeturn628934search0

So I'd use:

```text
NOW:
ST-Link
  ↓
CubeIDE Live Expressions
  ↓
g5mon structure

LATER:
controller telemetry
  ↓
PlotJuggler
  ↓
graphs/logging
```

And because we're using debug symbols, there are **no RAM addresses for you to type in manually**.

I would therefore modify our Phase-3 firmware to add the single `g5mon` diagnostic structure and make **CubeIDE Live Expressions our primary bring-up monitor**. That will be substantially simpler than the CubeMonitor setup I made.

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-06T03:26:09.595484+00:00

Yes—with one important caveat: **I would use STM32CubeIDE as the main IDE, build environment, debugger, and live-data interface, but not pretend the GD32F130C8 is an STM32 device.**

CubeIDE itself is officially an STM32 IDE, and ST's own ST-LINK GDB server is documented for STM32 Cortex-M devices. citeturn824438search0turn824438search23 Our controller is a **GD32F130C8**, so creating a normal CubeIDE “STM32 project” and selecting some approximately similar STM32 would be the wrong approach.

Instead, I suggest this setup:

```text
STM32CubeIDE
│
├── Edit/source browsing
├── GNU Arm build
├── errors/warnings
├── ELF symbol information
├── debugger UI
├── Live Expressions
├── Memory/Register views
│
└── OpenOCD GDB server
        ↓
     ST-Link
        ↓
   GD32F130C8
```

That gives us almost the whole workflow in one application while retaining the **GD32 headers, linker script, startup code, peripheral definitions, and flash layout** from the Gen2 project.

CubeIDE includes an OpenOCD GDB-server option, and ST documents Live Expressions as working with its supported GDB-server choices, including OpenOCD. citeturn824438search22 So this is the route I'd try rather than using CubeIDE's STM32-specific ST-LINK server for the GD32.

### What I would change in our firmware package

I'd make Phase 3 into a proper **CubeIDE-compatible Makefile project** rather than the Keil-oriented package we've been carrying along.

Something like:

```text
Gyroor_G5_FOC/
│
├── .project
├── .cproject
├── Makefile
│
├── OpenOCD/
│   ├── gyroor_gd32f130.cfg
│   └── stlink.cfg
│
├── HoverBoardGigaDevice/
│   ├── Inc/
│   ├── Src/
│   ├── RTE/
│   └── ...
│
├── FOC/
│   ├── BLDC_controller.c
│   ├── BLDC_controller_data.c
│   └── ...
│
└── Debug/
    └── Gyroor_G5_FOC.elf
```

Then the normal workflow becomes:

```text
Ctrl+B
  ↓
compile + link

Debug button
  ↓
OpenOCD connects through ST-Link

Program firmware
  ↓
halt at reset/main

Resume
  ↓
Live Expressions

g5mon
  ├── phase_a_adc
  ├── phase_b_adc
  ├── motor_ntc_adc
  ├── motor_ntc_ohm
  ├── battery voltage
  ├── current RMS
  ├── thermal model
  ├── allowable current
  ├── Hall state
  ├── RPM
  ├── Iq / Id
  └── faults
```

So we could largely retire:

```text
Keil                → not needed
CubeProgrammer      → backup/recovery tool
CubeMonitor         → optional later
command-line GDB    → only troubleshooting
manual OpenOCD      → normally launched by IDE
```

I would **keep STM32CubeProgrammer installed**, though. It has already proven useful for raw flash reads and recovery work on these boards. I just wouldn't make it our normal edit/build/flash/debug cycle.

### One other improvement

This is a good time to add the `g5mon` structure we discussed. Then we don't even need the separate CubeMonitor project for ordinary bring-up.

Our main screen can effectively be:

```text
Live Expressions

g5mon
  ADC
  Current
  Motor NTC
  Thermal limits
  FOC
  Hall/RPM
  Faults
```

and we can expand whichever section we're testing.

So **yes, I think moving the project to CubeIDE is the right direction**, provided we configure it as a **GD32 GNU-Arm/Makefile project using OpenOCD**, rather than converting it into an STM32-generated Cube project.

The next firmware package I make should therefore be a **CubeIDE-ready Phase 3**, including the `g5mon` structure, build configuration, linker settings, and OpenOCD/debug configuration so we can build, flash, debug, and monitor from the same application. memcite

========================================================================
is it possible to use / include some of the VESC opensource for this project?

========================================================================
ASSISTANT | 2026-09-16T14:57:05.183809+00:00
Node: 85017ef9-84e3-481c-8739-f9a42e120c58
Parent node: 810cab0f-8503-4e8f-b857-0d558e86d1f6
========================================================================
Yes. **Selective VESC [Vedder Electronic Speed Controller] reuse makes sense for this project**, but I would not try to port the complete VESC firmware onto the GD32F130C8.

The current VESC firmware is a large ChibiOS [Chibi Operating System]-based STM32F4-oriented codebase. Its main FOC [Field-Oriented Control] implementation alone is thousands of lines and depends on VESC's hardware layer, operating system, configuration system, communications stack, and STM32F4 peripherals. citeturn256727search7turn161422search4 Our GD32F130C8 has only 64 KiB flash and 8 KiB SRAM [Static Random-Access Memory], so attempting to transplant the whole thing would work against the compact Gen2.x/EFeru foundation we've already established.

What **does** make sense is adopting selected VESC subsystems and architecture.

- **Limit-management logic:** VESC already separates configured current limits, input-current limits, absolute current limits, battery limits, motor-temperature limits, MOSFET [Metal-Oxide-Semiconductor Field-Effect Transistor] temperature limits, speed limits, and runtime-overridden limits. That is very close to the capability-manager architecture we've been building. citeturn140957search0turn140957search1
- **Motor-temperature derating:** VESC explicitly has separate `l_temp_motor_start` and `l_temp_motor_end` configuration points. That is a good model for the PA6 motor-NTC [Negative Temperature Coefficient] sensor we have reconstructed, except initially ours should remain resistance-based until the NTC curve is characterized. citeturn140957search0turn140957search2
- **Current-command interface:** VESC distinguishes duty, motor-current, braking-current, RPM [Revolutions Per Minute], and position requests. That's a good API [Application Programming Interface] design for our eventual RC [Radio Control] and Pi [Raspberry Pi] layers. citeturn912929search0
- **Communication timeout/failsafe:** VESC's communication model includes a timeout and recommends continuously refreshing commands, with loss of communication causing the motor command to expire. That is exactly the kind of failsafe we want for an RC mower/snowplow. citeturn351604search4
- **Hall/FOC configuration concepts:** VESC exposes Hall tables, current-control gains, motor resistance, inductance, flux linkage, observer settings, and speed-control parameters cleanly in one motor configuration structure. citeturn140957search1
- **Telemetry protocol:** A reduced VESC-style UART [Universal Asynchronous Receiver/Transmitter] protocol could be very useful. VESC already has commands such as `GET_VALUES`, `SET_CURRENT`, `SET_CURRENT_BRAKE`, and `SET_RPM`. citeturn912929search0 There is even an official example showing the VESC packet protocol used over UART from another STM32. citeturn351604search3

I would **not replace EFeru's FOC core with VESC's `mcpwm_foc.c` at this stage**. VESC's current FOC implementation is about 4,500 source lines in a 172 KB source file and pulls in `mc_interface`, ChibiOS, STM32F4 headers, encoder support, commands, timing, filters, and other infrastructure. citeturn256727search7 EFeru's generated FOC core is much more realistic for this MCU [Microcontroller Unit].

Instead, I would reorganize our firmware so it looks conceptually more like this:

```text
Hardware layer
GD32F130 + Gen2.1.20
    │
    ├── ADC [Analog-to-Digital Converter]
    ├── PWM [Pulse-Width Modulation]
    ├── Hall sensors
    ├── PA6 motor NTC
    ├── IMU [Inertial Measurement Unit]
    └── UART
          │
          ▼
EFeru FOC motor-control core
          │
          ▼
Motor interface
    ├── set current
    ├── set brake current
    ├── set RPM
    ├── stop
    └── get motor state
          │
          ▼
VESC-inspired capability manager
    ├── absolute phase-current limit
    ├── I²t heating estimate
    ├── measured motor temperature
    ├── battery limits
    ├── speed limits
    ├── sensor validity
    └── fault state
          │
          ▼
Drive profiles
    ├── Normal
    ├── Snow
    ├── Boost
    └── future autonomous mode
```

One particularly useful VESC idea is its distinction between a **configured limit** and a **runtime overridden limit**. VESC has fields such as `l_current_max` and runtime `lo_current_max`. citeturn140957search1 That maps almost perfectly to what we're doing:

```c
configured_current_max
        ↓
profile_current_max
        ↓
thermal_current_max
        ↓
motor_ntc_current_max
        ↓
battery_current_max
        ↓
effective_current_max
```

The FOC controller only receives the final value.

I would also consider implementing a **small VESC-compatible UART subset** rather than inventing our own protocol from scratch. Initially we would only need something like:

```text
GET_VALUES
SET_CURRENT
SET_CURRENT_BRAKE
SET_RPM
ALIVE
GET_CUSTOM_VALUES
```

That could eventually let the Pi or another controller use existing VESC libraries and tooling. The full VESC command stack is much larger than we need, so I would write a compact implementation matching only the useful packet IDs and framing rather than bring across the whole communications subsystem. VESC itself exposes precisely those command types. citeturn912929search0turn912929search1

There is also no licensing conflict with the direction we've already taken: current VESC firmware is GPL-3.0 [GNU General Public License version 3], the RoboDurden Gen2.x-GD32 project is GPL-3.0, and EFeru's firmware source is likewise GPL-3-or-later. citeturn161422search0turn872640search0turn872640search1 If we directly copy/adapt VESC source, we should preserve its copyright/license notices and keep our combined firmware GPL-compatible.

So I would make **VESC the architectural/reference layer**, **Gen2.1.20 the hardware layer**, and **EFeru the compact FOC layer**. That gives us much of VESC's mature motor-controller behavior without trying to squeeze an STM32F4/ChibiOS firmware into a GD32F130.

A useful next firmware revision would therefore be to incorporate three VESC-derived concepts first: **runtime/configured limit separation, command-timeout/failsafe behavior, and the compact VESC-style UART command interface**. Those give us substantial functionality without consuming the C8's limited flash with the full VESC stack.

The Superpowers plugin can also work directly with source trees for this kind of multi-repository porting and build/debug work if you connect it.

Node: 9c16a261-b4e4-42fb-913d-0500042d3a8f
Parent node: 18382688-75aa-49b5-be16-36bde1f8a308

Yes. The parts of VESC [Vedder Electronic Speed Controller] that fit this project best are the **controller architecture and service layers**, not the full VESC firmware.

I’d use VESC as a reference/source for these pieces:

- runtime vs configured current limits;
- motor-temperature derating;
- current/torque, brake-current, and RPM [Revolutions Per Minute] command interfaces;
- communication timeout/failsafe behavior;
- fault-state handling;
- telemetry/command protocol, probably a deliberately small VESC-compatible UART [Universal Asynchronous Receiver/Transmitter] subset.

I would **not** replace the EFeru FOC [Field-Oriented Control] core with VESC’s full `mcpwm_foc.c` on the GD32F130C8. VESC’s current firmware assumes a much larger STM32F4-class environment with ChibiOS [Chibi Operating System] and a large supporting stack, while our MCU [Microcontroller Unit] only has 64 KiB flash and 8 KiB SRAM [Static Random-Access Memory]. The VESC FOC source also depends heavily on the rest of that ecosystem. citeturn0search0turn0search3

The clean architecture would be:

```text
Gen2.1.20 GD32 hardware layer
        ↓
EFeru FOC motor-control core
        ↓
VESC-inspired motor interface
        ↓
global adaptive capability manager
        ↓
Normal / Snow / Boost / autonomous modes
        ↓
VESC-style telemetry + command interface
```

That would give us something closer to a proper motor controller instead of just a hoverboard hack.

A particularly good VESC concept for us is the separation between a **configured motor-current maximum** and a **runtime effective limit**. In our version that would become roughly:

```text
configured hardware maximum
        ↓
profile request
        ↓
motor NTC limit
        ↓
I²t heating limit
        ↓
battery limit
        ↓
sensor/fault limit
        ↓
effective Iq/current limit
```

Every mode would pass through that same chain.

For communication, I’d strongly consider implementing a **small VESC-compatible packet layer** with only what we actually need:

```text
GET_VALUES
SET_CURRENT
SET_CURRENT_BRAKE
SET_RPM
ALIVE
GET_CUSTOM_VALUES
```

VESC already exposes those command concepts, so the Pi [Raspberry Pi] could eventually use existing VESC libraries rather than a completely custom protocol. citeturn0search8turn0search9

For the motor temperature side, VESC already has the idea of separate motor-temperature derating start/end points. That maps well to PA6 once we finish identifying and calibrating the NTC [Negative Temperature Coefficient] sensor. citeturn0search12

Licensing is also compatible with this direction: VESC is GPL-3.0 [GNU General Public License version 3], so directly adapted VESC code can coexist with the GPL-based hoverboard firmware, provided the notices and license obligations are preserved. citeturn0search1turn0search10

The next sensible firmware step would be to add three VESC-derived pieces first: **configured/runtime limit separation, watchdog-style command timeout, and a compact VESC-compatible UART command layer**. Those give us substantial benefit without dragging the full VESC firmware onto the C8.

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-16T15:13:42.838394+00:00

Yes. I searched the current Gen2.1.20 source and the long Gen2.1.20 hardware-discovery thread specifically for `PA6`. There are a few important references, and they change how confidently we should treat PA6.

The **current `defines_2-1-20.h` does not use PA6 at all**. It defines `PB0` and `PA0` as the two phase-current inputs, `PA4` as battery voltage, and leaves the DC-bus current input undefined. fileciteturn266file0

The interesting references are historical. In issue #90, which is specifically the Gen2.1.20/ClassyWalk board investigation, PA6 was initially included as an **analog-capable candidate pin** during autodetection. fileciteturn270file0 At one point PA6 was also being used as `CURRENT_DC` [direct-current bus current], and a user reported that this caused false current limiting and reduced unloaded speed from roughly 450 RPM [revolutions per minute] to 380 RPM because the PA6 reading did not track actual current. fileciteturn270file0

RoboDurden's immediate workaround was effectively: **do not define that current pin**, so the bogus PA6 reading does not trigger the current limiter. fileciteturn270file1

Then the hardware tracing became more useful. DDoru reported:

> “PA6 is just monitoring 3.3v I think, also to an test point which is not connected anywhere.”

and separately traced the dual op-amp current-sense outputs to **PA0 and PB0**, which is exactly what the current 2.1.20 definition now uses. fileciteturn270file0

So the repo's development history looks approximately like this:

```text
early 2.1.20 investigation
        ↓
PA6 considered as possible analog/current input
        ↓
CURRENT_DC = PA6 tried
        ↓
current limiting behaves incorrectly
        ↓
PCB [printed circuit board] traced
        ↓
PA0 + PB0 identified as phase-current op-amp outputs
        ↓
PA6 removed from current definition
        ↓
current 2.1.20 file:
    PHASE_A = PB0
    PHASE_B = PA0
    PA6 = unused
```

There is one other PA6 reference in that same issue: someone got `REMOTE_UARTBUS` working on **PA6/PA7** as an alternate serial pair during testing. fileciteturn270file6 That tells us PA6 is electrically accessible and can function as a GPIO [general-purpose input/output]/alternate-function pin, but that is separate from its stock analog role.

Also, PA6 being used as `CURRENT_DC` is very common in **other Gen2 layouts**—for example 2.1.1, 2.1.2, 2.1.6, 2.1.10, 2.1.11, 2.1.16, 2.1.18, and 2.1.22. fileciteturn265file2 fileciteturn265file3 fileciteturn265file6 fileciteturn265file7 fileciteturn265file10 fileciteturn265file11 This makes it quite plausible that the early PA6 current assignment for 2.1.20 was inherited from assumptions that apply to other Gen2 boards rather than from confirmed tracing of this board.

The key caution is that this **does not yet prove our factory-firmware PA6 interpretation wrong**. The GitHub comment saying PA6 monitors 3.3 V explicitly says “I think,” while our stock-firmware disassembly showed ADC [analog-to-digital converter] channel 6 being processed through resistance-like threshold logic. Those two pieces of evidence are in tension.

So I would now change our working status from:

```text
PA6 = tentative motor NTC
```

to:

```text
PA6 = unresolved analog input

Known:
- not phase current
- not used by current Gen2.1.20
- physically accessible
- stock firmware appears to sample ADC channel 6
- Gen2 issue tracing suggests a 3.3 V/test-point connection
- factory disassembly suggests a resistance/threshold function
```

Before we let PA6 participate in the adaptive thermal limiter, I think we should resolve that contradiction. The highest-value next step is to trace **the unidentified extra motor wire against PA6 and ground with a meter**, because that will tell us very quickly whether our motor-NTC hypothesis survives.

========================================================================
Yes — that makes the identification considerably stronger.

From your tracing, the red-circled pad is connected to the **extra motor wire**, and that wire goes onto the PCB [printed circuit board] inside the motor. Combined with what we found in the factory firmware, the evidence now lines up very well:

- The factory firmware samples **PA6 / ADC_IN6 [analog-to-digital converter input 6]**.
- It treats that value as a resistance using approximately  
  \[
  R = 10000 \times \frac{ADC}{FS-ADC}
  \]
  which is characteristic of a **10 kΩ resistive sensor divider**.
- It compares that calculated resistance against about **8 kΩ and 6 kΩ**, consistent with an NTC [negative temperature coefficient] thermistor getting hotter and dropping in resistance.
- You have now physically traced the corresponding board connection into the motor, rather than to something on the controller heatsink.

So I would now describe **motor temperature sensing as the leading explanation with high confidence**, rather than just a possibility.

There is one particularly useful check we can do on the controller board. On the GD32F130C8T6 MCU [microcontroller unit], **PA6 is physical pin 16**, and it is specifically capable of `ADC_IN6`. citeturn597293search12turn597293search15

With the dot/notch orientation shown in your image, PA6 should be the **fourth MCU leg along the bottom edge starting from the left**:

```text
              GD32F130C8T6
          ┌──────────────────┐
          │                  │
          │                  │
          │                  │
          └──────────────────┘
             │ │ │ │ │ │ ...
            13 14 15 16
                     ↑
                    PA6
```

That is also visually in the correct general area for the trace/components next to your circled pad.

### Two measurements would essentially settle it

With power disconnected:

1. Measure **resistance from the circled pad to MCU pin 16 (PA6)**.
   - It might not be exactly 0 Ω because there could be a small series resistor/filter network.
   - Follow the PCB [printed circuit board] trace/components if necessary.

2. Disconnect the motor from the controller and measure:
   **extra motor wire → Hall ground**
   - If this is the NTC [negative temperature coefficient] sensor, you should get a finite resistance, probably in the kΩ range.
   - Warm the motor gently with a hair dryer or heat gun on low.
   - The resistance should **decrease steadily as temperature rises**.

That second test is particularly definitive.

One nuance: if the NTC [negative temperature coefficient] is mounted on the small PCB [printed circuit board] inside the motor, it may technically be measuring **motor PCB/stator-area temperature**, rather than being buried directly in the copper winding. For our firmware purposes, though, it is still the physical motor thermal sensor and is far more useful than estimating motor temperature solely from current.

If you give me the resistance **extra wire → Hall ground at room temperature**, we can probably determine whether it is a nominal **10 kΩ NTC** and begin identifying its likely Beta curve.

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-16T15:33:29.864390+00:00
Node: 1d661ea5-621d-48df-8a4e-edf4133a6049
Parent node: b2f5f99b-6daf-40d0-94d2-d2f5955b33c1
========================================================================
That makes the motor-temperature interpretation much stronger.

If the PCB [printed circuit board] carrying that sensor is **thermally bonded to the stator**, then the sensor is effectively measuring **stator temperature** rather than just free-air temperature inside the hub.

That is exactly the quantity we care about for protecting the motor, because the stator carries the copper windings and accumulates most of the heat generated by \(I^2R\) losses.

So the physical picture is now likely:

```text
stator / windings
      ↓ thermal conduction
sensor PCB
      ↓
NTC [negative temperature coefficient] sensor
      ↓
extra motor wire
      ↓
controller PA6 ADC [analog-to-digital converter]
```

There will still be some thermal lag between the copper windings and the glued PCB, but much less than if the sensor were just hanging in the motor cavity. For our control logic, that means PA6 should be treated as a **direct motor thermal feedback signal**, while the current-based I²t model acts as a faster predictive layer.

That combination is actually ideal:

```text
current rises
   ↓
I²t model predicts heating immediately

stator actually warms
   ↓
PA6 NTC confirms real temperature

both feed the current limit
   ↓
lower permitted current wins
```

So I would now structure the thermal protection as:

```text
motor protection
├── fast model: I²t from phase current
└── measured model: stator NTC via PA6
```

and keep the inverter/MOSFET [metal-oxide-semiconductor field-effect transistor] thermal estimate separate.

The next useful step is to characterize the sensor curve. We only need a few points, for example around:

```text
20°C
30°C
40°C
50°C
60°C
70°C
```

and record the extra-wire resistance to Hall ground at each temperature. From that we can usually determine whether it is a standard 10 kΩ NTC and estimate the Beta value well enough to convert PA6 into actual °C.

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-16T15:35:33.613050+00:00
Node: a728a163-4884-4a9d-b8d8-5836fd02476f
Parent node: e58d9447-6f9a-42b5-aef1-5e0da93dd9af

Yes. I think that is the better sequence.

For now, **PA6 should be diagnostic only**. We should monitor it and characterize the scaling before allowing it to affect motor current. The previous Phase-3 concept went a little too far by already feeding the tentative NTC [negative temperature coefficient] limit into the global capability limiter.

I would change the firmware logic to separate **monitoring** from **protection**:

```c
#define CAP_MONITOR_MOTOR_NTC        1
#define CAP_ENABLE_MOTOR_NTC_DERATE  0
```

With derating disabled, PA6 would still be sampled continuously and Live Expressions would expose:

```text
g5mon.motor_ntc_adc_raw
g5mon.motor_ntc_adc_filtered
g5mon.motor_ntc_mv
g5mon.motor_ntc_ohm
g5mon.motor_ntc_valid
```

I'd keep the **raw ADC [analog-to-digital converter] value as the authoritative measurement**. `motor_ntc_ohm` can initially be marked as *tentative*, using the divider equation we recovered from the factory firmware. That way, if our assumption about the divider orientation or scaling is slightly wrong, it cannot affect motor operation.

The initial CubeIDE view should therefore look roughly like:

```text
g5mon
 ├─ motor_ntc_adc_raw
 ├─ motor_ntc_adc_filtered
 ├─ motor_ntc_mv
 ├─ motor_ntc_ohm_tentative
 ├─ motor_ntc_valid
 │
 ├─ phase_a_adc
 ├─ phase_b_adc
 ├─ phase_a_offset
 ├─ phase_b_offset
 │
 ├─ current_rms_cA
 ├─ thermal_i2t_permille
 │
 ├─ hall_state
 ├─ speed_rpm
 │
 └─ fault_flags
```

Then we can test it without the motor-temperature reading having any control authority.

The useful calibration experiment will be to compare three things simultaneously:

```text
Stator temperature
        ↕
multimeter resistance:
extra motor wire → Hall ground
        ↕
PA6:
raw ADC → tentative calculated resistance
```

Because the sensor PCB [printed circuit board] is thermally glued to the stator, we should get a very clean relationship as it warms.

For example, we can record:

| Stator temperature | Multimeter resistance | PA6 raw ADC | Calculated resistance |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| ambient | — | — | — |
| ~30 °C | — | — | — |
| ~40 °C | — | — | — |
| ~50 °C | — | — | — |
| ~60 °C | — | — | — |

If the PA6-calculated resistance tracks the meter closely, then we've verified the electrical scaling. With several temperature/resistance points we can then determine the NTC Beta curve and convert it reliably to °C.

Only after that would I enable something like:

```c
#define CAP_ENABLE_MOTOR_NTC_DERATE  1
```

and feed it into:

```text
effective current limit =
    min(
        hardware current ceiling,
        I²t heating limit,
        measured stator-temperature limit,
        battery limit,
        inverter thermal limit
    )
```

That also means **the I²t current-heating model stays active independently**. PA6 won't replace that model; eventually the two will complement one another: I²t provides fast predictive protection while PA6 provides actual stator-temperature feedback.

So yes: **PA6 goes into the CubeIDE monitoring structure now, but not into the active thermal limiter until we've validated its scale.**

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-18T05:50:18.101113+00:00
Node: 1d732c86-4211-4146-884a-b06697490e56
Parent node: 1d1c13ca-5d36-479a-b6a6-dfddbfb9c35b
========================================================================
I examined the exact-name repository **[`mianos/esp32foc`](https://github.com/mianos/esp32foc)**. It is relevant to our project, but there is one important point up front: despite the repository name, its present motor controller is **not closed-loop FOC [field-oriented control]**. The author explicitly describes it as an **open-loop V/Hz [volts-per-hertz] controller** that borrows several FOC techniques.

That actually makes it useful to us as a source of specific ideas rather than as a replacement FOC core.

| Part of `esp32foc` | What it does | Relevance to our GD32F130 project |
|---|---|---|
| Two-phase current sensing | Measures \(I_a\), \(I_b\), reconstructs \(I_c=-(I_a+I_b)\) | **Highly relevant.** Same basic topology we have on PB0/PA0 |
| PWM-synchronized current sampling | Current sample is triggered from the PWM [pulse-width modulation] timing path | **Highly relevant.** Closely matches our R2 roadmap |
| Current-zero calibration | Averages ADC [analog-to-digital converter] readings with bridge off and recalibrates before use | **Highly relevant.** We already planned this |
| Motor identification | Measures \(R_s\), \(L_s\), and inverter dead-voltage | **Very interesting for us** |
| Clarke transform | Calculates stationary-frame current magnitude | Useful for diagnostics/fault detection |
| Third-harmonic PWM | Uses \(\sin\theta+\frac16\sin3\theta\) to increase DC-bus utilization | Potentially useful in our modulation backend |
| Fixed-point ISR | ISR [interrupt service routine] does only integer/Q15 calculations | Good model for our small MCU [microcontroller unit] |
| Rotor alignment | Applies a controlled stationary field before starting | Relevant to commissioning and FOC startup |
| Stall detection | Uses filtered current magnitude and persistence timing | Useful concept for mower/snow-pusher protection |
| \(R/L\) identification | Injects known voltages and observes current response | Particularly valuable for automatic commissioning |
| Wi-Fi/HTTP/OTA | ESP32-specific management layer | Not relevant to the GD32 motor board itself |
| Closed-loop \(I_d/I_q\) | **Not implemented** | EFeru/our future FOC remains necessary |
| Hall/encoder rotor tracking | Essentially absent | Cannot replace our Hall-based servo work |
| Motor thermal sensing | Not present | Our PA6 work remains separate |

The most interesting piece for us is probably its **automatic motor identification routine** in [`main/motor.cpp`](https://github.com/mianos/esp32foc/blob/main/main/motor.cpp).

It measures three parameters:

\[
R_s
\]

the phase winding resistance,

\[
L_s
\]

the phase inductance, and

\[
V_{dead}
\]

the effective inverter/dead-time voltage loss.

For resistance, it applies several small DC voltage/current operating points and fits approximately:

\[
V=RI+V_{dead}
\]

The slope gives winding resistance and the intercept estimates the voltage lost through the inverter/gate-driver/dead-time system.

For inductance it applies a voltage step and watches the current rise. It finds the time at which current reaches:

\[
1-\frac1e\approx63.2\%
\]

of its final value. Since:

\[
\tau=\frac{L}{R}
\]

it obtains inductance from:

\[
L=R\tau
\]

with adjustment for its line-to-line measurement topology.

### That would fit our commissioning system extremely well

Our existing roadmap has:

```text
DISARMED
CURRENT_ZERO
ALIGN
HALL_PHASE_CAL
READY
RUN
FAULT
```

I think we should eventually expand commissioning to something conceptually like:

```text
DISARMED
    ↓
CURRENT_ZERO
    ↓
MOTOR_R_TEST
    ↓
MOTOR_L_TEST
    ↓
INVERTER_DROP_TEST
    ↓
ALIGN
    ↓
HALL_PHASE_CAL
    ↓
READY
```

Then instead of manually entering motor parameters, each hoverboard motor/controller pair could characterize itself.

That becomes especially useful if we eventually implement a more complete FOC [field-oriented control] current controller because \(R_s\) and \(L_s\) are useful for controller tuning and observers.

### Its current-sensing structure also validates our direction

Their [`current_sense.cpp`](https://github.com/mianos/esp32foc/blob/main/main/current_sense.cpp) does basically what we've been planning:

```text
current sensor A ──→ ADC
current sensor B ──→ ADC

bridge disabled
      ↓
average zero-current offsets
      ↓
PWM-synchronized samples
      ↓
Ia
Ib
Ic = -(Ia + Ib)
```

The implementation recalibrates current-sensor bias when the motor is disabled/enabled because amplifier offsets drift.

That strongly supports our decision not to treat the startup ADC offsets as immutable constants.

One difference is important: `esp32foc` only needs its current measurements for **stall detection and diagnostics**, so its normal current sampling/control rate is about **1 kHz**, despite the PWM carrier being 20 kHz.

For our closed-loop FOC:

```text
esp32foc:
20 kHz PWM
    ↓
~1 kHz current processing
    ↓
stall diagnostics

our controller:
~16–20 kHz PWM
    ↓
PWM-positioned ADC sample
    ↓
FOC current loop
```

We need substantially tighter timing because \(I_d/I_q\) feedback actually controls the bridge.

### The ISR architecture is worth copying conceptually

The ESP32 code very deliberately keeps expensive work out of its PWM ISR [interrupt service routine].

The task calculates things like amplitude and phase increment. The ISR receives compact fixed-point values and performs only:

```text
phase accumulator
      ↓
sine lookup table
      ↓
third-harmonic term
      ↓
scale
      ↓
PWM compare registers
```

It uses a 256-entry sine LUT [lookup table] and Q15 fixed point.

This is particularly relevant to the GD32F130C8 because **we have much less CPU/RAM headroom than an ESP32**. Our eventual production FOC loop should follow the same general principle:

```text
slow/background work
    motor models
    temperature
    limits
    command interpretation
    telemetry
         ↓

fast motor ISR
    ADC currents
    Clarke/Park
    PI current loops
    inverse Park
    modulation
    PWM update
```

No logging, configuration work, or expensive supervisory logic in the fast loop.

### The third-harmonic modulation is interesting too

Instead of plain sinusoidal PWM, it generates approximately:

\[
v(\theta)=\sin(\theta)+\frac16\sin(3\theta)
\]

That is a standard way of obtaining bus utilization approaching SVPWM [space-vector pulse-width modulation] without implementing a full sector-based SVPWM algorithm.

It is something we could consider later as an alternative modulation backend:

```text
FOC voltage vector
       ↓
inverse Park
       ↓
α/β voltage
       ↓
modulation backend

       ├── normal sinusoidal
       ├── third-harmonic injected
       └── SVPWM
```

I wouldn't change our current bring-up path for it yet, but it is worth recording.

### One thing I would **not** copy

Their stall detector computes:

\[
|I|=\sqrt{I_\alpha^2+I_\beta^2}
\]

then heavily filters it and trips when the motor is commanded above a certain electrical speed but current stays excessive.

That's appropriate for their **open-loop V/Hz pump** because rotor pull-out is the failure they're trying to detect.

Once ours is running proper FOC with Hall feedback, we know considerably more:

```text
commanded wheel speed
actual Hall speed
Iq
Id
phase current
IMU [inertial measurement unit]
```

So ours can have much better stall/traction detection. For example:

```text
high Iq command
+ high phase current
+ very low wheel acceleration/speed
= mechanical stall / obstruction
```

versus:

```text
wheel speed diverges from vehicle/other-wheel motion
= likely traction loss
```

That will be much more useful for the snow pusher.

### There is also a licensing issue

I don't find a `LICENSE` file in `mianos/esp32foc`, and the source files I checked do not contain an obvious license header.

So we should **not directly copy its source code into our GPL [GNU General Public License] repository unless the author clarifies the license**.

We can absolutely use the engineering concepts and independently implement the algorithms—resistance measurement, RL [resistance-inductance] step identification, third-harmonic injection, etc.—but direct source transplantation should wait for licensing clarification.

### What I think is worth bringing into our planning branch

The strongest contribution from this repository is **not its actual motor controller**, because EFeru is already much closer to what we need. It's the commissioning/measurement machinery:

```text
1. automatic current-zero calibration

2. automatic winding resistance measurement

3. automatic winding inductance measurement

4. inverter/dead-time voltage characterization

5. PWM-synchronized sampling discipline

6. fixed-point/minimal fast-loop architecture

7. controlled rotor alignment

8. persisted per-motor calibration values
```

The **\(R_s/L_s/V_{dead}\) automatic identification** is particularly worth adding to our roadmap. It fits naturally between current-sense bring-up and full FOC commissioning and would reduce how much motor-specific information we have to manually determine.

If you want, I can next examine its **identification routine line-by-line and compare it against our GD32 power stage**, because that is probably the most valuable part of this repository for our controller.

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-18T05:57:55.323580+00:00
Node: f9d231f0-6990-4f39-bf15-bd28d0937331
Parent node: a0fd5972-c651-4db3-9e77-85177c62fac6
========================================================================
Yes. I’d split calibration into **commissioning calibration** versus **normal startup checks**.

The detailed motor identification should be something we deliberately run when a motor/controller pairing is commissioned, not something that happens every time the machine powers up.

A sensible structure is:

```text
Motor commissioned / changed
        ↓
FULL CALIBRATION
    current-sensor polarity/gain
    phase/Hall relationship
    Rs winding resistance
    Ls winding inductance
    inverter Vdead
    pole pairs / motor constants as applicable
        ↓
save validated calibration profile to flash
        ↓
NORMAL STARTUPS
    current-zero offset calibration
    ADC sanity checks
    Hall sanity
    PA6 monitoring
    battery/bus checks
    use saved motor parameters
        ↓
RUN
```

The parameters naturally fall into two groups.

| Parameter | Normal boot? | Persist in flash? |
|---|---:|---:|
| Current ADC [analog-to-digital converter] zero offsets | Re-measure every boot | No |
| PA6 raw/temperature state | Read every boot | No |
| Battery voltage | Read every boot | No |
| Hall state/sanity | Check every boot | No |
| \(R_s\) motor resistance baseline | No | Yes |
| \(L_s\) motor inductance | No | Yes |
| Hall/phase mapping | No | Yes |
| Motor pole pairs | No | Yes |
| Current-sensor gain/polarity | Normally no | Yes |
| Inverter \(V_{dead}\) | Normally no | Yes |
| NTC [negative temperature coefficient] calibration curve | No | Yes |

There is a nuance with \(R_s\): copper resistance changes appreciably with motor temperature. I would store the **calibrated reference resistance and its reference temperature**, rather than continuously rerunning the resistance-identification routine. Once PA6 is characterized, we can compensate approximately:

\[
R(T)=R_{ref}[1+\alpha(T-T_{ref})]
\]

where copper has a well-known positive temperature coefficient. That gives the controller a temperature-adjusted resistance estimate without energizing the motor for another identification run.

### Previous-startup fault behavior

I agree with your condition. A saved calibration shouldn't automatically be discarded because of an unrelated runtime fault.

I'd make the persistent record distinguish faults such as:

```text
FAULT_CAL_CURRENT_SENSOR
FAULT_CAL_PHASE_CURRENT
FAULT_CAL_HALL_MAPPING
FAULT_CAL_MOTOR_MODEL
FAULT_CAL_IDENTIFICATION
```

from operational faults such as:

```text
FAULT_OVERCURRENT
FAULT_MOTOR_OVERTEMP
FAULT_COMMAND_TIMEOUT
FAULT_LOW_BATTERY
FAULT_STALL
```

Then the next startup behaves accordingly.

```text
saved calibration valid
+ no calibration-related previous fault
        ↓
normal startup
        ↓
NO Rs/Ls calibration
```

But:

```text
saved calibration valid
+ previous startup registered a calibration/model fault
        ↓
enter commissioning/revalidation
        ↓
repeat the affected tests
```

We could even avoid unnecessarily repeating everything. For example, a current-sensor calibration failure doesn't require re-measuring \(L_s\).

### Flash layout

I'd give the calibration block its own versioned structure, something approximately like:

```c
MotorCalibration {
    magic;
    version;
    crc;

    motor_rs_ref;
    motor_rs_ref_temp;
    motor_ls;

    pole_pairs;

    hall_map;
    phase_map;

    current_gain_a;
    current_gain_b;
    current_polarity_a;
    current_polarity_b;

    inverter_vdead;

    ntc_model;
    ntc_parameters;

    calibration_valid;
}
```

with a separate small persistent fault/status record.

That separation is important because **calibration data changes very rarely**, whereas faults may change during normal use. We don't want to rewrite the large motor calibration flash record every time something happens.

And I would only write the fault record when something meaningful actually changes, rather than writing a "boot was good" flag every power cycle. That keeps flash wear negligible.

### Motor replacement

One limitation needs to be explicit: these hoverboard motors don't appear to provide a unique electronic motor ID. Therefore the GD32 cannot inherently know that you physically unplugged motor A and connected motor B.

So the intended workflow should be:

```text
same motor
→ use saved profile indefinitely

motor replaced
→ operator selects "commission/calibrate motor"
→ overwrite or create calibration profile
→ use new saved values
```

We could later add a **lightweight plausibility fingerprint** at startup—Hall behavior, very-low-energy resistance sanity check, etc.—but I would not make a full \(R/L\) identification part of every boot just to detect a motor swap.

This also fits very nicely with our commissioning state machine. I'd revise it conceptually to:

```text
POWER ON
   ↓
LOAD SAVED CALIBRATION
   ↓
CURRENT_ZERO
   ↓
SENSOR_SANITY
   ↓
previous calibration fault?
   ├── no  → READY
   └── yes → REVALIDATE / FULL CAL
```

and a deliberate commissioning command gets:

```text
FULL_CAL
 ├─ CURRENT_ZERO
 ├─ CURRENT_SENSOR_VALIDATE
 ├─ MOTOR_R_IDENT
 ├─ MOTOR_L_IDENT
 ├─ INVERTER_DROP_IDENT
 ├─ ALIGN
 ├─ HALL_PHASE_CAL
 ├─ VALIDATE
 └─ SAVE_TO_FLASH
```

That is the version I would put into the planning branch: **expensive motor identification is persistent commissioning data; normal startup only recalibrates genuinely dynamic quantities.**

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-18T06:09:32.520162+00:00

Parent node: dfc9a560-d9fc-4306-bd2a-c1044111dded

Yes. I would give the persistent calibration data **independent validity/fault status by calibration domain**, rather than one separate record for every individual scalar. That gives us enough granularity to know what needs revalidation without wasting flash or creating excessive bookkeeping.

For example, the stored calibration can track independent status for current sensing, \(R_s\) [stator resistance], \(L_s\) [stator inductance], Hall/phase mapping, inverter/dead-time model, NTC [negative temperature coefficient] temperature model, and overall calibration integrity/version. If only the NTC-related checks fail, there is no reason to automatically invalidate \(L_s\) or Hall mapping.

Your \(R_s\) idea is particularly useful. Once the NTC is characterized, startup can compare the measured low-energy winding resistance against the temperature-corrected stored \(R_s\):

\[
R(T)=R_{ref}[1+\alpha(T-T_{ref})]
\]

That gives us an independent sanity check on the temperature reading. Importantly, if \(R_s\) and the NTC disagree, the firmware should initially record a **cross-check fault**, not immediately declare the NTC bad. The discrepancy could also indicate a damaged winding, poor motor connector, changed motor, or bad resistance measurement.

I updated the planning branch accordingly. Normal startup now loads the saved calibration, recalibrates only dynamic zero offsets, performs low-energy sanity checks, compares startup \(R_s\) against the NTC-derived expected temperature, and only requests detailed revalidation when the relevant calibration domain has a fault.

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-18T06:13:05.029003+00:00
Node: e8149483-929d-4621-8406-d6067184772a
Parent node: 09bb70f6-87c0-449e-916d-4ac1cdc7c192
========================================================================
Mostly because we inherited the project in the wrong direction for it.

The upstream Gen2.x GD32 firmware is built around a Keil project and vendor GD32F1x0 libraries, and when we started planning the ServoFOC branch we prioritized **preserving a known build baseline** before changing the motor-control architecture. On top of that, you wanted one environment that could build, flash, debug over SWD [serial wire debug], and watch live variables, which pushed us toward CubeIDE/Keil-style debugging rather than PlatformIO.

But PlatformIO is actually more viable here than I gave it credit for. There is a community GD32 PlatformIO platform that explicitly supports the GD32F1x0 family, including generic GD32F130 targets, SPL [standard peripheral library], OpenOCD, ST-Link, and debugging. citeturn565509search0turn565509search8 There is even an older working GD32F130C6/C8 PlatformIO example using the vendor SPL. citeturn703355search1

For this project, PlatformIO would give us several real advantages:

- reproducible toolchain and dependencies in `platformio.ini`
- one-click build/upload/debug from VS Code
- easier CI [continuous integration] builds
- much cleaner handling of multiple build configurations
- no dependence on Keil project metadata
- straightforward ST-Link/OpenOCD integration
- easier migration between Windows and Linux

PlatformIO's debugger is GDB [GNU Debugger]-based and can use ST-Link/OpenOCD, including custom debug-server configurations. citeturn703355search0turn703355search2 OpenOCD itself has explicit GD32F1x0 support. citeturn703355search4

The main downside is that the GD32 PlatformIO support is **community-maintained and still labels itself work-in-progress**, rather than being an official PlatformIO GD32 platform. citeturn565509search0 That means we should not blindly trust its startup files, clock configuration, linker script, or flash settings. For a motor controller, those details matter.

I think the better architecture now would be:

```text
source code
GD32 vendor SPL
startup assembly
linker script
        │
        ├── PlatformIO build/debug
        │      VS Code
        │      ST-Link
        │      OpenOCD
        │      GDB
        │
        └── optional original Keil build
               used as reference until parity proven
```

So PlatformIO becomes our primary developer environment, but we initially keep the existing Keil build as a **reference build** while we prove that both produce equivalent memory layout, interrupt vectors, clock setup, and peripheral behavior.

That also suits our growing calibration system much better. We are going to end up with multiple useful environments, for example:

```ini
[env:bringup]
; conservative, bridge disabled

[env:debug]
; full symbols and monitoring

[env:motor_test]
; commissioning/calibration enabled

[env:release]
; production limits and optimization
```

PlatformIO handles that kind of build matrix much more cleanly than maintaining several IDE project configurations.

The one thing CubeIDE still does better for our immediate work is the **Live Expressions-style continuously refreshing variable view**. PlatformIO/VS Code can watch variables through GDB, but it is not quite as convenient for continuously watching a large `g5mon` structure while the motor is running. We don't have to choose exclusively, though: the ELF [executable and linkable format] file produced by PlatformIO can still be debugged by other GDB-compatible tools, and later our telemetry stream will be better than either IDE for live motor data.

So, at this point, I think **PlatformIO deserves to be the primary build/upload/debug system for the ServoFOC branch**, with the original Keil build retained temporarily as our parity reference. That is a change I would make to the planning branch before we start R1 implementation.

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-18T13:15:34.560677+00:00
Node: 7baf3223-643e-4166-8147-d2f57b3a2058
Parent node: 5c18ce73-a715-43b0-bb1a-cdcd5e7ad597
========================================================================
Yes. After reviewing `RemoteAutodetect`, I think it is **very useful to our project**, but mostly as the basis for **commissioning/initial discovery and startup validation**, not as a module we leave running unchanged during normal operation.

The important distinction is that `RemoteAutodetect` is quite invasive. It dynamically changes GPIO [general-purpose input/output] modes, switches candidate pins between analog/input/pull-up/pull-down/output modes, changes ADC [analog-to-digital converter] channel assignments, deliberately drives the motor open-loop, and writes discovered results to persistent configuration. As currently written, it is a service/commissioning program rather than a passive monitor.

### What is worth reusing

| `RemoteAutodetect` function | Our use |
|---|---|
| Candidate-pin table with ADC capability | Initial board discovery |
| Hide already-claimed pins | Discovery safety |
| Hall pin identification | Commissioning |
| Hall-order identification | **Definitely reuse** |
| Hall-transition validation | **Runtime monitoring too** |
| Phase-current pin detection | Discovery clue, then independently validate |
| Battery-sense discovery | Initial discovery |
| Self-hold/button discovery | Initial board discovery |
| LED/buzzer discovery | Service utility only |
| `CURRENT_DC` discovery | **Do not use on our 2.1.20 board** |
| Persistent discovered mapping | Concept useful, storage format should change |
| Serial/RTT interactive menu | Potential commissioning interface |

The **new Hall-order code is particularly good material**. It does more than simply try six permutations. It records a complete six-state Hall cycle, then watches **18 transitions—three complete cycles**. It rejects transitions where more than one Hall bit changes, checks that all six legal Hall states have appeared with the `0x7e` state mask, and correlates the transitions with the commutation position that was actually being applied.

That is almost exactly what we want for our `HALL_PHASE_CAL` commissioning stage.

More importantly, half of that algorithm can remain active during normal operation without doing anything invasive. We can continuously monitor:

```text
raw Hall state
       ↓
must be 1..6
       ↓
only one bit may change per transition
       ↓
transition must match expected sequence/direction
       ↓
transition timing
       ↓
missed/duplicate/illegal transition counters
```

So the Hall logic naturally divides into:

```text
Commissioning:
actively determine Hall pin assignment + Hall/phase relationship

Runtime:
passively verify Hall behavior against saved calibration
```

That gives us substantially better fault detection than simply reading the Hall state.

### There is one part we specifically should disable

The `AUTODETECT_Stage_CurrentDC` stage is not appropriate for our Gen2.1.20 hardware.

It scans candidate ADC [analog-to-digital converter] pins while deliberately alternating the motor command roughly:

```text
+700
-700
+700
-700
```

and looks for the candidate producing the expected regenerative current response.

That makes sense on Gen2 boards that actually have a `CURRENT_DC` [direct-current bus current] sensor.

Our 2.1.20 board does **not**. We have the two phase-current measurements on PA0 and PB0 instead.

This is also quite possibly how PA6 ended up being incorrectly assigned as `CURRENT_DC` in some earlier 2.1.20 work: the generic autodetection machinery expects such a signal to exist and searches for one.

For our target, the discovery table should instead effectively say:

```text
PHASE_CURRENT_A    unknown → discover/confirm
PHASE_CURRENT_B    unknown → discover/confirm
CURRENT_DC         NOT PRESENT
PA6                analog input, purpose initially unclassified
```

Then PA6 gets characterized separately as we are already planning.

### The phase-current detection is useful, but I wouldn't trust it by itself

During its Hall-pin scanning, `RemoteAutodetect` also notices ADC-capable pins that transition much faster than Hall inputs and records them as `PHASE_A`, `PHASE_B`, `PHASE_C`.

That's clever for finding an unknown board, but it is heuristic. For our ServoFOC project I would use it as:

```text
autodetect says:
PA0 and PB0 look like phase-current signals
             ↓
low-energy current injection
             ↓
verify amplitude
verify polarity
verify zero offset
verify response to commanded phase
             ↓
VALIDATED
```

In other words, **discovery tells us where to look; commissioning establishes that it is safe to trust**.

That fits very well with the per-domain calibration status we just designed.

### We should not use `RemoteAutodetect` itself as the runtime monitor

There is another architectural reason. In `defines.h`, selecting `REMOTE_AUTODETECT` changes the compile-time board configuration. It includes the generic `defines_2-ad.h` instead of the normal board layout definition and configures the firmware specifically for autodetection.

So rather than doing:

```text
ServoFOC firmware
    ↓
call RemoteAutodetect.c continuously
```

I would extract the reusable algorithms:

```text
                 ServoFOC
                    │
        ┌───────────┴────────────┐
        │                        │
 CommissioningDiscovery     RuntimeMonitor
        │                        │
 active tests               passive only
 pin discovery              Hall validity
 Hall calibration           transition errors
 phase-current verify       current-sensor health
 motor identification       PA6 monitoring
 Rs/Ls calibration          calibration sanity
        │                        │
        └───────────┬────────────┘
                    │
            CalibrationStore
```

That prevents the runtime code from accidentally reconfiguring a pin that the motor controller is actively using.

### I would also change how autodetect saves results

`ConfigWriteAD()` currently stores the **index of a pin in the `aoPin[]` candidate array**, rather than the canonical physical pin value itself.

That works within the existing autodetect firmware, but it is not ideal for our persistent motor/controller calibration. If the candidate array changes between firmware revisions, an index could potentially acquire a different meaning unless the configuration version is changed perfectly every time.

For ours, I'd store something unambiguous, conceptually:

```text
port = GPIOA
pin  = 0
function = PHASE_CURRENT_A
status = VALIDATED
```

or the equivalent packed GD32 pin identifier.

Also, `ConfigWriteAD()` writes the configuration whenever discoveries are made. For commissioning we should instead accumulate discoveries in RAM [random-access memory]:

```text
discover
   ↓
test
   ↓
cross-check
   ↓
validate everything
   ↓
ONE flash commit
```

That is safer against partial calibration and avoids unnecessary flash writes.

### It can improve our startup sanity checking too

There are parts of the Hall autodetection logic that fit perfectly into the startup checks we discussed.

For example, immediately after power-up we can passively check that the saved Hall pins produce:

```text
001
010
011
100
101
110
```

and never:

```text
000
111
```

At standstill we obviously see only one state, so that doesn't prove the entire mapping. But as soon as the wheel moves—even during the first low-energy commissioning/alignment movement—we can verify that each transition is a one-bit transition and that the order agrees with the saved calibration.

Similarly, the saved phase-current channels can be checked during the very small excitation we use for the \(R_s\) measurement. That single low-energy startup test could verify several things at once:

```text
small known voltage excitation
          │
          ├── winding responds
          ├── Rs plausible
          ├── phase-current ADC A responds
          ├── phase-current ADC B responds
          ├── current polarity plausible
          └── NTC temperature vs Rs plausibility
```

That's much better than running several unrelated invasive tests.

### One thing I noticed in our planning branch

The `RemoteAutodetect.c` currently present on `servo/r0-baseline` **does not contain an explicit `HALL_INVERT_ALL` path**. The newer Hall-order algorithm may handle some inverted sequences through the mapping it derives, but it does not persist an obvious Hall-polarity/inversion field there.

Given the earlier work we did on opposite Hall/phase parity, I would explicitly make **Hall polarity/parity part of the saved Hall calibration**, rather than assuming pin permutation alone always solves it.

So my recommendation is:

**Use `RemoteAutodetect` as the foundation for our commissioning/discovery system, and extract its passive Hall validation logic into the runtime monitor. Do not run the existing complete autodetect state machine during normal operation.**

The most valuable pieces for us are the Hall discovery/order algorithm, generic pin-discovery framework, and the idea of validating discovered hardware before creating a persistent board/motor profile. The `CURRENT_DC` [direct-current bus current] stage should specifically be excluded for 2.1.20.

I think this is significant enough to add to **R5 commissioning and persistent calibration** in our roadmap, with `RemoteAutodetect` explicitly named as the source/reference for the discovery logic.

Parent node: 7baf3223-643e-4166-8147-d2f57b3a2058

so should we do some minor changes and use the autodetect for its intended purpose of pin function discovery, that way we can confirm the pin assignment that we have.

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-18T13:27:12.114389+00:00
Node: 23eff807-592c-4a3d-98ba-dce8444e592d
Parent node: ba917547-2899-4591-863b-03f7b398e2f8
========================================================================
Yes — with limits, that is the right next move.

We should use `RemoteAutodetect` for its intended job of **pin-function discovery**, but with a **trimmed and board-aware version**, not completely stock.

For your board, the goal is not “blindly autodetect everything and trust it.” The goal is:

1. confirm the pins we already think we know,
2. discover anything still uncertain,
3. avoid misleading detections from features your board probably does **not** have.

### My recommendation

Use autodetect as a **service/commissioning build** for one board first, with a few minor but important changes:

#### 1. Keep these detection stages
These are the ones most worth using:

- **Hall pin detection**
- **Hall order detection**
- **LED pin detection**
- **button / self-hold detection**
- **VBATT [battery voltage] detection**
- **phase-current candidate detection** as a clue only

Those are directly useful for confirming board pin assignments.

#### 2. Disable or fence off `CURRENT_DC [direct-current current]` autodetect
For your board, this is the main one I would not trust as-is.

Reason: on the Gen2.1.20-style hardware, we already suspect there is **no true DC bus current sense** in the same sense the generic autodetect expects. If we leave that stage active, it can “find” a pin that merely correlates with motor activity and give us a false assignment.

So the change should be:

- either **remove `AUTODETECT_Stage_CurrentDC` entirely**, or
- leave it in but mark output as **UNVERIFIED_CANDIDATE** and never auto-save it as a final definition.

#### 3. Add PA6 to the “watch closely” list
Since PA6 is one of the important unknowns for your board, we should explicitly keep it visible in the autodetect process and not let it get misclassified too early.

That means:

- do **not** let PA6 become auto-assigned as `CURRENT_DC`
- if it shows analog variation during motor warmup or operation, log it
- compare it against motor behavior later in the monitor build

#### 4. Change “save immediately” behavior
Stock autodetect writes discoveries fairly eagerly. For our use, better flow is:

```text
detect candidate
→ print result
→ manually confirm
→ only then save to config
```

That avoids contaminating saved config with one bad guess.

### So yes, but use it in two passes

I would do this in two distinct passes.

#### Pass A: discovery build
Use modified autodetect to answer:

- Which pins are Hall A/B/C?
- What Hall order is valid?
- Which pins are on/off button and self-hold?
- Which pins drive the LEDs?
- Which pin reads VBATT?
- Which ADC pins appear associated with phase current?
- Does PA6 behave like a temperature or analog telemetry signal?

This gives us a **candidate map**.

#### Pass B: verification build
Then use the monitor / commissioning build to verify the important ones:

- Hall states are legal and sequenced properly
- phase current channels respond correctly
- VBATT scaling is plausible
- PA6 scales plausibly with motor temperature, if that is what it is
- no “candidate” gets promoted to “real pin assignment” without evidence

That second pass is what turns autodetect from a guesser into a dependable board characterization tool.

### What I would change in the code before using it

For your board specifically, I would make these minor changes:

- default menu should expose:
  - `Hall`
  - `HallOrder`
  - `VBATT`
  - `Hold`
  - `Button`
  - `LED`
- disable:
  - `CurrentDC` auto-save
- add extra logging:
  - raw Hall state
  - detected Hall permutation
  - candidate phase-current pins
  - PA6 ADC value
- change save logic:
  - discoveries print first
  - save only on explicit command
- optionally add a summary output block like:
  ```c
  #define HALL_A       Pxx
  #define HALL_B       Pxx
  #define HALL_C       Pxx
  #define VBATT        Pxx
  #define SELF_HOLD    Pxx
  #define BUTTON       Pxx
  #define PHASE_A_CAND Pxx
  #define PHASE_B_CAND Pxx
  #define PA6_ROLE     UNKNOWN_ANALOG
  ```

That would make the results much easier to transfer into the real firmware.

### Practical answer

So the short answer is:

**Yes, we should make a lightly modified autodetect build and use it for pin-function discovery.**  
That is the cleanest way to confirm the pin assignments we think we have already, especially Halls, Hall order, self-hold, button, LEDs, and voltage sense.

But we should **not** treat all autodetect outputs as equally trustworthy. On your board:

- Hall-related results: high value
- VBATT / button / hold / LED results: useful
- `CURRENT_DC`: likely misleading unless specially adapted
- PA6: observe and log, do not prematurely classify

If you want, the next thing I can do is lay out the **exact minimal edits** to `RemoteAutodetect.c` and the related `defines` file so you can build a **safe discovery version** for your board.

`Arcturis144/Hoverboard-Firmware-Hack-Gen2.x-GD32-ServoFOC`
→ parent/source:
`RoboDurden/Hoverboard-Firmware-Hack-Gen2.x-GD32`

That fork relationship does **not** mean our commits automatically go back into RoboDurden’s repository. The commits we have been making are going specifically to **your `Arcturis144/...ServoFOC` repository**, and I also checked for open pull requests from both `servo/r0-baseline` and `main` into RoboDurden’s repository: there are **none**.

So currently there is no path where our work is automatically being pushed or merged upstream. A change would only get into RoboDurden’s repository if somebody deliberately opened a pull request there and RoboDurden accepted it.

ASSISTANT | 2026-09-19T18:46:23.859772+00:00
Node: b9612645-a7e4-40b5-9bef-2dcd752f494f
Parent node: 18a7ebcc-8d33-48e9-97da-4453b75d31a2
========================================================================
Yes. I went through the complete current fork network and then checked the other relevant repositories and non-default branches belonging to those accounts, rather than looking only at each fork's `main` branch.

GitHub currently reports **29 forks** of `RoboDurden/Hoverboard-Firmware-Hack-Gen2.x-GD32`, and I was able to account for all 29. Most are essentially snapshots with no unique work. A handful, however, contain things that are quite useful to our ServoFOC [servo field-oriented control] project.

| Source | What I found | Usefulness to us |
|---|---|---|
| **hoverboardhavoc – `foc-reimplement-from-spec-tidy`** | A substantial GD32F130C8 / layout 2-1-20 FOC [field-oriented control] implementation: synchronized phase-current sampling, Clarke/Park transforms, SVPWM [space-vector pulse-width modulation], Hall PLL [phase-locked loop], Hall-sector learning, PI [proportional-integral] current control, anti-windup, DTC [dead-time compensation], host tests | **Extremely high**. Probably the single most useful code source I found |
| **hoverboardhavoc – `hoverboard-gen2.1-hack-GD-imu`** | Firmware specifically ported to a ClassyWalk-style split GD32F130C8 motherboard. Includes working IMU [inertial measurement unit] investigation and PlatformIO support | **Extremely high** for our exact board family |
| **hoverboardhavoc – `HoverboardImu`** | Raw investigation of the mystery onboard IMU [inertial measurement unit]; device at I²C [inter-integrated circuit] address `0x68`, raw register dump, testing against several common IMU [inertial measurement unit] families | **High** for confirming our onboard IMU [inertial measurement unit] |
| **hoverboardhavoc – `gd32-pio-spl-package`** | Modified GD32 SPL [standard peripheral library] package used successfully with PlatformIO on GD32F130C8 | **High** for our planned PlatformIO migration |
| **hoverboardhavoc – `regtrace`** | Tooling that compares actual peripheral register programming between GD32 SPL [standard peripheral library], libopencm3, STM32-style implementations, etc. It includes tests modeled on hoverboard timer/ADC [analog-to-digital converter] operation | **High later**, especially for validating our low-level timer/ADC [analog-to-digital converter] setup |
| **WestlingPi – `debug-safety-limit` and related branches** | Command sanity checking, invalid-command counters, IMU [inertial measurement unit] telemetry, ROS2 [Robot Operating System 2] work, speed-feedback corrections | **High** for command ingress/failsafe design |
| **Homobonus – `spatialrag-agv`** | Link-age telemetry, master/slave communication diagnostics, direction fixes, command-mode-specific range handling, host UART [universal asynchronous receiver-transmitter] decoder/logger | **High** for communications and diagnostics |
| **Jodaille – sideboard firmware account** | A complete FlySky iBUS [FlySky digital serial bus] receiver parser, including checksum validation and timeout-to-zero behavior | **Very high** because you already have the FS-iA6B receiver |
| **lucai11 – `inserted_adc`** | Hardware-triggered inserted ADC [analog-to-digital converter] conversions and changes to make full RemoteAutodetect fit below 32 KiB | **Useful reference**, particularly for our discovery firmware |
| **YujiKF – `FirmwareHoverCR`** | ESP32 [Espressif 32-bit microcontroller] wireless bridge, remote control, telemetry, adjustable PID [proportional-integral-derivative], command timeout | **Useful later** for wireless service/control architecture |
| **RobDoozer / Erik-96 – HUGS [Hoverboard Utility Gateway System] lineage** | Old but interesting Gen2 dual-board robot controller: speed servo, low-speed sinusoidal stepping, Hall odometry, serial protocol, watchdog, ESTOP [emergency stop], telemetry | **Useful conceptually**, particularly servo/robot behavior |
| **adj00080 – FOC [field-oriented control] ConeRobot fork** | Large number of hands-on phase, ADC [analog-to-digital converter], ESP32 [Espressif 32-bit microcontroller], PWM [pulse-width modulation], and motor experiments | **Troubleshooting reference**, but too experimental to import as-is |
| **filagyuri – FOC [field-oriented control] bbcar fork** | Input validity handling, mode switching, emergency behavior, ADC [analog-to-digital converter] observations | **Moderate**, mostly safety/design ideas |

The biggest discovery is the **hoverboardhavoc account**. It is much more relevant to our hardware than I expected.

Their `foc-reimplement-from-spec-tidy` branch isn't just generic FOC [field-oriented control] code. It explicitly targets the **GD32F130C8 and layout 2-1-20**, and the author actually bench-tested it. It contains the pieces we have been planning independently: two-shunt phase-current reconstruction, PWM [pulse-width modulation]-synchronized current sampling, Clarke/Park transforms, Hall interpolation, torque-mode PI [proportional-integral] control and host-side unit tests.

Some particularly relevant commits are:

`b363f815` — ADC [analog-to-digital converter] valley-trigger scaffolding  
`d7162fb4` — first functioning FOC [field-oriented control] core  
`145674d4` — closed-loop torque/current control with anti-windup  
`7650dffa` — bench tuning and implementation notes  
`5d21dbe5` — TIMER0 repetition-counter timing change

I would **not cherry-pick that branch wholesale**. There are board-specific tuning values in it, and its open upstream PR [pull request] has had technical review identifying issues. But its pure mathematical core, tests, Hall PLL [phase-locked loop] implementation, ADC [analog-to-digital converter] timing approach and lessons from actual hardware are extremely valuable references. In some areas we can compare our implementation against theirs instead of independently rediscovering everything.

There is also a particularly important finding for our **IMU [inertial measurement unit]**. The separate `hoverboardhavoc/hoverboard-gen2.1-hack-GD-imu` repository is explicitly described as a port for a **split hoverboard motherboard with a GD32F130C8**, not the normal little sideboard. The author found that on the C8 the I²C [inter-integrated circuit] interrupt handlers did not run until these interrupt sources were explicitly enabled:

```c
i2c_interrupt_enable(MPU_I2C, I2C_INT_ERR);
i2c_interrupt_enable(MPU_I2C, I2C_INT_EV);
i2c_interrupt_enable(MPU_I2C, I2C_INT_BUF);
```

That is the sort of subtle hardware-specific issue that could have cost us days.

They also identify the mystery IMU [inertial measurement unit] marking as:

```text
16A
3C6A
C3C
```

and found it responds at I²C [inter-integrated circuit] address `0x68`. Their conclusion was similar to ours: it behaves sufficiently like an MPU6050 to obtain raw motion data, but its DMP [digital motion processor] does **not** appear to behave like a genuine MPU6050. They disabled DMP [digital motion processor] operation rather than depending on it. That makes our plan of using raw accelerometer/gyro data the sensible route.

That same project provides a **tested PlatformIO configuration for `genericGD32F130C8`**. It uses the hoverboardhavoc GD32 SPL [standard peripheral library] package and the internal 8 MHz oscillator / 72 MHz clock setup. That substantially strengthens the case for moving our project to PlatformIO; we're no longer talking about merely generic GD32F130 support—someone has already run PlatformIO on essentially this exact class of split board.

The scan also turned up something very useful for your **FlySky FS-iA6B**. Jodaille's account has the EFeru sideboard firmware, which contains an actual iBUS [FlySky digital serial bus] parser. It operates at 115200 baud, accepts the normal 32-byte iBUS [FlySky digital serial bus] channel packet, checks the iBUS [FlySky digital serial bus] checksum before accepting the command, and zeroes commands when the serial link times out. That gives us an existing implementation to adapt instead of writing the FS-iA6B receiver path from scratch.

There are also a couple of safety ideas I think should make it into ServoFOC [servo field-oriented control]. WestlingPi encountered corrupted-but-apparently-valid serial commands and changed the receiver so a physically impossible command causes **zero output rather than merely being clamped into the allowable range**. Homobonus separately added “age of last valid frame” telemetry so the controller can distinguish a stale communications link from a genuinely stationary or slow motor. Those fit our architecture very well:

```text
frame received
      │
      ▼
CRC/checksum valid?
      │
      ▼
command structurally valid?
      │
      ▼
command physically plausible?
      │
      ▼
command fresh enough?
      │
      ▼
safety state permits torque?
      │
      ▼
FOC current/speed/position command
```

That is considerably better than trusting a checksum alone.

For the **RemoteAutodetect** work we're discussing right now, `lucai11/inserted_adc` is worth looking at before we modify ours. That branch specifically contains a “full Autodetect under 32 kB” change and moves ADC [analog-to-digital converter] operation toward timer-triggered inserted conversions. I still would not adopt its generic `CURRENT_DC [direct-current current]` assumptions because they do not match our board, but some of the structural changes may let us build our safe discovery firmware without reinventing that part.

There is another nice piece of evidence supporting our use of autodetect: `russt6` used `REMOTE_AUTODETECT` to correct the Hall definitions for layout 2-1-18. So the tool has actually been used by other developers to determine real board pin mappings rather than merely being dead experimental code.

The older **HUGS [Hoverboard Utility Gateway System]** project is also more relevant than it first appears. It was explicitly built around split Gen2 controllers for robot/assistive applications. It isn't FOC [field-oriented control], but it already implemented Hall-based wheel position, linear-speed control, feed-forward plus PI [proportional-integral] regulation, a special low-speed sinusoidal drive mode, a watchdog, ESTOP [emergency stop], and a reasonably structured external command/telemetry protocol. I wouldn't reuse its motor-control core, but there are useful ideas there for our eventual **servo API [application programming interface]**, position accounting and low-speed behavior.

One more tool caught my attention: `hoverboardhavoc/regtrace`. That project can compare the *actual peripheral-register state* produced by different driver implementations. The author has already used it against GD32F1x0 timer, DMA [direct memory access], USART [universal synchronous/asynchronous receiver-transmitter], ADC [analog-to-digital converter], flash, watchdog and clock code, including a test configuration patterned after the hoverboard FOC [field-oriented control] ADC [analog-to-digital converter] setup. It even uncovered a misleading PWM [pulse-width modulation] mode-name mapping when translating from the GD32 SPL [standard peripheral library] to libopencm3. If we migrate the build system or rewrite low-level timer code, this could provide a very good regression check.

A lot of the remaining forks don't contain anything we need to mine. `0voltex0`, `Bluebuddy84`, `MoltenHydrogen`, `Vicky-lp01`, `gggfaffff`, `hexplode13`, `hiliving`, `kneave`, `leideno`, `mike7442`, `nitelife-media`, `ntindle`, `sergioshimura`, `thallesgate`, `timtom04`, `willhess92` and several others either have no unique commits relative to the present upstream or only board-specific configuration changes. I checked non-default branches as well where they existed rather than assuming their `main` branch told the whole story.

So this search has materially changed what I think we should build ourselves. I would keep **our persistent calibration, PA6 thermal characterization, fault architecture and commissioning state machine**, because none of these forks provides the complete architecture we've planned. But I would specifically mine existing code for:

**FOC [field-oriented control] mathematics/tests and Hall interpolation → hoverboardhavoc; exact C8 IMU [inertial measurement unit] and PlatformIO bring-up → hoverboardhavoc; iBUS [FlySky digital serial bus] receiver → Jodaille/EFeru sideboard code; command validation/freshness → WestlingPi + Homobonus; autodetect refinements → lucai11; bench control/logger → hoverboardhavoc + Homobonus; robot/servo protocol concepts → HUGS [Hoverboard Utility Gateway System].**

I think the next useful step is to put these findings into our repository as a **source inventory**, including exact repository, branch/commit, what we intend to reuse or independently reimplement, and whether the code is already upstream. That will keep us from losing these discoveries or accidentally mixing experimental code into the production path. I have **not made any repository changes yet**.
